1
JSS1 ENGLISH STUDIES LESSON NOTES
FIRST TERM
WEEK 1 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO SPEECH; ORGANS OF SPEECH
WHAT IS SPEECH? Speech is the ability to produce sounds using the vocal organs to
communicate ideas, thoughts, and feelings.
Components of Speech:
 Sound production
 Voice modulation
 Articulation (clear pronunciation)
 Intonation (rise and fall of voice)
THE ORGANS OF SPEECH:
1. THE LUNGS:
 Provide air for speech
 Push air through windpipe
 Control breath for speaking
2. THE LARYNX (Voice Box):
 Located in the throat
 Contains vocal cords
 Produces voice/sound
2
 Controls pitch
3. THE VOCAL CORDS:
 Two bands of muscle in larynx
 Vibrate to create sound
 Open for breathing, close for speaking
4. THE PHARYNX (Throat):
 Passage between mouth and larynx
 Resonates sound
 Shapes voice quality
5. THE SOFT PALATE (Velum):
 Soft back part of roof of mouth
 Raises/lowers to direct air
 Controls nasal vs oral sounds
6. THE HARD PALATE:
 Hard front part of roof of mouth
 Helps shape sounds
 Tongue touches it for certain sounds
7. THE TONGUE:
 Most important articulator
 Very flexible, moves in all directions
 Different parts: tip, blade, front, back
 Shapes vowels and consonants
8. THE TEETH:
3
 Upper and lower teeth
 Help produce sounds like /f/, /v/, /θ/, /ð/
 Tongue touches teeth for some sounds
9. THE LIPS:
 Shape the mouth opening
 Round for sounds like /w/, /u:/
 Spread for sounds like /i:/
 Press together for /p/, /b/, /m/
10. THE ALVEOLAR RIDGE:
 Bumpy ridge behind upper teeth
 Tongue touches it for /t/, /d/, /n/, /l/, /s/, /z/
11. THE JAW:
 Opens and closes mouth
 Lowers for open sounds
 Position affects sound quality
12. THE NASAL CAVITY:
 Space behind nose
 Produces nasal sounds /m/, /n/, /ŋ/
 Air passes through for nasal consonants
HOW SPEECH IS PRODUCED:
Three Processes:
1. RESPIRATION (Breathing):
4
 Lungs provide air
 Air pushed up through windpipe
 Controlled exhalation for speech
2. PHONATION (Voice Production):
 Air passes through larynx
 Vocal cords vibrate
 Creates voiced sounds
3. ARTICULATION (Sound Shaping):
 Organs modify airflow
 Tongue, lips, teeth shape sounds
 Creates specific phonemes
TYPES OF SPEECH SOUNDS:
1. VOWELS:
 Air flows freely
 No obstruction
 Voice always used
 Examples: /a/, /e/, /i/, /o/, /u/
2. CONSONANTS:
 Air partially or completely blocked
 Uses articulators (tongue, lips, teeth)
 May be voiced or voiceless
 Examples: /p/, /t/, /k/, /b/, /d/, /g/
5
ARTICULATORS:
Active Articulators (Move):
 Tongue (most mobile)
 Lower lip
 Soft palate
 Lower jaw
Passive Articulators (Stationary):
 Upper teeth
 Alveolar ridge
 Hard palate
 Upper lip (less mobile)
IMPORTANCE OF ORGANS OF SPEECH:
 Enable human communication
 Allow language production
 Differentiate humans from animals
 Create variety of sounds
 Express emotions and ideas
EVALUATION:
1. Define speech and state three components of speech production
2. List and explain the functions of five organs of speech
6
3. Describe the three processes involved in producing speech sounds
4. What is the difference between vowels and consonants in terms of speech production?
5. Draw and label a simple diagram showing five organs of speech
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List all twelve organs of speech and write one sentence describing each organ's function
2. Explain the role of the tongue in speech production (5 sentences)
3. Identify which organs of speech are used to produce these sounds: (a) /p/ (b) /n/ (c) /s/ (d)
/m/ (e) /l/
4. Describe how air flows from the lungs to produce speech sounds (8 sentences)
5. Why are the organs of speech important for communication? Give five reasons
7
WEEK 1 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: PARTS OF SPEECH; NOUNS – MEANING; TYPES OF NOUNS WITH
EXAMPLES
PARTS OF SPEECH:
The parts of speech are the categories into which words are classified based on their function in a
sentence.
The Eight Parts of Speech:
1. Noun – naming word
2. Pronoun – replaces noun
3. Verb – action/state word
4. Adjective – describes noun
5. Adverb – modifies verb/adjective
6. Preposition – shows relationship
7. Conjunction – connects words/sentences
8. Interjection – expresses emotion
NOUNS:
Definition: A noun is a naming word. It names people, places, things, animals, ideas, or qualities.
Examples:
 People: boy, teacher, Mary, doctor
 Places: school, Lagos, market, home
 Things: book, table, car, phone
8
 Animals: dog, cat, lion, bird
 Ideas: love, happiness, freedom, honesty
TYPES OF NOUNS:
1. COMMON NOUNS: General names for people, places, things, or animals. They do not start
with capital letters (unless at the beginning of a sentence).
Examples:
 boy, girl, man, woman, teacher, student
 city, school, market, house, river
 book, pen, car, table, chair
 dog, cat, bird, fish, cow
Sentences:
 The boy went to school
 A teacher gave me a book
 The dog ate its food
2. PROPER NOUNS: Specific names of particular people, places, or things. They always begin
with capital letters.
Examples:
 People: John, Mary, Dr. Adeleke, President Buhari
 Places: Nigeria, Lagos, River Niger, Aso Rock
 Days: Monday, Tuesday, Sunday
 Months: January, February, December
9
 Organizations: United Nations, Red Cross
 Titles: Mr., Mrs., Dr., Chief
Sentences:
 John lives in Lagos
 Mary goes to Queen's College
 We celebrate on Christmas Day
3. ABSTRACT NOUNS: Names of things we cannot see, touch, or measure but can feel or
think about. They represent ideas, qualities, feelings, or states.
Examples:
 Feelings: love, happiness, sadness, anger, joy, fear
 Qualities: honesty, bravery, kindness, wisdom, beauty
 Ideas: freedom, justice, peace, democracy
 States: childhood, sleep, death, life
Sentences:
 Honesty is the best policy
 Love conquers all
 Wisdom comes with age
 His bravery saved many lives
4. CONCRETE NOUNS: Names of things we can see, touch, smell, taste, or hear. They are
physical and tangible.
Examples:
10
 Things we see: tree, house, book, car
 Things we touch: stone, cloth, water
 Things we smell: flower, perfume, food
 Things we taste: sugar, salt, bread
 Things we hear: bell, music, thunder
Sentences:
 The tree is tall
 I touched the stone
 The flower smells sweet
5. COLLECTIVE NOUNS: Names for groups of people, animals, or things considered as one
unit.
Common Examples:
People:
 a team of players
 a class of students
 a crowd of people
 a choir of singers
 an army of soldiers
 a crew of sailors
Animals:
 a herd of cattle/elephants
 a flock of sheep/birds
11
 a pack of wolves/dogs
 a swarm of bees/insects
 a pride of lions
 a school of fish
Things:
 a bunch of keys/flowers
 a bundle of sticks
 a pack of cards
 a library of books
Sentences:
 The team won the match
 A flock of birds flew away
 The class is studying
6. COUNTABLE NOUNS: Nouns that can be counted and have both singular and plural forms.
Examples:
 Singular: book, pen, boy, dog, house
 Plural: books, pens, boys, dogs, houses
Rules:
 Can use numbers: one book, two books, three dogs
 Can use "a/an": a pen, an apple
 Can use "many/few": many books, few students
Sentences:
12
 I have three books
 There are many students in class
 She bought five oranges
7. UNCOUNTABLE NOUNS (Mass Nouns): Nouns that cannot be counted individually. They
have no plural form.
Examples:
 Liquids: water, milk, oil, tea, coffee
 Substances: sugar, salt, rice, flour, sand
 Abstract: advice, information, knowledge, news
 Materials: wood, paper, gold, silver
Rules:
 Cannot use numbers: ✗ two waters, ✗ three rices
 Cannot use "a/an": ✗ a rice, ✗ an advice
 Use "much/little": much water, little sugar
 Use "some/any": some rice, any milk
Sentences:
 I need some water
 She gave me good advice
 There is little sugar left
Note: To count uncountable nouns, use measurement words:
 a cup of tea, two cups of tea
 a bag of rice, three bags of rice
13
 a piece of advice, some pieces of advice
FORMING PLURALS:
Regular Rules:
 Add -s: book → books, pen → pens
 Add -es (ending in s, x, z, ch, sh): class → classes, box → boxes
 Consonant + y → change y to ies: baby → babies, city → cities
 Vowel + y → add s: boy → boys, key → keys
 f/fe → change to ves: knife → knives, wife → wives
Irregular Plurals:
 man → men, woman → women, child → children
 tooth → teeth, foot → feet, mouse → mice
 sheep → sheep, fish → fish, deer → deer
EVALUATION:
1. Define noun and give five examples of each type: common, proper, and abstract nouns
2. What is the difference between countable and uncountable nouns? Give three examples
of each
3. Write the collective nouns for: (a) sheep (b) cattle (c) students (d) bees (e) singers
4. Identify the types of nouns in these sentences: "John showed great courage when he
saved the child from the fire"
5. Write the plural forms of: (a) baby (b) knife (c) sheep (d) woman (e) box
14
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List the eight parts of speech with one example for each
2. Write ten common nouns, ten proper nouns, and ten abstract nouns
3. Give five examples of collective nouns and use each in a sentence
4. Explain the difference between concrete and abstract nouns with five examples each
5. Identify and classify all nouns in this passage: "Mary went to the market in Lagos with
her mother. They bought rice, tomatoes, and fish. The crowd was large, and Mary felt
happiness when they returned home."
WEEK 1 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: THE FAMILY; VOCABULARY – INTRODUCTION TO LANGUAGE SKILLS
(LSRW)
PART A: THE FAMILY
DEFINITION: A family is a group of people related by blood, marriage, or adoption who live
together or maintain close relationships.
TYPES OF FAMILIES
1. Nuclear Family
 Father, mother, and children
 Small family unit
 Common in urban areas
2. Extended Family
 Includes grandparents, aunts, uncles, cousins
15
 Large family unit
 Several generations together
 Common in African culture
3. Single-Parent Family
 One parent raising children alone
 Due to death, divorce, or choice
4. Blended/Step Family
 Parents remarried with children from previous marriages
 Children have step-parents and step-siblings
FAMILY MEMBERS AND RELATIONSHIPS
Immediate Family:
 Father/Dad/Papa – male parent
 Mother/Mum/Mama – female parent
 Son – male child
 Daughter – female child
 Brother – male sibling
 Sister – female sibling
Extended Family:
 Grandfather/Grandpa – father's/mother's father
 Grandmother/Grandma – father's/mother's mother
 Uncle – father's/mother's brother, aunt's husband
 Aunt/Aunty – father's/mother's sister, uncle's wife
16
 Cousin – uncle's/aunt's child
 Nephew – brother's/sister's son
 Niece – brother's/sister's daughter
 In-laws – spouse's family (father-in-law, mother-in-law, brother-in-law, sister-in-law)
FAMILY ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES
Father:
 Provides for family
 Protects family members
 Guides and disciplines children
 Makes important decisions
 Role model
Mother:
 Cares for children
 Manages household
 Prepares meals
 Nurtures and comforts
 Teaches values
Children:
 Respect and obey parents
 Help with household chores
 Study and attend school
 Care for younger siblings
17
 Learn family values
IMPORTANCE OF FAMILY
 Provides love and security
 Teaches values and morals
 Offers emotional support
 Meets basic needs (food, shelter, clothing)
 Preserves culture and traditions
 Prepares children for society
 Creates sense of belonging
PART B: INTRODUCTION TO LANGUAGE SKILLS (LSRW)
THE FOUR LANGUAGE SKILLS: Abilities needed to communicate effectively
TWO CATEGORIES:
RECEPTIVE SKILLS (Input) – Receiving and understanding information PRODUCTIVE
SKILLS (Output) – Producing and expressing information
1. LISTENING (Receptive)
Definition: Receiving and understanding spoken information
Activities:
 Listening to teacher in class
 Following instructions
 Listening to radio/news
18
 Understanding conversations
How to Improve:
 Pay attention when others speak
 Avoid distractions
 Ask questions if unclear
 Practice active listening
 Listen to English programs
Importance:
 Understand instructions
 Learn from others
 Build relationships
 Avoid misunderstandings
2. READING (Receptive)
Definition: Understanding written information
Types:
 Reading aloud (oral)
 Silent reading
 Intensive reading (detailed)
 Extensive reading (general)
Activities:
 Reading textbooks
 Reading newspapers and stories
19
 Reading instructions
How to Improve:
 Read daily
 Read various materials
 Look up new words
 Practice pronunciation
 Read fiction and non-fiction
Importance:
 Gain knowledge
 Improve vocabulary
 Enjoy literature
 Access information
3. SPEAKING (Productive)
Definition: Expressing ideas orally
Types:
 Formal speaking (presentations, speeches)
 Informal speaking (conversations)
 Monologue (one person)
 Dialogue (conversation)
Activities:
 Answering questions in class
 Giving presentations
20
 Telling stories
 Participating in discussions
How to Improve:
 Practice speaking English daily
 Participate in class discussions
 Join debate clubs
 Record and listen to yourself
 Learn correct pronunciation
Importance:
 Express thoughts and feelings
 Participate in discussions
 Build confidence
 Make presentations
4. WRITING (Productive)
Definition: Expressing ideas in written form
Types:
 Formal writing (letters, reports, essays)
 Informal writing (notes, messages)
 Creative writing (stories, poems)
 Academic writing (assignments, exams)
Activities:
 Writing essays and letters
21
 Taking notes
 Answering exam questions
 Writing stories
How to Improve:
 Write daily (diary, journal)
 Practice different types of writing
 Read to improve vocabulary
 Learn grammar rules
 Proofread your work
Importance:
 Record information
 Communicate formally
 Express creativity
 Demonstrate knowledge
RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN THE FOUR SKILLS
Integration: All skills work together for effective communication
 Listening → Speaking (hear, then respond)
 Reading → Writing (read examples, then write)
 Listening/Reading → Understanding (input leads to comprehension)
 Speaking/Writing → Expression (output shows learning)
Order of Acquisition: Natural language learning order
1. Listening (babies listen first)
22
2. Speaking (then start talking)
3. Reading (learn to read)
4. Writing (learn to write)
SUMMARY TABLE
Skill Type Definition Example Activities
Listening Receptive Understanding spoken
language
Following instructions, listening to
news
Reading Receptive Understanding written
language
Reading books, newspapers, textbooks
Speaking Productive Expressing ideas orally Presentations, discussions,
conversations
Writing Productive Expressing ideas in writing Essays, letters, notes, stories
EVALUATION:
1. Define family and name 3 types with examples
2. List 5 extended family members and explain their relationships
3. What are the four language skills? Group them into receptive and productive
4. Explain 3 responsibilities of parents in a family
5. How can you improve: (a) listening skills (b) reading skills (c) speaking skills?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Draw your family tree showing at least 10 family members with their relationships to you
2. Write a paragraph (10 sentences) describing your family (type, members, roles)
23
3. Explain the four language skills (LSRW) with 2 examples of activities for each
4. List 5 ways each skill is important: (a) Listening (b) Reading (c) Speaking (d) Writing
5. Describe how the four language skills work together in a classroom setting (8 sentences)
24
4. COMPOSITION
WEEK 1: MEANING OF COMPOSITION; TYPES – NARRATIVE, DESCRIPTIVE,
ARGUMENTATIVE, EXPOSITORY
CONTENT:
WHAT IS COMPOSITION?
Definition: Composition is the art of putting words together in a structured and organized way to
express ideas, thoughts, or tell a story. It is written expression of ideas in sentences and
paragraphs.
Other Names:
 Essay writing
 Continuous writing
 Creative writing
Purpose:
 Express ideas clearly
 Communicate thoughts
 Demonstrate language skills
 Inform, entertain, or persuade
CHARACTERISTICS OF GOOD COMPOSITION:
1. Clear Purpose: Know what you want to achieve
2. Good Organization: Logical arrangement of ideas
3. Unity: All parts relate to main idea
25
4. Coherence: Ideas flow smoothly
5. Correct Grammar: Proper sentence structure
6. Appropriate Vocabulary: Right words for the topic
7. Proper Punctuation: Correct use of marks
8. Good Paragraphing: Ideas grouped logically
TYPES OF COMPOSITION:
1. NARRATIVE COMPOSITION (NARRATIVE ESSAY):
Definition: A narrative composition tells a story or relates events in chronological order (time
sequence). It has characters, setting, plot, and usually teaches a lesson.
Characteristics:
 Tells a story
 Has beginning, middle, end
 Uses past tense (usually)
 Includes characters and dialogue
 Has a plot (sequence of events)
 Often written in first person (I, we)
 Descriptive and engaging
Purpose:
 Tell a story
 Share personal experiences
 Entertain readers
 Teach lessons through stories
26
Common Topics:
 My First Day at School
 A Day I Will Never Forget
 My Last Holiday
 An Unforgettable Experience
 The Happiest Day of My Life
 A Journey I Made
Key Elements:
 Characters: People in the story (I, my friend, my mother)
 Setting: Where and when the story happens
 Plot: What happens (events in order)
 Climax: Most exciting part
 Lesson: What was learned
Example Opening: "I will never forget the day I got lost in the market. It was a bright Saturday
morning in December when my mother and I went to buy Christmas items..."
Useful Expressions:
 Once upon a time...
 One day...
 Last week/month/year...
 First, Then, Next, After that, Finally
 Suddenly, Immediately, Later
 At that moment, Meanwhile
27
2. DESCRIPTIVE COMPOSITION (DESCRIPTIVE ESSAY):
Definition: A descriptive composition paints a picture with words. It describes a person, place,
object, or event in detail using sensory language (what you see, hear, smell, taste, touch).
Characteristics:
 Creates vivid mental images
 Uses sensory details (five senses)
 Rich in adjectives and adverbs
 Present tense (usually)
 Organized spatially (top to bottom, left to right)
 Focuses on details
Purpose:
 Describe something clearly
 Help readers visualize
 Create mental pictures
 Appeal to senses
Common Topics:
 My School
 My Best Friend
 My Village/Town
 My Bedroom
 A Market Scene
 A Festival
 An Interesting Place I Visited
28
Key Elements:
 Physical appearance: What it looks like
 Size and shape: Dimensions and form
 Color: Shades and hues
 Texture: How it feels
 Sounds: What you hear
 Smells: What you smell
 Tastes: What you taste (if applicable)
 Location: Where things are positioned
Example Opening: "My school stands majestically at the end of Kings Road. The large, whitepainted
building has two stories, with wide windows that shine brightly in the morning sun..."
Useful Expressions:
 It looks like...
 It is located...
 To the left/right of...
 In the center/middle...
 Surrounding it are...
 The most striking feature is...
 Beautiful, colorful, magnificent, enormous
 Bright, sparkling, gleaming, radiant
29
3. ARGUMENTATIVE COMPOSITION (ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY):
Definition: An argumentative composition presents a position on a topic and supports it with
reasons, evidence, and examples to convince readers. It includes opposing views and refutes
them.
Characteristics:
 States a clear position/thesis
 Presents arguments with evidence
 Addresses counter-arguments
 Uses logical reasoning
 Formal tone
 Present tense (usually)
 Persuasive language
Purpose:
 Convince readers
 Present and defend a viewpoint
 Show logical thinking
 Persuade through reasoning
Common Topics:
 School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory
 Television Does More Harm Than Good
 Boarding School is Better Than Day School
 Sports Are More Important Than Academics
 Mobile Phones Should Be Banned in Schools
30
 Capital Punishment Should Be Abolished
Structure:
 Introduction: State your position (thesis)
 Body Paragraph 1-3: Present arguments with evidence
 Counter-argument: Present opposing view
 Refutation: Explain why opposing view is wrong
 Conclusion: Restate position, summarize arguments
Example Opening: "School uniforms should be compulsory in all schools. While some argue
that uniforms limit self-expression, the benefits far outweigh this concern. This essay will
demonstrate why uniforms promote equality, discipline, and school identity..."
Useful Expressions:
 I strongly believe that...
 It is evident that...
 First/Firstly, Second/Secondly, Finally
 For example, For instance
 However, Nevertheless, On the other hand
 Some people argue that...
 This view is flawed because...
 In conclusion, Therefore, Thus
4. EXPOSITORY COMPOSITION (EXPOSITORY ESSAY):
31
Definition: An expository composition explains, informs, or describes a topic clearly and
objectively using facts, statistics, and examples. It does not include personal opinions or try to
persuade.
Characteristics:
 Informative and educational
 Objective (factual, no opinions)
 Clear and organized
 Uses facts and examples
 Formal tone
 Third person (he, she, it, they)
 Present tense (usually)
Purpose:
 Explain how something works
 Inform about a topic
 Describe a process
 Define concepts
 Compare and contrast
Common Topics:
 How to Prepare Jollof Rice
 The Importance of Education
 Types of Transportation
 HIV/AIDS: Understanding the Disease
 The Water Cycle
32
 Causes of Road Accidents
 Benefits of Reading
Types of Expository Essays:
 Process (How-to): Explains steps (How to make...)"
 Cause and Effect: Shows relationships (Causes of...Effects of...)
 Compare and Contrast: Shows similarities/differences
 Problem-Solution: Presents problem and solutions
 Definition: Explains what something means
Example Opening: "Education is the foundation of personal and national development. It equips
individuals with knowledge, skills, and values necessary for productive living. This essay
examines the importance of education in modern society..."
Useful Expressions:
 This essay explains...
 There are several types/kinds...
 First, Second, Third, Finally
 The process involves...
 As a result, Consequently, Therefore
 For example, For instance, Such as
 In addition, Furthermore, Moreover
 In summary, In conclusion
COMPARISON OF TYPES:
Type Purpose Tone Person Tense
33
Narrative Tell story Personal, engaging First person (I, we) Past
Descriptive Describe/paint picture Vivid, sensory First/third person Present
Argumentative Persuade/convince Formal, persuasive Third person mainly Present
Expository Explain/inform Objective, formal Third person Present
CHOOSING THE RIGHT TYPE:
Question/Topic Analysis:
 "Describe..." → Descriptive
 "Narrate...," "Tell a story..." → Narrative
 "Do you agree...?," "Should...?" → Argumentative
 "Explain...," "Discuss...," "What is...?" → Expository
EVALUATION:
1. Define composition and state four characteristics of good composition
2. Explain the four types of composition with one example topic for each
3. What is the difference between narrative and descriptive composition?
4. List five topics suitable for: (a) narrative composition (b) argumentative composition
5. Which type of composition would you use for these topics: (a) My Last Birthday (b) Why
Students Should Read More (c) The Market Near My House (d) How to Make Fried Rice?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Create a comparison table showing the four types of composition with their: (a) definition
(b) purpose (c) characteristics (d) common topics
34
2. Write the opening paragraph (5 sentences) for: (a) A narrative essay about "My First Day
at School" (b) A descriptive essay about "My Classroom"
3. List ten transitional expressions commonly used in: (a) narrative composition (b)
argumentative composition
4. Explain three differences between expository and argumentative composition
5. For each topic, identify the type of composition: (a) Life in the Village (b) A Narrow
Escape (c) Television is Harmful to Children (d) The Causes of Examination Malpractice
(e) An Exciting Football Match
35
5. LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
WEEK 1: WHAT IS LITERATURE?
CONTENT:
DEFINITION OF LITERATURE:
Literature is the art of written works that express ideas, emotions, and experiences in beautiful,
imaginative, and meaningful ways. It is creative writing that has lasting artistic value.
Simple Definition: Literature is any written work that is considered to have artistic or
intellectual value.
CHARACTERISTICS OF LITERATURE:
1. CREATIVE/IMAGINATIVE:
 Uses imagination and creativity
 Not just stating facts
 Creates new worlds and characters
 Original and unique
2. ARTISTIC:
 Beautiful use of language
 Careful choice of words
 Attention to style and form
 Aesthetic appeal
3. MEANINGFUL:
 Conveys important messages
36
 Explores human experiences
 Teaches lessons about life
 Has depth and significance
4. PERMANENT/LASTING:
 Survives across time
 Relevant to different generations
 Universal themes
 Enduring value
5. EMOTIONAL:
 Appeals to feelings
 Evokes emotions in readers
 Makes readers laugh, cry, think
 Creates connections
6. ENTERTAINING:
 Provides pleasure
 Engages readers
 Holds attention
 Makes reading enjoyable
PURPOSES/FUNCTIONS OF LITERATURE:
1. ENTERTAINMENT:
 Provides enjoyment and pleasure
 Offers escape from reality
37
 Makes leisure time valuable
 Creates memorable experiences
2. EDUCATION:
 Teaches about life, culture, history
 Increases knowledge
 Develops vocabulary
 Improves language skills
3. MORAL INSTRUCTION:
 Teaches right from wrong
 Shows consequences of actions
 Develops character
 Instills values
4. CULTURAL PRESERVATION:
 Preserves traditions and customs
 Records history
 Passes knowledge to next generation
 Maintains cultural identity
5. SOCIAL COMMENTARY:
 Reflects society's problems
 Criticizes social ills
 Promotes social change
 Raises awareness
6. EMOTIONAL EXPRESSION:
38
 Allows writers to express feelings
 Helps readers understand emotions
 Provides emotional release
 Creates empathy
SOURCES OF LITERATURE:
1. PERSONAL EXPERIENCE:
 Writer's own life events
 Autobiographical elements
 Direct observations
2. IMAGINATION:
 Creative invention
 Fantasy and fiction
 What-if scenarios
3. HISTORY:
 Past events
 Historical figures
 Real occurrences
4. CULTURE AND TRADITION:
 Folktales and myths
 Cultural practices
 Traditional beliefs
5. SOCIETY:
39
 Current events
 Social issues
 Community experiences
6. OTHER LITERATURE:
 Influenced by other works
 Adaptations and retellings
 Literary traditions
QUALITIES OF GOOD LITERATURE:
1. UNIVERSALITY:
 Appeals to people everywhere
 Transcends time and place
 Deals with universal human experiences
 Relevant across cultures
2. ORIGINALITY:
 Unique and creative
 Fresh perspective
 Not copied or imitated
 Original voice and style
3. INTELLECTUAL VALUE:
 Makes readers think
 Raises important questions
 Explores complex ideas
40
 Stimulates the mind
4. ARTISTIC MERIT:
 Beautiful language
 Well-crafted structure
 Effective use of literary devices
 Stylistic excellence
5. EMOTIONAL APPEAL:
 Touches the heart
 Evokes genuine feelings
 Creates emotional impact
 Memorable and moving
6. TRUTHFULNESS:
 Authentic portrayal of life
 Honest exploration of human nature
 Believable characters and situations
 Rings true to experience
LITERATURE VS NON-LITERATURE:
LITERATURE:
 Creative and imaginative
 Artistic use of language
 Focuses on human experiences
 Has lasting value
41
 Examples: novels, poems, plays, short stories
NON-LITERATURE:
 Factual and informative
 Straightforward language
 Focuses on information/facts
 May have temporary value
 Examples: textbooks, news articles, instruction manuals, reports
WHY STUDY LITERATURE?
1. LANGUAGE DEVELOPMENT:
 Improves vocabulary
 Enhances grammar
 Develops writing skills
 Improves communication
2. CULTURAL UNDERSTANDING:
 Learn about different cultures
 Understand traditions
 Appreciate diversity
 Build tolerance
3. CRITICAL THINKING:
 Analyze and interpret
 Make connections
 Evaluate ideas
42
 Think deeply
4. EMPATHY:
 Understand others' perspectives
 Experience different viewpoints
 Develop compassion
 Connect with humanity
5. IMAGINATION:
 Stimulates creativity
 Encourages original thinking
 Expands possibilities
 Inspires innovation
6. LIFE LESSONS:
 Learn from characters' experiences
 Understand consequences
 Make better decisions
 Grow personally
7. ENTERTAINMENT:
 Enjoyable and pleasurable
 Escape and relaxation
 Fun and engaging
 Lifelong hobby
LITERATURE AND LIFE:
43
Literature Reflects Life:
 Shows real human experiences
 Mirrors society's problems and joys
 Presents true emotions
 Depicts real relationships
Literature Shapes Life:
 Influences how we think
 Changes perspectives
 Inspires actions
 Molds character
Literature Enriches Life:
 Makes life more meaningful
 Provides beauty and pleasure
 Offers wisdom and insight
 Creates shared experiences
ELEMENTS THAT MAKE WRITING LITERARY:
1. FIGURATIVE LANGUAGE:
 Metaphors, similes, personification
 Creates vivid images
 Adds depth and beauty
2. SYMBOLISM:
 Objects represent ideas
44
 Deeper meanings
 Layered significance
3. THEME:
 Central message or idea
 Universal truths
 Meaningful insights
4. CHARACTERIZATION:
 Well-developed characters
 Realistic personalities
 Character growth
5. PLOT/STRUCTURE:
 Organized sequence
 Engaging storyline
 Effective pacing
6. STYLE:
 Distinctive voice
 Unique expression
 Artistic presentation
EVALUATION:

2. Explain five purposes or functions of literature with examples

45

examples, and lasting value

WEEK 2 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: PURE VOWEL SOUNDS (MONOPHTHONGS) WITH EXAMPLES
CONTENT:
WHAT ARE VOWELS?
Vowels are speech sounds produced with an open vocal tract. Air flows freely from the lungs
through the mouth without obstruction.
Characteristics:
 Voice is always used (voiced sounds)
 No blockage of airflow
 Tongue position varies but doesn't block
 Form the nucleus of syllables
 More sonorous than consonants
TYPES OF VOWELS:
1. PURE VOWELS (MONOPHTHONGS): Single vowel sounds where the tongue stays in
one position throughout pronunciation.
2. DIPHTHONGS: Vowel sounds that glide from one position to another (studied later).
THE TWELVE PURE VOWEL SOUNDS IN ENGLISH:
SHORT VOWELS (7):
1. /ɪ/ (as in "sit"):
 Short, quick sound
47
 Tongue high and front
 Lips slightly spread
 Examples: sit, bit, his, give, women, busy, England
2. /e/ (as in "bed"):
 Short sound
 Tongue mid-front
 Lips spread
 Examples: bed, red, pen, any, said, friend, bread
3. /æ/ (as in "cat"):
 Short sound
 Tongue low-front
 Mouth open fairly wide
 Examples: cat, hat, man, bad, apple, happy, family
4. /ɒ/ (as in "hot"):
 Short sound
 Tongue low-back
 Lips slightly rounded
 Examples: hot, pot, dog, want, wash, because (British)
5. /ʊ/ (as in "book"):
 Short sound
 Tongue high-back
 Lips rounded
 Examples: book, look, good, could, put, woman
48
6. /ʌ/ (as in "cup"):
 Short sound
 Tongue mid-central
 Lips neutral
 Examples: cup, but, sun, love, come, blood, done
7. /ə/ (schwa - as in "about"):
 The most common English sound
 Very short and weak
 Only in unstressed syllables
 Tongue central
 Examples: about, again, teacher, doctor, banana (last 'a')
LONG VOWELS (5):
1. /iː/ (as in "see"):
 Long sound
 Tongue very high-front
 Lips spread and tense
 Examples: see, meet, tea, he, people, key, field
2. /ɑː/ (as in "car"):
 Long sound
 Tongue low-back
 Mouth wide open
 Examples: car, father, arm, heart, class, grass (British)
49
3. /ɔː/ (as in "door"):
 Long sound
 Tongue mid-back
 Lips rounded
 Examples: door, four, ball, thought, daughter, walk, war
4. /uː/ (as in "too"):
 Long sound
 Tongue high-back
 Lips tightly rounded
 Examples: too, food, blue, true, move, soup, through
5. /ɜː/ (as in "bird"):
 Long sound
 Tongue mid-central
 Lips neutral
 Examples: bird, her, turn, work, learn, journey
KEY DIFFERENCES:
Short vs Long:
 Short: Quick, unstressed, relaxed
 Long: Prolonged, can be stressed, tense
Front vs Back:
 Front vowels: Tongue toward front (/iː/, /ɪ/, /e/, /æ/)
 Back vowels: Tongue toward back (/ɑː/, /ɒ/, /ɔː/, /ʊ/, /uː/, /ʌ/)
50
 Central vowels: Tongue in center (/ə/, /ɜː/)
Rounded vs Unrounded:
 Rounded: Lips rounded (/ɒ/, /ɔː/, /ʊ/, /uː/)
 Unrounded: Lips spread or neutral (most others)
THE VOWEL CHART:
Position by Tongue Height:
 High/Close: /iː/, /ɪ/, /ʊ/, /uː/
 Mid: /e/, /ə/, /ɜː/, /ɔː/, /ʌ/
 Low/Open: /æ/, /ɑː/, /ɒ/
Position by Tongue Placement:
 Front: /iː/, /ɪ/, /e/, /æ/
 Central: /ə/, /ɜː/, /ʌ/
 Back: /ɑː/, /ɒ/, /ɔː/, /ʊ/, /uː/
PRACTICE WORDS:
Minimal Pairs (words differing by one vowel):
 sit /ɪ/ - seat /iː/
 bit /ɪ/ - beat /iː/
 pet /e/ - pat /æ/
 cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔː/
 full /ʊ/ - fool /uː/
 cup /ʌ/ - carp /ɑː/
51
COMMON SPELLING PATTERNS:
/iː/: ee (see), ea (tea), e (he), ie (field), ei (receive) /ɪ/: i (sit), y (happy), e (England) /e/: e (bed),
ea (head), a (any) /æ/: a (cat) /ɑː/: a (father), ar (car), ear (heart) /ɒ/: o (hot), a (want) /ɔː/: or
(door), au (daughter), aw (law), ough (thought) /ʊ/: oo (book), u (put), ou (could) /uː/: oo (food),
u (true), ue (blue), ou (soup) /ʌ/: u (cup), o (love), ou (country) /ɜː/: ir (bird), er (her), ur (turn),
or (work), ear (learn) /ə/: a (about), e (teacher), o (doctor)
PRONUNCIATION TIPS:
1. Length matters: Hold long vowels longer
2. Tongue position: Practice front, central, back placement
3. Lip shape: Round or spread as needed
4. Stress: Vowels in stressed syllables are clearer
5. Context: Same letter can make different sounds
6. Practice: Use minimal pairs to hear differences
EVALUATION:
1. What is a monophthong/pure vowel? How many pure vowels are in English?
2. List the five long vowel sounds with three examples for each
3. Pronounce and identify the vowel sounds in: (a) seat (b) book (c) bird (d) father (e) pot
4. What is the difference between /ɪ/ and /iː/? Give two examples of each
5. Write five minimal pairs showing different pure vowel sounds
ASSIGNMENT:
52
1. Create a table showing all twelve pure vowels with: (a) phonetic symbol (b) example
word (c) whether short or long (d) tongue position
2. List ten words each containing: (a) /iː/ sound (b) /ɑː/ sound (c) /ɔː/ sound
3. Identify all pure vowel sounds in this sentence: "The good teacher put the book on the
seat"
4. Explain the difference between short and long vowels with five examples
5. Practice and write ten minimal pairs (words that differ by only one vowel sound)
53
WEEK 2: GRAMMAR
TOPIC: PRONOUNS – MEANING, IDENTIFICATION, TYPES
CONTENT:
WHAT IS A PRONOUN?
Definition: A pronoun is a word used in place of a noun to avoid repetition. It refers to a person,
place, thing, or idea without naming it again.
Purpose:
 Avoid repeating nouns
 Make sentences flow better
 Make language less awkward
 Create variety
Example:
 Without pronoun: John went to John's school. John met John's friend there.
 With pronoun: John went to his school. He met his friend there.
THE ANTECEDENT:
The antecedent is the noun that the pronoun replaces or refers to.
Example:
 Mary lost her book. (Mary = antecedent; her = pronoun)
 The boys played until they were tired. (boys = antecedent; they = pronoun)
TYPES OF PRONOUNS:
54
1. PERSONAL PRONOUNS:
Refer to specific persons or things. They change form based on:
 Person (1st, 2nd, 3rd)
 Number (singular, plural)
 Case (subject, object, possessive)
Subject Pronouns (do the action):
 Singular: I, you, he, she, it
 Plural: we, you, they
Examples:
 I am a student
 She reads books
 They play football
Object Pronouns (receive the action):
 Singular: me, you, him, her, it
 Plural: us, you, them
Examples:
 The teacher called me
 I saw him yesterday
 Give it to them
Chart:
Person Subject Object
1st Singular I me
55
2nd Singular you you
3rd Singular (male) he him
3rd Singular (female) she her
3rd Singular (thing) it it
1st Plural we us
2nd Plural you you
3rd Plural they them
2. POSSESSIVE PRONOUNS:
Show ownership or possession.
Two Forms:
Possessive Adjectives (before nouns):
 my, your, his, her, its, our, your, their
Examples:
 My book is new
 Her mother is a teacher
 Their house is big
Possessive Pronouns (stand alone):
 mine, yours, his, hers, ours, yours, theirs
Examples:
 This book is mine
 The car is theirs
 Is this pen yours?
56
Note: "Its" (possessive adjective) vs "It's" (it is) - don't confuse!
3. REFLEXIVE PRONOUNS:
Refer back to the subject. Formed by adding -self (singular) or -selves (plural).
List:
 myself, yourself, himself, herself, itself
 ourselves, yourselves, themselves
Uses:
a) When subject and object are the same:
 I hurt myself
 She looked at herself in the mirror
 They enjoyed themselves
b) For emphasis:
 I myself did the work
 The president himself came
 She herself cooked the food
Examples:
 He taught himself to play guitar
 We prepared ourselves for the exam
 The cat cleaned itself
Common Mistake:
 ✗ Me and John went (should be: John and I)
57
 ✗ Myself will come (should be: I will come)
4. DEMONSTRATIVE PRONOUNS:
Point to specific things. Show whether something is near or far, singular or plural.
List:
 this (singular, near)
 that (singular, far)
 these (plural, near)
 those (plural, far)
Examples:
 This is my book (near me)
 That is your house (far from me)
 These are fresh fruits (near, plural)
 Those are old buildings (far, plural)
Usage:
 As pronouns: This is good (stands alone)
 As adjectives: This book is good (before noun - technically "demonstrative adjective")
5. INTERROGATIVE PRONOUNS:
Used to ask questions about people or things.
List:
 who (asks about people - subject)
58
 whom (asks about people - object)
 whose (asks about possession)
 which (asks about choice/selection)
 what (asks about things/information)
Examples:
 Who is that man?
 Whom did you see?
 Whose book is this?
 Which do you prefer?
 What happened?
Who vs Whom:
 Who: Subject of verb (Who called? - who is doing the calling)
 Whom: Object of verb (Whom did you call? - whom receives the action)
6. RELATIVE PRONOUNS:
Connect clauses and relate back to a noun (antecedent) mentioned earlier. Introduce relative
clauses.
List:
 who (for people - subject)
 whom (for people - object)
 whose (possession)
 which (for things/animals)
 that (for people, things, animals)
59
Examples:
 The boy who won the race is my friend (who = the boy)
 The book which I bought is interesting (which = the book)
 The man whose car was stolen reported to police (whose shows possession)
 The dog that barked is big (that = the dog)
Differences:
 Who/Whom: People only
 Which: Things/animals
 That: People, things, animals (more flexible)
 Whose: Shows possession
7. INDEFINITE PRONOUNS:
Refer to non-specific persons or things. Don't identify exactly who or what.
Common Indefinite Pronouns:
Referring to people:
 someone, somebody, anyone, anybody, no one, nobody, everyone, everybody
Referring to things:
 something, anything, nothing, everything
Referring to quantity:
 some, any, none, all, both, few, several, many, much, more, most, each, either, neither,
one, other, another
Examples:
 Everyone is present
60
 Someone knocked on the door
 Nothing is impossible
 Few understand this
 Each has a role to play
 Neither is correct
Agreement:
 Most indefinite pronouns are singular: Everyone is here (not "are")
 Some are plural: few, many, several, both
 Some can be both: all, some, none (depends on context)
8. RECIPROCAL PRONOUNS:
Show mutual action between two or more people.
List:
 each other (two people)
 one another (more than two people)
Examples:
 John and Mary love each other (two people)
 The students help one another (more than two)
 They greeted each other
Note: Some grammar books say this distinction isn't strict anymore, and both can be used
interchangeably.
PRONOUN AGREEMENT:
61
Pronouns must agree with their antecedents in:
1. Number (singular/plural):
 The boy lost his book (singular)
 The boys lost their books (plural)
2. Gender (male/female/neutral):
 John did his homework (male)
 Mary did her homework (female)
 The dog wagged its tail (neutral)
3. Person (1st, 2nd, 3rd):
 I finished my work (1st person)
 You finished your work (2nd person)
 She finished her work (3rd person)
COMMON PRONOUN ERRORS:
1. Wrong case:
 ✗ Me and John went (should be: John and I)
 ✗ Between you and I (should be: Between you and me)
2. Unclear antecedent:
 ✗ John told Peter that he was late (Who was late? Unclear)
 ✓ John told Peter, "I am late" or "You are late"
3. Agreement error:
62
 ✗ Everyone brought their book (should be: his/her book - technically, though "their" is
increasingly accepted)
 ✗ The team won their match (should be: its match - collective noun is singular)
4. Reflexive misuse:
 ✗ Myself and Mary will go (should be: Mary and I)
 ✗ Give it to myself (should be: Give it to me)
EVALUATION:
1. Define pronoun and explain its purpose with three examples
2. List and explain five types of pronouns with two examples each
3. What is the difference between: (a) subject and object pronouns (b) possessive adjectives
and possessive pronouns?
4. Fill in with correct pronouns: (a) Mary lost _____ book (b) The boys enjoyed _____ (c)
This pen is _____ (mine/me)
5. Correct these errors: (a) Me and John went home (b) The dog wagged it's tail (c)
Everyone brought their books
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Create a chart showing all personal pronouns (subject, object, possessive adjective,
possessive pronoun) for all persons and numbers
2. Write ten sentences using different types of pronouns. Identify the type and underline the
antecedent
3. Explain five types of pronouns not covered in personal pronouns (demonstrative,
interrogative, relative, indefinite, reflexive) with three examples each
63
4. Identify all pronouns in this passage and classify them: "I saw Mary yesterday. She told
me that her brother lost his book. This made him very sad. They searched everywhere but
found nothing. Someone must have taken it."
5. Correct the pronoun errors in these sentences: (a) Him and me are friends (b) The teacher
gave we the books (c) John hurt hisself (d) Between you and I (e) Everyone must do their
best
64
WEEK 2 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: READING STRATEGIES
CONTENT:
WHAT ARE READING STRATEGIES?
Reading strategies are techniques or methods used to improve understanding, speed, and
retention when reading. They help you become an active, efficient reader.
Why Use Reading Strategies?
 Improve comprehension
 Increase reading speed
 Remember information better
 Identify important points
 Make reading purposeful
 Enjoy reading more
TYPES OF READING:
1. SKIMMING:
Definition: Reading quickly to get the general idea or main points without reading every word.
Purpose:
 Get overview of text
 Decide if text is relevant
 Find specific section
 Preview before detailed reading
65
How to Skim:
 Read title and headings
 Read first and last paragraphs
 Read first sentence of each paragraph
 Look at pictures, graphs, charts
 Notice bold/italicized words
 Don't read every word
When to Skim:
 Previewing a chapter
 Reading newspaper headlines
 Browsing through book in library
 Deciding what to read in detail
Example: Before reading a chapter on "The Water Cycle," skim by reading the title, headings
(Evaporation, Condensation, Precipitation), first paragraph, and last paragraph to get the general
idea.
2. SCANNING:
Definition: Looking quickly through text to find specific information without reading everything.
Purpose:
 Find particular fact, number, name, date
 Locate specific information quickly
 Answer specific questions
How to Scan:
66
 Know what you're looking for
 Move eyes quickly over page
 Look for keywords
 Don't read sentences fully
 Stop when you find information
When to Scan:
 Looking up word in dictionary
 Finding phone number in directory
 Locating date in text
 Searching for specific fact
Example: To find "When was Nigeria's independence?", scan the text looking for numbers that
might be years (1960), or the word "independence."
3. INTENSIVE READING:
Definition: Reading carefully and slowly to understand every detail.
Purpose:
 Understand completely
 Learn new information thoroughly
 Prepare for exams
 Analyze text deeply
How to Read Intensively:
 Read slowly and carefully
 Read every word
67
 Look up unknown words
 Take notes
 Reread difficult parts
 Analyze meaning
When to Read Intensively:
 Studying textbooks
 Reading exam questions
 Following instructions
 Learning new concepts
Example: Reading a mathematics problem or science experiment instructions requires intensive
reading to understand every step.
4. EXTENSIVE READING:
Definition: Reading longer texts for general understanding and enjoyment without focusing on
every detail.
Purpose:
 Develop reading habit
 Improve overall reading ability
 Enjoy literature
 Build vocabulary naturally
How to Read Extensively:
 Read for pleasure
 Don't stop for every unknown word
68
 Focus on overall meaning
 Read at comfortable pace
 Choose interesting materials
When to Read Extensively:
 Reading novels/stories for fun
 Reading magazines
 Leisure reading
 Building reading stamina
Example: Reading a Harry Potter book for enjoyment, not worrying about every single word,
but understanding the story.
BEFORE READING STRATEGIES:
1. PREVIEW:
 Look at title, headings, pictures
 Check length
 Read introduction/conclusion
 Get general sense of content
2. SET PURPOSE:
 Why am I reading this?
 What do I want to learn?
 What questions do I want answered?
3. ACTIVATE PRIOR KNOWLEDGE:
 What do I already know about this topic?
69
 Have I read something similar?
 What do I expect to learn?
4. PREDICT:
 What will this be about?
 What might happen?
 What questions might be answered?
DURING READING STRATEGIES:
1. MONITOR UNDERSTANDING:
 Am I understanding this?
 Does this make sense?
 Do I need to reread?
2. VISUALIZE:
 Create mental images
 Picture what you're reading
 Imagine scenes and characters
3. MAKE CONNECTIONS:
 Text-to-self: Relate to personal experience
 Text-to-text: Connect to other readings
 Text-to-world: Link to world knowledge
4. ASK QUESTIONS:
 What does this mean?
 Why did this happen?
70
 What will happen next?
5. ANNOTATE:
 Underline important points
 Write notes in margins
 Mark unfamiliar words
 Highlight key ideas
6. ADJUST READING SPEED:
 Slow down for difficult parts
 Speed up for easier sections
 Reread when confused
AFTER READING STRATEGIES:
1. SUMMARIZE:
 What was this about?
 What were the main points?
 Retell in your own words
2. REVIEW:
 Look back at notes
 Check highlighted sections
 Recall important details
3. REFLECT:
 What did I learn?
 What was important?
71
 How does this connect to what I knew?
4. EVALUATE:
 Did I achieve my reading purpose?
 Do I need to reread anything?
 What questions remain?
STRATEGIES FOR DIFFICULT TEXT:
1. BREAK IT DOWN:
 Read one paragraph at a time
 Summarize each paragraph
 Build understanding gradually
2. USE CONTEXT CLUES:
 Figure out unknown words from surrounding words
 Look for definitions in text
 Use examples given
3. REREAD:
 Go back and read again
 Read aloud
 Read more slowly
4. SEEK HELP:
 Use dictionary for difficult words
 Ask teacher/friend for clarification
 Look for supplementary materials
72
VOCABULARY STRATEGIES WHILE READING:
1. CONTEXT CLUES: Look at surrounding words to guess meaning
Types:
 Definition: "A herbivore, an animal that eats only plants..."
 Example: "Reptiles such as snakes and lizards..."
 Contrast: "Unlike mammals, reptiles are cold-blooded"
 Synonym: "The enormous, huge building..."
2. WORD PARTS:
 Prefix: un-, re-, pre-, dis-
 Root: Latin/Greek roots
 Suffix: -tion, -ness, -ly, -ful
3. SKIP AND CONTINUE:
 If word isn't crucial, skip it
 Continue reading
 Meaning may become clear later
4. USE DICTIONARY:
 Look up important unknown words
 Write down definitions
 Use words in sentences
IMPROVING READING SPEED:
Tips:
73
1. Avoid reading word by word
2. Read in phrases/chunks
3. Don't vocalize (say words aloud or in head)
4. Limit regression (going back)
5. Practice regularly
6. Use finger/pointer to guide eyes
7. Expand peripheral vision
8. Read more often
ACTIVE READING TECHNIQUES:
1. SQ3R METHOD (will be covered in detail next week):
 Survey, Question, Read, Recite, Review
2. NOTE-TAKING:
 Write key points
 Create outlines
 Use symbols and abbreviations
3. QUESTIONING:
 Ask before, during, after reading
 Who, what, when, where, why, how
4. DISCUSSING:
 Talk about what you read
 Share ideas with others
 Join reading groups
74
CHOOSING APPROPRIATE STRATEGY:
Consider:
 Purpose: Why are you reading?
 Text type: Fiction, textbook, article?
 Time available: Quick or thorough?
 Difficulty level: Easy or challenging?
Match Strategy to Purpose:
 Entertainment → Extensive reading
 Finding fact → Scanning
 Getting overview → Skimming
 Studying for exam → Intensive reading
 Learning new concept → Intensive + SQ3R
EVALUATION:
1. What are reading strategies and why are they important?
2. Explain the difference between skimming and scanning with examples of when to use
each
3. Describe three strategies you should use before, during, and after reading
4. What is intensive reading and when should you use it?
5. How can you figure out the meaning of an unknown word while reading? Give three
methods
75
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Create a comparison table showing skimming, scanning, intensive, and extensive reading
with their: (a) definitions (b) purposes (c) when to use (d) how to do it
2. Choose a passage from your textbook and: (a) Preview it (b) Write what you predict it's
about (c) List three questions you expect it to answer (d) After reading, check if your
predictions were correct
3. Explain five "during reading" strategies with examples of how to use each
4. Describe a situation where you would use: (a) skimming (b) scanning (c) intensive
reading (d) extensive reading
5. List ten tips for improving reading speed and explain why each helps
76
WEEK 2 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: ELEMENTS OF COMPOSITION – INTRODUCTION, BODY, CONCLUSION
CONTENT:
STRUCTURE OF A COMPOSITION:
Every good composition has three main parts that work together to create a complete, wellorganized
piece of writing:
1. Introduction (Beginning)
2. Body (Middle)
3. Conclusion (End)
Why Structure Matters:
 Organizes ideas logically
 Guides reader through your thoughts
 Makes writing clear and easy to follow
 Ensures completeness
 Creates professional appearance
1. THE INTRODUCTION:
Definition: The introduction is the opening paragraph that introduces the topic, captures
attention, and prepares the reader for what follows.
Length: Usually one paragraph (4-6 sentences for JSS1)
PURPOSES OF INTRODUCTION:
77
1. CAPTURE ATTENTION (Hook): Get reader interested immediately
2. INTRODUCE TOPIC: Tell what the composition is about
3. PROVIDE BACKGROUND: Give necessary context
4. STATE THESIS/MAIN IDEA: Present your position or main point
5. PREVIEW CONTENT: Hint at what's coming in body paragraphs
TYPES OF HOOKS (Attention-Getters):
1. QUESTION: Start with an interesting question
 "Have you ever wondered why the sky is blue?"
 "What would you do if you found a million naira?"
2. QUOTATION: Begin with a relevant saying or quote
 "As the saying goes, 'Rome wasn't built in a day'"
 "My grandmother always said, 'Education is the key to success'"
3. INTERESTING FACT/STATISTIC: Present surprising information
 "Did you know that an ostrich's eye is bigger than its brain?"
 "Over 70% of the Earth is covered by water"
4. ANECDOTE (Short Story): Tell a brief, relevant personal experience
 "Last Tuesday, something happened that changed my life forever"
 "I was only seven when I learned the importance of honesty"
5. DESCRIPTIVE SCENE: Paint a vivid picture
 "The market was bustling with activity as vendors called out to potential buyers"
 "Rain pounded on the roof as I sat alone in the dark room"
6. DRAMATIC STATEMENT: Make bold, attention-grabbing claim
78
 "That day taught me a lesson I would never forget"
 "Reading saved my life"
COMPONENTS OF A GOOD INTRODUCTION:
1. Hook (1-2 sentences): Attention-grabber
2. Background/Context (2-3 sentences): Necessary information to understand topic
3. Thesis Statement (1 sentence): Main idea or position (especially in argumentative/expository)
4. Preview (1 sentence - optional): What will be covered
SAMPLE INTRODUCTIONS:
Narrative Essay - "My First Day at School":
I will never forget the 5th of September, 2023. It was my first day at Government Secondary
School, and I was both excited and nervous. As my mother helped me put on my new uniform that
morning, I had no idea what adventures awaited me. That day turned out to be one of the most
memorable days of my life.
Analysis:
 Hook: Dramatic statement about remembering the date
 Background: First day at school, emotions
 Preview: Hints at adventures
 Sets narrative tone
Descriptive Essay - "My Classroom":
Walking into my classroom is like entering a world of learning and friendship. Our classroom,
located on the second floor of the school building, is where we spend most of our day. It is a
79
spacious, well-organized room filled with desks, books, and educational charts. This special
place has become my second home.
Analysis:
 Hook: Comparison "like entering a world"
 Background: Location and purpose
 Preview: Mentions what will be described
 Sets descriptive tone
Argumentative Essay - "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory":
The debate about school uniforms has continued for decades. Some students complain that
uniforms limit their freedom of expression, while others appreciate the sense of equality they
create. However, the benefits of school uniforms far outweigh any disadvantages. This essay will
demonstrate why school uniforms should be compulsory in all schools.
Analysis:
 Hook: Mentions ongoing debate
 Background: Different viewpoints
 Thesis: Clear position stated
 Preview: What essay will prove
Expository Essay - "The Importance of Education":
Education is often called the key to success, and for good reason. Throughout history, educated
people have been leaders, innovators, and change-makers in society. Education transforms
individuals and nations, opening doors to opportunities and development. This essay explores
the many ways education impacts our lives and why it is essential for everyone.
Analysis:
80
 Hook: Common saying about education
 Background: Historical perspective
 Main idea: Education's transformative power
 Preview: What essay will cover
WHAT TO AVOID IN INTRODUCTIONS:
1. Too Broad/General:
 ✗ "Many things happen in life"
 ✗ "Since the beginning of time..."
2. Dictionary Definitions (overdone):
 ✗ "According to the dictionary, friendship is..."
3. Apologizing:
 ✗ "I don't know much about this but..."
 ✗ "This may not be interesting but..."
4. Announcing Intentions Obviously:
 ✗ "In this essay, I am going to tell you about..."
 ✗ "I will now describe..."
5. Too Long:
 Should be about 10-15% of total composition
6. Revealing Everything:
 Save details for body
 Don't tell the whole story in introduction
81
2. THE BODY:
Definition: The body is the main part of the composition where you develop your ideas, present
arguments, tell your story, or provide detailed description.
Length: Usually 2-4 paragraphs (depending on topic and total length)
PURPOSES OF BODY:
1. DEVELOP MAIN IDEAS: Expand on points introduced
2. PROVIDE DETAILS: Give specific information, examples, evidence
3. TELL THE STORY: Present events in order (narrative)
4. DESCRIBE THOROUGHLY: Paint complete picture (descriptive)
5. PRESENT ARGUMENTS: Support thesis with reasons (argumentative)
6. EXPLAIN CLEARLY: Provide information and explanations (expository)
ORGANIZING BODY PARAGRAPHS:
Each Body Paragraph Should Have:
1. TOPIC SENTENCE: States the main idea of the paragraph
 First sentence usually
 Introduces what paragraph is about
2. SUPPORTING SENTENCES: Provide details, examples, evidence, explanations
 4-6 sentences usually
 Develop the topic sentence
3. CONCLUDING SENTENCE: Wraps up paragraph and transitions to next
82
 Last sentence
 Links to next paragraph
PARAGRAPH UNITY:
All sentences in a paragraph should relate to one main idea (topic sentence).
Good Paragraph (Unity): Topic: My mother is a hardworking woman
 She wakes up at 5 AM every day
 She prepares breakfast for the family
 She goes to work and returns late
 She still cooks dinner when she returns
 On weekends, she does laundry and cleaning
Poor Paragraph (No Unity): Topic: My mother is hardworking
 She wakes up at 5 AM
 I have three siblings
 Our house is big
 I like football
 She cooks dinner
PARAGRAPH COHERENCE:
Ideas should flow smoothly using transitions.
Transitional Words/Phrases:
Time Order (Narrative):
 First, Then, Next, After that, Finally
83
 Later, Meanwhile, Soon, Eventually
 Before, During, After
Spatial Order (Descriptive):
 To the left/right, In front of, Behind
 Above, Below, Beside, Near
 In the center, At the top/bottom
Logical Order (Argumentative/Expository):
 First/Firstly, Second/Secondly, Third/Thirdly
 Furthermore, Moreover, Additionally, Also
 However, Nevertheless, On the other hand
 Therefore, Thus, Consequently, As a result
 For example, For instance, Such as
 In fact, Indeed, Actually
BODY ORGANIZATION BY TYPE:
NARRATIVE:
 Chronological order (time sequence)
 Beginning → Middle → End
 Build to climax
 Include dialogue, action, description
DESCRIPTIVE:
 Spatial order (physical arrangement)
 General to specific OR specific to general
84
 Most to least important features
 Sensory details throughout
ARGUMENTATIVE:
 Order of importance (usually weakest to strongest)
 One argument per paragraph
 Evidence and examples for each
 Counter-argument paragraph
EXPOSITORY:
 Logical order
 Categories, causes/effects, comparisons
 Clear explanations
 Examples and facts
SAMPLE BODY PARAGRAPHS:
Narrative (from "My First Day at School"):
First Paragraph: The morning began with a mixture of excitement and nervousness. I woke up
earlier than usual and carefully put on my new uniform. My mother had prepared my favorite
breakfast, jollof rice, to give me energy for the day. As we walked to school, she kept giving me
advice: "Be respectful to your teachers, make new friends, and study hard." I nodded, but my
mind was racing with questions about what school would be like.
Analysis:
 Topic: Morning preparations
 Chronological order
 Details about actions, thoughts, emotions
85
 Dialogue included
 Transitions: "As we walked," "but"
Second Paragraph: When we arrived at the school gate, I was amazed by what I saw. Hundreds
of students in the same uniform rushed around the compound. Some were chatting with friends,
while others stood alone looking as nervous as I felt. The school building was much larger than I
had imagined, with three stories and many classrooms. My mother walked me to my assigned
class, JSS 1A, where I met my class teacher, Mrs. Adeleke. She welcomed me warmly and
showed me to my seat.
Analysis:
 Topic: Arrival at school
 Descriptive details
 Comparison ("as nervous as I felt")
 Action and dialogue
 Transitions: "When," "while," "where"
Descriptive (from "My Classroom"):
First Paragraph: As you enter through the blue wooden door, the first thing you notice is the
neat arrangement of desks. Twenty brown desks are arranged in four rows of five, each desk
designed for two students. In front of the desks stands the teacher's large table, made of dark
wood with drawers on both sides. Behind the teacher's table, a green chalkboard spans almost
the entire wall, usually covered with notes and diagrams. On the left side of the board, our class
timetable is permanently displayed.
Analysis:
 Topic: Physical layout
86
 Spatial organization (as you enter → front → behind → left)
 Specific details (numbers, colors, materials)
 Descriptive vocabulary
 Transitions: "As you enter," "In front of," "Behind," "On the left"
Argumentative (from "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory"):
First Argument: First and foremost, school uniforms promote equality among students. When
everyone wears the same clothes, economic differences become less visible. Rich students cannot
show off expensive designer clothes, and poor students don't feel embarrassed about wearing
cheaper clothing. This creates a more inclusive environment where students are judged by their
character and achievements rather than their parents' wealth. For example, in schools with
uniforms, bullying related to clothing choices decreases significantly.
Analysis:
 Topic: Uniforms promote equality
 Clear argument with explanation
 Cause and effect reasoning
 Example provided
 Transitions: "First and foremost," "When," "For example"
Expository (from "The Importance of Education"):
First Paragraph: Education provides individuals with knowledge and skills necessary for
success in life. Through education, people learn to read, write, and perform mathematical
calculations—basic skills required in almost every job. Furthermore, education teaches critical
thinking, enabling individuals to solve problems creatively and make informed decisions. For
87
instance, an educated person can analyze information, distinguish between fact and opinion, and
draw logical conclusions. These skills are invaluable in both personal and professional life.
Analysis:
 Topic: Education provides knowledge and skills
 Clear explanations
 Examples provided
 Cause and effect
 Transitions: "Through," "Furthermore," "For instance"
3. THE CONCLUSION:
Definition: The conclusion is the final paragraph that wraps up the composition, summarizes
main points, and leaves a lasting impression.
Length: Usually one paragraph (4-5 sentences for JSS1)
PURPOSES OF CONCLUSION:
1. SIGNAL THE END: Let reader know composition is finishing
2. SUMMARIZE MAIN POINTS: Briefly recap what was discussed
3. RESTATE THESIS: Return to main idea (in different words)
4. PROVIDE CLOSURE: Give sense of completeness
5. LEAVE LASTING IMPRESSION: Make final statement reader will remember
6. REFLECTION (for narrative): Share what was learned or how you felt
COMPONENTS OF A GOOD CONCLUSION:
88
1. Transition Signal (1 phrase/sentence): Indicates conclusion is beginning
 In conclusion, To conclude, Finally, In summary
 As we have seen, To sum up
2. Summary/Restatement (2-3 sentences): Recap main points or restate thesis
3. Final Thought (1-2 sentences): Memorable closing statement:
 Call to action
 Prediction
 Recommendation
 Thought-provoking statement
 Personal reflection
TYPES OF CONCLUSIONS:
1. SUMMARY CONCLUSION: Restates main points briefly
2. RECOMMENDATION CONCLUSION: Suggests what should be done
3. CALL-TO-ACTION CONCLUSION: Urges reader to do something
4. PREDICTION CONCLUSION: Forecasts future implications
5. REFLECTIVE CONCLUSION: Shares personal insights or feelings (narrative)
6. THOUGHT-PROVOKING CONCLUSION: Leaves reader with something to ponder
SAMPLE CONCLUSIONS:
Narrative (from "My First Day at School"):
That first day at school was truly unforgettable. Although I started the day feeling nervous and
uncertain, I ended it with new friends and exciting experiences. I learned that trying new things,
89
even when scary, often leads to wonderful opportunities. Looking back now, I realize that first
day was just the beginning of an amazing journey. I'm grateful my mother encouraged me to be
brave and embrace the adventure.
Type: Reflective Elements:
 Refers back to beginning
 Shares what was learned
 Personal feeling/reflection
 Sense of closure
Descriptive (from "My Classroom"):
In conclusion, my classroom is more than just a room with desks and a chalkboard. It is a
special place where we learn, grow, and build friendships. Every element, from the educational
charts on the walls to the library corner filled with books, contributes to creating an environment
that inspires learning. I am proud to call this well-organized, welcoming space my classroom. It
truly is my second home.
Type: Summary + Reflection Elements:
 Restates main idea ("more than just a room")
 Summarizes key features
 Personal connection
 Returns to introduction ("second home")
Argumentative (from "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory"):
In conclusion, school uniforms should be mandatory in all educational institutions. As
demonstrated, uniforms promote equality, enhance discipline, and create a sense of school
identity. While some may argue that uniforms limit self-expression, these minor concerns are far
90
outweighed by the significant benefits uniforms provide. Therefore, schools, parents, and
policymakers should work together to implement uniform policies. Our students deserve an
environment focused on learning, not fashion competitions.
Type: Summary + Call to Action Elements:
 Clear conclusion signal
 Restatement of position
 Summary of arguments
 Addresses counter-argument briefly
 Call to action
 Strong final statement
Expository (from "The Importance of Education"):
To summarize, education plays a vital role in individual and societal development. It provides
knowledge and skills, opens economic opportunities, promotes social mobility, and fosters
informed citizenship. Without education, progress would be impossible and potential would
remain unrealized. Every child deserves access to quality education. As Nelson Mandela said,
"Education is the most powerful weapon which you can use to change the world." Let us
recognize and support education as the foundation of a better future.
Type: Summary + Quotation + Call to Action Elements:
 Conclusion signal
 Summary of main points
 Broader implications
 Relevant quotation
 Call to action
 Forward-looking statement
91
WHAT TO AVOID IN CONCLUSIONS:
1. New Information:
 ✗ Don't introduce new ideas or arguments
 ✗ Don't bring up points not discussed in body
2. Apologizing:
 ✗ "I'm sorry this wasn't very good"
 ✗ "I know I didn't explain this well"
3. Contradicting Your Position:
 ✗ Don't suddenly change your stance
 ✗ Don't undermine your arguments
4. Being Too Long:
 Should be brief (about 10% of composition)
 Don't repeat everything in detail
5. Using "The End":
 ✗ Don't write "The End" at the end
 Composition should naturally conclude
6. Exact Repetition:
 ✗ Don't copy sentences from introduction or body word-for-word
 Rephrase and summarize
7. Phrases Like "In this essay I talked about...":
 Too obvious and unnecessary
92
 Better: naturally summarize
CONNECTING THE THREE PARTS:
Flow and Coherence:
Introduction → Body:
 Thesis/main idea introduced in introduction should be developed in body
 Preview in introduction prepares for body content
Body → Conclusion:
 Main points in body are summarized in conclusion
 Arguments/descriptions/events lead to conclusion
Introduction ↔ Conclusion:
 Echo each other
 Conclusion often returns to idea/image from introduction
 Creates circular structure
Example - Narrative:
 Introduction: "I will never forget that day"
 Conclusion: "That day taught me a lesson I'll never forget"
COMPLETE COMPOSITION EXAMPLE:
Topic: My Best Friend
INTRODUCTION: Everyone needs a friend they can trust and rely on. I am lucky to have
Sarah as my best friend. We met three years ago when she moved into the house next to mine,
and we have been inseparable ever since. Sarah is not just a friend; she is like a sister to me.
93
BODY PARAGRAPH 1: Sarah is one of the most kind-hearted people I know. She always
thinks about others before herself. Whenever I am sad or worried, she listens patiently and offers
comforting words. She also helps other students in class who struggle with their schoolwork.
Last week, she spent her lunch break tutoring a classmate in Mathematics instead of playing with
us. Her kindness makes everyone around her feel valued and cared for.
BODY PARAGRAPH 2: Moreover, Sarah is very reliable and trustworthy. I can share my
secrets with her knowing she will never tell anyone. She keeps her promises, no matter how small.
Once, she promised to help me with my Science project, and even when she fell ill, she still found
time to assist me. Her loyalty and dependability have strengthened our friendship over the years.
I know I can always count on her.
BODY PARAGRAPH 3: In addition to being kind and reliable, Sarah has a great sense of
humor. She knows how to make me laugh even when I'm having a bad day. Her funny jokes and
stories brighten any situation. During our break time at school, she entertains our group with
hilarious imitations of our favorite cartoon characters. Life is never boring when Sarah is
around.
CONCLUSION: In conclusion, Sarah is truly my best friend. Her kindness, reliability, and
sense of humor make her a wonderful person to have in my life. I am grateful for our friendship
and the joy she brings to every day. True friends like Sarah are rare and precious. I hope our
friendship lasts forever.
ANALYSIS OF EXAMPLE:
Structure:
 ✓ Clear introduction with hook and thesis
 ✓ Three body paragraphs, each with one main point
 ✓ Logical organization
 ✓ Proper conclusion
94
Unity:
 ✓ Each paragraph focuses on one trait
 ✓ All sentences relate to topic sentence
 ✓ Everything connects to main idea (Sarah as best friend)
Coherence:
 ✓ Transitions used (Moreover, In addition, Once, During)
 ✓ Ideas flow smoothly
 ✓ Logical progression
Content:
 ✓ Specific examples provided
 ✓ Details support main points
 ✓ Personal connection clear
EVALUATION:
1. What are the three main parts of a composition? Explain the purpose of each
2. List and explain five types of hooks that can be used in an introduction
3. What should a good body paragraph contain? Explain each component
4. Write an introduction paragraph (5 sentences) for a narrative essay titled "An
Unforgettable Experience"
5. What should you avoid in a conclusion? Give three things and explain why
ASSIGNMENT:
95
1. Write a complete short composition (150-200 words) on "My School" with clearly
labeled introduction, body (2 paragraphs), and conclusion
2. Explain five characteristics of a good introduction and five characteristics of a good
conclusion
3. Create an outline for a composition on "The Happiest Day of My Life" showing: (a)
Introduction (hook, background, thesis) (b) Three body paragraphs (topic sentences) (c)
Conclusion (summary and final thought)
4. Identify and label the introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion in any composition
from your textbook. Explain what makes each part effective
5. Write three different types of conclusions (summary, reflective, and call-to-action) for the
topic "The Importance of Reading"
96
WEEK 2 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: FEATURES OF LITERATURE; TYPES – ORAL, HISTORICAL, DANCE
DRAMA, MIMING
PART A: FEATURES OF LITERATURE
Key Features:
1. IMAGINATIVE/CREATIVE
o Product of writer's imagination
o Creates new worlds and characters
2. USE OF LANGUAGE
o Beautiful, artistic expression
o Figurative language (metaphors, similes)
3. EMOTIONAL APPEAL
o Evokes feelings and empathy
o Engages emotions
4. FORM AND STRUCTURE
o Organized presentation
o Attention to format (prose, poetry, drama)
5. PERMANENCE
o Lasting value
o Transcends time
6. UNIVERSALITY
o Appeals across cultures
97
o Timeless themes
7. MULTIPLE INTERPRETATIONS
o Layers of meaning
o Encourages analysis
8. AESTHETIC QUALITY
o Beautiful and pleasing
o Artistic craftsmanship
PART B: TYPES OF LITERATURE
1. ORAL LITERATURE
Definition: Literature composed, performed, and transmitted by word of mouth, passed from
generation to generation through speech and memory.
Characteristics:
 Verbal Transmission: Spoken, not written; relies on memory
 Performance-Based: Uses voice, gesture, facial expressions
 Communal: Belongs to community, performed at gatherings
 Flexible/Variable: Changes with each performance
 Functional: Serves education, entertainment, ritual purposes
 Traditional: Part of cultural heritage
Types of Oral Literature:
1. Folktales - Traditional stories teaching lessons
2. Myths - Sacred stories explaining origins
3. Legends - Stories about heroes and historical figures
98
4. Proverbs - Short wise sayings (e.g., "When two elephants fight, the grass suffers")
5. Riddles - Puzzles for entertainment and education
6. Praise Poetry - Poems praising individuals or deities
7. Songs - Work songs, lullabies, war songs
8. Oral Epics - Long narrative poems about heroic deeds
Performance Features:
 Call and response
 Repetition and formulaic language
 Rhythmic patterns
 Music and drumming
 Audience participation
Functions:
 Entertainment and education
 Historical preservation
 Moral instruction
 Cultural identity
 Social control
Performers: Griots, elders, parents, community members
2. HISTORICAL LITERATURE
Definition: Literary works based on or set in historical periods, dealing with real historical
events and figures, often with fictional elements.
Characteristics:
99
 Based on historical events and real figures
 Accurate historical setting and period details
 Educational value
 Blend of fact and fiction
Types:
1. Historical Fiction - Novels set in the past
2. Historical Drama/Plays - Plays about historical events
3. Historical Poetry - Poems commemorating battles and heroes
4. Biographies - Life stories of real people
5. Chronicles - Written accounts blending history and storytelling
Purposes:
 Preserve and educate about history
 Inspire and build identity
 Learn from the past
 Commemorate heroes
Examples: Stories of Queen Amina, Shaka Zulu, resistance against colonialism
3. DANCE DRAMA
Definition: Dramatic performance combining storytelling with dance movements, told primarily
through dance and gesture with minimal or no spoken dialogue.
Characteristics:
 Movement-Based: Dance is primary expression
 Musical Accompaniment: Drums and instruments
100
 Symbolic Gestures: Movements represent actions and emotions
 Costumes and Props: Elaborate, colorful costumes
 Minimal Dialogue: Story understood through action
Elements:
 Plot/Story conveyed through dance
 Characters portrayed by dancers
 Music and rhythm
 Choreography
 Costumes and makeup
Types of Movements:
 Representational: Mimic real actions (planting, fighting)
 Symbolic: Abstract movements with cultural meaning
 Narrative: Sequence tells complete story
Purposes: Entertainment, cultural preservation, storytelling, ritual, education
Examples: Masquerade performances (Egungun, Ekpo), festival dances, harvest celebrations
4. MIMING (MIME/PANTOMIME)
Definition: Dramatic performance where story is told entirely through body movements,
gestures, and facial expressions without words.
Characteristics:
 Silent: No spoken words
 Exaggerated Gestures: Clear and visible movements
 Facial Expressions: Express emotions clearly
101
 Physical Acting: Entire body involved
 Imaginary Objects: Creates illusion of invisible props
 Universal Understanding: No language barrier
Techniques:
1. Illusion Creation: Pretending to touch/hold objects, climbing invisible stairs
2. Isolation: Moving one body part while keeping others still
3. Spatial Awareness: Defining boundaries and environment
4. Weight and Resistance: Showing heaviness or effort
5. Character Through Movement: Posture reveals traits
Common Scenarios:
 Trapped in a box
 Walking against wind
 Climbing rope/ladder
 Carrying heavy object
 Opening/closing doors
Purposes: Entertainment, universal communication, artistic expression, education
Elements of Successful Mime: Clarity, precision, imagination, expression, practice
COMPARISON TABLE
Type Main Feature Primary Medium Audience Role
Oral Literature Spoken word Voice, memory Often participatory
Historical Literature Based on history Written/oral text Passive
102
Dance Drama Movement & music Dance, gesture Observers
Miming Silent gesture Body movement Observers
RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN TYPES
Common Elements:
 All are performance arts
 Storytelling element
 Cultural preservation
 Entertainment and education
MODERN APPLICATIONS
 Oral Literature: Radio storytelling, spoken word poetry, traditional festivals
 Historical Literature: Historical novels, biographies, films
 Dance Drama: Cultural festivals, theatre productions, school performances
 Miming: Street performances, theatre scenes, drama education
IMPORTANCE
Cultural Value: Preserve heritage, maintain traditions, build identity
Educational Value: Teach history and values, develop creativity, improve communication
Social Value: Bring communities together, create shared experiences
EVALUATION
1. Define oral literature and list four characteristics
103
2. Explain three types of oral literature with examples
3. What is historical literature? Give three African examples
4. Define dance drama and miming. State three differences
5. Why are these forms important for cultural preservation?

purposes (d) examples

drama (c) miming
