WEEK 3 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /I:/
CONTENT:
THE LONG VOWEL SOUND /i:/
Characteristics:
 Long, prolonged sound
 Tongue very high at front of mouth
 Lips spread wide (tense smile position)
 Tense, not relaxed
 Higher and longer than /ɪ/
Mouth Position:
 Tongue tip behind lower front teeth
 Front of tongue raised very high
 Lips stretched horizontally
 Jaw barely open
 Teeth almost together
SPELLING PATTERNS FOR /i:/ SOUND:
1. "ee" spelling (most common):
 see, bee, tree, free, three
 feel, feet, meet, sweet, street
 need, feed, seed, speed
105
 sleep, keep, deep, sheep
 green, between, screen
2. "ea" spelling:
 sea, tea, pea, flea
 eat, meat, heat, seat, beat, treat
 read (present tense), lead (verb), teach, reach
 clean, mean, bean,ream
 please,ease, tease
3. "e" at end of syllable:
 he, she, we, me, be
 even, evening, female
 decent, recent, secret
 complete, delete
4. "ie/ei" spelling:
 field, yield, shield, wield
 piece, niece, brief, grief, belief, relief, chief, thief
 receive, deceive, perceive, ceiling, conceit
5. "i" spelling (rare):
 machine, police, prestige
 ski, kiwi, regime
6. "ey/y" spelling (word endings):
 key, donkey, monkey, hockey
 happy, lucky, funny, sunny, baby, lady (unstressed)
106
7. "eo" spelling:
 people
8. Other rare spellings:
 quay /ki:/ (dock)
 amoeba /əˈmi:bə/
COMMON WORDS WITH /i:/ SOUND:
Short words:
 be, he, me, we, she, see, tea, sea, key, bee, tree, free
Medium words:
 feet, meet, sweet, green, clean, dream, speak, teach, reach, beach
Long words:
 believe, achieve, complete, between, fifteen, thirteen -Receive, deceive, ceiling, people,
machine
PRACTICE SENTENCES:
 We need to meet at three
 She can see the green tree
 Please keep the seat clean
 He dreams of the sea and beach
 The people believe in freedom
 Fifteen sheep sleep under the tree
107
MINIMAL PAIRS: /i:/ vs /ɪ/
These pairs show the difference between the long /i:/ and short /ɪ/ sounds:
 seat /i:/ - sit /ɪ/
 feet /i:/ - fit /ɪ/
 beat /i:/ - bit /ɪ/
 sheep /i:/ - ship /ɪ/
 seen /i:/ - sin /ɪ/
 deal /i:/ - dill /ɪ/
 feel /i:/ - fill /ɪ/
 keen /i:/ - kin /ɪ/
 wheel /i:/ - will /ɪ/
 week /i:/ - wick /ɪ/
Key Difference:
 /i:/ is LONG - hold the sound: "seeeee"
 /ɪ/ is SHORT - quick sound: "sit"
Test yourself:
 "I need to sit in my seat" (both sounds in one sentence)
 "She will turn the wheel" (both sounds)
PRONUNCIATION TIPS:
To Produce /i:/ Correctly:
1. Smile wide:
o Stretch lips horizontally
108
o Feel corners of mouth pull back
o Like showing your teeth
2. Raise tongue high:
o Front of tongue up
o Almost touching roof of mouth
o Tip stays behind lower teeth
3. Make it long:
o Hold the sound longer
o Don't cut it short
o Steady, continuous sound
4. Keep tense:
o Muscles tight, not relaxed
o More effort than /ɪ/
o Definite, clear sound
5. Close jaw slightly:
o Teeth nearly together
o Just small opening
o Don't open wide
COMMON MISTAKES:
1. Confusion with /ɪ/:
 ✗ Saying "sit" when you mean "seat"
109
 ✗ Short sound instead of long
 Practice: Exaggerate the length difference
2. Incorrect spelling pronunciation:
 ✗ "great" is /eɪ/ NOT /i:/ (irregular)
 ✗ "head" is /e/ NOT /i:/ (irregular)
 ✗ "break" is /eɪ/ NOT /i:/ (irregular)
3. Adding extra sounds:
 ✗ "feel-uh" (adding schwa at end)
 ✓ "feel" (clean ending)
4. Not tensing enough:
 ✗ Relaxed, short sound
 ✓ Tense, long, deliberate sound
IRREGULAR PRONUNCIATIONS TO WATCH:
Not all "ea" spellings are /i:/:
 bread, head, dead, ready = /e/ sound
 break, great, steak = /eɪ/ sound
 heart, hearth = /ɑ:/ sound
Context helps determine pronunciation:
 read (present): /i:/ - "I read books"
 read (past): /e/ - "I read that book yesterday"
 lead (verb): /i:/ - "Lead the way"
110
 lead (metal): /e/ - "Lead is heavy"
PRACTICE EXERCISES:
1. Reading Practice: Read these sentences aloud, emphasizing /i:/ sounds:
 The teacher needs to meet three people
 We believe she can achieve her dream
 Please keep the green leaves clean
 He feels weak after fifteen weeks
2. Listening Practice: Which word do you hear: /i:/ or /ɪ/?
 ship or sheep?
 fit or feet?
 fill or feel?
 bin or bean?
3. Speaking Practice: Say these word groups, maintaining long /i:/:
 sea, see, seat, seek
 tea, team, teach, tease
 fee, feed, feel, feet
4. Sentence Creation: Make sentences using these /i:/ words:
 need, sleep, dream
 people, believe, achieve
 clean, green, between
WORD STRESS AND /i:/ SOUND:
111
In stressed syllables (clear /i:/):
 beLIEVE, comPLETE, aGREE
 reCEIVE, deCEIVE, rePEAT
In unstressed syllables:
 Sometimes shortened or less clear
 "happy" - the 'y' may sound closer to /ɪ/ in casual speech
 But maintain /i:/ in careful pronunciation
HOMOPHONES WITH /i:/ SOUND:
Words that sound the same but spelled differently:
 meet/meat - "Let's meet at the meat shop"
 see/sea - "Can you see the sea?"
 be/bee - "Be careful of that bee"
 peace/piece - "We need peace, not just a piece of it"
 week/weak - "After a week, I still feel weak"
 feat/feet - "Running on your feet is a great feat"
LINKING /i:/ SOUNDS:
When /i:/ sound comes at the end of a word followed by a vowel:
 Add slight /j/ sound (like 'y')
 "see it" sounds like "see-yit"
 "three apples" sounds like "three-yapples"
 "she is" sounds like "she-yis"
112
Practice:
 We are → "we-yare"
 See everyone → "see-yeveryone"
 The apple → "the-yapple"
CONNECTED SPEECH:
In natural, fast speech:
 /i:/ may be slightly shortened (but still longer than /ɪ/)
 Still distinguishable from short /ɪ/
 Maintain difference even when speaking quickly
Example: "I need to see three people between three and four"
 Even said quickly, all /i:/ sounds remain longer than /ɪ/
TONGUE TWISTERS FOR PRACTICE:
1. Three free trees for me to see
2. She sees cheese in the breeze by the sea
3. Peter Piper peacefully sleeps beneath the peach tree
4. Fifteen people believe in the teacher's scheme
5. Keep the green tea clean and sweet between meals
PHYSICAL PRACTICE:
Exaggeration Exercise:
1. Start with extreme smile - lips pulled back very far
113
2. Make /i:/ sound as long as possible (5 seconds)
3. Feel tongue position - very high and front
4. Gradually reduce exaggeration to natural
5. Maintain correct position
Comparison Exercise:
1. Say "sit" (short /ɪ/)
2. Then "seat" (long /i:/)
3. Back to "sit"
4. Notice the difference
5. Repeat 10 times
Mirror Exercise:
 Watch your mouth in mirror
 See lips stretch for /i:/
 Compare with /ɪ/ (less stretched)
 Check consistent position
EVALUATION:
1. Describe how to produce the /i:/ sound (tongue position, lip shape, length)
2. Write ten words with "ee" spelling that have /i:/ sound
3. Write ten words with "ea" spelling that have /i:/ sound
4. What is the difference between /i:/ and /ɪ/? Give five minimal pairs
5. Identify which words have /i:/ sound: (a) bread (b) feet (c) head (d) see (e) great (f) meat
(g) sit (h) complete
114
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List twenty words containing /i:/ sound, showing different spelling patterns (ee, ea, e, ie,
ei, i)
2. Write ten sentences using words with /i:/ sound, underlining the /i:/ words
3. Create five minimal pairs showing /i:/ vs /ɪ/ contrast and use each pair in a sentence
4. Practice reading aloud and record: "Three clean sheep sleep between fifteen green trees.
The teacher believes people need to achieve their dreams. Please keep the sweet tea by

correct them
115
WEEK 3 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: VERBS – DEFINITION, IDENTIFICATION; TYPES
WHAT IS A VERB?
Definition: A verb is a word that expresses an action, occurrence, or state of being.
Simple Definition: An action or being word.
Functions:
1. Show action: run, jump, eat, write
2. Show occurrence: happen, become, occur
3. Show state/condition: be, seem, appear, feel
Identifying Verbs - Ask:
 What is happening?
 What is being done?
 Can you put "to" before it? (to run, to eat)
 Does it change with time? (walk/walked)
 Can you add -ing? (walking, eating)
TYPES OF VERBS
CLASSIFICATION 1: BY OBJECT REQUIREMENT
1. TRANSITIVE VERBS
Definition: Require a direct object to complete meaning. Action passes to an object.
Structure: Subject + Transitive Verb + Object
How to Identify: Ask "What?" or "Whom?" after verb
116
Examples:
 She bought a book (Bought what? A book)
 I love my mother (Love whom? My mother)
 He kicks the ball (Kicks what? The ball)
Common Transitive Verbs: eat, drink, buy, sell, make, take, love, hate, like, want, need, write,
read, speak, teach, kick, throw, catch, bring, send, give, show, tell
2. INTRANSITIVE VERBS
Definition: Do NOT require direct object. Action is complete by itself.
Structure: Subject + Intransitive Verb (+ optional adverb/phrase)
How to Identify: Ask "What?" or "Whom?" - no answer needed
Examples:
 The baby cried (Cried what? No answer needed)
 Birds fly (Complete without object)
 She laughed loudly ("loudly" describes how, not object)
Common Intransitive Verbs: sleep, wake, sit, stand, lie, rise, come, go, arrive, depart, return,
cry, laugh, smile, die, live, exist, grow
Note: Some verbs can be both depending on context
 "She runs every morning" (intransitive)
 "She runs a business" (transitive - business = object)
CLASSIFICATION 2: BY FORM CHANGE
3. REGULAR VERBS
117
Definition: Form past tense and past participle by adding -ed, -d, or -t
Pattern: Base form + -ed = Past form
Rules:
 Most verbs - add -ed: walk → walked, play → played
 Verbs ending in -e - add -d: love → loved, dance → danced
 Consonant + y - change y to i, add -ed: study → studied, cry → cried
 CVC pattern - double final consonant, add -ed: stop → stopped, plan → planned
Common Regular Verbs: ask → asked, call → called, clean → cleaned, cook → cooked, finish
→ finished, help → helped, listen → listened, open → opened, visit → visited, wash → washed
4. IRREGULAR VERBS
Definition: Do NOT add -ed for past forms. Must be memorized.
Categories:
a) All three forms same:
 cut → cut → cut
 put → put → put
 hit → hit → hit
 hurt → hurt → hurt
b) Past and past participle same:
 bring → brought → brought
 buy → bought → bought
 teach → taught → taught
 sit → sat → sat
118
 make → made → made
 say → said → said
c) All three forms different:
 go → went → gone
 see → saw → seen
 eat → ate → eaten
 write → wrote → written
 take → took → taken
 give → gave → given
d) Vowel pattern change:
 sing → sang → sung
 drink → drank → drunk
 swim → swam → swum
 begin → began → begun
Common Irregular Verbs:
Base Past Past Participle
be was/were been
come came come
do did done
find found found
get got got/gotten
have had had
119
know knew known
speak spoke spoken
tell told told
think thought thought
CLASSIFICATION 3: BY FUNCTION
5. AUXILIARY VERBS (Helping Verbs)
Definition: Help main verb form tenses, moods, or voices
a) Primary Auxiliaries:
 be (am, is, are, was, were, been, being)
 have (has, had, having)
 do (does, did)
Uses:
 be: Continuous tenses (I am eating), Passive voice (It was done)
 have: Perfect tenses (I have eaten)
 do: Questions (Do you like it?), Negatives (I do not know), Emphasis (I do love you)
Examples:
 She is reading (auxiliary: is, main: reading)
 They have finished (auxiliary: have, main: finished)
 I do not understand (auxiliary: do, main: understand)
b) Modal Auxiliaries:
Express possibility, permission, ability, obligation, advice
120
List:
 can - ability, possibility
 could - past ability, polite request
 may - permission, possibility
 might - slight possibility
 must - obligation, necessity
 shall - future (formal)
 should - advice, expectation
 will - future, willingness
 would - polite request, hypothetical
 ought to - advice
Characteristics:
 Followed by base form of verb (no -s, -ing, -ed)
 No third person -s
 Cannot be used alone
Examples:
 I can swim (ability)
 You should study (advice)
 She will come (future)
 They must obey (obligation)
Common Errors:
 ✗ He cans swim → ✓ He can swim
121
 ✗ She must goes → ✓ She must go
 ✗ They will going → ✓ They will go
6. LEXICAL VERBS (Main Verbs)
Definition: Carry main meaning in sentence; the "real" action or state verbs
Characteristics:
 Express main action/state
 Can stand alone
 Have full meaning
Examples:
 I run every day (lexical verb only)
 She ate rice (lexical verb only)
Lexical vs. Auxiliary:
 "I have a pen" (have = lexical/main verb)
 "I have eaten" (have = auxiliary, eaten = lexical)
VERB PHRASES
Definition: Main verb + one or more auxiliary verbs
Examples:
 is eating (auxiliary + main verb)
 have been studying (aux + aux + main verb)
 will be going (modal + aux + main verb)
122
 should have come (modal + aux + main verb)
SUMMARY TABLE
Type Definition Examples
Transitive Needs object eat, buy, love, kick
Intransitive No object needed sleep, arrive, laugh, die
Regular Add -ed for past walk → walked
Irregular Special past forms go → went, see → saw
Auxiliary Help main verb am, is, have, do, can, will
Lexical Main meaning verb run, eat, sleep, study
COMMON ERRORS
1. Transitive/Intransitive confusion:
o ✗ "I explained him the answer" → ✓ "I explained the answer to him"
o ✗ "She married with John" → ✓ "She married John"
2. Irregular verb forms:
o ✗ "I goed home" → ✓ "I went home"
o ✗ "She has wrote" → ✓ "She has written"
3. Modal auxiliary mistakes:
o ✗ "He cans swim" → ✓ "He can swim"
o ✗ "They will going" → ✓ "They will go"
4. Missing verb:
123
o ✗ "She to school" → ✓ "She goes to school"
EVALUATION:
1. Define verb and state 3 functions
2. Explain difference between transitive and intransitive verbs with 3 examples each
3. What's the difference between regular and irregular verbs? Give 5 examples of each
4. List modal auxiliary verbs and what each expresses
5. Identify and classify verbs: (a) She has eaten breakfast (b) Birds fly (c) I bought a pen
yesterday (d) They can swim well (e) He sleeps soundly
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write 20 irregular verbs with base form, past form, and past participle
2. Identify and classify all verbs as transitive/intransitive: "Mary woke up early. She ate
breakfast quickly and went to school. The teacher arrived late. Students waited patiently.
She taught Mathematics. They listened carefully."
3. Create sentences using: (a) run, sing, eat, write, read as transitive AND intransitive (b)
have, will, can, should, must with main verbs
4. Fill in correct verb forms: (a) I _____ (go) to school yesterday (b) She _____ (eat) rice
now (c) They _____ (be) happy tomorrow (d) He _____ (write) three letters (e) We
_____ (study) when you came
5. Explain difference between lexical and auxiliary verbs with 5 examples showing same
verb used both ways
124
WEEK 3 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: SQ3R TECHNIQUE; VOCABULARY – MEMBERS OF THE FAMILY
PART A: THE SQ3R TECHNIQUE
What is SQ3R? A proven reading and study method that improves comprehension and retention.
Full Form:
 S - Survey
 Q - Question
 R - Read
 R - Recite
 R - Review
Why Use SQ3R?
 Improves understanding and retention
 Makes studying more active
 Saves time in exam preparation
 Identifies important information
THE FIVE STEPS
STEP 1: SURVEY (Preview)
Definition: Quickly look over material to get the "big picture"
How to Survey:
 Read title, introduction, and conclusion
 Read all headings and subheadings
125
 Look at pictures, diagrams, charts
 Read first sentence of each paragraph
 Notice bold/italicized words
Time: 5-10 minutes for a chapter
Example: Surveying "The Water Cycle"
 Title and headings: Evaporation, Condensation, Precipitation, Collection
 Diagram shows cycle process
 Creates mental framework
STEP 2: QUESTION
Definition: Turn headings into questions to give reading purpose
How to Create Questions:
 Use: What, Who, When, Where, Why, How
 Write questions down
Example Questions for "Evaporation":
 What is evaporation?
 What causes water to evaporate?
 Where does evaporation occur?
 Why is evaporation necessary?
Benefits: Creates focus and improves concentration
STEP 3: READ
Definition: Read actively to answer your questions
126
How to Read Effectively:
 Read one section at a time
 Look for answers to your questions
 Highlight/underline key points
 Take notes on main ideas and definitions
 Make connections to prior knowledge
Example: Reading "Evaporation"
 Answer: Evaporation is liquid water changing to water vapor
 Note: Caused by sun's heat
 Connection: Like puddles drying after rain
STEP 4: RECITE
Definition: After each section, recall main points in your own words without looking at text
How to Recite:
 Close book, look away
 Answer questions aloud or in writing
 Use your own words
 Summarize key ideas
 Check text to verify accuracy
 If you can't recite, reread
Time: 2-3 minutes per section
127
Example: After reading "Evaporation" "Evaporation is when liquid water becomes water vapor
due to sun's heat. It happens from oceans, lakes, rivers. Water vapor rises into atmosphere. First
step of water cycle."
Benefits: Strengthens memory, identifies gaps
STEP 5: REVIEW
Definition: Go over entire chapter to consolidate learning
How to Review:
Immediate Review (right after reading):
 Skim entire chapter
 Review notes and highlights
 Answer end-of-chapter questions
 Recite main points
Ongoing Review:
 Review next day, before class, before exam
 Make summary/outline
 Apply knowledge to examples
Time: 10-15 minutes immediately, then brief reviews later
Benefits: Moves information to long-term memory, reduces forgetting
COMPLETE SQ3R EXAMPLE: "Photosynthesis"
1. SURVEY (2 min): Title, headings (What is it, Requirements, Process, Importance),
diagram
128
2. QUESTION (1 min): What is photosynthesis? What do plants need? How does it work?
3. READ (10 min):
o Photosynthesis = plants make food using sunlight
o Needs: sunlight, water, CO₂, chlorophyll
o Process: CO₂ + water + light → glucose + oxygen
4. RECITE (4 min): Close book and summarize each section
5. REVIEW (5 min): Plants are food factories; review key equation and diagram
TIPS FOR EFFECTIVE SQ3R
DO:
 Take time with each step
 Write things down
 Use your own words in recitation
 Review multiple times
DON'T:
 Skip steps
 Rush through
 Just highlight without thinking
 Read passively
PART B: VOCABULARY – MEMBERS OF THE FAMILY
IMMEDIATE FAMILY (NUCLEAR FAMILY)
Parents:
129
 Father/Dad/Papa
 Mother/Mum/Mama
 Parent (gender-neutral)
Children:
 Son, Daughter, Child
 Offspring (formal)
Siblings:
 Brother, Sister, Sibling
 Twin, Triplets, Quadruplets
Birth Order:
 Elder/Older, Younger
 Eldest/Oldest (first-born)
 Youngest (last-born)
 Middle child
EXTENDED FAMILY
Grandparents:
 Grandfather/Grandpa, Grandmother/Grandma
 Great-grandfather, Great-grandmother
Grandchildren:
 Grandson, Granddaughter, Grandchild
Uncles and Aunts:
 Uncle: Father's/mother's brother or aunt's husband
130
o Paternal uncle (father's side)
o Maternal uncle (mother's side)
 Aunt/Aunty: Father's/mother's sister or uncle's wife
o Paternal aunt, Maternal aunt
Nieces and Nephews:
 Nephew: Brother's/sister's son
 Niece: Brother's/sister's daughter
Cousins:
 Cousin: Uncle's/aunt's child
 First cousin, Second cousin
IN-LAWS (RELATIVES BY MARRIAGE)
Parent's In-Laws:
 Father-in-law: Spouse's father
 Mother-in-law: Spouse's mother
Sibling's In-Laws:
 Brother-in-law: Sister's husband, spouse's brother
 Sister-in-law: Brother's wife, spouse's sister

 Son-in-law: Daughter's husband
 Daughter-in-law: Son's wife

131

 Stepfather: Mother's new husband (not biological father)
 Stepmother: Father's new wife (not biological mother)

 Stepbrother, Stepsister
Half-Siblings:
 Half-brother, Half-sister (share one parent only)
SPECIAL RELATIONSHIPS
Guardians:
 Guardian, Foster parent, Foster child
 Adopted child, Adoptive parent
Godparents:
 Godfather, Godmother, Godchild
Others:
 Spouse, Husband, Wife
 Partner (not married)
 Fiancé (male), Fiancée (female)
 Widow (woman), Widower (man)
 Orphan (parents died)

132

Describing Relationships:

 "I am an only child"
Talking About Family:

WEEK 3 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: ELEMENTS OF COMPOSITION – INTRODUCTION, BODY, CONCLUSION
(Reinforcement and Practice)
REVIEW: THE THREE PARTS
1. Introduction: Opens composition, introduces topic, hooks reader
2. Body: Develops ideas with details, examples, explanations
3. Conclusion: Wraps up, summarizes, leaves lasting impression
ANALYZING SAMPLE COMPOSITIONS
SAMPLE 1: NARRATIVE - "A Visit to the Zoo"
INTRODUCTION: "Last Saturday was one of the most exciting days of my life. My school
organized an excursion to the National Zoo, and I had been looking forward to it for weeks. As
our bus pulled into the zoo's parking lot, I could hardly contain my excitement. Little did I know
that this visit would teach me important lessons about wildlife conservation."
Analysis:
 ✓ Hook: "most exciting days"
 ✓ Background and personal feeling
 ✓ Preview of lesson learned
 ✓ Sets scene
BODY PARAGRAPHS (3 paragraphs):
 Paragraph 1: Lions - descriptive details, specific facts, personal actions
 Paragraph 2: Monkeys - entertaining incident, guide's warning, reflection
136
 Paragraph 3: Reptile house - contrasting emotions, conservation issue
Key Features:
 Each paragraph has clear topic
 Specific details (golden mane, monkey stealing cap)
 Transitions: "When," "Next," "The highlight"
 Personal involvement and reflection
 Chronological order
CONCLUSION: "As we boarded the bus to return to school, I felt both happy and thoughtful.
The zoo visit had been educational and fun, exposing me to animals I had only read about in
books. More importantly, I learned about the threats facing wildlife and the need to protect these
beautiful creatures. I promised myself to support conservation efforts in whatever way I could.
That day at the zoo changed my perspective on animals and nature. I will never forget it."
Analysis:
 ✓ Mixed feelings expressed
 ✓ Summarizes main points
 ✓ States main lesson
 ✓ Personal commitment
 ✓ Returns to introduction theme
 ✓ Provides closure
SAMPLE 2: DESCRIPTIVE - "My Favorite Teacher"
INTRODUCTION: "Among all the teachers in my school, Mrs. Okafor stands out as my
favorite. She teaches English Language, my best subject, but it's not just her subject that makes
her special. Mrs. Okafor has a unique combination of qualities that make her an exceptional
137
teacher. Her teaching style, personality, and genuine care for students have earned her the
respect and admiration of everyone in our school."
Analysis:
 ✓ Clear hook
 ✓ Thesis statement
 ✓ Preview: teaching style, personality, care
BODY PARAGRAPHS (3 paragraphs):
 Paragraph 1: Physical appearance - systematic description (height, complexion, clothing,
accessories)
 Paragraph 2: Teaching style - interactive methods, real-life examples, patience
 Paragraph 3: Personality - patient, respectful, humorous, fair, caring
Key Features:
 One main idea per paragraph
 Specific examples throughout
 Varied sentence structure
 Smooth transitions
CONCLUSION: "Mrs. Okafor is more than just a teacher; she is a mentor and role model. Her
combination of professional excellence and personal warmth makes her truly exceptional. She
has taught me not just English Language but also important life lessons about kindness, patience,
and dedication. Every student who has been fortunate enough to be in her class leaves with
improved knowledge and character. I am grateful to have such an inspiring teacher, and I hope
to emulate her qualities in my own life. Mrs. Okafor embodies what every teacher should aspire
to be."
138
Analysis:
 ✓ Elevation of subject
 ✓ Summary of qualities
 ✓ Dual learning emphasized
 ✓ Broader impact noted
 ✓ Personal gratitude and aspiration
IMPROVING WEAK WRITING
WEAK INTRODUCTION:
"I will write about my best friend. Her name is Sarah. She is a good person. I like her."
Problems: Too simple, no hook, announcing intention, lacks interest
IMPROVED:
"Everyone needs a true friend who stands by them through thick and thin. I am fortunate to have
found such a friend in Sarah Adamu. Since we met three years ago when she transferred to my
school, Sarah has proven to be not just a friend but a sister. Her loyalty, kindness, and sense of
humor make her truly special."
Improvements: Universal statement hook, natural introduction, background, preview,
sophisticated language
WEAK BODY PARAGRAPH:
"My mother is nice. She cooks for us. She helps me with homework. She is kind. I love my mother.
She works hard. She is the best mother."
Problems: Choppy sentences, repetitive, no examples, no transitions, list-like
139
IMPROVED:
"My mother's kindness touches everyone around her. Every morning, she wakes up at 5 AM to
prepare breakfast for our family, ensuring we start the day well-nourished. Despite working fulltime
as a nurse, she still finds time to help me with my homework, patiently explaining difficult
concepts until I understand. Her kindness extends beyond our family; she often visits sick
neighbors and brings them food. Last month, when our elderly neighbor fell ill, my mother
visited her daily for two weeks, bringing meals and medicine. Such selfless acts demonstrate why
I admire her so deeply."
Improvements: Topic sentence, specific examples (5 AM, nurse, neighbor), varied sentences,
transitions, unified focus
WEAK CONCLUSION:
"That is all about my school. My school is good. I like my school. Thank you. The end."
Problems: Abrupt, repetitive, inappropriate phrases, no summary, no closure
IMPROVED:
"Government Secondary School has shaped me into the person I am today. Its excellent teachers,
supportive environment, and diverse opportunities have provided me with both academic
knowledge and life skills. The friendships I've formed and lessons I've learned extend far beyond
textbooks. I am proud to be a student of this institution and grateful for everything it has given
me. As I continue my education here, I know I am in a place that will prepare me well for the
future."
Improvements: Reflects on impact, summarizes key points, expresses gratitude, forwardlooking,
provides closure
SELF-CHECK QUESTIONS
Introduction:
140
 ✓ Hook present?
 ✓ Topic clearly introduced?
 ✓ Preview included?
Body:
 ✓ Each paragraph has one main idea?
 ✓ Specific details and examples?
 ✓ Logical flow?
 ✓ Transitions used?
Conclusion:
 ✓ Summarizes main points?
 ✓ Final thought or reflection?
 ✓ Provides closure?
 ✓ No new information?
Overall:
 ✓ Well-organized?
 ✓ Unified (stays on topic)?
 ✓ Coherent (flows smoothly)?
 ✓ Complete?
EVALUATION:
1. Identify introduction, body, and conclusion in "A Visit to the Zoo"
141
2. List 5 characteristics of an effective body paragraph
3. What improvements were made to the weak introduction about "My Best Friend"?
4. Why are transitions important? Give 5 examples
5. Write an introduction paragraph (5 sentences) for "My Favorite Sport"

1. My Best Friend

4. The Market Near My House
5. An Act of Kindness I Witnessed
Requirements:
 Clear introduction with hook
142
 2-3 body paragraphs with topic sentences

 Effective conclusion
 Proper paragraphing and grammar

WEEK 3 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: PURPOSE/FUNCTIONS OF LITERATURE; INTRODUCTION TO PROSE
PART A: FUNCTIONS OF LITERATURE
12 Main Functions:
1. Entertainment/Pleasure
 Provides enjoyment and escape
 Creates suspense, excitement, humor
 Relaxation and stress relief
2. Education/Instruction
 Teaches language, history, culture, life skills
 Makes learning enjoyable and memorable
 Simplifies complex ideas
3. Moral/Ethical Instruction
 Teaches values: honesty, kindness, courage, respect
 Shows consequences of actions
 Shapes character and guides behavior
 Example: Folktales teach "Honesty is the best policy"
4. Cultural Preservation
 Records traditions, language, history, beliefs
 Maintains cultural identity
 Connects generations
 Example: Myths, legends, folktales preserve heritage
144
5. Social Commentary/Criticism
 Reflects and critiques society
 Exposes problems (poverty, corruption, injustice)
 Advocates for change
 Example: Achebe's "Things Fall Apart" critiques colonialism
6. Emotional Expression/Catharsis
 Allows expression and release of emotions
 Helps process feelings safely
 Develops empathy
7. Aesthetic Pleasure
 Provides beauty through language
 Appreciation of artistry and craftsmanship
 Poetry's musical quality, prose's flowing sentences
8. Imagination/Creativity Stimulation
 Fires imagination and encourages original thinking
 Presents alternative worlds and possibilities
 Enhances problem-solving
9. Communication/Language Development
 Improves vocabulary, grammar, expression
 Enhances reading, writing, speaking skills
 Builds language confidence
10. Empathy and Understanding
 Helps understand different perspectives
145
 Reduces prejudice, increases tolerance
 Builds compassion
11. Identity Formation
 Helps individuals/groups develop sense of self
 Builds national and cultural pride
 Example: Nigerian literature builds Nigerian identity
12. Historical Documentation
 Records events, daily life, attitudes
 Preserves history for future study
 Example: Slave narratives document slavery
Note: Most literary works serve multiple functions simultaneously.
PART B: INTRODUCTION TO PROSE
DEFINITION: Prose is writing organized in sentences and paragraphs, following natural speech
patterns (not poetry or drama).
CHARACTERISTICS:
1. Written in sentences and paragraphs
2. Natural, grammatical language
3. No regular meter or rhyme
4. Detailed and expansive
5. Narrative focus
TYPES OF PROSE:
146
A. FICTION (Imaginative)
1. Novel
 Long narrative (50,000+ words)
 Complex plot, multiple characters
 Examples: "Things Fall Apart," "Purple Hibiscus"
2. Novella
 Medium-length (20,000-50,000 words)
 Focused plot, fewer characters
 Examples: "Animal Farm," "The Old Man and the Sea"
3. Short Story
 Brief narrative (under 20,000 words)
 Single plot, few characters, one theme
 Read in one sitting
 Examples: "The Sacrificial Egg," "Civil Peace"
B. NON-FICTION (Factual)
1. Biography: Life story written by someone else
2. Autobiography: Life story written by oneself (first person)
3. Memoir: Account of specific period/theme from author's life
4. Essay: Short piece on single subject (expository, argumentative, descriptive, etc.)
5. Travel Writing: Accounts of journeys and places
6. Journalism: Reports of current events
7. Historical Writing: Accounts of past events
147
ELEMENTS OF PROSE (Fiction):
1. PLOT
 Sequence of events
 Structure: Exposition → Rising Action → Climax → Falling Action → Resolution
2. CHARACTER
 Types: Protagonist, antagonist, major, minor
 Complexity: Flat (simple) vs. Round (complex)
 Development: Static (no change) vs. Dynamic (changes)
 Characterization: Direct (author tells) vs. Indirect (through actions, speech, thoughts,
effects, looks)
3. SETTING
 Time and place where story occurs
 Includes historical period, location, social environment, atmosphere
 Influences characters and plot
4. THEME
 Central idea or message
 Usually inferred, not stated directly
 Can have multiple themes
 Examples: good vs. evil, tradition vs. change, coming of age
5. POINT OF VIEW
 First Person: "I" (narrator is character)
 Third Person Limited: "He/She" (knows one character's thoughts)
 Third Person Omniscient: "He/She" (knows all characters' thoughts)
148
 Second Person: "You" (rare)
6. STYLE
 Author's distinctive use of language
 Includes: diction, sentence structure, tone, imagery, figurative language, voice
7. CONFLICT
 Problem driving the story
 Types: Man vs. Man, Man vs. Self, Man vs. Society, Man vs. Nature, Man vs. Fate
PROSE VS. OTHER GENRES:
Aspect Prose Poetry Drama
Form Sentences, paragraphs Lines, stanzas Dialogue, stage directions
Language Natural, expansive Compressed, musical Spoken
Purpose Tell stories Express emotions Show action
HOW TO STUDY YOUR PROSE TEXT:
1. First Reading: Read for understanding and enjoyment
2. Second Reading: Identify plot, characters, setting, theme; take notes
3. Analysis: Character study, plot outline, theme identification, style observation
4. Critical Thinking: Why characters acted; author's message; real-life connections
5. Exam Prep: Character profiles, plot summary, themes with examples, practice answers
EVALUATION:
1. List 5 functions of literature with examples
149
2. Define prose and state 4 characteristics
3. Explain 3 elements of prose
4. Differentiate: (a) novel/short story (b) biography/autobiography (c) first/third person
POV
5. State title, author, and type of your recommended prose text
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Explain 10 functions of literature with examples
2. Create comparison table: prose vs. poetry vs. drama
3. Read first 3 chapters of prose text; write: (a) summary (b) main characters (c) setting (d)
one theme (e) opinion
4. Define 7 elements of prose with examples
5. Choose a short story; identify: (a) conflict type (b) POV (c) theme (d)
protagonist/antagonist (e) 3 literary devices
150
WEEK 4 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɪ/
THE SHORT VOWEL SOUND /ɪ/
Characteristics:
 Short, quick sound
 Tongue high at front (lower than /i:/)
 Lips slightly spread
 Relaxed, not tense
 Common in unstressed syllables
Mouth Position:
 Tongue raised toward front
 Lips neutral/slightly spread
 Jaw slightly open
 Tongue tip behind lower teeth
SPELLING PATTERNS FOR /ɪ/ SOUND
1. "i" spelling (most common):
 sit, bit, hit, big, dig, swim
 pin, tin, win, begin
 fish, dish, wish
 milk, silk
151
 give, live (verb)
2. "y" spelling (unstressed syllables):
 happy, lucky, funny, pretty
 baby, lady, city
 study, carry, worry
3. "e" spelling (rare):
 pretty, English, women
4. "ui" spelling (rare):
 build, building, guilt, biscuit
5. Other rare spellings:
 sieve (ie), women (o)
COMMON WORDS WITH /ɪ/
Short words:
 in, is, if, it, his, this, which, will, with, think
Medium words:
 sister, little, children, minute, begin, finish, visit, ticket
Long words:
 difficult, important, interesting, business, different, minister
MINIMAL PAIRS: /ɪ/ vs /i:/
Key Difference:
 /ɪ/ is SHORT and RELAXED (sit)
152
 /i:/ is LONG and TENSE (seat)
Practice Pairs:
 sit /ɪ/ - seat /i:/
 fit /ɪ/ - feet /i:/
 bit /ɪ/ - beat /i:/
 ship /ɪ/ - sheep /i:/
 live /ɪ/ - leave /i:/
 fill /ɪ/ - feel /i:/
 chip /ɪ/ - cheap /i:/
 slip /ɪ/ - sleep /i:/
 rich /ɪ/ - reach /i:/
Test Sentences:
 This ship carries sheep
 Can you feel the fill in the bottle?
 He will slip if he doesn't sleep well
 The bit of food helped him beat hunger
PRONUNCIATION TIPS
To Produce /ɪ/ Correctly:
1. Keep it short: Don't prolong the sound; quick and brief
2. Relax: Don't tense your mouth; comfortable position
3. Tongue position: High and front, but not as high as /i:/
4. Lips: Slightly spread or neutral, not stretched wide
153
5. Common in unstressed syllables: "begin," "happy"
WORD STRESS AND /ɪ/
In Stressed Syllables:
 SIT, BIG, MISS, FISH (clear /ɪ/ sound)
In Unstressed Syllables:
 hapPY, luckY, citY, baBY (even shorter/weaker)
Examples:
 VISit (first syllable stressed)
 DIFficult (first syllable stressed)
COMMON MISTAKES
1. Using /i:/ instead of /ɪ/:
 ✗ Saying "feel" when you mean "fill"
 Fix: Keep /ɪ/ short and relaxed
2. Using /e/ instead of /ɪ/:
 ✗ "pen" when you mean "pin"
 Fix: Raise tongue higher for /ɪ/
3. Making it too long:
 ✗ "siiit" (sounds like "seat")
 ✓ "sit" (quick)
4. Confusing "live" and "leave":
154
 live /ɪ/ (verb): "I live in Lagos"
 leave /i:/: "I will leave tomorrow"
PRACTICE EXERCISES
1. Minimal Pair Drills:
 sit/seat, bit/beat, hit/heat
 ship/sheep, chip/cheap
 fill/feel, will/wheel
2. Word Lists:
 List 1: big, sit, fit, hit, bit
 List 2: fish, wish, dish, finish, English
 List 3: begin, ticket, minute, visit, limit
3. Sentence Practice:
 It is difficult to finish this in a minute
 The big fish swim in the river
 Women and children visit the city
 Which sister will begin to sing?
4. Tongue Twisters:
 Six sick sheep sit in the ship
 Which wristwatch is a Swiss wristwatch?
 The myth of Miss Smith's mist list
DISTINGUISHING /ɪ/, /i:/, /e/
155
Three different sounds:
/ɪ/ (sit): Short, tongue high-front, relaxed
/i:/ (seat): Long, tongue very high-front, tense
/e/ (set): Short, tongue mid-front, relaxed
Practice Trio:
 sit /ɪ/ - seat /i:/ - set /e/
 bit /ɪ/ - beat /i:/ - bet /e/
 fill /ɪ/ - feel /i:/ - fell /e/
CONNECTED SPEECH
Weak Forms (common words use /ɪ/):
 it: "Get it done"
 is: "He is coming"
 his: "His book"
 been: "I've been there"
High-Frequency Words:
 in, is, it, if, his, with, this, which, will
SPELLING VS. PRONUNCIATION
Tricky Spellings:
"i" can be /ɪ/ or /aɪ/:
 bit /ɪ/ vs bite /aɪ/
 sit /ɪ/ vs site /aɪ/
156
"y" at end usually /ɪ/:
 happy, lucky, city
PHYSICAL PRACTICE
Exaggeration Exercise:
1. Say /i:/ (seat) - stretch lips wide, tongue very high
2. Relax to /ɪ/ (sit) - lips relax, tongue slightly lower
3. Feel the difference - /ɪ/ is more comfortable
4. Practice switching: seat-sit-seat-sit
Length Practice:
 Hold /i:/ for 3 seconds: "seeeeeat"
 Make /ɪ/ as short as possible: "sit" (instant)
EVALUATION
1. Describe how to produce the /ɪ/ sound (tongue position, lip shape, length)
2. Write ten words with "i" spelling that have /ɪ/ sound
3. Write ten words with "y" spelling that have /ɪ/ sound
4. What is the main difference between /ɪ/ and /i:/? Give five minimal pairs
5. Identify which words have /ɪ/ sound: (a) city (b) tree (c) big (d) sleep (e) fish (f) teach (g)
sit (h) difficult
ASSIGNMENT
1. List twenty words containing /ɪ/ sound with different spelling patterns (i, y, e, ui)
157
2. Write ten sentences using minimal pairs of /ɪ/ and /i:/ to show the difference (e.g., "Please
sit in your seat")
3. Create five sets of three-way contrasts: /ɪ/ vs /i:/ vs /e/ (like sit/seat/set) and use each in a
sentence
4. Practice and record yourself reading: "It is difficult for children to finish this interesting
activity. His sister will visit the city to begin business with the minister. Which women
will swim in the big river?"
5. Explain three common mistakes people make when pronouncing /ɪ/ and how to correct
them
158
WEEK 4 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: VERBS (CONTINUED) – TYPES AND FUNCTIONS
REVIEW: MAIN VERB TYPES
1. Transitive - needs object
2. Intransitive - no object needed
3. Regular - add -ed for past
4. Irregular - special past forms
5. Auxiliary - help main verb
6. Lexical - main meaning verb
FINITE VERBS
Definition: Verbs that show tense and agree with subject. Can stand alone.
Examples:
 I go, He goes (present)
 I went, She went (past)
 I will go (future)
NON-FINITE VERBS
Definition: Do NOT show tense or agree with subject. Cannot stand alone.
a) INFINITIVES
Forms: to go (to-infinitive), go (bare infinitive)
Uses:
159
 Subject: To read is important
 Object: I want to go
 Purpose: She came to help
 After modals: I can swim (bare)
 After certain verbs: Let me go (bare)
b) PARTICIPLES
PRESENT PARTICIPLE (-ing):
 Continuous tenses: I am going
 As adjective: running water
 In phrases: Walking home, I saw her
PAST PARTICIPLE (-ed/irregular):
 Perfect tenses: I have finished
 Passive voice: The door was closed
 As adjective: broken window
c) GERUNDS (-ing as noun)
 Subject: Swimming is fun
 Object: I enjoy dancing
 After prepositions: Thank you for helping
 Compound nouns: swimming pool
GERUND VS. PRESENT PARTICIPLE:
 Gerund (noun): Swimming is fun (can replace with "Music is fun")
 Participle (verb): I am swimming (cannot replace with noun)
160
COMPARISON TABLE
Type Example Shows Tense?
Finite goes, went Yes
Infinitive to go, go No
Present Participle going No
Past Participle gone, written No
Gerund swimming No
VERB FUNCTIONS
1. Action: run, think, speak
2. State: be, seem, remain
3. Possession: have, own
4. Linking: be, feel, look, taste
CAUSATIVE VERBS
Show someone causing something to happen.
Patterns:
 make + object + bare infinitive: She made me go
 have + object + bare infinitive: I'll have him call you
 get + object + to-infinitive: I got him to help
 let + object + bare infinitive: Let me go
 help + object + (to) infinitive: Help me (to) carry this
161
STATIVE VS. DYNAMIC VERBS
STATIVE (NOT used in continuous):
 Emotions: love, hate, like, want
 Thinking: know, believe, understand
 Senses: see, hear, smell (ability)
 Possession: have, own, belong
 Examples: ✓ I know / ✗ I am knowing
DYNAMIC (CAN use continuous):
 Actions: run, eat, write, study
 Examples: ✓ I am eating / ✓ She is running
BOTH:
 think: I think so (opinion) / I am thinking (process)
 have: I have a car (possess) / I am having dinner (activity)
 see: I see it (ability) / I am seeing him tomorrow (meet)
PHRASAL VERBS
Verb + particle = new meaning
Separable: turn off the light / turn the light off
Inseparable: look after the baby (NOT: look the baby after)
Common Examples:
 give up = quit
 look after = take care of
162
 put off = postpone
 break down = stop working
 find out = discover
SUBJECT-VERB AGREEMENT
Rules:
 Singular: The boy runs / She eats
 Plural: The boys run / They eat
 Collective nouns: The team is ready
 "And": John and Mary are friends
 "Or/nor": agree with nearest
 Indefinite pronouns:
o Singular: everyone, somebody, each (Everyone is here)
o Plural: few, many, both (Few are ready)
COMMON ERRORS
1. ✗ Yesterday I go → ✓ Yesterday I went
2. ✗ He go → ✓ He goes
3. ✗ I can goes → ✓ I can go
4. ✗ I have went → ✓ I have gone
5. ✗ I am knowing → ✓ I know
163
EVALUATION
1. Explain finite vs. non-finite verbs with examples
2. What are the three types of non-finite verbs? Give examples
3. Explain gerund vs. present participle with examples
4. What are stative verbs? List five
5. Define phrasal verbs. Give five examples
ASSIGNMENT
1. Identify and classify verbs as finite/non-finite: "I want to go home. Mary is studying now.
Having finished her work, she decided to rest. She enjoys reading."
2. Write ten sentences using gerunds: 5 as subjects, 5 as objects
3. Write two example sentences for each causative verb: make, have, get, let, help
4. Create sentences using: give up, look after, turn off, run into, get over
5. Correct and explain:
o I am knowing him
o She can goes
o They was playing
o He have went home
o Yesterday I am eating rice
164
WEEK 4 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: IDENTIFYING MAIN AND SUPPORTING IDEAS; VOCABULARY – YOUR
SCHOOL SUBJECTS
PART A: IDENTIFYING MAIN AND SUPPORTING IDEAS
WHAT ARE MAIN IDEAS?
Definition: The most important point or central message in a paragraph. What the paragraph is
mostly about.
Also Called: Central idea, Topic sentence, Main point
Characteristics:
 Most general statement
 Covers the whole paragraph
 Supported by other sentences
 Answers: "What is this paragraph mainly about?"
WHERE TO FIND MAIN IDEAS
1. Beginning (Most Common - 60-70%): Topic sentence first, then supporting details.
2. End (20-25%): Supporting details build to main idea at end.
3. Middle (5-10%): Introduction, then main idea, then more details.
4. Implied (5-10%): Not directly stated; reader must infer from details.
HOW TO IDENTIFY MAIN IDEAS
165
Steps:
1. Read the entire paragraph
2. Ask: "What is the main point?"
3. Look for clues: repeated words, first or last sentence, general statement
4. Test: Does it cover the whole paragraph?
MAIN IDEA VS. TOPIC
Topic: General subject (1-2 words) Main Idea: Complete thought about topic (full sentence)
Example:
 Topic: Nigeria's diversity
 Main Idea: Nigeria's ethnic diversity is both a strength and a challenge
WHAT ARE SUPPORTING IDEAS?
Definition: Details, facts, examples, and explanations that develop or prove the main idea.
Purpose: Prove, explain, give examples, and clarify the main idea
TYPES OF SUPPORTING IDEAS
1. FACTS AND STATISTICS: Specific, verifiable information
2. EXAMPLES: Specific instances
3. REASONS: Explanations for why
4. DESCRIPTIONS: Detailed characteristics
5. EXPLANATIONS: Clarifications of how or why
166
MAJOR VS. MINOR SUPPORTING IDEAS
Major: Directly support main idea; essential to understanding
Minor: Support major ideas; provide additional detail; can be removed
Example:
 Main Idea: Exercise is essential for good health
o Major: It strengthens the heart
 Minor: Hearts pump blood more efficiently
PRACTICE PASSAGE
"Studying in a quiet environment improves concentration. When there are no distractions,
students focus better. Research shows students in quiet areas remember 40% more information.
Libraries provide such quiet spaces. Therefore, students should seek quiet places when
studying."
Analysis:
 Topic: Studying in quiet environments
 Main Idea: First sentence
 Supporting Ideas: Reason (focus better), Fact (40% more), Example (libraries),
Conclusion
TIPS FOR SUCCESS
DO: Read entire paragraph, look for repeated words, check first/last sentences
DON'T: Choose a detail as main idea, pick something too general
PART B: VOCABULARY – YOUR SCHOOL SUBJECTS
167
CORE SUBJECTS
1. MATHEMATICS: Numbers, quantities, shapes
 Words: add, subtract, equation, fraction, calculate
2. ENGLISH LANGUAGE: English communication
 Words: noun, verb, essay, vocabulary, spelling
3. SCIENCE:
 Biology: Living things - cell, photosynthesis, organism
 Chemistry: Substances - atom, molecule, acid, laboratory
 Physics: Matter and energy - force, gravity, electricity
4. SOCIAL STUDIES:
 History: Past events - civilization, war, artifact
 Geography: Earth features - continent, mountain, climate, map
 Civic Education: Citizenship - rights, democracy, election
5. RELIGIOUS STUDIES: Religions and morals
 Words: scripture, prayer, faith, morality
GENERAL SCHOOL VOCABULARY
Classroom Items: desk, board, textbook, pen, ruler
Actions: study, read, write, calculate, discuss, revise
Assessment: test, examination, homework, grade, pass
Time: period, lesson, term, timetable
People: teacher, student, principal, classmate
168
EVALUATION
1. Explain the difference between main idea and supporting ideas
2. Where are main ideas usually located? Give three positions
3. List four types of supporting details
4. Name ten school subjects and what is studied in each
5. Identify main idea and three supporting details in this passage: "Mathematics is useful in
everyday life. We use it shopping, cooking, telling time, and managing money."
ASSIGNMENT
1. Write five paragraphs (5 sentences each) with clear main ideas. Underline main idea in
each
2. Read any passage. Identify: (a) topic (b) main idea (c) three supporting details (d) types
of support
3. Create vocabulary list for five favorite subjects: (a) 10 words per subject (b) use 5 words
in sentences
4. Explain major vs. minor supporting details with examples
5. Match details to main ideas:
o Main Ideas: (1) Exercise is beneficial (2) Reading improves knowledge (3) Water
is essential
o Details: (a) Books provide information (b) Bodies need hydration (c) Physical
activity strengthens muscles (d) Literature expands vocabulary (e) Water
regulates temperature
169
WEEK 4 – COMPOSITION
TOPIC: DIFFERENCES BETWEEN DESCRIPTIVE AND NARRATIVE ESSAYS
BASIC DEFINITIONS
Descriptive Essay: Writing that paints a picture with words, describing a person, place, object,
or event using sensory language.
Narrative Essay: Writing that tells a story or relates events in chronological order, with
characters, setting, and plot.
KEY DIFFERENCES
1. PRIMARY PURPOSE
Descriptive:
 Purpose: To describe; show what something is like
 Focus: How something looks, sounds, feels, smells, tastes
 Answer: "What is it like?"
 Example: "My Classroom" - help reader visualize
Narrative:
 Purpose: To tell a story; relate events
 Focus: What happened, when, to whom
 Answer: "What happened?"
 Example: "My First Day in That Classroom" - tell what occurred
2. ORGANIZATION
170
Descriptive:
 Spatial order: top to bottom, left to right, near to far
 Order of importance: most to least noticeable
 No time sequence needed
Narrative:
 Chronological order: what happened first, next, then, finally
 Clear beginning, middle, end
 Time is essential
3. TENSE
Descriptive:
 Usually present tense
 Examples: "The school stands majestically..." "My father is a tall man..."
Narrative:
 Usually past tense
 Examples: "I woke up early..." "She walked to school..."
4. CONTENT FOCUS
Descriptive:
 Static: Describes things as they are
 Detailed descriptions: colors, sizes, textures, sounds, smells
 Sensory language: appeals to five senses
 Abundant adjectives and adverbs
171
 Figurative language: metaphors, similes
Key Elements:
 Physical characteristics
 Sensory details
 Spatial relationships
 Atmosphere created
Example: "The ancient tree towers over the schoolyard, its thick trunk scarred with carved
initials. Gnarled branches spread like protective arms, casting cool shade. The leaves rustle softly
in the breeze, creating a gentle whisper."
Narrative:
 Dynamic: Describes actions and changes
 Event descriptions: what happened, who did what
 Action verbs and dialogue
 Plot progression
Key Elements:
 Characters (who)
 Actions (what they did)
 Sequence (when, order)
 Cause and effect
 Conflict and resolution
Example: "I arrived at school early that Monday morning. As I walked toward the ancient tree, I
noticed something strange. Someone had carved a huge message on the trunk overnight. My
friends and I spent the entire break trying to figure out who had done it."
172
5. POINT OF VIEW
Descriptive:
 Third person most common: "The school has..." "It stands..."
 Observer perspective
Narrative:
 First person very common: "I went..." "We saw..."
 Active participation in events
6. STRUCTURE
Descriptive:
 Introduction: General impression, what is being described
 Body: Organized by space or importance; rich sensory details
 Conclusion: Overall impression, significance, emotional response
Narrative:
 Introduction: Setting scene, introducing characters, creating interest
 Body: Events in time order, rising action, complications, climax
 Conclusion: Resolution, what was learned, reflection
7. LANGUAGE FEATURES
Descriptive:
 Adjectives: beautiful, tall, bright, magnificent
 Sensory words: shimmering, fragrant, melodious, rough
173
 Similes/Metaphors: "as white as snow," "like a fortress"
 Spatial transitions: to the left, above, below, nearby
Example: "The majestic building stands proudly at the corner, its white walls gleaming like
pearls in the sunlight. Surrounding it are lush green gardens that spread like a soft carpet."
Narrative:
 Action verbs: ran, jumped, shouted, discovered
 Time adverbs: yesterday, then, suddenly, finally
 Dialogue: Direct speech
 Time transitions: first, then, next, after, meanwhile, finally
Example: "I ran quickly to the building. Suddenly, the door flew open and my friend appeared.
'Quick!' she shouted, 'Come see what we found!' I hurried inside, my heart pounding."
8. READER EXPERIENCE
Descriptive:
 Reader sees: creates mental picture
 Like looking at a photograph (static)
 Emotional response to beauty, atmosphere
Narrative:
 Reader follows: accompanies the journey
 Like watching a movie (dynamic)
 Emotional response to action, conflict, resolution
174
COMPARISON TABLE
Aspect Descriptive Narrative
Purpose Describe/paint picture Tell story
Organization Spatial/importance Chronological
Tense Usually present Usually past
Focus What it's like What happened
Language Adjectives, sensory words Action verbs, dialogue
Transitions Spatial (left, above) Time (then, next)
Point of View Often 3rd person Often 1st person
Movement Static Dynamic
RECOGNIZING THE TYPE FROM TOPIC
Descriptive Topics (Key Words: Describe, What...like):
 Describe your school
 My best friend
 The market near my house
 A beautiful place I visited
Narrative Topics (Key Words: Tell, Narrate, Day, Experience):
 My first day at school
 An unforgettable experience
 The day I learned a lesson
 How I spent my last holiday
175
CAN A TOPIC BE BOTH?
Yes! Example: "My Grandmother"
Descriptive Approach: Describe her appearance, personality, characteristics "My grandmother
is a petite woman with silver hair always tied in a neat bun. Her warm brown eyes crinkle when
she smiles. She wears traditional wrappers in bright colors and always smells faintly of palm
oil..."
Narrative Approach: Tell a story about an experience with her "I will never forget the day my
grandmother taught me to make jollof rice. It was a hot Saturday morning when she called me
into the kitchen. 'Today, you learn our family secret,' she announced..."
MIXING BOTH IN ONE ESSAY
In Narrative Essays: Include description to set scenes and make story vivid
Example: "I entered the dimly lit room (narrative). Dust particles danced in the sunlight
streaming through the cracked window. The air smelled musty (description). As I took another
step, the floorboard creaked (narrative continues)."
In Descriptive Essays: Include brief narrative moments to illustrate characteristics
PRACTICE: IDENTIFYING THE TYPE
Passage 1: "The old library building stands at the end of University Road. Its red brick walls
have weathered fifty years. Three wide steps lead to massive wooden doors. Inside, the smell of
old books fills the air. Tall shelves line every wall."
Type: Descriptive Clues: Present tense, spatial organization, sensory details, adjectives, no
events
176
Passage 2: "Last Saturday, I decided to visit the old library. I climbed the three steps and pushed
open the heavy door. It creaked loudly. Embarrassed, I quickly found a table and sat down. I
spent three hours reading. When I looked up, the library was closing."
Type: Narrative Clues: Past tense, chronological order, events in sequence, first-person
experience
WRITING TIPS
For Descriptive Essays:
 Use all five senses
 Be specific
 Use figurative language
 Organize logically (spatial order)
 Show, don't just tell
For Narrative Essays:
 Have a clear story arc
 Include dialogue
 Build to a climax
 Use specific details
 Include reflection/lesson learned
COMMON MISTAKES
In Descriptive:
 ✗ Including too much action
177
 ✗ Using past tense for current descriptions
 ✗ Being too vague
In Narrative:
 ✗ Describing instead of telling what happened
 ✗ Mixing up chronological order
 ✗ No clear point or lesson
EVALUATION
1. State five main differences between descriptive and narrative essays
2. What tense is typically used in: (a) descriptive essays (b) narrative essays? Why?
3. How is organization different in descriptive vs. narrative essays?
4. Identify whether these require descriptive or narrative essays:
o (a) My Last Birthday
o (b) The Busiest Market in Town
o (c) An Act of Kindness I Witnessed
o (d) My Best Friend
o (e) How I Learned to Swim
5. Write two short paragraphs (5 sentences each) on "My School": one descriptive, one
narrative
ASSIGNMENT
1. Create a comparison table showing ten differences between descriptive and narrative
essays with examples
178
2. Take the topic "My Mother" and write:
o (a) A descriptive paragraph (100 words) on her appearance and character
o (b) A narrative paragraph (100 words) telling a story about an experience with her
3. Identify which passage is descriptive and which is narrative, giving three reasons for each:
o Passage A: "The sun rose slowly over the mountains, painting the sky pink and
orange. The air was crisp and cool. Birds began their morning songs. Dew
sparkled on the grass like tiny diamonds."
o Passage B: "I woke up early to watch the sunrise. I climbed to the top of the hill
and waited. When the sun finally appeared, I gasped at its beauty. I stayed there
for an hour, just watching."
4. List ten transitions commonly used in descriptive essays and ten used in narrative essays
5. Explain why dialogue is common in narrative but not descriptive essays. Give examples
179
WEEK 4 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: ELEMENTS OF PROSE – PLOT, STYLE, CHARACTERIZATION; TYPES OF
ORAL LITERATURE
PART A: ELEMENTS OF PROSE
1. PLOT
Definition: The sequence of events that make up a story.
PLOT STRUCTURE (Five Parts):
a) EXPOSITION:
 Introduction to story
 Characters and setting introduced
 Background information
 Example: "John was a hardworking farmer in a small village..."
b) RISING ACTION:
 Conflict introduced
 Problems develop
 Tension builds
 Example: "Strange things began happening. Crops failed. Villagers blamed John..."
c) CLIMAX:
 Most intense moment
 Highest point of tension
 Turning point
180
 Example: "John confronted the spirit king. The battle was fierce..."
d) FALLING ACTION:
 Events after climax
 Tension decreases
 Conflicts begin resolving
 Example: "The spirit king explained and lifted the curse..."
e) RESOLUTION:
 Conclusion
 All conflicts resolved
 Final outcome
 Example: "John returned as a hero. The village lived in harmony..."
TYPES OF CONFLICT:
 Man vs. Man
 Man vs. Self
 Man vs. Society
 Man vs. Nature
 Man vs. Fate
2. CHARACTERIZATION
Definition: Methods an author uses to develop characters.
A. DIRECT CHARACTERIZATION: Author directly tells us about character
181
 Example: "John was a brave man."
B. INDIRECT CHARACTERIZATION (STEAL Method):
S - Speech: What character says
 Example: "Please, kind sir..." (polite person)
T - Thoughts: What character thinks
 Example: John smiled but thought, "This man is a fool." (diplomatic but judgmental)
E - Effects on Others: How others react
 Example: Everyone smiled when Mary entered. (shows she's loved)
A - Actions: What character does
 Example: She gave her coat to the shivering child. (selfless, compassionate)
L - Looks: Physical appearance
 Example: His clothes were always neat. (orderly, disciplined)
CHARACTER TYPES:
By Complexity:
 Flat: Simple, one-dimensional, stereotypical
 Round: Complex, multi-dimensional, realistic
By Development:
 Static: Don't change throughout story
 Dynamic: Change and grow
By Role:
 Protagonist: Main character
182
 Antagonist: Opposes protagonist
 Supporting: Help tell the story
 Minor: Brief appearances
3. STYLE
Definition: The distinctive way an author uses language.
ELEMENTS OF STYLE:
A. DICTION (Word Choice):
 Formal: Sophisticated vocabulary, proper grammar
 Informal: Everyday language, conversational, slang
 Technical: Specialized vocabulary
 Regional: Dialect, local expressions
B. SENTENCE STRUCTURE:
 Simple: Short, direct, creates pace
 Complex: Long, detailed, elaborate
 Varied: Mix of both, creates rhythm
C. TONE: Author's attitude toward subject
 Serious, humorous, ironic, sarcastic, formal, informal, optimistic, cynical, nostalgic
D. IMAGERY: Descriptive language appealing to senses
 Visual (sight), Auditory (sound), Olfactory (smell), Gustatory (taste), Tactile (touch)
E. FIGURATIVE LANGUAGE:
 Simile: Comparison using "like/as" ("Her voice was like honey")
183
 Metaphor: Direct comparison ("Time is money")
 Personification: Human qualities to non-human ("Wind whispered")
 Hyperbole: Exaggeration ("I've told you a million times")
 Symbolism: Object represents idea (dove = peace)
F. VOICE:
 Unique sound/personality of writing
 Author's recognizable style
PART B: TYPES OF ORAL LITERATURE
Definition: Literature composed, performed, and transmitted orally.
1. FOLKTALES: Traditional fictional stories teaching lessons
Types:
 Animal Tales: Animals as characters teaching lessons
 Trickster Tales: Clever character overcomes stronger opponents
 "Why" Stories: Explain natural phenomena (Why Tortoise has cracked shell)
 Moral Tales: Directly teach lessons
Characteristics: "Once upon a time" openings, stock characters, moral lessons
2. MYTHS: Sacred stories explaining origins, featuring gods and supernatural beings
Types:
 Creation Myths: How world was created
 Explanatory Myths: Why things are the way they are
184
 Divine Myths: Stories of gods and goddesses
Example: Yoruba creation myth (Olodumare creating world)
3. LEGENDS: Traditional stories about heroes based partially on fact but embellished
Examples:
 Sundiata (Mali)
 Queen Amina of Zaria
 Shaka Zulu
 Queen Moremi of Ile-Ife
Characteristics: Historical basis, heroic deeds, cultural pride
4. PROVERBS: Short, wise sayings expressing truths
Examples:
 "When two elephants fight, the grass suffers"
 "A tree cannot make a forest"
 "A river that forgets its source will dry up"
Functions: Teach wisdom, guide behavior, settle arguments
5. RIDDLES: Puzzles testing wit and intelligence
Examples:
 "I have teeth but cannot eat." (A comb)
 "What runs but never walks?" (A river)
 "What gets wetter as it dries?" (A towel)
185
6. PRAISE POETRY: Poems praising people, gods, or places
Types:
 Oriki (Yoruba): Praise names for individuals, families, deities
 Praise for Rulers: Kings, chiefs, leaders
 Praise for Warriors: Celebrates bravery
Characteristics: Repetition, rhythmic, often with music/drums
7. SONGS:
Types:
 Work Songs: Sung during labor
 Lullabies: Soothe babies
 War Songs: Inspire courage
 Festival Songs: Celebrate occasions
 Love Songs: Express romance
8. ORAL EPICS: Long narrative poems about heroes
Examples:
 Sundiata Epic (Mali)
 Ozidi Saga (Nigeria/Ijaw)
Characteristics: Very long, heroic deeds, performed by griots
9. ORAL HISTORY: Historical accounts passed down orally
186
Content: Migrations, wars, founding of kingdoms, important rulers
10. TONGUE TWISTERS: Phrases difficult to say quickly
Examples:
 "She sells seashells by the seashore"
 "Red lorry, yellow lorry"
PERFORMANCE FEATURES:
 Evening gatherings, festivals, ceremonies
 Storyteller uses voice modulation, gestures, facial expressions
 Audience participation: call and response, singing, clapping
 Music and drumming
 Formulaic language: "Once upon a time..."
FUNCTIONS OF ORAL LITERATURE:
1. Entertainment
2. Education
3. Moral instruction
4. Cultural preservation
5. Social control
6. Historical record
7. Community bonding
8. Language development
187
EVALUATION
1. Explain the five parts of plot structure with an example for each
2. Describe the STEAL method of indirect characterization with examples
3. What is the difference between flat and round characters? Between static and dynamic?
4. Explain five elements of an author's style
5. Compare folktales, myths, and legends: (a) Definition (b) Characteristics (c) Purpose (d)
Examples
ASSIGNMENT
1. Choose any story and analyze its plot: Identify (a) Exposition (b) Rising Action (c)
Climax (d) Falling Action (e) Resolution
2. Create a character using indirect characterization: Write a paragraph (100 words)
showing personality through STEAL method WITHOUT directly stating traits
3. Read a passage and analyze style: (a) Diction type (b) Sentence structure (c) Tone (d)
Imagery (e) Figurative language (f) Voice
4. Collect and write: (a) 5 proverbs with meanings (b) 3 riddles with answers (c) One
folktale summary (150 words)
5. Explain direct vs. indirect characterization with three examples of each from any story
188
JSS1 ENGLISH STUDIES LESSON NOTES
WEEK 6 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /e/
THE SHORT VOWEL /e/ (as in "bed"):
Production:
 Tongue mid-front position
 Lips slightly spread
 Jaw moderately open
 Short, quick sound
Common Spellings:
 "e": bed, red, pen, men, tell, best
 "ea": head, bread, dead, ready, weather, heavy
 "ai": said, again
 "a": any, many
 "ie": friend
 "eo": leopard
 "u": bury
Minimal Pairs (/e/ vs /æ/):
 pen /e/ - pan /æ/
 bed /e/ - bad /æ/
 met /e/ - mat /æ/
189
 set /e/ - sat /æ/
Practice Sentence: "Ten men said the red pen was better than any pen in the test."
EVALUATION:
1. Produce /e/ sound and give 10 words containing it
2. Differentiate /e/ from /æ/ with 5 minimal pairs
3. Identify /e/ words: (a) head (b) hat (c) bed (d) bad (e) said
4. Write 5 sentences using /e/ words
5. What spelling patterns produce /e/ sound?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List 20 words with /e/ sound (different spellings)
2. Practice: "Ten red hens fed on bread and eggs. Any friend can lend me a pen."
3. Create 5 minimal pairs: /e/ vs /æ/ and /e/ vs /ɪ/
4. Identify the vowel sound in: ready, head, any, friend, said, many, test, best
5. Write 10 sentences using words with /e/ sound
190
WEEK 6 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: ADJECTIVES – DEFINITION, TYPES; COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES
DEFINITION: An adjective is a word that describes or modifies a noun or pronoun.
TYPES OF ADJECTIVES:
1. Descriptive (Qualitative): Describe quality, size, shape, color, etc.
 beautiful, large, round, red, happy, old
2. Quantitative: Show quantity (how much/many)
 some, many, few, much, little, several, enough
3. Demonstrative: Point to specific things
 this, that, these, those
4. Possessive: Show ownership
 my, your, his, her, its, our, their
5. Interrogative: Used in questions
 which, what, whose
6. Numeral:
 Cardinal: one, two, three (how many)
 Ordinal: first, second, third (order)
7. Proper Adjectives: From proper nouns
 Nigerian food, African culture, Christian values
COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES:
Three Degrees:
1. POSITIVE (Absolute): Basic form, no comparison
191
 tall, big, beautiful, good
2. COMPARATIVE: Comparing two things
 Formation:
o Add -er: tall → taller, big → bigger
o Use "more": beautiful → more beautiful
o Irregular: good → better, bad → worse
 Pattern: adjective + -er + than OR more + adjective + than
 Examples: She is taller than me. This is more beautiful than that.
3. SUPERLATIVE: Comparing three or more (highest degree)
 Formation:
o Add -est: tall → tallest, big → biggest
o Use "most": beautiful → most beautiful
o Irregular: good → best, bad → worst
 Pattern: the + adjective + -est OR the most + adjective
 Examples: She is the tallest girl. This is the most beautiful flower.
Comparison Table:
Positive Comparative Superlative
tall taller tallest
big bigger biggest
happy happier happiest
beautiful more beautiful most beautiful
good better best
192
bad worse worst
many/much more most
little less least
Rules:
 One syllable: add -er/-est (small, smaller, smallest)
 Two syllables ending in -y: change y to i, add -er/-est (happy, happier, happiest)
 Two+ syllables: use more/most (careful, more careful, most careful)
 Irregular forms must be memorized
EVALUATION:
1. Define adjective and give 5 examples
2. List 6 types of adjectives with 2 examples each
3. Write the comparative and superlative forms: (a) strong (b) careful (c) good (d) bad (e)
little
4. Fill in: John is _____ (tall) than Peter but Mary is the _____ (tall) in the class
5. Identify adjectives and their types: "That beautiful Nigerian girl has three blue bags"
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List 10 adjectives of each type: descriptive, quantitative, demonstrative, possessive
2. Write 10 sentences using different types of adjectives
3. Compare these in three degrees: fast, intelligent, many, far, old
193
4. Correct errors: (a) She is more taller (b) He is the most good student (c) This is the bigger
house

WEEK 6 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: DIFFERENTIATING MAIN IDEAS FROM SUPPORTING IDEAS IN A
PASSAGE
MAIN IDEA:
 The most important point in a paragraph
 What the paragraph is mostly about
 Usually the topic sentence
 General statement
WHERE TO FIND:
 Beginning (most common - 70%)
 End (20%)
 Middle (5%)
 Implied (not stated - 5%)
SUPPORTING IDEAS:
 Details that explain, prove, or develop the main idea
 Examples, facts, reasons, descriptions
 Specific statements
TYPES OF SUPPORT:
1. Facts/Statistics: specific data
2. Examples: instances illustrating main idea
3. Reasons: why main idea is true
4. Descriptions: detailed characteristics
195
5. Explanations: how or why something works
HOW TO IDENTIFY:
Main Idea:
 Ask: "What is this paragraph mainly about?"
 Look for general statement
 Check first/last sentence
 Find repeated concepts
Supporting Ideas:
 Ask: "What details explain the main idea?"
 Look for specific information
 Find examples and evidence
PRACTICE PASSAGE:
"Regular exercise is essential for good health. It strengthens the heart and improves circulation.
Exercise also helps maintain healthy weight and reduces disease risk. Studies show that people
who exercise regularly live longer. Additionally, physical activity boosts mental health by
reducing stress and anxiety."
Analysis:
 Main Idea: Regular exercise is essential for good health (first sentence)
 Supporting Ideas:
1. Strengthens heart, improves circulation
2. Maintains weight, reduces disease
3. Studies show longer life
4. Boosts mental health, reduces stress
196
EVALUATION:
1. Define main idea and supporting idea
2. Where are main ideas usually located in paragraphs?
3. List 5 types of supporting details
4. Read this passage and identify the main idea: "Water is vital for life. The human body is
60% water. We need water for digestion, circulation, and temperature regulation.
Without water, a person cannot survive more than three days."
5. From the passage in Q4, list 3 supporting details
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a paragraph with a clear main idea and 3 supporting details on "Benefits of
Reading"
2. Read any passage from your textbook. Identify: (a) Topic (b) Main idea (c) Three
supporting details (d) Type of support used
3. Create a graphic organizer showing main idea and supporting details for this topic: "Why
students should eat breakfast"
4. Differentiate between main idea and topic using examples
5. Analyze this passage - identify main and supporting ideas: "My grandmother is very kind.
She helps sick neighbors daily. She volunteers at the orphanage every weekend. Last year,
she paid school fees for poor children. Everyone loves and respects her."
197
WEEK 6 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: PARAGRAPH WRITING – LOGICAL SEQUENCE WITH INTRODUCTION &
CONCLUSION
WHAT IS A PARAGRAPH? A group of sentences about one main idea.
STRUCTURE OF A PARAGRAPH:
1. TOPIC SENTENCE (Introduction):
 States main idea
 Usually first sentence
 General statement about the topic
2. SUPPORTING SENTENCES (Body):
 3-6 sentences
 Develop the main idea
 Give details, examples, explanations
 All relate to topic sentence
3. CONCLUDING SENTENCE (Conclusion):
 Last sentence
 Wraps up the paragraph
 Restates main idea or provides final thought
 May link to next paragraph
LOGICAL SEQUENCE: Ideas must flow in order:
 Time order: first, then, next, finally (for events)
 Space order: left to right, top to bottom (for descriptions)
198
 Order of importance: least to most important
TRANSITIONS: Connect ideas smoothly:
 Addition: also, furthermore, moreover, additionally
 Time: first, then, next, after, finally, meanwhile
 Contrast: but, however, although, on the other hand
 Result: therefore, thus, consequently, as a result
 Example: for example, for instance, such as
PARAGRAPH UNITY: All sentences must relate to the topic sentence.
SAMPLE PARAGRAPH:
"My school library is a wonderful place to study. (TOPIC SENTENCE) It has comfortable chairs
and quiet study areas where students can concentrate. (SUPPORT 1) The library contains over
5,000 books on various subjects, making research easy. (SUPPORT 2) Friendly librarians are
always available to help students find materials. (SUPPORT 3) Therefore, the library is truly an
ideal environment for learning. (CONCLUDING SENTENCE)"
EVALUATION:
1. Define a paragraph and state its three parts
2. What is a topic sentence and where is it usually found?
3. What is paragraph unity?
4. List 5 transitions used for: (a) time order (b) adding information
5. Write a paragraph (6 sentences) on "My Best Friend" with clear topic, supporting, and
concluding sentences
ASSIGNMENT:
199
1. Write 3 paragraphs (6-7 sentences each) on different topics showing clear structure
2. Label the topic sentence, supporting sentences, and concluding sentence in each
paragraph you write
3. Identify the parts in this paragraph: "Football is the most popular sport in Nigeria.
Millions of people watch matches every weekend. Children play football in streets and
fields across the country. Nigeria has produced world-class players who compete
internationally. Clearly, football unites Nigerians from all backgrounds."
4. Write a paragraph using time order transitions on "How I Prepare for School"
5. Explain why logical sequence is important in paragraph writing
200
WEEK 6 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO DRAMA; TYPES & ELEMENTS OF DRAMA;
CHARACTERIZATION & THEMES IN RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXT
PART A: INTRODUCTION TO DRAMA
DEFINITION: Drama is literature written to be performed by actors before an audience.
CHARACTERISTICS:
1. Written in dialogue form
2. Meant for performance
3. Uses stage directions
4. Has acts and scenes
5. Requires actors, stage, audience
DIFFERENCE FROM PROSE:
 Drama: dialogue, performance, stage directions
 Prose: narrative, paragraphs, description
TYPES OF DRAMA:
1. TRAGEDY:
 Serious, sad ending
 Hero suffers/dies
 Explores serious themes
 Example: Macbeth
2. COMEDY:
201
 Humorous, happy ending
 Makes audience laugh
 Light themes
 Example: School comedies
3. TRAGICOMEDY:
 Mix of tragedy and comedy
 Serious themes with humor
 May have mixed ending
ELEMENTS OF DRAMA:
1. PLOT: Sequence of events in the play
2. CHARACTER: People in the play
 Protagonist (hero)
 Antagonist (opponent)
 Supporting characters
3. DIALOGUE: Conversations between characters
 Reveals character
 Advances plot
4. THEME: Central message/idea
5. SETTING: Time and place
6. STAGE DIRECTIONS: Instructions for actors
 [Enter John], [Exit], [Aside], [Angrily]
 Written in italics/brackets
202
7. CONFLICT: Struggle/problem driving the action
8. ACT & SCENE:
 Act: Major division
 Scene: Smaller division within act
PART B: CHARACTERIZATION & THEMES IN PROSE TEXT
CHARACTERIZATION (Review):
Methods:
 Direct: Author tells us about character
 Indirect (STEAL):
o Speech: what character says
o Thoughts: what character thinks
o Effect: how others react
o Actions: what character does
o Looks: physical appearance
Character Types:
 Protagonist vs. Antagonist
 Major vs. Minor
 Round (complex) vs. Flat (simple)
 Dynamic (changes) vs. Static (no change)
THEMES:
Definition: Central idea or message in the text
203
Common Themes:
 Love and sacrifice
 Good vs. evil
 Coming of age
 Tradition vs. change
 Courage and bravery
 Family relationships
 Social injustice
 Power and corruption
Finding Themes:
 What is the author trying to say about life?
 What lessons does the story teach?
 What universal truths are explored?
In Your Prose Text:
 Identify 2-3 main themes
 Support with evidence from text
 Explain how themes are developed
EVALUATION:
1. Define drama and state 3 differences from prose
2. Name and explain 3 types of drama
3. List 8 elements of drama
4. What are stage directions and why are they important?
5. In your prose text: (a) Who is the protagonist? (b) Name one main theme
204
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a short drama scene (1 page) with 3 characters, including dialogue and stage
directions
2. Explain 5 elements of drama with examples
3. Compare how character is revealed in drama vs. prose
4. Read Chapters 4-6 of your prose text. Write: (a) Character analysis of the protagonist (b)
Two themes identified so far with examples
5. Define these drama terms: soliloquy, aside, monologue, act, scene

THE SHORT VOWEL /æ/ (as in "cat"):

 Lips spread
 Short sound
Common Spellings:
 "a": cat, hat, man, bad, apple, family, happy

 cat /æ/ - cut /ʌ/
 man /æ/ - men /e/

 bad /æ/ - bed /e/
