WEEK 8 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: ADVERBS – DEFINITION, IDENTIFICATION, TYPES
DEFINITION: An adverb is a word that modifies a verb, adjective, or another adverb. It tells
how, when, where, or to what extent.
IDENTIFICATION:
 Ask: How? When? Where? How often? To what extent?
 Many end in -ly (quickly, slowly, carefully)
 Not all -ly words are adverbs (lovely, friendly are adjectives)
TYPES OF ADVERBS:
1. ADVERBS OF MANNER (How?): Tell how an action is done
 Formation: adjective + ly (quick → quickly, careful → carefully)
 Examples: slowly, fast, well, hard, badly, beautifully, quietly, loudly
 Position: usually after verb or object
 "She sings beautifully." "He ran quickly."
2. ADVERBS OF TIME (When?): Tell when an action occurs
 Examples: now, today, tomorrow, yesterday, soon, later, early, late, already, recently,
eventually
 Position: usually at beginning or end of sentence
 "I will go tomorrow." "Yesterday, she came."
3. ADVERBS OF PLACE (Where?): Tell where an action occurs
 Examples: here, there, everywhere, nowhere, inside, outside, above, below, nearby, far
208
 Position: usually after verb or at end
 "Come here." "They went outside."
4. ADVERBS OF FREQUENCY (How often?): Tell how often an action occurs
 Examples: always (100%), usually (90%), often (70%), sometimes (50%), rarely (10%),
never (0%), daily, weekly, monthly, yearly, once, twice
 Position: usually before main verb, after 'be'
 "I always wake up early." "She is never late."
5. ADVERBS OF DEGREE (To what extent?): Tell the intensity or degree
 Examples: very, quite, too, almost, nearly, extremely, rather, fairly, completely, totally,
hardly, barely
 Position: usually before adjective or adverb
 "She is very tall." "He ran too fast."
COMPARISON OF ADVERBS:
Positive Comparative Superlative
fast faster fastest
hard harder hardest
early earlier earliest
quickly more quickly most quickly
carefully more carefully most carefully
well better best
badly worse worst
Rules:
209
 One syllable: add -er/-est
 Two+ syllables: use more/most
 Irregular: memorize (well-better-best)
EVALUATION:
1. Define adverb and state what it modifies
2. Give 5 examples each of adverbs of: manner, time, place, frequency
3. Form adverbs from: quick, careful, happy, easy, good
4. Identify adverbs and their types: "She always sings beautifully here."
5. Write 5 sentences using different types of adverbs
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List 10 adverbs of each type: manner, time, place, frequency, degree
2. Write 10 sentences, each using a different type of adverb
3. Convert these adjectives to adverbs: slow, loud, quiet, bad, angry, peaceful, heavy, soft,
bright, clear
4. Compare these adverbs: fast, carefully, well, badly, early
5. Identify and classify all adverbs: "Yesterday, she spoke very softly and walked slowly
outside. She never talks loudly here."
210
WEEK 8 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: READING TO ANSWER SPECIFIC QUESTIONS (INTERPRETATION &
PROJECTION LEVELS)
LEVELS OF COMPREHENSION QUESTIONS:
1. LITERAL LEVEL (Easy):
 Answers found directly in text
 Facts, details explicitly stated
 Who? What? When? Where?
 Example: "What is the boy's name?"
2. INTERPRETATION LEVEL (Medium):
 Read between the lines
 Infer meaning not directly stated
 Understand implications
 Why? How? What does this mean?
 Example: "Why do you think the boy was sad?"
3. PROJECTION LEVEL (Difficult):
 Go beyond the text
 Predict, imagine, apply
 Personal opinion with text support
 What if? What might happen? What would you do?
 Example: "What might happen next?"
211
INTERPRETATION QUESTIONS:
What They Ask:
 Infer character feelings/motivations
 Understand cause-effect relationships
 Identify author's purpose/message
 Explain why something happened
 Draw conclusions from facts
How to Answer:
 Use clues from text
 Combine facts to draw conclusions
 Think about "why" behind actions
 Consider context
Signal Words:
 Why, How, What does this suggest, What can you infer, This implies, The author means
PROJECTION QUESTIONS:
What They Ask:
 Predict future events
 Give personal opinions
 Apply to own life
 Suggest alternatives
 Create new scenarios
How to Answer:
212
 Base predictions on text evidence
 Use logical reasoning
 Connect to personal experience
 Imagine possibilities
Signal Words:
 What might, What if, What would you, Predict, In your opinion, Do you think, How
would you
PRACTICE PASSAGE:
"Tunde had studied only one hour before the exam. His friends studied for weeks. As he sat
looking at the questions, his hands shook. The questions looked unfamiliar. He glanced at his
friend Bola, who was writing confidently."
Questions:
Literal: What is the boy's name? (Tunde)
Interpretation: Why were Tunde's hands shaking? (He was nervous because he didn't prepare
well)
Projection: What do you think will happen to Tunde in the exam? (He will likely fail because he
didn't study enough)
EVALUATION:
1. Name and explain the three levels of comprehension questions
2. What is the difference between interpretation and projection questions?
3. Read this and answer: "Mary found a wallet with money but no ID. She thought about
keeping it but remembered her mother's advice about honesty."
213
o Literal: What did Mary find?
o Interpretation: Why did Mary think about keeping the wallet?
o Projection: What do you think Mary will do?
4. Give 3 signal words for interpretation questions
5. Give 3 signal words for projection questions
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Read any passage from your textbook. Write 3 questions at each level: literal,
interpretation, projection (9 total)
2. Explain how to answer interpretation questions vs. literal questions
3. Practice: "The village was silent. Shops were closed. People hurried past with covered
faces. The town crier had made an announcement at dawn." Write: (a) 2 literal questions
(b) 2 interpretation questions (c) 2 projection questions - provide answers
4. Why are projection questions more difficult than literal questions?
5. Create a passage (100 words) and write 5 questions: 2 literal, 2 interpretation, 1
projection

WEEK 8 – COMPOSITION
TOPIC: DESCRIPTIVE ESSAY – A MARKET PLACE
DESCRIPTIVE ESSAY REVIEW:
 Purpose: Paint vivid picture with words
 Uses: Sensory details (sight, sound, smell, taste, touch)
 Organization: Spatial order (location-based)
 Tense: Usually present
 Language: Rich adjectives, imagery, figurative language
WRITING ABOUT A MARKET PLACE:
WHAT TO DESCRIBE:
1. General Appearance:
 Size (large, small, sprawling)
 Location (in town center, by the road)
 Structure (open-air, covered, modern, traditional)
 Overall atmosphere
2. Sights (Visual):
 Stalls and arrangement
 Goods displayed (fruits, vegetables, clothes, etc.)
 Colors (bright, colorful, vibrant)
 People (traders, buyers, children)
 Activities (selling, bargaining, carrying)
215
3. Sounds (Auditory):
 Traders calling out: "Fresh tomatoes! Buy now!"
 Bargaining conversations
 Laughter, greetings
 Vehicle horns
 General noise and bustle
4. Smells (Olfactory):
 Fresh fruits and vegetables
 Cooked food (suya, akara)
 Fish section (strong smell)
 Spices (pepper, ginger)
 Mix of various scents
5. Touch/Physical Sensations:
 Crowded, jostling
 Hot (sun beating down)
 Humid
 Bumping into people
6. Activities/Movement:
 Traders arranging goods
 Buyers examining items
 Haggling/bargaining
 Porters carrying loads
 Children running around
216
ORGANIZATION:
Introduction:
 General impression of market
 Location and time
 Why it's interesting/important
Body: Paragraph 1: General appearance and layout Paragraph 2: Sights - what you see
Paragraph 3: Sounds and smells Paragraph 4: Activities and atmosphere
Conclusion:
 Overall impression
 Significance of market
 Personal feelings
USEFUL VOCABULARY:
Adjectives: bustling, crowded, vibrant, colorful, noisy, lively, chaotic, organized, sprawling,
vast, busy
Verbs: bustle, jostle, bargain, haggle, call out, display, arrange, examine, weigh, pack
Nouns: stall, vendor, trader, buyer, goods, produce, merchandise, section, aisle, canopy
SAMPLE OUTLINE:
Title: The Bustling City Market
I. Introduction
 Located in city center
217
 Vibrant, lively atmosphere
 Heart of community
II. Physical Description
 Sprawling open-air market
 Hundreds of stalls
 Organized by sections
III. Sights
 Colorful displays
 Various goods
 Busy people
IV. Sounds and Smells
 Traders calling
 Mix of aromas
 Noisy atmosphere
V. Conclusion
 Exciting, essential place
 Reflects community life
SAMPLE PARAGRAPH:
"The city market is a feast for the senses. Rows upon rows of stalls stretch as far as the eye can
see, each overflowing with colorful goods. The vegetable section displays pyramids of bright red
tomatoes, green peppers, and golden oranges. Traders in vibrant wrapper sit behind their wares,
calling out to potential customers in melodious voices. The air is thick with the mingled scents of
fresh fish, ripe mangoes, and sizzling akara from nearby food stalls. Shoppers weave through the
218
narrow aisles, baskets on heads, bargaining loudly over prices. The constant hum of activity -
voices, laughter, and the occasional blaring horn - creates an energetic symphony that is
unmistakably the market."
EVALUATION:
1. What is the purpose of a descriptive essay?
2. List 5 things you would describe about a market place
3. What sensory details are important in describing a market?
4. Write a topic sentence for a descriptive paragraph about a market
5. List 10 descriptive words suitable for describing a busy market
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a complete descriptive essay (250-300 words) titled "A Market Place" or "The
Busy Market"
2. Create an outline for your essay showing: introduction, 3 body paragraph topics,
conclusion
3. Write one paragraph (6-7 sentences) describing the sights in a market
4. Write one paragraph (6-7 sentences) describing the sounds and smells in a market
5. List 20 vocabulary words (adjectives, nouns, verbs) useful for describing a market

Elements:

220

221

TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɑ:/
THE LONG VOWEL /ɑ:/ (as in "car"):
Production:
 Tongue low and back

 No lip rounding
Common Spellings:

 "al": calm, palm, half

 barn /ɑ:/ - ban /æ/

WEEK 9 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: PREPOSITIONS – DEFINITION, IDENTIFICATION, TYPES & FUNCTIONS
DEFINITION: A preposition is a word that shows the relationship between a noun/pronoun and
another word in the sentence.
COMMON PREPOSITIONS: at, in, on, by, with, from, to, for, of, about, under, over, between,
among, through, during, before, after, since, until
TYPES OF PREPOSITIONS:
1. PREPOSITIONS OF TIME: Show when something happens
 at: specific times (at 3 o'clock, at noon, at night)
 in: months, years, centuries, long periods (in June, in 2024, in the morning)
 on: days and dates (on Monday, on June 5th, on my birthday)
 Others: before, after, during, since, for, until, by
Examples:
 I wake up at 6 AM
 She was born in 2010
 The meeting is on Friday
 We arrived before noon
 He has lived here since 2020
2. PREPOSITIONS OF PLACE: Show where something is located
 at: specific points (at the door, at the bus stop, at home)
228
 in: enclosed spaces (in the room, in Lagos, in the box)
 on: surfaces (on the table, on the wall, on the floor)
 Others: under, over, above, below, beside, behind, in front of, between, among, near, by
Examples:
 The book is on the table
 She lives in Lagos
 The cat is under the chair
 Stand beside me
 The bank is between the school and the hospital
3. PREPOSITIONS OF DIRECTION/MOVEMENT: Show movement from one place to
another
 to: toward a destination (go to school, walk to the market)
 from: starting point (come from home, travel from Lagos)
 into: entering (walk into the room)
 out of: exiting (come out of the house)
 through: passing through (walk through the gate)
 across: from one side to another (across the road)
 along: following a line (along the street)
Examples:
 I go to school daily
 She came from Abuja
 He walked into the room
229
 They ran across the field
4. PREPOSITIONS OF MANNER: Show how something is done
 by: method/means (by car, by train, by hand)
 with: using/accompanying (with a knife, with friends)
 in: manner/way (in a hurry, in style)
Examples:
 She travels by bus
 Cut it with a knife
 He spoke in anger
5. PREPOSITIONS OF AGENT: Show who does the action (used in passive voice)
 by: the doer of an action
 with: instrument used
Examples:
 The book was written by Achebe
 The tree was cut with an axe
FUNCTIONS OF PREPOSITIONS:
1. Show Time Relationships:
 "I'll see you at 5 PM"
2. Show Location:
 "The book is on the shelf"
230
3. Show Direction:
 "She went to school"
4. Show Manner:
 "He spoke with confidence"
5. Show Possession:
 "The house of my father"
6. Connect Words:
 Link nouns/pronouns to other words
 Create meaning in sentences
PREPOSITIONAL PHRASES: Preposition + noun/pronoun = prepositional phrase
Examples:
 at the door (preposition: at; object: the door)
 in the morning (preposition: in; object: the morning)
 with my friend (preposition: with; object: my friend)
COMMON MISTAKES:
Time:
 ✗ in Monday (✓ on Monday)
 ✗ at morning (✓ in the morning)
 ✗ on night (✓ at night)
Place:
231
 ✗ in the table (✓ on the table)
 ✗ at Lagos (✓ in Lagos)
EVALUATION:
1. Define preposition and give 10 examples
2. List 5 prepositions of: (a) time (b) place (c) direction
3. Fill in with correct prepositions: (a) I live ___ Lagos (b) She arrives ___ Monday (c) The
book is ___ the table (d) He goes ___ school ___ 7 AM
4. What is a prepositional phrase? Give 5 examples
5. Correct the errors: (a) She lives at Nigeria (b) I wake up on the morning (c) The pen is
under the table (correct if wrong)
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write 20 sentences using different prepositions (at least one from each type)
2. List 15 prepositional phrases and use 10 in sentences
3. Fill in with appropriate prepositions: "I woke up ___ 6 AM ___ Monday morning. I went
___ the bathroom and took my bath. After breakfast, I walked ___ school ___ my friends.
We arrived ___ school ___ 7:30 AM. Our classroom is ___ the second floor ___ the
administrative block."
4. Identify all prepositions and state their types: "In the morning, she walked to school with
her sister. They arrived at 8 o'clock and sat on the bench under the tree."
5. Write 5 sentences each using: (a) prepositions of time (b) prepositions of place (c)
prepositions of direction
232
WEEK 9 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: APPLYING SQ3R TO A GIVEN PASSAGE
SQ3R TECHNIQUE (Review):
S - SURVEY: Quickly preview the material
 Read title, headings, subheadings
 Look at pictures, diagrams
 Read introduction and conclusion
 Scan first sentences of paragraphs
 Time: 2-5 minutes
Q - QUESTION: Turn headings into questions
 Use: Who? What? When? Where? Why? How?
 Create questions you want answered
 Write questions down
R - READ: Read carefully to answer questions
 Read actively, not passively
 Look for answers to your questions
 Take notes
 Highlight key points
 Time: Main reading time
R - RECITE: Recall what you read
 Look away from text
 Recite main points aloud or in writing
233
 Answer your questions
 Summarize in own words
 Check accuracy by looking back
R - REVIEW: Go over material again
 Review notes
 Recite main points again
 Answer questions
 Make sure you understand
 Time: 5-10 minutes
APPLYING SQ3R TO A PASSAGE:
SAMPLE PASSAGE:
Title: The Importance of Water
Water is essential for all living things. The human body is about 60% water, and we need to
drink water daily to replace what we lose through breathing, sweating, and digestion.
Water has many important functions in the body. It helps transport nutrients to cells and removes
waste products. Water also regulates body temperature through sweating. Without enough water,
the body cannot function properly.
Experts recommend drinking at least 8 glasses of water per day. However, the exact amount
needed depends on factors like age, activity level, and climate. People who exercise or live in hot
climates need more water.
Dehydration occurs when the body loses more water than it takes in. Symptoms include thirst,
dry mouth, tiredness, and headache. Severe dehydration can be dangerous and requires medical
attention. Therefore, it is important to drink water regularly throughout the day.
234
STEP-BY-STEP APPLICATION:
1. SURVEY (2 minutes):
 Title: "The Importance of Water"
 4 paragraphs
 Topics: water in body, functions, daily needs, dehydration
 Seems to explain why water is important
2. QUESTION (1 minute):
 Why is water essential?
 How much water does the body contain?
 What does water do in the body?
 How much water should we drink?
 What is dehydration?
3. READ (5 minutes): Read carefully, looking for answers:
 Body is 60% water
 Water transports nutrients, removes waste, regulates temperature
 Need 8 glasses daily (varies by person)
 Dehydration = too little water; causes thirst, tiredness, headache
4. RECITE (2 minutes): Close book and recall: "Water is essential because the body is 60%
water. It transports nutrients, removes waste, and controls temperature. We need about 8 glasses
daily. Without enough water, we get dehydrated, which causes thirst and tiredness."
Check for accuracy.
5. REVIEW (2 minutes):
 Main point: Water is vital for health
235
 Key facts: 60% body water, 8 glasses daily, multiple functions
 Can I answer my questions? Yes
 Do I understand? Yes
BENEFITS OF SQ3R:
 Improves comprehension
 Better retention (remembering)
 Active reading (not passive)
 Organized approach
 Saves time in long run
EVALUATION:
1. What does SQ3R stand for?
2. Explain the 5 steps of SQ3R briefly
3. Why is the Survey step important?
4. What happens in the Recite step?
5. Apply SQ3R to a short passage from your textbook: write what you did in each step
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Apply SQ3R to a passage from your textbook (at least 300 words). Write a report
showing what you did at each step
2. Explain the difference between the Read and Recite steps
3. Why is it important to turn headings into questions in the Question step?
236
4. Apply SQ3R to this passage and answer: [Provide or use a passage from their textbook]
Write: (a) What you found during Survey (b) 3 questions you created (c) Main points
from Reading (d) What you recited (e) Review summary
5. Create a poster or diagram showing the 5 steps of SQ3R with brief explanations
237
WEEK 9 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: LETTER WRITING – INTRODUCTION AND TYPES
WHAT IS A LETTER? A written message from one person to another.
PURPOSES OF LETTERS:
 Communicate with distant people
 Request information or permission
 Apply for jobs/admission
 Express feelings
 Maintain relationships
 Conduct business
TYPES OF LETTERS:
1. FORMAL LETTERS: Official letters to authorities, organizations, strangers
Types:
 Application letters (job, admission)
 Business letters
 Complaint letters
 Request letters
 Official correspondence
Characteristics:
 Formal language
 Polite tone
 Complete addresses
238
 Proper format
 No slang/contractions
 Respectful
2. INFORMAL LETTERS (Personal/Friendly): Letters to family, friends, people you know
well
Types:
 Letters to friends
 Letters to family members
 Thank you letters
 Congratulation letters
 Invitation letters (informal)
Characteristics:
 Casual, friendly language
 Personal tone
 Can use contractions
 Emotional expression
 Conversational style
DIFFERENCES:
Aspect Formal Informal
Recipient Authorities, strangers Friends, family
239
Language Formal, polite Casual, friendly
Tone Professional Personal, warm
Address Both addresses required Usually one address
Purpose Official business Personal matters
Closing Yours faithfully/sincerely Yours lovingly, Best wishes
BASIC LETTER FORMAT:
Both types include:
1. Writer's address (top right)
2. Date
3. Salutation (greeting)
4. Body (message)
5. Closing
6. Signature
7. Name
Formal letters add:
 Recipient's address (left, below date)
 Subject line
FORMAL LETTER FORMAT:
23, Benson Street,
Ikeja,
240
Lagos State.
18th October, 2024.
The Principal,
Kings College,
Ikeja,
Lagos State.
Dear Sir,
REQUEST FOR PERMISSION TO BE ABSENT
Body paragraphs...
Yours faithfully,
[Signature]
John Adebayo
INFORMAL LETTER FORMAT:
241
23, Benson Street,
Ikeja,
Lagos State.
18th October, 2024.
Dear Tunde,
Body paragraphs...
Yours lovingly,
[Signature]
John
SALUTATIONS:
Formal:
 Dear Sir, (unknown man)
 Dear Madam, (unknown woman)
 Dear Sir/Madam, (unknown gender)
 Dear Mr. Ojo, (known man)
 Dear Mrs. Ojo, (known married woman)
 Dear Ms. Ojo, (known woman, marital status unknown)
Informal:
242
 Dear Tunde,
 My dear Brother,
 Dearest Mother,
 Hello Mary,
CLOSINGS:
Formal:
 Yours faithfully, (if Dear Sir/Madam)
 Yours sincerely, (if Dear Mr./Mrs./Ms.)
 Yours respectfully,
Informal:
 Yours lovingly,
 With love,
 Best wishes,
 Warm regards,
 Your friend,
LETTER WRITING STEPS:
1. Plan: What to write, order of ideas
2. Write address and date
3. Write salutation
4. Write body: 3-4 paragraphs
o Opening paragraph: State purpose
243
o Middle paragraphs: Details
o Closing paragraph: Conclusion, request action
5. Write closing and sign
6. Proofread: Check spelling, grammar, format
EVALUATION:
1. Define letter and state 3 purposes of letter writing
2. What are the two main types of letters? Explain the difference
3. List the parts of a formal letter in correct order
4. Write appropriate salutations for: (a) Unknown principal (b) Your friend (c) Known
teacher Mr. Ojo
5. What closing is used with "Dear Sir"? What closing is used with "Dear Brother"?
ASSIGNMENT:

mother (c) A company manager (d) Your friend (e) An unknown person

Complaint letter (d) Letter to a friend (e) Request letter

WEEK 9 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: FOLKTALES
Introduction, Definition, Types; Narration of a Folktale; Recommended Drama Text – Plot &
Setting
PART A: FOLKTALES
DEFINITION: Traditional stories passed down orally, teaching lessons through entertainment.
Features animals or people, with moral lessons.
CHARACTERISTICS:
1. Oral tradition (told, not written)
2. Passed through generations
3. Teach moral lessons
4. Entertain
5. Simple language
6. Formulaic openings/closings ("Once upon a time..." / "That is why...")
7. Stock characters (tortoise, hare, lion)
8. Magical elements
TYPES OF FOLKTALES:
1. ANIMAL TALES: Animals as main characters, acting like humans
 Example: Tortoise and the Birds, Why Tortoise has Cracked Shell
 Lesson: Consequences of greed, trickery
2. TRICKSTER TALES: Clever character uses wit to overcome stronger opponents
245
 Characters: Tortoise (Ijapa), Anansi Spider, Hare
 Lesson: Intelligence over strength; but trickery has consequences
3. "WHY" STORIES (Origin Tales): Explain natural phenomena or animal characteristics
 Examples: Why Sky is Far, Why Bat Flies at Night, Why Mosquito Buzzes
 Lesson: Various moral lessons
4. MORAL TALES: Directly teach values and behavior
 Focus: Honesty, kindness, respect, hard work
 Lesson: Clear right and wrong, consequences of actions
COMMON CHARACTERS:
 Tortoise (Ijapa): Cunning, greedy, clever but often foolish
 Hare/Rabbit: Quick, witty, escapes danger
 Lion: Powerful, king of animals, sometimes proud
 Spider (Anansi): Trickster, wise, mischievous
 Elephant: Strong, wise, sometimes slow
 Hyena: Foolish, greedy, laughable
FUNCTIONS OF FOLKTALES:
1. Entertainment: Amuse audience
2. Education: Teach about life, culture
3. Moral Instruction: Right vs. wrong
4. Cultural Preservation: Maintain traditions
5. Social Control: Enforce norms
246
6. Community Bonding: Bring people together
SAMPLE FOLKTALE NARRATION:
Title: Why Tortoise Has a Cracked Shell
Long ago, there was a great feast in the sky. All the birds were invited. Tortoise, who loved food,
wanted to go but had no wings.
Cleverly, Tortoise convinced the birds to each lend him a feather. He promised to be their
spokesperson at the feast. The birds agreed.
At the feast, Tortoise asked, "Who is all this food for?" The hosts replied, "For all of you."
Tortoise then announced to the birds, "My name is 'All of You,' so this food is mine!" He ate
everything while the birds watched hungrily.
Angry, the birds took back their feathers. Tortoise, now wingless in the sky, asked Parrot to tell
his wife to bring soft things for his landing. But Parrot told the wife to bring hard things instead.
Tortoise fell from the sky onto the hard objects. His shell broke into many pieces. A medicine
man put it back together, but the cracks remain.
Moral Lesson: Greed and trickery lead to punishment. Those who deceive others will eventually
suffer consequences.
PART B: DRAMA TEXT - PLOT & SETTING
PLOT IN DRAMA:
Definition: Sequence of events in the play
Plot Structure:
1. Exposition: Introduction, background, characters
2. Rising Action: Conflict develops, complications arise
3. Climax: Turning point, most intense moment
247
4. Falling Action: After climax, moving toward resolution
5. Resolution: Conclusion, conflicts resolved
In Your Drama Text:
 What is the main conflict?
 Who is involved in the conflict?
 What complications arise?
 What is the climax?
 How is it resolved?
SETTING IN DRAMA:
Definition: Time and place where play occurs
Elements:
1. Time Period: When (historical era, season, time of day)
2. Place: Where (country, city, specific location)
3. Social Context: Cultural, economic, political environment
4. Atmosphere: Mood created
Stage Directions Show Setting:
 [A village square in the morning]
 [Inside the palace, evening]
 [The forest, night time]
Importance:
 Influences characters and actions
 Creates mood
248
 Provides context
 Affects plot development
ANALYZING YOUR DRAMA TEXT:
Plot Questions:
 What happens in each act/scene?
 What is the central conflict?
 Who are the main characters involved?
 What complications develop?
 What is the climax of the play?
 How are conflicts resolved?
Setting Questions:
 When does the play take place?
 Where is it set?
 How does setting affect characters?
 What is the social/cultural context?
 How is setting established (stage directions, dialogue)?
EVALUATION:
1. Define folktale and state 4 characteristics
2. Name and explain 4 types of folktales with examples
3. What are the functions of folktales in society?
4. Narrate a folktale you know (150 words) and state the moral lesson
249
5. In your drama text: (a) Summarize the plot (b) Describe the setting
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a summary of 3 folktales you know (100 words each), stating the moral lesson for
each
2. Explain 5 common characters in folktales and their typical traits
3. Create your own short folktale (200 words) with a clear moral lesson
4. Read Acts/Scenes _____ of your drama text. Write: (a) Plot summary (What happens?) (b)
Setting description (When and where?) (c) Main conflict identified (d) Important events
in this section
5. Explain how setting influences the plot in your drama text (5 examples)
250
WEEK 10 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɒ/
THE SHORT VOWEL /ɒ/ (as in "hot"):
Production:
 Tongue low-back position
 Lips slightly rounded
 Mouth fairly open
 Short sound
Common Spellings:
 "o": hot, pot, got, box, top, dog, shop, clock, doctor, coffee, option, problem
 "a": want, wash, watch, what, squad, quality
Minimal Pairs:
 pot /ɒ/ - port /ɔ:/
 cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔ:/
 not /ɒ/ - nought /ɔ:/
 shot /ɒ/ - short /ɔ:/
Practice Words: hot, pot, got, lot, not, dog, fog, log, shop, stop, top, box, fox, clock, block, rock,
doctor, problem
Practice Sentence: "The doctor got a hot pot from the shop and stopped at the top of the rock."
Note: British vs. American pronunciation differs for some words (e.g., "hot" - British /ɒ/,
American /ɑ:/)
EVALUATION:
251
1. Produce /ɒ/ sound correctly with 10 words

4. Write 5 sentences using /ɒ/ words
5. List 5 words with "o" spelling that have /ɒ/ sound
ASSIGNMENT:

WEEK 10 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: CONJUNCTIONS – DEFINITION, TYPES (SUBORDINATING &
COORDINATING), FUNCTIONS
DEFINITION: A conjunction is a word that connects words, phrases, or clauses.
FUNCTIONS:
 Join words, phrases, sentences
 Show relationships between ideas
 Create complex sentences
 Make writing flow smoothly
TYPES OF CONJUNCTIONS:
1. COORDINATING CONJUNCTIONS:
Definition: Join words, phrases, or independent clauses of equal importance
The Seven: FANBOYS
 F - For (reason)
 A - And (addition)
 N - Nor (negative addition)
 B - But (contrast)
 O - Or (choice)
 Y - Yet (contrast)
 S - So (result)
Uses:
253
Joining Words:
 John and Mary
 Red or blue
 Smart but lazy
Joining Phrases:
 In the morning and in the evening
 Over the river and through the woods
Joining Independent Clauses:
 I studied hard, and I passed.
 She wanted to go, but she was sick.
 He must leave now, or he will be late.
 I was tired, so I slept.
Punctuation: Use comma before conjunction when joining independent clauses.
2. SUBORDINATING CONJUNCTIONS:
Definition: Join a dependent clause to an independent clause, showing relationship
Common Subordinating Conjunctions:
Time: when, while, before, after, since, until, as soon as, whenever
 "I will call you when I arrive."
Reason/Cause: because, since, as
 "She stayed home because she was sick."
Condition: if, unless, provided that, in case
 "If it rains, we will cancel."
254
Contrast: although, though, even though, whereas, while
 "Although he studied, he failed."
Purpose: so that, in order that
 "I study hard so that I can pass."
Manner: as, as if, as though
 "She sings as if she's on stage."
Place: where, wherever
 "Go where you like."
STRUCTURE:
Pattern 1: Independent clause + subordinating conjunction + dependent clause
 "I will wait until you come back."
Pattern 2: Subordinating conjunction + dependent clause, independent clause
 "Until you come back, I will wait."
 (Use comma after dependent clause when it comes first)
3. CORRELATIVE CONJUNCTIONS:
Definition: Work in pairs to connect equal elements
Common Pairs:
 both...and: Both John and Mary came.
 either...or: You can have either tea or coffee.
 neither...nor: Neither John nor Mary came.
 not only...but also: She is not only smart but also hardworking.
255
 whether...or: I don't know whether she'll come or go.
DIFFERENCES:
Coordinating Subordinating
Connect equal parts Connect unequal parts
FANBOYS Many types
Both clauses can stand alone Creates dependent clause
Comma before (for clauses) Comma after (if dependent comes first)
COMMON MISTAKES:
1. Comma Splices:
 ✗ I went home, she stayed. (Two independent clauses, no conjunction)
 ✓ I went home, and she stayed. (Add conjunction)
 ✓ I went home. She stayed. (Separate sentences)
2. Run-on Sentences:
 ✗ I went home she stayed. (No punctuation or conjunction)
 ✓ I went home, and she stayed.
3. Comma Misuse:
 ✗ When it rains we stay indoors. (Need comma after dependent clause)
 ✓ When it rains, we stay indoors.
EVALUATION:
1. Define conjunction and state its function
256
2. What does FANBOYS stand for? Give an example sentence for each
3. List 5 subordinating conjunctions showing: time, reason, condition
4. Join these with appropriate conjunctions: (a) I was tired. I went to bed. (b) She studied
hard. She wanted to pass. (c) You can have tea. You can have coffee.
5. Identify conjunctions and their types: "I will wait until you come, but I cannot stay long
because I have work."
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write 10 sentences using different coordinating conjunctions (FANBOYS)
2. Write 10 sentences using different subordinating conjunctions (at least 2 from each
category: time, reason, condition, contrast)
3. Join these sentences with appropriate conjunctions: (a) It was raining. We went out. (b)
Study hard. You will fail. (c) She is smart. She is hardworking. (d) I woke up. The sun
rose. (e) He didn't study. He was sick.
4. Correct these errors: (a) I went home she stayed (b) Although he was sick, but he came (c)
When it rains we stay inside
5. Use these correlative conjunctions in sentences: both...and, either...or, neither...nor, not
only...but also
257
WEEK 10 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: ASKING QUESTIONS TO COVER ALL COMPREHENSION LEVELS
(who, where, when, why, how, what)
THE 6 QUESTION WORDS:
1. WHO (Person):
 Asks about people/characters
 Literal: Who is the main character?
 Interpretation: Who do you think is responsible?
 Projection: Who would you choose to help you?
2. WHAT (Thing/Action):
 Asks about objects, events, actions
 Literal: What happened in the story?
 Interpretation: What does this event mean?
 Projection: What might happen next?
3. WHEN (Time):
 Asks about time
 Literal: When did this happen?
 Interpretation: When do you think he realized his mistake?
 Projection: When should she have acted differently?
4. WHERE (Place):
 Asks about location
 Literal: Where does the story take place?
258
 Interpretation: Where do you think they were going?
 Projection: Where would you go in this situation?
5. WHY (Reason):
 Asks about causes, reasons, purposes
 Literal: Why did he go? (if stated)
 Interpretation: Why do you think she acted that way? (infer)
 Projection: Why would you make that choice?
6. HOW (Manner/Process):
 Asks about method, manner, process
 Literal: How did he do it? (if described)
 Interpretation: How do you think she felt?
 Projection: How would you solve this problem?
COVERING ALL COMPREHENSION LEVELS:
LITERAL LEVEL (Easy): Information directly stated in text
 Who is the main character? (Answer in text)
 What is his name? (Stated)
 When did they arrive? (Stated)
 Where does she live? (Stated)
INTERPRETATION LEVEL (Medium): Read between the lines, infer
 Why do you think he was sad? (Not directly stated)
 What does this suggest about her character? (Infer)
 How did she feel? (Infer from clues)
259
 Who do you think is responsible? (Analyze)
PROJECTION LEVEL (Difficult): Beyond the text, predict, apply
 What might happen next? (Predict)
 What would you do in this situation? (Apply)
 How would you solve this problem? (Personal response)
 Why would this be important in your life? (Relate)
CREATING BALANCED QUESTIONS:
For Any Passage, Create:
 2-3 literal questions (facts)
 2-3 interpretation questions (analysis)
 1-2 projection questions (application)
Use All Question Words:
 Include questions starting with who, what, when, where, why, how
PRACTICE PASSAGE:
"Tunde woke up early on Saturday morning. He had an important football match at 10 AM. He
quickly ate breakfast and gathered his gear. As he left, his mother reminded him to play fairly.
The match was tough, and Tunde's team was losing. In the final minutes, Tunde had a chance to
cheat and win, but he remembered his mother's words. He played fairly, and though they lost, he
felt proud."
Sample Questions:
Literal:
1. Who is the main character? (Tunde)
260
2. What day did this happen? (Saturday)
3. When was the match? (10 AM)
4. Where was Tunde going? (Football match)
Interpretation: 5. Why do you think Tunde felt proud? (He chose honesty over winning) 6.
How did his mother's advice affect his decision? (It reminded him of values) 7. What does this
tell us about Tunde's character? (He is honest, has integrity)
Projection: 8. What might happen if Tunde had cheated? (He might have won but felt guilty) 9.
What would you do in Tunde's situation? (Personal response) 10. Why is playing fairly
important in life? (Apply to real life)
EVALUATION:
1. List the 6 question words and what each asks about
2. Create 2 literal, 2 interpretation, and 1 projection question for this passage: "Mary found
a wallet full of money. She looked around but saw no one. She thought about keeping it
but decided to take it to the police station."
3. Explain the difference between asking "What happened?" (literal) and "What might
happen?" (projection)
4. Why is it important to ask questions at all three comprehension levels?
5. Which question words are most common at: (a) literal level (b) interpretation level (c)
projection level?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Read a passage from your textbook. Create 15 questions: 5 literal, 5 interpretation, 5
projection (use all 6 question words)
261
2. For each comprehension level, write 2 questions starting with: Who, What, When, Where,
Why, How (18 total questions)
3. Analyze this passage and create balanced questions: [Use a passage from their textbook]
4. Explain with examples: (a) What is a literal question? (b) What is an interpretation
question? (c) What is a projection question?
5. Practice: Write 10 questions (mixed levels) for any short story you know
262
WEEK 10 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: FEATURES OF AN INFORMAL LETTER
INFORMAL LETTER REVIEW:
Definition: Personal letter to friends, family, or people you know well
Purpose:
 Share news
 Maintain relationships
 Express feelings
 Make plans
 Thank, congratulate, invite
FEATURES OF INFORMAL LETTERS:
1. WRITER'S ADDRESS:
 Top right corner
 Your full address
 No name
Example:
15, Ademola Street,
Ikeja,
Lagos State.
2. DATE:
263
 Below writer's address
 Various formats acceptable
 Day, Month, Year
Examples:
 20th November, 2024
 November 20, 2024
 20/11/2024
3. SALUTATION (Greeting):
 Friendly, personal
 Comma after name
 Left side
Examples:
 Dear Tunde,
 My dear Brother,
 Dearest Mother,
 Hello Mary,
 Dear Friend,
4. BODY:
Opening Paragraph:
 Greet recipient warmly
 Ask about their welfare
264
 State purpose of letter
Example: "How are you? I hope you and your family are doing well. I am writing to tell you
about my recent experience..."
Middle Paragraphs:
 Main message (2-3 paragraphs)
 Details and descriptions
 Personal sharing
 Can be emotional
Closing Paragraph:
 Final thoughts
 Greetings to family
 Hope to hear back
 Future plans
Example: "I hope to hear from you soon. Give my regards to your parents. Take care of
yourself."
5. COMPLIMENTARY CLOSE:
 Warm, friendly
 Comma after
 Right side (below body)
Examples:
 Yours lovingly,
 With love,
265
 Best wishes,
 Warm regards,
 Your friend,
 Affectionately yours,
6. SIGNATURE:
 Below closing
 Your name (first name usually sufficient)
 Can be just nickname
Example:
Yours lovingly,
[Signature]
Bola
LANGUAGE FEATURES:
1. Tone:
 Friendly, warm, conversational
 Emotional expression allowed
 Personal feelings shared
2. Language:
 Informal, casual
 Can use contractions (I'm, don't, can't)
 Simple, everyday words
266
 Can use slang (appropriately)
 Personal pronouns (I, you, we)
3. Style:
 Conversational
 Like talking to the person
 Can include jokes, expressions
 Personal anecdotes
SAMPLE INFORMAL LETTER:
23, Benson Street,
Ikeja,
Lagos State.
20th November, 2024.
Dear Tunde,
How are you doing? I hope this letter meets you well. I'm writing to share some exciting news
with you!
Guess what? I won the first prize in our school's debate competition last week! I still can't
believe it. Remember how nervous I was when I told you about it? Well, I practiced every day
just like you advised, and it really paid off. The topic was "Technology and Education," and I
argued that technology has improved learning. My teachers were really impressed!
267
The prize was a beautiful trophy and a scholarship worth 50,000 naira. My parents are so proud,
and I immediately thought of you because you always encouraged me to join the debate club.
Thank you for believing in me!
How are things at your new school? Have you made new friends? I really miss having you
around. Please write back soon and tell me all about your experiences.
Give my warm regards to your family. I hope we can see each other during the holidays.
Your friend,
[Signature]
Bola
DIFFERENCES FROM FORMAL LETTERS:
Feature Informal Formal
Recipient's address Not required Required
Salutation Dear + first name Dear Sir/Madam
Language Casual, contractions OK Formal, no contractions
Tone Warm, friendly Professional
Closing Yours lovingly Yours faithfully
Purpose Personal matters Official business
COMMON MISTAKES:
268
1. Including recipient's address (not needed in informal)
2. Using formal language (Dear Sir instead of Dear Tunde)
3. Being too stiff and formal
4. Forgetting to ask about recipient's welfare
5. Using "Yours faithfully" (that's formal!)
6. No comma after salutation
7. Too brief (should be warm and detailed)
EVALUATION:
1. List and explain 6 features of an informal letter
2. What is the difference between formal and informal letter salutations? Give 3 examples
each
3. Write an appropriate opening paragraph for an informal letter to your friend
4. What closings are used in informal letters? Give 5 examples
5. What language features make a letter informal?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a complete informal letter (200 words) to your friend telling them about your
recent school experience
2. Draw and label all parts of an informal letter format
3. Write 5 appropriate salutations and 5 appropriate closings for informal letters
4. Compare formal and informal letters: list 5 differences in a table
269
5. Write the body (3 paragraphs) of an informal letter to your cousin inviting them to visit
during holidays

WEEK 10 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO FIGURES OF SPEECH – SIMILE, METAPHOR, IRONY
PART A: DRAMA TEXT STUDY
Continue studying your drama text focusing on:
Characters:
 Main characters and their roles
 Character development
 Relationships between characters
 Conflicts
Themes:
 Main themes in the play
 How themes are developed
 Evidence from text
Plot:
 Summary of each act/scene
 Main conflicts
 Climax and resolution
Setting:
 Time and place
 How setting affects action
PART B: FIGURES OF SPEECH
271
DEFINITION: Figures of speech are expressions that use words in non-literal ways to create
special effects or meanings.
Purpose:
 Make language more interesting
 Create vivid images
 Express ideas creatively
 Add beauty to language
 Make writing memorable
1. SIMILE:
Definition: Direct comparison using "like" or "as"
Pattern:
 X is like Y
 X is as [adjective] as Y
Examples:
 Her smile is like sunshine
 He runs like the wind
 She is as brave as a lion
 The baby's skin is as soft as silk
 Time passes like a river flowing
Purpose:
 Show similarity
 Create vivid image
272
 Help reader understand by comparing to something familiar
In Sentences:
 "The classroom was as quiet as a graveyard."
 "Her voice was like music to my ears."
 "He fought like a warrior."
2. METAPHOR:
Definition: Direct comparison WITHOUT "like" or "as"; saying one thing IS another
Pattern:
 X is Y (not literally true)
Examples:
 Time is money (time = money)
 Her smile is sunshine (smile = sunshine)
 Life is a journey
 He is a lion in battle
 The classroom is a zoo
Difference from Simile:
 Simile: Her smile is like sunshine (comparison with "like")
 Metaphor: Her smile is sunshine (direct statement)
Purpose:
 Create stronger, more direct comparison
 More powerful than simile
 Makes writing vivid
273
In Sentences:
 "The world is a stage." (Shakespeare)
 "Knowledge is power."
 "Laughter is the best medicine."
3. IRONY:
Definition: When what happens or is said is opposite of what is expected or meant
Types:
a) Verbal Irony: Saying opposite of what you mean (like sarcasm)
 Saying "What lovely weather!" during a storm
 Calling a giant man "Tiny"
 "Oh great! I failed again" (meaning: This is not great)
b) Situational Irony: When outcome is opposite of what's expected
 A fire station burns down
 A traffic policeman gets a speeding ticket
 A swimming champion drowns
 You study wrong topics and those are the ones that appear in exam
c) Dramatic Irony: Audience knows something characters don't
 In Romeo and Juliet, we know Juliet is alive but Romeo doesn't
 Horror movie: we see the danger but character doesn't
Purpose:
 Create humor
 Show contrast
274
 Make point strongly
 Surprise audience
Examples:
 "How nice of you to break my phone!" (verbal irony - not really nice)
 A thief's house gets robbed (situational irony)
 In a play, character says "Nothing bad will happen" but audience knows danger is coming
(dramatic irony)
COMPARISON TABLE:
Figure Definition Example Uses
Simile Comparison with like/as She is like a rose Like, as
Metaphor Direct comparison (is) She is a rose No like/as
Irony Opposite of expected The fire station burned Contrast, humor
OTHER FIGURES OF SPEECH:
Personification: Giving human qualities to non-human things
 "The wind whispered"
 "Death smiled"
Hyperbole: Exaggeration
 "I'm so hungry I could eat a horse"
 "I've told you a million times"
Alliteration: Repetition of beginning sounds
 "Peter Piper picked..."
275
IDENTIFYING FIGURES OF SPEECH:
In Your Drama/Prose Text:
 Look for comparisons
 Find "like" or "as" = simile
 Find "is/are" with unusual comparison = metaphor
 Look for opposite situations = irony
 Note the effect created
EVALUATION:
1. Define figures of speech and state their purpose
2. What is a simile? Give 5 examples
3. What is a metaphor? Give 5 examples
4. Explain the difference between simile and metaphor with examples
5. What is irony? Give 3 examples of different types
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Find 5 similes and 5 metaphors in your recommended texts (drama or prose). Write them
down with page numbers
2. Create 10 similes and 10 metaphors of your own
3. Identify the figure of speech in each: (a) Life is a roller coaster (b) She sings like an angel
(c) The stars danced in the sky (d) A fire station burned down (e) He is as strong as an ox
4. Write 5 sentences using similes and 5 using metaphors
276
5. Write a short paragraph (100 words) describing your school using at least 3 similes and 2
metaphors
277
WEEK 11 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /Ɔ:/
THE LONG VOWEL /ɔ:/ (as in "door"):
Production:
 Tongue mid-back position
 Lips rounded and protruded
 Long, prolonged sound
Common Spellings:
 "or": door, floor, form, born, horn, torn, worn, sport, short, morning
 "au": August, author, daughter, cause, because, pause, fault
 "aw": law, draw, saw, raw, dawn, yawn, awful
 "ough": bought, brought, thought, fought, ought, taught, caught
 "al": talk, walk, chalk, always, also, ball, call, tall, fall, wall
 "our": four, your, pour, court
 "oa": broad, board
Minimal Pairs:
 pot /ɒ/ - port /ɔ:/
 cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔ:/
 not /ɒ/ - nought /ɔ:/
 cod /ɒ/ - cord /ɔ:/
Practice Words: door, floor, more, store, before, four, your, pour, born, torn, form, storm, short,
sport, morning, call, tall, ball, walk, talk, law, saw, draw, bought, brought, thought
278
Practice Sentence: "Your daughter bought four balls and thought about sport before dawn this
morning."
EVALUATION:
1. Produce /ɔ:/ sound correctly with 10 words
2. Differentiate /ɔ:/ from /ɒ/ with 5 minimal pairs
3. Identify /ɔ:/ words: (a) door (b) dog (c) caught (d) cot (e) sport
4. Write 5 sentences using /ɔ:/ words
5. List 5 different spelling patterns that produce /ɔ:/ sound with examples
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List 30 words with /ɔ:/ sound (covering different spellings: or, au, aw, ough, al)
2. Practice: "Your daughter bought four balls before dawn and thought about sport in the
morning"
3. Create 5 minimal pairs: /ɔ:/ vs /ɒ/ and /ɔ:/ vs /əʊ/
4. Write 10 sentences using words with /ɔ:/ sound
5. Group these words by sound /ɔ:/ or /ɒ/: door, dog, bought, hot, sport, spot, call, doll,
taught, top
279
WEEK 11 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: ADVERBIALS – FEATURES, FUNCTIONS, TYPES
DEFINITION: Adverbials are words, phrases, or clauses that function like adverbs, modifying
verbs, adjectives, or entire sentences.
ADVERB vs. ADVERBIAL:
 Adverb: Single word (quickly, often, very)
 Adverbial: Can be word, phrase, or clause (very quickly, in the morning, when I arrived)
FEATURES OF ADVERBIALS:
1. Modify verbs, adjectives, other adverbs, or sentences
2. Answer questions: How? When? Where? Why? How often? To what extent?
3. Can be moved in sentence (flexible position)
4. Optional (not essential for grammatical correctness)
5. Various forms: single word, phrase, clause
TYPES OF ADVERBIALS:
1. ADVERBIALS OF MANNER (How?): Tell how an action is done
Forms:
 Single adverb: quickly, carefully, well
 Prepositional phrase: with care, in a hurry, in style
 Clause: as if he were angry
Examples:
 She spoke softly
280
 He left in a hurry
 She acted as if nothing happened
2. ADVERBIALS OF TIME (When?): Tell when an action occurs
Forms:
 Single adverb: yesterday, soon, now, today
 Prepositional phrase: in the morning, on Monday, at noon
 Clause: when I arrived, before she left
Examples:
 I will go tomorrow
 We met in the evening
 Call me when you arrive
3. ADVERBIALS OF PLACE (Where?): Tell where an action occurs
Forms:
 Single adverb: here, there, everywhere
 Prepositional phrase: in the room, at school, on the table
 Clause: where you left it
Examples:
 Come here
 She lives in Lagos
 Put it where you found it
281
4. ADVERBIALS OF FREQUENCY (How often?): Tell how often
Forms:
 Single adverb: always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never
 Prepositional phrase: every day, once a week
 Clause: whenever I can
Examples:
 I always wake up early
 We visit every Sunday
 Come whenever you want
5. ADVERBIALS OF DEGREE (To what extent?): Tell intensity or extent
Forms:
 Single adverb: very, quite, too, extremely, completely
 Prepositional phrase: to a great extent
 Clause: as much as possible
Examples:
 She is very tall
 He succeeded beyond expectations
 Try as hard as you can
6. ADVERBIALS OF REASON (Why?): Tell why something happens
Forms:
 Prepositional phrase: because of the rain, due to illness
282
 Clause: because he was sick, since she was tired
Examples:
 She stayed home because of the rain
 Because he was sick, he missed school
POSITION OF ADVERBIALS:
Beginning (Initial Position):
 Yesterday, I went to school
 Fortunately, nobody was hurt
 In the morning, we exercise
Middle (Medial Position):
 I often go there
 She always arrives early
 He is never late
End (Final Position):
 She spoke softly
 We will meet tomorrow
 They live in Lagos
Note: Some adverbials can move, others have fixed positions
 Adverbs of frequency usually go before main verb
 Adverbs of manner usually go after verb/object
FUNCTIONS OF ADVERBIALS:
283
1. Modify verbs:
o She runs quickly
2. Modify adjectives:
o He is very tall
3. Modify other adverbs:
o She spoke too quickly
4. Modify entire sentences:
o Unfortunately, we lost
5. Show relationships:
o Between actions and time/place/manner
6. Add information:
o About how, when, where, why, how often
COMMON ADVERBIAL PHRASES:
Time:
 in the morning, at night, on Monday, last week, next year
Place:
 at home, in the garden, on the table, under the tree
Manner:
 with care, in a hurry, like a professional
Frequency:
 every day, once a week, from time to time
284
IDENTIFYING ADVERBIALS:
Ask questions:
 How? → manner
 When? → time
 Where? → place
 Why? → reason
 How often? → frequency
 To what extent? → degree
Example Sentence: "Yesterday, I carefully walked to school because of the rain."
 Yesterday = time (When?)
 carefully = manner (How?)
 to school = place (Where?)
 because of the rain = reason (Why?)
EVALUATION:
1. Define adverbial and explain how it differs from an adverb
2. State 5 types of adverbials with 2 examples each
3. Identify the adverbials and their types: "Yesterday, she quickly ran to school because she
was late."
4. Give 3 examples each of adverbials in: initial, medial, and final positions
5. What questions do adverbials answer?
ASSIGNMENT:
285
1. Write 10 sentences using different types of adverbials (at least one of each type)
2. Create adverbial phrases (not single words) for: time, place, manner, reason, frequency
3. Identify all adverbials and classify them: "Last week, my friend very carefully walked
into the room because of the wet floor. She always moves slowly in such situations."
4. Rewrite these sentences, adding appropriate adverbials: (a) She spoke (b) We will meet (c)
He studies (d) They arrived (e) I was happy
5. Explain the three positions where adverbials can appear in sentences with examples
286
WEEK 11 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: ASKING SPECIFIC QUESTIONS TO ELICIT STUDENT OPINIONS
OPINION QUESTIONS:
Definition: Questions that ask for personal views, judgments, or feelings about a topic. No
single "correct" answer.
Purpose:
 Encourage critical thinking
 Personal engagement with text
 Apply to own life
 Evaluate and judge
 Express preferences
CHARACTERISTICS:
1. Subjective (based on personal view)
2. No right/wrong answer (opinions vary)
3. Require reasoning (support your opinion)
4. Based on text (but go beyond it)
5. Personal response (what YOU think)
TYPES OF OPINION QUESTIONS:
1. AGREEMENT/DISAGREEMENT: Do you agree or disagree?
Examples:
287
 Do you agree with the character's decision?
 Do you think the author is right?
 Should the character have acted differently?
How to Answer:
 State your position (agree/disagree)
 Give reasons
 Use examples from text
 Explain your thinking
2. EVALUATION/JUDGMENT: Judge the quality, rightness, goodness
Examples:
 Was the character's action right or wrong?
 Is this a good solution to the problem?
 Did the character make the best choice?
How to Answer:
 State your judgment
 Explain why
 Consider alternatives
 Use evidence
3. PERSONAL PREFERENCE: What do you prefer, like, favor?
Examples:
 Which character do you like best?
288
 What is your favorite part?
 Which solution do you prefer?
How to Answer:
 State preference clearly
 Explain reasons
 Compare options
 Give examples
4. APPLICATION TO SELF: Relate to your own life
Examples:
 What would you do in this situation?
 How would you feel?
 Have you experienced something similar?
 Would you make the same choice?
How to Answer:
 Connect to personal experience
 Explain your response
 Compare to character's action
 Be honest
5. ADVICE/RECOMMENDATION: What should be done?
Examples:
 What advice would you give the character?
289
 What should happen next?
 How would you solve this problem?
How to Answer:
 Give specific advice
 Explain why
 Consider consequences
 Be practical
SIGNAL WORDS FOR OPINION QUESTIONS:
 Do you think...?
 In your opinion...
 Do you agree...?
 What would you...?
 How do you feel...?
 Should...?
 Would you...?
 What is your view...?
HOW TO ANSWER OPINION QUESTIONS:
Structure:
1. State Your Opinion Clearly:
 "I think..."
 "In my opinion..."
290
 "I believe..."
 "I agree/disagree because..."
2. Give Reasons:
 "Firstly,..."
 "The reason is..."
 "This is because..."
3. Provide Evidence/Examples:
 From text
 From personal experience
 From general knowledge
4. Conclude:
 Restate opinion
 Sum up reasoning
SAMPLE PASSAGE:
"John found 10,000 naira on the school playground. No one was around. He thought about
keeping it to buy the sneakers he wanted. But he remembered his father's teaching about honesty.
He took the money to the principal's office. A week later, a poor woman came to claim it - it was
all she had for her sick child's treatment."
Opinion Questions:
1. Agreement: "Do you agree with John's decision to return the money?"
Sample Answer: "Yes, I agree with John's decision. Honesty is more important than material
things like sneakers. Also, the money was very important to someone else - a sick child needed it.
John did the right thing by following his values."
291
2. Evaluation: "Was John's action right or wrong?"
Sample Answer: "John's action was absolutely right. He could have kept the money, but he
chose to be honest. Because of his honesty, a sick child got the treatment needed. This shows that
doing the right thing can save lives."
3. Personal Application: "What would you do in John's situation?"
Sample Answer: "I would do the same thing as John. Even though I might want something for
myself, someone else's need is more important, especially if it's for medical treatment. I would
feel guilty keeping money that isn't mine."
4. Advice: "What advice would you give to someone who finds money?"
Sample Answer: "I would advise them to try to find the owner or report it to authorities. Money
lost could be someone's savings or emergency fund. By being honest, you help others and build
good character."
TIPS FOR ANSWERING:
DO:
 State opinion clearly
 Give reasons
 Use examples
 Be honest
 Support with evidence
 Consider different views
DON'T:
 Just say "yes" or "no"
 Leave it unsupported
292
 Copy from text without thinking
 Be vague
 Contradict yourself
PRACTICE:
Passage: "A student saw his friend cheating during an exam. He knew his friend needed to pass
to avoid repeating the class. He also knew cheating was wrong."
Opinion Questions:
1. Should the student report his friend?
2. What would you do in this situation?
3. Is it ever okay to cheat? Why or why not?
4. What advice would you give to the friend who is cheating?
5. How do you think the student felt when he saw his friend cheating?
EVALUATION:
1. What are opinion questions? How are they different from literal questions?
2. List 5 signal words/phrases that indicate opinion questions
3. Read this passage: "A girl had to choose between helping her sick grandmother or going
to an important interview." Write 3 opinion questions about this passage
4. What should you include when answering an opinion question?
5. Answer this opinion question: "Do you think students should have homework every day?
Why or why not?"
293
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Read any passage from your textbook. Write 5 different types of opinion questions:
agreement, evaluation, preference, application, advice
2. For each opinion question you created in Q1, write a full answer (3-4
sentences) showing how to properly support your opinion 3. Explain the difference between: (a)
A literal question and an opinion question (b) An interpretation question and an opinion question
4. Practice: Answer these opinion questions with full support: (a) Should students be allowed to
use phones in school? (b) Is it better to have a few close friends or many acquaintances? (c)
What is the most important subject in school? 5. Create 5 opinion questions about your prose or
drama text and answer one of them fully
294

Writer's Address (top right)
Date
Salutation,
Opening paragraph (greet, ask about welfare, state purpose)
Middle paragraphs (2-3) - main message, details
Closing paragraph (final thoughts, greetings, hope to hear back)
Complimentary Close,
Signature
Name
COMMON INFORMAL LETTER PURPOSES:
1. SHARING NEWS: Telling about recent events in your life
Topics:
 School experiences
295
 Family events
 Achievements
 New activities
 Changes in life
Example Opening: "I have exciting news to share with you! Last week, I won first prize in the
science competition..."
2. INVITING: Asking someone to visit or attend an event
Include:
 What event
 When (date, time)
 Where (location)
 Why (occasion)
 Request for response
Example: "I am writing to invite you to my birthday celebration on December 15th at 4 PM..."
3. THANKING: Expressing gratitude for gift, help, or hospitality
Include:
 What you're thankful for
 Why it was meaningful
 How you felt/feel
 Impact on you
Example: "Thank you so much for the wonderful birthday gift. The book you gave me is exactly
what I wanted..."
296
4. APOLOGIZING: Saying sorry for something
Include:
 What you're sorry for
 Why it happened
 How you feel
 Promise to do better
Example: "I am writing to apologize for missing your birthday party last Saturday. I was really
looking forward to it, but I fell sick..."
5. CONGRATULATING: Celebrating someone's success or good news
Include:
 What you're congratulating them for
 Your happiness for them
 Encouraging words
 Wishes for continued success
Example: "Congratulations on your excellent exam results! I was so happy when I heard the
news..."
6. GIVING ADVICE: Helping friend with a problem
Include:
 Understanding of their situation
 Thoughtful advice
297
 Support and encouragement
 Offer to help further
Example: "I understand you're having difficulty choosing subjects. Let me share my thoughts..."
LANGUAGE TIPS:
Opening Paragraphs - Warm Greetings:
 "How are you? I hope this letter finds you in good health."
 "I hope you and your family are doing well."
 "It's been so long since we last talked! How have you been?"
 "I was so happy to receive your letter yesterday."
Middle Paragraphs - Main Message:
 Be conversational
 Use personal pronouns (I, you, we)
 Share details
 Express emotions
 Ask questions
 Use contractions (I'm, don't, can't)
Closing Paragraphs:
 "I hope to hear from you soon."
 "Please write back and tell me your news."
 "Give my regards to your family."
 "I can't wait to see you during the holidays."
 "Take care of yourself and study hard."
298
PRACTICE LETTER - SHARING NEWS:
45, Church Street,
Surulere,
Lagos State.
15th November, 2024.
Dear Amina,
How are you doing? I hope you, your parents, and your little brother are all in good health. I'm
writing to share some exciting news with you!
You'll never guess what happened! I was chosen to represent our school at the inter-school
debate competition next month. Remember how nervous I used to be about speaking in public?
Well, I've been practicing, and our English teacher said I've improved a lot. The topic is "The
Role of Youth in Nation Building," and I'm really excited about it.
Also, my elder brother got admission to study Medicine at the University of Lagos! We're all so
proud of him. He's been working towards this for years, and it finally paid off. The whole family
celebrated last weekend.
How are things at your school? Have you started preparing for your exams? I know you
mentioned you were worried about Mathematics. If you need any help, I'd be happy to share the
notes our teacher gave us.
299
Please give my warm regards to your parents. I miss our conversations and hope we can see each
other soon. Maybe you can visit during the mid-term break?
Your best friend,
[Signature]
Zainab
Analysis:
 ✓ Proper format and structure
 ✓ Warm, friendly tone
 ✓ Personal details and emotions
 ✓ Questions showing interest
 ✓ Appropriate closing
 ✓ Natural, conversational language
COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID:
1. Too formal:
o ✗ "I am writing this epistle to inform you..."
o ✓ "I'm writing to tell you..."
2. Too brief:
o ✗ One paragraph only
o ✓ At least 3 paragraphs
3. Forgetting to ask about recipient:
300
o ✗ Only talking about yourself
o ✓ Ask about their welfare and life
4. Wrong closing:
o ✗ "Yours faithfully" (that's formal!)
o ✓ "Yours lovingly," "Your friend,"
5. Including recipient's address:
o Not needed in informal letters
6. Being too stiff:
o Should sound like talking to friend
TRANSITION WORDS FOR INFORMAL LETTERS:
To add information:
 Also, Besides, By the way, Additionally
To show time:
 Recently, Last week, Yesterday, Soon, Later
To show contrast:
 However, But, Although, On the other hand
To give examples:
 For example, For instance, Like
To conclude:
 Finally, In conclusion, Anyway, So
301
EVALUATION:
1. List the 6 parts of an informal letter in correct order
2. Write an appropriate opening paragraph for an informal letter to your cousin
3. What are 5 common purposes of informal letters?
4. Write 3 appropriate closings for informal letters
5. What makes language in informal letters different from formal letters?
ASSIGNMENT:

recent school event OR (b) Inviting them to visit during holidays OR (c) Thanking them
for a gift

[provide sample letters]

Writing recipient's address (c) Using "Yours faithfully" as closing (d) Too formal
language throughout

letters

PART B: FIGURES OF SPEECH (Continued Practice)
REVIEW:
1. SIMILE: Comparison using like/as 2. METAPHOR: Direct comparison without like/as 3.
IRONY: Opposite of what's expected
ADVANCED PRACTICE:
IDENTIFYING IN TEXTS:
From Drama/Prose: Look for:
 Comparisons (simile/metaphor)
 Unexpected outcomes (irony)
 Descriptive language
 Powerful imagery
305
Example from Text: "Her heart was like stone" - SIMILE "Her heart was stone" - METAPHOR
Character says "I'll never leave" but leaves immediately - IRONY
CREATING FIGURES OF SPEECH:
Simile Creation:
Formula: [subject] is like [comparison]
Examples:
 Her voice is like honey
 The classroom is as cold as ice
 He runs like the wind
 She is as gentle as a lamb
Your Turn: Create similes for:
 A busy market → "The market is like a beehive"
 A difficult exam → "The exam was as hard as climbing a mountain"
 A kind teacher → "Our teacher is like a second mother"
Metaphor Creation:
Formula: [subject] is [comparison]
Examples:
 Time is money
 She is sunshine
 The world is a stage
 Life is a journey
306
Your Turn: Create metaphors for:
 A book → "This book is a treasure"
 Knowledge → "Knowledge is power"
 Hope → "Hope is a light in darkness"
Irony Creation:
Think of opposites:
 What's unexpected?
 What's contradictory?
 What's surprising?
Examples:
 A doctor who is always sick (situational)
 "Oh wonderful!" when something bad happens (verbal)
 In a play, audience knows danger but character doesn't (dramatic)
ANALYZING EFFECT:
Why Authors Use These:
Simile/Metaphor:
 Make description vivid
 Help readers visualize
 Create memorable images
 Express complex ideas simply
 Add beauty to language
307
Irony:
 Create humor
 Show contrast
 Emphasize point
 Surprise audience
 Make readers think
FINDING FIGURES OF SPEECH:
In Your Texts:
Steps:
1. Read carefully
2. Look for comparisons
3. Check for "like" or "as" (simile)
4. Check for direct comparison (metaphor)
5. Note unexpected situations (irony)
6. Record page numbers
7. Explain the effect
Recording Format:
 Quote: "Her smile was like sunshine"
 Type: Simile
 Page: 45
 Effect: Shows she has a bright, warm smile that makes others happy
308
PRACTICE EXERCISES:
Exercise 1: Identify the Figure of Speech:
1. "Life is a roller coaster" - METAPHOR
2. "She sings like an angel" - SIMILE
3. A fire station burns down - IRONY (situational)
4. "The stars danced in the sky" - PERSONIFICATION (bonus!)
5. "He is as brave as a lion" - SIMILE
6. "What lovely weather!" (during storm) - IRONY (verbal)
7. "The world is your oyster" - METAPHOR
8. "Her eyes were diamonds" - METAPHOR
Exercise 2: Create Your Own:
Similes for:
 Happy child → "The child was as happy as..."
 Fast car → "The car moved like..."
 Beautiful sunset → "The sunset was like..."
Metaphors for:
 Difficult problem → "This problem is..."
 Good friend → "A good friend is..."
 Busy city → "The city is..."
Exercise 3: Find in Your Text:
From your drama or prose text, find:
309
 3 similes (with page numbers)
 3 metaphors (with page numbers)
 2 examples of irony (describe the situation)
OTHER FIGURES OF SPEECH:
PERSONIFICATION: Giving human qualities to non-human things
 "The wind whispered secrets"
 "Death knocked on his door"
 "The sun smiled down"
HYPERBOLE: Extreme exaggeration for effect
 "I'm so hungry I could eat a horse"
 "I've told you a million times"
 "She's older than the hills"
ALLITERATION: Repetition of beginning consonant sounds
 "Peter Piper picked a peck..."
 "She sells seashells..."
 "Big brown bear"
ONOMATOPOEIA: Words that sound like what they mean
 Buzz, hiss, bang, crash, meow, moo
USING FIGURES OF SPEECH IN WRITING:
Tips:
 Don't overuse (too many becomes confusing)
310
 Make them original (avoid clichés like "white as snow")
 Make them relevant (fit the context)
 Make them clear (readers should understand)
Good: "Her eyes sparkled like stars in the night sky" Overused: "Her eyes were as bright as
stars" (cliché)
EXAM PREPARATION:
Common Questions:
1. Identify the figure of speech in this line: "___"
2. Explain the meaning of this metaphor/simile
3. What effect does this figure of speech create?
4. Find two examples of similes in the passage
5. Create a metaphor for [subject]
How to Answer:
 Identify correctly
 Quote if asked
 Explain the meaning
 Discuss the effect
 Give examples when asked

and page number)

JSS1 ENGLISH STUDIES LESSON NOTES
SECOND TERM
WEEK 1 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUNDS /u/ AND /u:/
CONTENT:
The Short Vowel Sound /u/
 This is a short, relaxed sound produced with lips slightly rounded
 The tongue is raised towards the back of the mouth
 Examples: book, look, cook, good, foot, put, pull, full, wood, stood, hook, took, could,
should, would
The Long Vowel Sound /u:/
 This is a longer sound with lips more tightly rounded
 The tongue is raised higher at the back
 Examples: food, mood, moon, soon, spoon, school, cool, pool, tool, boot, root, shoot,
troop, group, soup
Key Differences:
 /u/ is SHORT and relaxed (as in "book")
 /u:/ is LONG and tense (as in "boot")
 Incorrect pronunciation can change meaning: "fool" /u:/ vs "full" /u/
Practice Pairs:
 look /u/ - Luke /u:/
314
 pull /u/ - pool /u:/
 soot /u/ - suit /u:/
 good /u/ - food /u:/
EVALUATION:
1. Pronounce these words correctly: book, boot, look, Luke, full, fool
2. Which vowel sound /u/ or /u:/ appears in: (a) school (b) wood (c) moon (d) foot (e) soup
3. Give five words containing the /u/ sound
4. Give five words containing the /u:/ sound
5. Pronounce and differentiate: pull/pool, look/Luke, good/food
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write ten words containing the /u/ sound
2. Write ten words containing the /u:/ sound
3. Write five sentences using words with /u/ sound
4. Write five sentences using words with /u:/ sound
5. Create five word pairs that show the difference between /u/ and /u:/ sounds
315
WEEK 1 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: ADVERBIALS – FREQUENCY AND MANNER
Adverbials of Frequency These tell us HOW OFTEN an action happens.
Common Adverbials of Frequency:
 Always (100%)
 Usually/Normally/Generally (90%)
 Often/Frequently (70%)
 Sometimes/Occasionally (50%)
 Seldom/Rarely (10%)
 Never (0%)
 Daily, weekly, monthly, yearly
 Once, twice, three times (a day/week/month)
Position in Sentences:
 Usually before the main verb: "She always comes early"
 After the verb 'to be': "He is always late"
 At the beginning or end for emphasis: "Sometimes I walk to school"
Examples:
 I always do my homework
 They usually play football on Saturdays
 We rarely go to the cinema
 She is never absent from school
 He visits his grandmother twice a month
316
Adverbials of Manner These tell us HOW an action is performed.
Formation: Most are formed by adding -ly to adjectives
 Quick → quickly
 Careful → carefully
 Beautiful → beautifully
 Quiet → quietly
Irregular Forms:
 Good → well
 Fast → fast
 Hard → hard
 Late → late
Position in Sentences:
 Usually after the verb or object: "She speaks English fluently"
 Can come at the beginning for emphasis: "Carefully, he opened the door"
Examples:
 The boy ran quickly
 She sings beautifully
 He answered the question correctly
 They worked hard on the project
 The baby sleeps peacefully
EVALUATION:
1. Define adverbials of frequency and give five examples
317
2. Arrange in order from most frequent to least frequent: rarely, always, sometimes, never,
often
3. Form adverbs of manner from: slow, happy, bad, good, easy
4. Write three sentences using adverbials of frequency
5. Write three sentences using adverbials of manner
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List ten adverbials of frequency
2. List ten adverbials of manner
3. Write five sentences using both adverbials of frequency and manner in each sentence
4. Identify the adverbials in these sentences and state their types: (a) She always sings
beautifully (b) They rarely speak loudly (c) He often works hard
5. Rewrite these sentences adding appropriate adverbials: (a) I go to church (b) She
completed the task (c) They play football
318
WEEK 1 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: ASKING SPECIFIC QUESTIONS REQUIRING CONCLUSIONS
What are Conclusion Questions? These are questions that require you to:
 Make inferences from the passage
 Draw logical conclusions based on given information
 Go beyond what is directly stated
 Use reasoning and evidence from the text
Types of Conclusion Questions:
1. "What can we conclude/infer from the passage?"
2. "The passage suggests that..."
3. "Based on the information, we can say that..."
4. "Why do you think...?" (requiring reasoning)
5. "What is the main idea/lesson/message?"
How to Answer Conclusion Questions:
Step 1: Read the passage carefully Step 2: Identify key facts and details Step 3: Look for
patterns, causes, and effects Step 4: Think about what the facts suggest Step 5: Make logical
connections Step 6: Support your conclusion with evidence from the text
Sample Passage: "Amina woke up at 5:30 AM every morning. She studied for two hours before
breakfast. During break time at school, while others played, she revised her notes. Her teachers
often praised her. At the end of term, she received the Best Student Award."
Direct Question: When did Amina wake up? Answer: 5:30 AM (stated directly)
Conclusion Questions:
1. What can we conclude about Amina's character?
319
o Answer: She is hardworking, dedicated, and disciplined (we infer this from her
actions)
2. Why do you think Amina received the Best Student Award?
o Answer: Because she was committed to her studies and made sacrifices to excel
(we conclude from the evidence)
Key Signal Words in Conclusion Questions:
 Infer, conclude, suggest, imply
 Probably, likely, presumably
 Why, how, what caused
 Main idea, purpose, lesson
Common Mistakes to Avoid:
 Making wild guesses without evidence
 Including personal opinions unrelated to the text
 Copying direct statements instead of inferring
 Ignoring important details in the passage
EVALUATION: Read the passage and answer the questions:
"The village marketplace was empty. The shops were closed, and no traders displayed their
goods. Streets that usually buzzed with activity were silent. Only a few people hurried past, their
faces covered. The town crier's bell had announced the news at dawn."
1. What can we conclude about what happened in the village?
2. Why do you think people's faces were covered?
3. What does "the town crier's bell had announced the news at dawn" suggest?
4. How would you describe the atmosphere in the village? Give reasons from the passage.
320
5. Explain the difference between a direct question and a conclusion question using
examples from the passage.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Read any short story or article of your choice (200-300 words)
2. Write three direct questions and their answers from the passage
3. Write three conclusion questions and their answers from the passage
4. Explain how you arrived at your conclusions for question 3
5. State three signal words that help identify conclusion questions
321
WEEK 1 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: FORMAL LETTER – FEATURES; WRITING TO PRINCIPAL FOR
PERMISSION
What is a Formal Letter? A formal letter is an official letter written to people in authority,
organizations, or institutions using polite, respectful, and standard language.
Features of a Formal Letter:
1. Writer's Address (top right corner)
2. Date (below writer's address)
3. Recipient's Address (left side, below date)
4. Salutation (Dear Sir/Madam, Dear Principal)
5. Title/Subject (underlined or bold)
6. Body (Introduction, Main content, Conclusion)
7. Closing (Yours faithfully/sincerely)
8. Signature and Full Name
Format Layout:
23, Ademola Street,
Ikeja,
Lagos State.
18th October, 2025.
The Principal,
Government Junior Secondary School,
322
Ikeja,
Lagos State.
Dear Sir,
REQUEST FOR PERMISSION TO BE ABSENT FROM SCHOOL
Introduction paragraph...
Body paragraphs...
Conclusion paragraph...
Yours faithfully,
[Signature]
Adeyemi Johnson
Language Features:
 Formal/polite tone
 No contractions (use "I am" not "I'm")
 No slang or informal expressions
 Clear and direct sentences
 Respectful language
 Proper punctuation
323
Sample Letter:
15, Olowu Street,
Mushin,
Lagos State.
18th October, 2025.
The Principal,
Community Grammar School,
Mushin,
Lagos State.
Dear Ma,
REQUEST FOR PERMISSION TO ATTEND MY SISTER'S WEDDING
I write to request permission to be absent from school on Friday, 25th October, 2025.
My elder sister's wedding ceremony has been scheduled for that date. As a member of the family,
my presence is important and required. The ceremony will take place in our hometown in Ibadan,
Oyo State.
324
I promise to collect notes from my classmates and make up for any work missed during my
absence. I will also ensure that I catch up with all lessons taught on that day.
I hope you will grant my request. Thank you for your understanding.
Yours faithfully,
[Signature]
Blessing Okonkwo
JSS 1B
Common Reasons for Writing to the Principal:
 Permission to be absent
 Request for transfer certificate
 Complaint about facilities
 Request for recommendation letter
 Permission for excursion/trip
 Explanation for lateness/absence
Important Points:
 Be polite and respectful
 State your reason clearly
 Be brief and direct
 Use proper format
 Check spelling and grammar
 Sign your letter
325
EVALUATION:
1. State five features of a formal letter
2. What is the difference between "Yours faithfully" and "Yours sincerely"?
3. Where is the writer's address positioned in a formal letter?

known female person
