WEEK 4 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɜ:/
The Long Vowel Sound /ɜ:/
 This is a long, central vowel sound produced in the middle of the mouth
 The tongue is in the center, neither high nor low
 The lips are slightly spread, not rounded
 It is a tense, prolonged sound
 Similar to the sound when hesitating: "Er..."
Spelling Patterns for /ɜ:/ Sound:
1. "ir" spelling:
 bird, girl, first, shirt, skirt, third, thirst, birthday
 dirt, firm, fir, sir, stir, whirl, twirl
 circle, circus, confirm, dirty, thirty, thirteen
2. "er" spelling:
 her, term, serve, verb, nerve, herb
 certain, person, perfect, merchant, verse
 teacher, mother, father, sister, brother (unstressed syllables)
 prefer, refer, infer, defer, confer
3. "ur" spelling:
 turn, burn, church, hurt, nurse, purse, turkey
 fur, blur,pur, spur, slur
 disturb, furniture, purple, purpose, curtain
394
 Thursday, surname, burden, murder
4. "ear" spelling:
 learn, earth, early, earn, earnest, search, pearl
 heard, rehearse
5. "or" spelling (after 'w'):
 word, work, world, worm, worth, worship
 worse, worst
6. "our" spelling:
 journey, journal, courtesy, courtesy
Common Words with /ɜ:/ Sound:
 return, concern, urgent, service, surface
 german, perfect, merchant, deserve
 burden, curtain, murder, turkey
 circular, emergency, commercial
Practice Sentences:
 The girl with the purple shirt was first
 My sister is a nurse at the church
 The merchant returned early from his journey
 I heard the birds chirping on Thursday
 Her thirst for learning is perfect
Words That Look Similar But Sound Different:
395
 her /ɜ:/ - here /ɪə/ (different sound)
 were /ɜ:/ - where /eə/ (different sound)
 for /ɔ:/ - fur /ɜ:/ (different sounds)
Articulation Tips:
 Position your tongue in the center of your mouth
 Don't raise or lower it too much
 Keep lips slightly spread and relaxed
 Make the sound long and steady
 Don't round your lips
Practice Pairs:
 bad /æ/ - bird /ɜ:/
 bun /ʌ/ - burn /ɜ:/
 hot /ɒ/ - hurt /ɜ:/
 word /ɜ:/ - ward /ɔ:/
Common Mistakes: Many speakers confuse /ɜ:/ with /ɔ:/ or /ʌ/
 fur /ɜ:/ NOT for /ɔ:/
 burn /ɜ:/ NOT born /ɔ:/
 hurt /ɜ:/ NOT heart /ɑ:/
EVALUATION:
1. Produce the /ɜ:/ sound correctly and describe how your tongue and lips are positioned
2. Give fifteen words containing the /ɜ:/ sound using different spelling patterns
396
3. Identify which words contain /ɜ:/ sound: (a) bird (b) board (c) turn (d) town (e) word (f)
world (g) worm (h) warm
4. Write five sentences using words with /ɜ:/ sound, underlining the words
5. Explain the difference in pronunciation between: (a) fur/for (b) burn/born (c) word/ward
(d) hurt/heart
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List ten words each with "ir," "er," and "ur" spellings that contain the /ɜ:/ sound
2. List five words with "ear" and "or" (after w) spellings that produce /ɜ:/ sound
3. Write ten sentences using different words with /ɜ:/ sound
4. Practice reading aloud: "The nurse in the purple shirt served turkey at the church on
Thursday. She heard the birds singing early in the morning and learned a new verse"
5. Group these words by their vowel sounds - /ɜ:/, /ɔ:/, or /ɑ:/: bird, board, burn, born, cart,
curl, first, force, farm, fur, for, father
397
WEEK 4 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: TENSES – PRESENT CONTINUOUS TENSE
What is the Present Continuous Tense? The Present Continuous Tense (also called Present
Progressive Tense) describes actions that are happening now, at this moment, or around this time.
It shows ongoing or temporary actions.
Formation of Present Continuous Tense:
Structure: Subject + am/is/are + verb-ing
Using am/is/are:
 I + am (I'm)
 He/She/It + is (He's/She's/It's)
 You/We/They + are (You're/We're/They're)
Examples:
 I am reading
 She is cooking
 They are playing
 We are studying
Rules for Adding -ing:
1. Most verbs - add -ing directly:
 play → playing
 read → reading
 cook → cooking
 sing → singing
398
 talk → talking
2. Verbs ending in -e - drop the -e and add -ing:
 write → writing
 make → making
 come → coming
 dance → dancing
 take → taking
 have → having
 NOTE: Exceptions: be → being, see → seeing, agree → agreeing
3. One-syllable verbs ending in consonant-vowel-consonant - double the final consonant:
 run → running
 sit → sitting
 stop → stopping
 swim → swimming
 cut → cutting
 put → putting
 get → getting
4. Two-syllable verbs with stress on second syllable - double the final consonant:
 begin → beginning
 forget → forgetting
 prefer → preferring
 admit → admitting
5. Verbs ending in -ie - change -ie to -y and add -ing:
399
 lie → lying
 die → dying
 tie → tying
Uses of Present Continuous Tense:
1. Actions happening now (at the moment of speaking):
 I am writing a letter now
 She is eating lunch at the moment
 They are watching TV right now
 Look! The baby is crying
 Listen! Someone is knocking at the door
2. Temporary actions (happening around this time but not necessarily right now):
 I am reading a novel these days
 She is staying with her aunt this week
 He is working in Lagos this month
 We are studying English this term
3. Changing or developing situations:
 The weather is getting colder
 My English is improving
 The population is increasing
 Technology is advancing rapidly
4. Planned future arrangements:
 I am traveling to Abuja tomorrow
400
 We are having a party next week
 She is visiting her grandmother on Sunday
 They are starting school on Monday
5. Annoying habits (with 'always'):
 He is always complaining
 She is always arriving late
 You are always losing your keys
 They are always making noise
Negative Form:
 Subject + am/is/are + not + verb-ing
Examples:
 I am not sleeping
 She is not (isn't) coming today
 They are not (aren't) playing football
 We are not (aren't) watching TV
Question Form:
 Am/Is/Are + subject + verb-ing...?
Examples:
 Am I disturbing you?
 Is she cooking dinner?
 Are they playing outside?
 Are you listening to me?
Wh- Questions:
401
 What/Where/Why/Who/How + am/is/are + subject + verb-ing?
Examples:
 What are you doing?
 Where is he going?
 Why are they crying?
 Who is she calling?
 How are you feeling?
Time Expressions Used with Present Continuous:
 Now, right now, at the moment, at present
 Today, this week, this month, this year, this term
 Currently, presently
 Look! Listen! (to draw attention)
 Still (for ongoing action: He is still sleeping)
Verbs NOT Usually Used in Continuous Tenses: Some verbs describe states, not actions, and
are rarely used in continuous form:
Thinking/Feeling:
 know, believe, understand, remember, forget, prefer, want, need, like, love, hate, seem
Possession:
 have (meaning possess), own, belong, possess
Senses:
 see, hear, smell, taste (when describing ability, not action)
Others:
402
 be, exist, contain, consist, depend, matter, mean
Use Simple Present instead:
 ✓ I know the answer (Correct)
 ✗ I am knowing the answer (Wrong)
 ✓ She loves chocolate (Correct)
 ✗ She is loving chocolate (Wrong)
Present Continuous vs Simple Present:
Present Continuous:
 Temporary: I am living in Lagos (temporarily)
 Now: She is eating (at this moment)
 Changing: The weather is getting cold
Simple Present:
 Permanent: I live in Lagos (permanent home)
 Habitual: She eats breakfast at 7 AM (routine)
 General truth: The weather is cold in December
Common Mistakes to Avoid:
 ✗ I am knowing (Correct: I know)
 ✗ She is haveing a car (Correct: She has a car)
 ✗ They runing now (Correct: They are running)
 ✗ Is you coming? (Correct: Are you coming?)
 ✗ He working now (Correct: He is working now)
403
EVALUATION:
1. Define Present Continuous Tense and state four uses with examples
2. Write the -ing form of these verbs: (a) run (b) write (c) study (d) lie (e) stop (f) begin (g)
dance (h) get (i) prefer (j) have
3. Change to Present Continuous Tense: (a) I write a letter (b) She reads a book (c) They
play football (d) He eats rice (e) We go to school
4. Change to negative: (a) I am sleeping (b) She is coming today (c) They are watching TV
(d) He is working now (e) We are studying
5. Change to questions: (a) You are listening (b) She is cooking (c) They are playing (d) He
is reading (e) It is raining
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Explain the difference between Present Continuous and Simple Present Tense with five
examples each
2. Write ten sentences about what is happening in your classroom right now using Present
Continuous Tense
3. Fill in the blanks with the correct form: (a) I _____ (read) a book now (b) She _____
(not/sleep) at the moment (c) _____ they _____ (come) today? (d) What _____ you
_____ (do) now? (e) The baby _____ (cry)
4. Correct the errors: (a) He running now (b) I am knowing the answer (c) Is you coming?
(d) They are haveing lunch (e) She am studying
5. Rewrite this paragraph in Present Continuous Tense: "The students write in their books.
The teacher explains the lesson. Some students ask questions. One boy sleeps. The girls
laugh."
404
WEEK 4 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: READING TO UNDERSTAND AUTHOR'S MOOD (ANGER, DOUBT, JOY,
ETC.)
CONTENT:
What is Author's Mood? The author's mood (or tone) is the feeling or attitude the writer
expresses toward the subject or audience through their choice of words, style, and the way they
present information.
Why Understanding Mood is Important:
 Helps you understand the author's purpose
 Gives deeper meaning to the text
 Helps you interpret the message correctly
 Enhances your reading comprehension
 Allows you to respond appropriately to the text
Common Moods/Tones in Writing:
1. HAPPY/JOYFUL MOOD:
 Words: delightful, wonderful, exciting, cheerful, pleased, thrilled, overjoyed, elated, glad
 Tone: Enthusiastic, positive, optimistic, celebratory
 Indicators: Exclamation marks, positive adjectives, pleasant descriptions
405
Example Passage: "What a wonderful day it was! The sun shone brilliantly, birds sang
melodiously, and everyone walked with smiles on their faces. The atmosphere was filled with
laughter and joy."
2. SAD/SORROWFUL MOOD:
 Words: unfortunate, tragic, devastating, heartbreaking, miserable, gloomy, depressing
 Tone: Melancholic, mournful, sympathetic, regretful
 Indicators: Negative descriptions, expressions of loss, emotional language
Example Passage: "The village was engulfed in deep sorrow. Tears flowed freely as people
mourned the loss of their beloved leader. The once lively streets were now silent and empty,
reflecting the heavy hearts of the people."
3. ANGRY/FURIOUS MOOD:
 Words: outrageous, unacceptable, disgusting, infuriating, intolerable, ridiculous, absurd
 Tone: Hostile, indignant, bitter, resentful, critical
 Indicators: Strong accusations, harsh words, rhetorical questions, exclamation marks
Example Passage: "This is absolutely unacceptable! How dare they treat us with such
disrespect! It is outrageous that they continue to ignore our complaints. We will not tolerate this
injustice any longer!"
4. FEARFUL/ANXIOUS MOOD:
 Words: terrifying, frightening, worried, nervous, uneasy, concerned, alarmed, dangerous
 Tone: Apprehensive, tense, uncertain, cautious
 Indicators: Descriptions of danger, uncertainty, tension-building language
406
Example Passage: "The dark forest loomed ahead. Strange sounds echoed in the distance,
making her heart pound rapidly. Every shadow seemed threatening. She hesitated, uncertain
whether to proceed or turn back."
5. DOUBTFUL/SKEPTICAL MOOD:
 Words: questionable, uncertain, supposedly, allegedly, claims, dubious, unclear
 Tone: Questioning, suspicious, unconvinced, hesitant
 Indicators: Question marks, words like "perhaps," "maybe," "supposedly," "if"
Example Passage: "The official claims the project will be completed in six months, but many
doubt this promise. Previous projects were delayed for years. Is it realistic to believe this one
will be different? Perhaps, but history suggests otherwise."
6. HOPEFUL/OPTIMISTIC MOOD:
 Words: promising, encouraging, positive, bright, potential, opportunity, possible,
confident
 Tone: Encouraging, positive, forward-looking
 Indicators: Future-focused language, positive predictions, encouraging words
Example Passage: "Despite the challenges, there is reason for hope. New opportunities are
emerging, and the future looks promising. With determination and hard work, success is
certainly achievable. Better days lie ahead."
7. SERIOUS/FORMAL MOOD:
 Words: important, significant, crucial, essential, vital, critical, necessary
 Tone: Professional, objective, matter-of-fact, authoritative
 Indicators: Formal language, no emotional expressions, factual presentation
407
Example Passage: "The research indicates significant findings. It is essential that immediate
action be taken to address these issues. The data clearly demonstrates the need for policy reform
in this area."
8. HUMOROUS/PLAYFUL MOOD:
 Words: funny, amusing, hilarious, comical, entertaining, ridiculous (in a funny way)
 Tone: Light-hearted, witty, sarcastic (positively), joking
 Indicators: Jokes, exaggerations for effect, funny descriptions, wordplay
Example Passage: "My cat thinks he's the king of the house. He struts around like royalty,
demanding meals at precisely 6 AM, and gives me that look of utter disdain if I'm late. Who's the
owner here, really?"
How to Identify Author's Mood:
1. Look at Word Choice (Diction):
 Positive words suggest positive mood
 Negative words suggest negative mood
 Strong words suggest intense emotion
2. Notice Punctuation:
 Exclamation marks (!) = excitement, anger, emphasis
 Question marks (?) = doubt, inquiry, uncertainty
 Ellipsis (...) = hesitation, uncertainty, trailing thought
3. Observe Sentence Structure:
 Short, abrupt sentences = tension, anger, urgency
 Long, flowing sentences = calmness, description, reflection
 Incomplete sentences = confusion, hesitation
408
4. Consider the Context:
 What is the subject matter?
 What is the author's purpose?
 Who is the intended audience?
5. Look for Direct Emotional Expressions:
 "I am delighted to announce..."
 "It is with deep regret that..."
 "I strongly disagree with..."
6. Identify Figurative Language:
 Metaphors and similes reveal feelings
 Imagery creates mood
 Personification adds emotion
Practice Identifying Mood:
Passage 1: "The examination results were excellent. Out of 50 students, 48 passed with flying
colors. The principal praised the teachers and students for their outstanding performance. It was
indeed a moment of celebration."
Mood: Joyful/Proud
Clue Words: excellent, flying colors, praised, outstanding, celebration
Passage 2: "Corruption continues to plague our society. Officials steal public funds with
impunity while citizens suffer. This shameful behavior must stop immediately. We demand
accountability and justice!"
Mood: Angry/Indignant
409
Clue Words: plague, steal, impunity, shameful, demand, exclamation mark
Passage 3: "The storm approached rapidly. Dark clouds gathered overhead, and thunder
rumbled ominously. The villagers watched nervously, wondering if their homes could withstand
the coming tempest."
Mood: Fearful/Anxious
Clue Words: storm, dark, ominously, nervously, wondering
Multiple Moods: Sometimes an author may express different moods in different parts of a
passage. Always consider the overall dominant mood and any shifts in tone.
EVALUATION:
1. Define author's mood and explain why it is important to identify it when reading
2. List and explain five different moods an author can express in writing
3. Read this passage and identify the mood: "The team celebrated wildly as the final whistle
blew. They had won! Jumping, cheering, and hugging each other, they could hardly
contain their excitement. What a glorious victory!" Explain your answer with evidence
from the passage
4. What clues help readers identify an author's mood? List five
5. Write two short paragraphs (4 sentences each): one showing an angry mood and another
showing a joyful mood
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Identify the mood in each of these passages and give reasons: (a) "The abandoned house
stood in ruins. Broken windows stared like empty eyes. Weeds choked the pathway, and
silence hung heavily in the air." (b) "Can we really believe these promises? They have
410
failed us before. What guarantee do we have that this time will be different?" (c) "Success
is within reach! With dedication and perseverance, we can achieve our goals. The future
is bright with possibilities."
2. Write a paragraph (80 words) expressing a sad mood about a lost pet
3. Write a paragraph (80 words) expressing an excited mood about a school excursion
4. List ten words associated with: (a) angry mood (b) happy mood
5. Rewrite this neutral sentence in three different moods: "The government announced a
new policy." (a) Angry mood (b) Happy mood (c) Doubtful mood
411
WEEK 4 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: NARRATIVE ESSAY – THE MOST MEMORABLE DAY OF MY LIFE
What is a Narrative Essay? A narrative essay is a type of writing that tells a story about a
personal experience. It has characters (usually including yourself), a setting, a plot with a
beginning, middle, and end, and often teaches a lesson or leaves an impression.
Features of a Narrative Essay:
1. First-Person Point of View:
 Uses "I," "me," "my," "we"
 Tells your personal experience
 Shares your thoughts and feelings
2. Chronological Order:
 Events arranged in time sequence
 What happened first, next, then, finally
 Clear progression of events
3. Descriptive Details:
 Vivid descriptions of people, places, events
 Sensory details (what you saw, heard, felt, smelled, tasted)
 Helps readers experience the story
4. Characters:
 You are the main character
 Other people involved in the story
 Character descriptions and actions
5. Setting:
412
 Where the story takes place
 When it happened
 Description of the environment
6. Plot:
 Introduction (background)
 Rising action (events building up)
 Climax (most important/exciting moment)
 Resolution (how it ended)
 Conclusion (reflection/lesson learned)
7. Dialogue (Optional):
 Conversations between characters
 Makes story more alive and interesting
 Shows character personalities
8. Emotional Connection:
 Express your feelings
 Show why the day was memorable
 Make readers feel your emotions
Structure of "The Most Memorable Day of My Life":
INTRODUCTION (Paragraph 1):
 Hook: Attention-grabbing opening sentence
 Brief introduction to the memorable day
 When and where it happened
413
 Why it was special
Example Opening: "I will never forget the 15th of August, 2024. It was the most memorable day
of my life, a day filled with joy, excitement, and unexpected surprises."
BODY PARAGRAPHS (2-4 paragraphs):
Paragraph 2 - Beginning of the Day:
 How the day started
 Your expectations or feelings
 Setting the scene
Paragraph 3 - Main Events:
 What happened (chronologically)
 Important details
 Actions and reactions
 Conversations (if any)
Paragraph 4 - The Climax:
 The most important/exciting moment
 Peak of the story
 Your feelings at that moment
CONCLUSION (Final Paragraph):
 How the day ended
 Your feelings afterward
 Reflection on why it was memorable
414
 Lesson learned or lasting impact
 Final thoughts
USEFUL EXPRESSIONS:
Time Connectors:
 First, second, third, finally
 At first, then, next, after that, later, eventually
 In the morning, in the afternoon, by evening
 Before, during, after
 Suddenly, immediately, meanwhile
 Soon, shortly after
Describing Feelings:
 I felt excited/nervous/happy/scared/proud
 My heart was pounding/racing
 I could hardly contain my excitement
 I was overwhelmed with joy/fear
 I felt as if I was dreaming
 Tears of joy filled my eyes
 A wave of happiness washed over me
Descriptive Adjectives:
 Wonderful, amazing, incredible, unforgettable
415
 Spectacular, magnificent, extraordinary
 Thrilling, exciting, nerve-wracking
 Surprising, unexpected, shocking
SAMPLE NARRATIVE ESSAY:
THE MOST MEMORABLE DAY OF MY LIFE
The 10th of December, 2024, is a date permanently etched in my memory. It was the day I won
first prize in the National Mathematics Competition, and it remains the most memorable day of
my life. That day brought me joy, pride, and a sense of achievement I had never experienced
before.
The day began like any other Saturday morning. I woke up early, feeling nervous and excited
about the competition. My mother prepared my favorite breakfast, jollof rice and chicken, hoping
it would calm my nerves. As we drove to the National Stadium where the competition was being
held, I kept reviewing formulas in my head. The stadium was packed with students from all over
Nigeria, and the atmosphere was electric with anticipation.
The competition started at 10 AM sharp. I sat at my designated seat, my hands trembling slightly
as I received the question paper. The first few questions were challenging, but I took a deep
breath and focused. Time seemed to fly as I worked through each problem carefully. Some
questions were extremely difficult, and I could see other students struggling as well. During the
break, I met a girl from Kano who was equally nervous, and we encouraged each other. When
the final round began, I gave it my absolute best, double-checking every answer before
submitting my paper.
The most thrilling moment came during the award ceremony. As the judges announced the third
and second positions, my heart pounded so loudly I thought everyone could hear it. Then I heard
my name called for first position! I couldn't believe my ears. I walked to the stage in a daze, my
legs feeling weak with excitement. As I received the trophy and a scholarship certificate worth
two million naira, I looked into the crowd and saw my parents. My mother was crying with joy,
416
and my father had the biggest smile on his face. The applause was deafening, and at that moment,
I felt like I was on top of the world.
That evening, our house was filled with relatives and friends who came to celebrate. Everyone
congratulated me, and I felt so proud and grateful. As I lay in bed that night, holding my trophy,
I reflected on the months of hard work and sacrifice that had led to this moment. That memorable
day taught me that dedication and perseverance truly pay off. It boosted my confidence and
confirmed my dream of becoming a mathematician. Years from now, when I look back on my
life, I know December 10th, 2024, will always shine brightly in my memory as the day that
changed my life forever.
WRITING TIPS:
DO:
 Choose a truly memorable experience
 Include specific details and descriptions
 Show emotions, don't just tell them
 Use dialogue to bring scenes alive
 Write in past tense
 Maintain chronological order
 Make your conclusion meaningful
 Proofread for errors
DON'T:
 Rush through important moments
 Use vague descriptions
 Jump around in time confusingly
417
 Forget to explain why it was memorable
 Make it too long or too short (aim for 250-350 words for JSS1)
 Copy someone else's experience
 Exaggerate unrealistically
Possible Topics for Practice:
 My first day at secondary school
 The day I won a competition
 When I received unexpected good news
 A special birthday celebration
 The day I overcame my fear
 Meeting someone important
 A family reunion or celebration
 An accident or scary experience
 The day I learned an important lesson
 A surprise that changed everything
EVALUATION:
1. Define a narrative essay and list five features of narrative writing
2. Explain the structure of a narrative essay (introduction, body, conclusion) and what each
part should contain
3. List ten time connectors used in narrative writing
4. Why is it important to include descriptive details and emotions in a narrative essay?
418
5. What is the difference between narrative and formal letter writing?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a complete narrative essay (250-300 words) titled "The Most Memorable Day of
My Life"
2. Identify the introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion in your essay by labeling them
3. List ten descriptive adjectives you could use to make a narrative essay more interesting
4. Write the opening paragraph (introduction) for a narrative essay titled "The Day I
Learned an Important Lesson"
5. Explain three things you should avoid when writing a narrative essay
419
WEEK 4 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: DRAMA – FEATURES (THEME, COSTUMES, AUDIENCE, ETC.);
IDENTIFYING FEATURES OF DRAMA IN A TEXT
What is Drama? Drama is a type of literature written to be performed by actors on a stage
before an audience. It tells a story through dialogue and action rather than narrative description.
Differences Between Drama and Other Literary Forms:
Drama Prose Poetry
Written to be performed Written to be read Written to be read/recited
Uses dialogue and action Uses narration and description Uses verse and imagery
Divided into acts and scenes Divided into chapters/paragraphs Divided into lines/stanzas
Needs actors and stage Needs only a reader Can be read or performed
FEATURES OF DRAMA:
1. THEME
Definition: The central idea, message, or lesson that the play conveys.
Common Themes in Drama:
 Love and relationships
 Conflict (internal or external)
 Good vs. evil
 Revenge and justice
 Ambition and power
 Betrayal and loyalty
 Social issues (corruption, poverty, discrimination)
420
 Family relationships
 Coming of age/growing up
 Death and mortality
Examples:
 In Macbeth, the theme is unchecked ambition leads to destruction
 In Romeo and Juliet, the theme is the power of love and the tragedy of family feuds
Multiple themes: Most plays have more than one theme.
2. PLOT
Definition: The sequence of events in the play; the story being told.
Structure of Plot:
 Exposition: Introduction of characters, setting, and background
 Rising Action: Complications and conflicts develop
 Climax: The turning point; most intense moment
 Falling Action: Events after the climax
 Resolution/Denouement: Conclusion; conflicts resolved
3. CHARACTERS
Definition: The people (or sometimes animals/objects) who perform the actions in the play.
Types of Characters:
 Protagonist: Main character; hero
 Antagonist: Character opposing the protagonist; villain
 Supporting characters: Help tell the story
421
 Minor characters: Brief appearances
Character Development:
 Revealed through dialogue
 Revealed through actions
 Revealed through stage directions
 Revealed through interaction with others
4. DIALOGUE
Definition: Conversations between characters; the words they speak.
Purpose of Dialogue:
 Advances the plot
 Reveals character personalities
 Creates conflict
 Provides information
 Shows relationships
Types:
 Dialogue: Conversation between two or more characters
 Monologue: Long speech by one character to others
 Soliloquy: Character speaks thoughts aloud when alone
 Aside: Character speaks to audience; other characters can't hear
5. STAGE DIRECTIONS
422
Definition: Instructions in the script telling actors how to move, speak, and behave; describing
the setting and atmosphere.
Written in: Italics or brackets [like this]
Types of Information:
 Character entrances and exits
 Movements and gestures
 Tone of voice and emotions
 Setting descriptions
 Props and lighting
 Sound effects
Examples:
 [Enter Lady Macbeth]
 [He speaks angrily]
 [Aside]
 [Thunder and lightning]
 [Exit]
6. ACT AND SCENE
Act: Major division of a play (like chapters in a book)
 Most plays have 1-5 acts
 Each act may contain multiple scenes
Scene: Smaller division within an act
 Usually represents a change in time or location
423
 Indicated as Scene 1, Scene 2, etc.
Format:
 Act I, Scene 1
 Act II, Scene 3
7. SETTING
Definition: Where and when the play takes place.
Includes:
 Time period (past, present, future)
 Location (country, city, specific place)
 Physical environment (palace, village, school)
 Atmosphere/mood
Revealed through:
 Stage directions
 Dialogue
 Props and scenery
8. CONFLICT
Definition: The struggle or problem that drives the plot.
Types of Conflict:
 Man vs. Man: Character against another character
 Man vs. Self: Internal struggle; character's own doubts/fears
 Man vs. Society: Character against social norms/rules
424
 Man vs. Nature: Character against natural forces
 Man vs. Fate/Supernatural: Character against destiny or supernatural forces
9. COSTUMES
Definition: Clothing worn by actors to represent their characters.
Purpose:
 Shows character's social status
 Indicates time period
 Reveals personality
 Distinguishes characters
 Shows profession or role
 Creates visual impact
Examples:
 Royal characters wear crowns and robes
 Soldiers wear uniforms
 Traditional plays use cultural attire
10. PROPS (Properties)
Definition: Objects used by actors during the performance.
Examples:
 Furniture (chairs, tables)
 Hand props (swords, books, cups)
 Decorative items (paintings, curtains)
425
Purpose:
 Make scenes realistic
 Help tell the story
 Reveal character information
11. AUDIENCE
Definition: The people watching the performance.
Importance:
 Drama is incomplete without an audience
 Actors perform for the audience
 Audience reactions affect performance
 Drama aims to entertain, educate, or move the audience
Audience Roles:
 Watch and listen
 Respond with emotions (laugh, cry, applaud)
 Interpret the message
 Appreciate the performance
12. LIGHTING AND SOUND
Lighting:
 Creates mood and atmosphere
 Indicates time of day
 Focuses attention
426
 Suggests weather
Sound:
 Music and songs
 Sound effects (thunder, bells, footsteps)
 Background noises
 Creates atmosphere
IDENTIFYING DRAMA IN A TEXT:
Textual Indicators:
1. Character Names Before Dialogue:
JOHN: Good morning, Mary!
MARY: Good morning! How are you?
2. Stage Directions in Brackets or Italics:
JOHN: [Walking toward her] I've been looking for you.
3. Act and Scene Divisions:
ACT I
SCENE 1
[A classroom in Government Secondary School]
4. No Narrative Description:
 Drama shows, doesn't tell
 No "he said" or "she thought"
427
 Everything revealed through dialogue and action
5. List of Characters (Dramatis Personae):
 Usually at the beginning
 Names and brief descriptions
SAMPLE DRAMA EXTRACT:
THE WISE DECISION
A One-Act Play
CHARACTERS:
CHIEF ADEBAYO - A wealthy village chief (60s)
AMINA - His daughter (16)
TAIWO - A poor but honest young man (20)
KUNLE - A rich but dishonest merchant (35)
ACT I
SCENE 1
[Chief Adebayo's compound. Morning. The Chief sits on a stool under a tree. Enter KUNLE
with gifts]
KUNLE: [Bowing low] Great Chief! I bring gifts to honor you.
CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Looking at the gifts] You are generous, Kunle. What brings you here?
KUNLE: I wish to marry your daughter, Amina. I am wealthy and can give her everything.
CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Thoughtfully] Amina must choose for herself.
428
[Enter TAIWO, empty-handed but confident]
TAIWO: Good morning, Chief. I've come to ask for Amina's hand in marriage.
KUNLE: [Laughing mockingly] You? You have nothing!
TAIWO: [Calmly] I have honesty, hard work, and true love for Amina.
CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Standing] Let Amina decide. [Calling] Amina!
[Enter AMINA]
AMINA: [Looking at both men] Father, I choose Taiwo. Wealth can be lost, but character lasts
forever.
CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Smiling] You have chosen wisely, my daughter.
[KUNLE exits angrily. TAIWO and AMINA embrace.]
[CURTAIN]
Features Identified:
 ✓ Character names before dialogue (KUNLE:, CHIEF:, etc.)
 ✓ Stage directions [in brackets]
 ✓ Act and Scene divisions
 ✓ Setting description
 ✓ List of characters
 ✓ Theme: True love and character over wealth
 ✓ Conflict: Taiwo vs. Kunle (Man vs. Man)
 ✓ Props: Gifts, stool
 ✓ Plot: Beginning, middle, end
429
 ✓ Costumes: Mentioned (Chief's traditional attire)
EVALUATION:
1. Define drama and state three ways it differs from prose and poetry
2. Explain five features of drama with examples
3. What are stage directions and why are they important in drama?
4. Identify the features of drama in this extract:
TEACHER: [Entering the classroom] Good morning, class!
STUDENTS: [Standing] Good morning, sir!
TEACHER: [Opening a book] Today we'll study verbs. Who can give an example?
BOLA: [Raising her hand] "Run" is a verb, sir.
5. Explain the difference between dialogue, monologue, and soliloquy
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a short drama (one scene) with at least three characters, showing all the features of
drama studied
2. Define and give examples of: (a) protagonist (b) antagonist (c) aside (d) soliloquy (e) act
3. Explain five types of conflict in drama with examples from any play you know
4. List ten props that might be used in a drama set in: (a) a palace (b) a school (c) a
marketplace
5. Why are costumes, lighting, and sound important in drama performance? Give three
reasons for each
430
WEEK 6 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /Ə/
The Schwa Sound /ə/
 The schwa /ə/ is the most common vowel sound in English
 It is a weak, unstressed, neutral vowel sound
 Pronounced with the mouth in a relaxed position
 The tongue is in the center of the mouth
 It sounds like a very short "uh" sound
 It ONLY occurs in unstressed syllables
Characteristics of the Schwa:
 Always found in unstressed syllables
 Very short and weak
 Neutral position of lips and tongue
 Many different letter combinations can produce this sound
 Native speakers often use it without realizing
Spelling Patterns for /ə/ Sound:
1. Letter "a" in unstressed syllables:
 about, above, again, ago, alone, along, away, awake
 China, drama, sofa, pizza, camera, banana (last 'a')
 woman, human, postman, policeman
 breakfast, elephant, elegant
2. Letter "e" in unstressed syllables:
431
 the, open, happen, golden, chicken, garden
 become, before, believe, beside
 moment, different, children, problem
 teacher, mother, father, brother, sister
3. Letter "i" in unstressed syllables:
 animal, definite, family, holiday
 pencil, fossil, pupil
 terrible, possible, horrible
4. Letter "o" in unstressed syllables:
 today, tomorrow, tonight, police, potato
 person, melon, lemon, pardon, lesson
 bacon, bottom, problem, freedom
 doctor, actor, sailor, visitor
5. Letter "u" in unstressed syllables:
 upon, suppose, support, success
 minus, circus, focus, campus
 difficult, particular, popular
6. Letter combinations:
 "ous" endings: famous, dangerous, nervous, curious
 "ion" endings: nation, action, question, position (first syllable)
 "er/or/ar" endings: teacher, doctor, sugar, collar
Important Rules:
432
The schwa appears in:
1. Unstressed syllables in multi-syllable words:
o a-GAIN (first 'a' is schwa)
o ba-NA-na (first and last 'a' are schwa)
o CAM-er-a (second and third vowels are schwa)
2. Function words (when unstressed in sentences):
o a, an, the, of, to, from, for, and, but
o Example: "a cup of tea" - both 'a' and 'o' in 'of' are schwa
3. Unstressed grammatical endings:
o -er: teacher, worker
o -or: actor, doctor
o -ar: sugar, collar
NOT Schwa When Stressed: When a syllable is stressed, the vowel is NOT pronounced as
schwa:
 about /ə-BOUT/ (first 'a' is schwa, second is /au/)
 banana /bə-NA-nə/ (middle 'a' is NOT schwa because it's stressed)
Practice Words - Identifying Schwa Position:
Two-syllable words:
 a-BOUT (first vowel = schwa)
 A-go (first vowel = schwa)
 to-DAY (first vowel = schwa)
433
 teach-ER (second vowel = schwa)
 doc-TOR (second vowel = schwa)
Three-syllable words:
 ba-NA-na (1st and 3rd = schwa)
 CAM-er-a (2nd and 3rd = schwa)
 el-e-PHANT (1st and 2nd = schwa)
 choc-o-LATE (2nd = schwa)
 to-MOR-row (1st and 3rd = schwa)
Function Words with Schwa: In connected speech, these small words often have schwa:
 a: "a book" /ə bʊk/
 the: "the man" /ðə mæn/ (before consonants)
 to: "to school" /tə skuːl/
 of: "cup of tea" /kʌp əv tiː/
 for: "for you" /fə juː/
 from: "from home" /frəm həʊm/
 and: "fish and chips" /fɪʃ ən tʃɪps/
Common Mistakes: Students often pronounce unstressed syllables too clearly:
 ✗ cam-ER-a (pronouncing 'e' as /e/)
 ✓ CAM-ər-ə (using schwa)
 ✗ choc-O-late (pronouncing 'o' as /o/)
434
 ✓ CHOC-ə-lət (using schwa)
Why Schwa is Important:
 Makes English sound natural and fluent
 Distinguishes stressed from unstressed syllables
 Essential for understanding native speakers
 Improves listening comprehension
 Makes your English pronunciation more authentic
Practice Sentences: (Schwa sounds underlined)
 A cup of tea and a piece of bread
 The teacher asked a question about the lesson
 My mother is a doctor at the hospital
 Children love chocolate and banana
 The police arrived in the afternoon
Articulation Tips:
 Relax your mouth completely
 Don't make any special shape with your lips
 Keep the sound very short and weak
 Don't stress the syllable
 Think of it as a very lazy vowel sound
435
EVALUATION:
1. What is the schwa sound /ə/ and where does it occur in words?
2. Identify the schwa sound(s) in these words: (a) banana (b) teacher (c) about (d) elephant
(e) camera
3. Why is the schwa sound important in English pronunciation?
4. Write ten words containing the schwa sound and mark where it appears
5. Explain why these vowels are NOT schwa sounds: (a) the 'i' in "machine" (b) the 'a' in
"father" (c) the middle 'a' in "banana"
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List twenty words containing the schwa sound and indicate which syllable(s) contain
schwa
2. Practice pronouncing these words with correct schwa placement: about, again, today,
police, problem, family, animal, chocolate, different, banana
3. Identify all the schwa sounds in this sentence: "The teacher gave a lesson about the
history of Africa"
4. Explain the difference between stressed and unstressed syllables using five examples
5. Write five sentences using at least three words with schwa sounds in each sentence,
underlining the words with schwa
436
WEEK 6 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: TENSES – PAST CONTINUOUS TENSE
What is the Past Continuous Tense? The Past Continuous Tense (also called Past Progressive
Tense) describes actions that were in progress at a specific time in the past, or actions that were
happening when another action interrupted them.
Formation of Past Continuous Tense:
Structure: Subject + was/were + verb-ing
Using was/were:
 I/He/She/It + was
 You/We/They + were
Examples:
 I was reading
 She was cooking
 They were playing
 We were studying
Forming the -ing Verb: (Same rules as Present Continuous)
1. Most verbs - add -ing: play → playing, read → reading
2. Verbs ending in -e - drop -e, add -ing: write → writing, make → making
3. One-syllable CVC verbs - double final consonant: run → running, sit → sitting
4. Verbs ending in -ie - change to -y: lie → lying, die → dying
Uses of Past Continuous Tense:
1. Actions in progress at a specific time in the past:
437
 I was sleeping at 10 PM last night
 They were studying at 7 AM yesterday
 She was cooking at 5 o'clock
 What were you doing at noon?
2. When one action interrupted another (with Simple Past):
 Structure: Past Continuous + when + Simple Past
 OR: Simple Past + while + Past Continuous
Examples:
 I was reading when the phone rang
 She was cooking when her friend arrived
 They were playing football when it started to rain
 While I was sleeping, someone knocked on the door
 He was driving when he saw the accident
Pattern:
 Longer action (background) = Past Continuous
 Shorter action (interruption) = Simple Past
3. Two or more simultaneous actions in the past:
 Structure: Past Continuous + while + Past Continuous
Examples:
 I was reading while she was cooking
 They were playing while we were studying
 He was watching TV while his sister was doing homework
 The children were singing while their teacher was playing the piano
438
4. Describing the scene/atmosphere in a story:
 The sun was shining brightly
 Birds were singing in the trees
 People were walking in the park
 A gentle breeze was blowing
5. Repeated actions in the past (showing annoyance with 'always'):
 He was always complaining about something
 She was always arriving late
 They were always fighting
Negative Form:
 Subject + was/were + not + verb-ing
Examples:
 I was not (wasn't) sleeping
 She was not (wasn't) cooking
 They were not (weren't) playing
 We were not (weren't) studying
Question Form:
 Was/Were + subject + verb-ing...?
Examples:
 Was I disturbing you?
 Was she cooking?
 Were they playing?
439
 Were you studying?
Wh- Questions:
 What/Where/Why/Who/How + was/were + subject + verb-ing?
Examples:
 What were you doing?
 Where was he going?
 Why were they crying?
 Who was she calling?
 How were you feeling?
Time Expressions Used with Past Continuous:
 At that time, at that moment
 At 5 o'clock yesterday, at noon
 All day yesterday, all morning, all night
 When, while, as
 This time last week/month/year
 Then, at the time
Past Continuous vs Simple Past:
Simple Past:
 Completed action: I read a book (finished)
 Short action: The phone rang
 Sequence: I woke up, brushed my teeth, and left
Past Continuous:
440
 Action in progress: I was reading a book (not finished, was doing)
 Longer background action: I was reading when the phone rang
 Simultaneous actions: I was reading while she was cooking
Common Combinations:
Past Continuous + when + Simple Past:
 I was walking when I met John
 They were eating when the power went off
 She was studying when her mother called
While + Past Continuous + Simple Past:
 While I was watching TV, the phone rang
 While they were playing, it started to rain
While + Past Continuous + Past Continuous:
 While I was cooking, she was cleaning
 While he was reading, they were playing
Verbs NOT Usually Used in Continuous Form: (Same as Present Continuous)
 State verbs: know, believe, understand, like, love, hate, want, need
 Possession verbs: have (possess), own, belong
 Sense verbs: see, hear, smell, taste (ability)
441
Use Simple Past instead:
 ✓ I knew the answer (Correct)
 ✗ I was knowing the answer (Wrong)
 ✓ She loved him (Correct)
 ✗ She was loving him (Wrong)
Common Mistakes to Avoid:
 ✗ I was went home (Correct: I was going home)
 ✗ She was cook (Correct: She was cooking)
 ✗ They was playing (Correct: They were playing)
 ✗ While I studied, he came (Correct: While I was studying, he came)
 ✗ I was knowing him (Correct: I knew him)
EVALUATION:
1. Define Past Continuous Tense and state four uses with examples
2. Write the Past Continuous form of these verbs: (a) I (read) (b) She (cook) (c) They (play)
(d) He (write) (e) We (study)
3. Combine these sentences using 'when': (a) I was sleeping. The phone rang. (b) She was
cooking. Her friend arrived. (c) They were playing. It rained.
4. Change to negative: (a) I was working (b) She was singing (c) They were dancing (d) He
was eating (e) We were watching TV
5. Change to questions: (a) You were listening (b) She was reading (c) They were playing
(d) He was sleeping (e) It was raining
442
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Explain the difference between Simple Past and Past Continuous Tense with five
examples each
2. Write ten sentences using Past Continuous Tense to describe what was happening in your
house yesterday evening
3. Combine these pairs using 'when' or 'while': (a) I (walk) to school. I (see) my teacher. (b)
She (read). Her sister (watch) TV. (c) They (play) football. It (start) raining. (d) He
(drive). He (hear) a loud noise. (e) We (have) dinner. The lights (go) off.
4. Fill in the blanks with correct forms: (a) At 8 PM yesterday, I _____ (watch) TV. (b)
While she _____ (cook), I _____ (clean) the house. (c) What _____ you _____ (do) when
I called? (d) They _____ (not/sleep) at midnight. (e) _____ it _____ (rain) when you left?
5. Correct the errors: (a) I was went to school (b) She was cook dinner (c) They was playing
(d) While I study, he came (e) Was you sleeping?
443
WEEK 6 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: READING BETWEEN THE LINES TO DETECT AUTHOR'S ATTITUDE;
VOCABULARY – CHILD LABOUR/TRAFFICKING
PART A: READING BETWEEN THE LINES TO DETECT AUTHOR'S ATTITUDE
What is Author's Attitude? Author's attitude is the writer's feelings, opinions, or stance toward
the subject matter or audience. It goes beyond mood (emotional tone) to reveal the author's
position—whether they support, oppose, question, or remain neutral about a topic.
Why Detect Author's Attitude?
 Helps identify bias or objectivity
 Reveals the author's purpose
 Helps evaluate credibility
 Enables critical reading
 Shows whether information is factual or opinion-based
Common Author Attitudes:
1. SUPPORTIVE/FAVORABLE:
 Author approves or agrees with the subject
 Uses positive language
 Highlights benefits and advantages
 Minimizes or ignores drawbacks
Word Clues: beneficial, excellent, commendable, admirable, impressive, effective, successful,
valuable, worthwhile
Example: "The new education policy is commendable. It addresses key challenges and provides
practical solutions that will benefit millions of students. The government deserves praise for this
progressive step."
444
Attitude: Supportive/Positive toward the policy
2. CRITICAL/OPPOSING:
 Author disagrees or disapproves
 Points out flaws and problems
 Uses negative language
 Questions effectiveness
Word Clues: problematic, flawed, inadequate, disappointing, questionable, unfortunate,
concerning, alarming, unacceptable
Example: "The new education policy is deeply flawed. It fails to address fundamental issues and
creates more problems than it solves. The government's approach is disappointing and shows
lack of proper consultation."
Attitude: Critical/Negative toward the policy
3. NEUTRAL/OBJECTIVE:
 Author presents information without taking sides
 Balanced presentation of facts
 No obvious emotional language
 Shows multiple perspectives
Word Clues: however, on the other hand, according to, statistics show, research indicates,
reports suggest
Example: "The new education policy includes several provisions. According to officials, it aims
to improve quality. Critics, however, question its implementation strategy. Time will reveal its
effectiveness."
445
Attitude: Neutral/Objective
4. SARCASTIC/MOCKING:
 Author ridicules or makes fun of the subject
 Says the opposite of what is meant
 Uses irony and exaggeration
 Dismissive tone
Word Clues: supposedly, allegedly, so-called, claims, apparently, of course (used ironically)
Example: "Of course, our 'efficient' government completed the project on time—only five years
late! The 'quality' work speaks for itself: the bridge collapsed within a week. Truly impressive!"
Attitude: Sarcastic/Mocking
5. CONCERNED/WORRIED:
 Author expresses worry about consequences
 Highlights potential dangers
 Urges caution or action
 Shows care about the issue
Word Clues: unfortunately, worrying, concerning, alarming, dangerous, risky, threatening,
urgent
Example: "The alarming increase in deforestation is deeply concerning. If urgent action is not
taken, the consequences will be devastating for future generations. We must act now before it's
too late."
Attitude: Concerned/Worried
446
6. SKEPTICAL/DOUBTFUL:
 Author questions truth or validity
 Expresses uncertainty
 Challenges claims
 Demands evidence
Word Clues: allegedly, supposedly, claims, questionable, unclear, doubtful, unproven, debatable
Example: "The company claims their product is '100% natural,' but this assertion is
questionable. No independent testing has verified these claims. Consumers should be skeptical of
such unproven marketing statements."
Attitude: Skeptical/Doubtful
How to Detect Author's Attitude:
1. Analyze Word Choice (Diction):
 Positive vs. negative words
 Strong vs. mild words
 Objective vs. subjective language
2. Look for Qualifying Words:
 "Supposedly," "allegedly" = skepticism
 "Obviously," "clearly" = strong conviction
 "Perhaps," "possibly" = uncertainty
3. Examine What's Emphasized:
 What points get most attention?
 What is minimized or ignored?
447
 What examples are chosen?
4. Note Facts vs. Opinions:
 Objective: presents facts without judgment
 Subjective: includes personal views
5. Consider Omissions:
 What information is left out?
 Which perspectives are ignored?
 What questions remain unanswered?
6. Identify Rhetorical Devices:
 Questions (suggest doubt or engagement)
 Repetition (emphasizes importance)
 Comparisons (show preference)
Practice Passage:
"Child labour continues to rob millions of children of their childhood, education, and future.
While some argue it helps poor families survive, this view ignores the severe physical and
psychological harm inflicted on innocent children. How can any society justify exploiting its
most vulnerable members? The practice must be condemned unequivocally and eliminated
immediately."
Analysis:
 Attitude: Strongly opposed/Critical
 Clues:
o "rob," "harm inflicted," "exploiting" (negative words)
o "must be condemned," "eliminated immediately" (strong stance)
448
o Rhetorical question shows disapproval
o Dismisses opposing view ("this view ignores")
o Emotional appeal ("innocent children")
PART B: VOCABULARY – CHILD LABOUR/TRAFFICKING
Definitions:
Child Labour: The exploitation of children through any form of work that deprives them of
childhood, education, and dignity, and is harmful to their physical and mental development.
Child Trafficking: The recruitment, transportation, transfer, or harboring of children for
exploitation, including forced labor, sexual exploitation, or illegal activities.
KEY VOCABULARY:
Related to Child Labour:
1. General Terms:
 Exploitation: Unfair treatment for someone's benefit
 Minor: Person under 18 years old
 Hazardous work: Dangerous jobs harmful to children
 Forced labour: Work done against one's will
 Bonded labour: Work to repay a debt
 Domestic servitude: Working as house help in harsh conditions
 Street vending: Selling goods on streets
 Agriculture work: Farming, fishing, plantation work
 Manufacturing: Factory work, production
449
 Child labourer: Child who works inappropriately
2. Effects on Children:
 Deprivation: Being denied basic needs/rights
 Abuse: Physical, emotional, or sexual mistreatment
 Malnutrition: Poor nutrition, hunger
 Illiteracy: Inability to read or write
 Trauma: Deep psychological injury
 Exploitation: Being used unfairly for others' gain
 Vulnerability: Being easily hurt or influenced
 Stunted growth: Impaired physical development
3. Rights Violations:
 Right to education: Learning opportunity denied
 Right to play: Childhood activities denied
 Right to protection: Safety not provided
 Right to health: Medical care denied
 Child rights: Legal entitlements of children
 Violation: Breaking of rules/rights
Related to Child Trafficking:
1. Process Terms:
 Recruitment: Finding and engaging victims
 Transportation: Moving victims from one place to another
 Harboring: Hiding or sheltering victims
450
 Trafficker: Person who trafficks children
 Victim: Person trafficked
 Syndicate: Criminal organization
 Network: Connected group of traffickers
2. Types of Exploitation:
 Sexual exploitation: Forcing into prostitution/pornography
 Forced begging: Making children beg for money
 Organ trafficking: Stealing body organs
 Drug trafficking: Using children to transport drugs
 Armed conflict: Forcing children to fight
 Slavery: Ownership and control of a person
 Servitude: Being a servant/slave
3. Prevention & Protection:
 Awareness: Knowledge about the problem
 Prevention: Stopping before it happens
 Rescue: Saving victims
 Rehabilitation: Helping victims recover
 Prosecution: Legal action against traffickers
 Legislation: Laws against trafficking
 NGO (Non-Governmental Organization): Groups helping victims
 UNICEF: UN Children's Fund
451
 Helpline: Phone number for reporting/getting help
4. Legal Terms:
 Illegal: Against the law
 Criminal: Person who breaks the law
 Prosecution: Taking legal action
 Penalty: Punishment for crime
 Imprisonment: Jail sentence
 Fine: Money paid as punishment
 Conviction: Finding guilty in court
Warning Signs of Child Trafficking:
 Child living with non-relatives
 Working excessive hours
 Signs of abuse or fear
 Not attending school
 Restricted movement
 Malnourished or poorly clothed
 No possession of documents
Organizations Fighting Child Labour/Trafficking:
 UNICEF
 ILO (International Labour Organization)
452
 Save the Children
 NAPTIP (National Agency for the Prohibition of Trafficking in Persons) - Nigeria
 Anti-Slavery International
 Child Rights International Network
Important Statistics/Facts:
 Minimum working age in most countries: 14-16 years
 Millions of children worldwide affected
 More prevalent in developing countries
 Girls and boys both affected
 Often linked to poverty
EVALUATION:
1. Explain what "author's attitude" means and why it's important to detect it when reading
2. Read this passage and identify the author's attitude with evidence: "Technology has
revolutionized education in remarkable ways. Students now have access to unlimited
information, interactive learning tools, and global collaboration opportunities. These
advances deserve celebration."
3. Define child labour and child trafficking, stating three differences between them
4. List five harmful effects of child labour on children
5. Explain five vocabulary terms related to child trafficking
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Read this passage and answer the questions: "Some business owners claim child labour is
necessary for survival. What a convenient excuse for exploitation! These 'employers'
453
conveniently ignore international laws and children's rights. Meanwhile, innocent

unacceptable." Questions: (a) What is the author's attitude? (b) Identify three
words/phrases that reveal this attitude (c) What rhetorical device does the author use? (d)
Is the author objective or subjective? (e) Does the author support or oppose child labour?

Prosecution (d) Trafficking (e) Vulnerability

WEEK 6 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY – ROAD TRANSPORTATION IS SAFER THAN
AIR
What is an Argumentative Essay? An argumentative essay is a type of writing where you take
a position on a topic and support it with reasons, evidence, and examples to convince readers that
your viewpoint is correct or valid. You also address opposing views and explain why your
position is better.
Characteristics of Argumentative Essay:
1. Clear Position/Thesis:
 States your stand clearly
 Usually in the introduction
 Tells readers what you believe
2. Logical Arguments:
 Presents reasons supporting your position
 Uses facts, statistics, examples
 Each paragraph develops one main point
3. Evidence and Examples:
 Concrete proof supporting claims
 Real-life examples
 Statistics, expert opinions, research
 Personal experiences (if relevant)
4. Counter-Arguments:
 Acknowledges opposing views
 Shows you've considered other perspectives
455
 Refutes (argues against) them with evidence
5. Persuasive Language:
 Convincing tone
 Strong vocabulary
 Logical connectors
 Confident but respectful
6. Formal Tone:
 Objective language
 Third person (mostly)
 Professional vocabulary
 No slang or contractions
Structure of Argumentative Essay:
INTRODUCTION (Paragraph 1):
 Hook: Attention-grabbing opening
 Background: Brief context about the topic
 Thesis Statement: Your clear position
 Preview: Mention main arguments briefly
BODY PARAGRAPHS (3-4 paragraphs):
Paragraph 2 - First Argument:
 Topic sentence stating your first point
 Explanation and elaboration
 Evidence/examples supporting it
 Concluding sentence linking to thesis
456
Paragraph 3 - Second Argument:
 Topic sentence stating your second point
 Explanation and elaboration
 Evidence/examples supporting it
 Concluding sentence
Paragraph 4 - Third Argument:
 Topic sentence stating your third point
 Explanation and elaboration
 Evidence/examples supporting it
 Concluding sentence
Paragraph 5 - Counter-Argument & Refutation:
 Acknowledge opposing view
 Present it fairly
 Refute it with evidence
 Show why your position is stronger
CONCLUSION (Final Paragraph):
 Restate thesis (in different words)
 Summarize main arguments
 Final thought or call to action
 Strong closing statement
USEFUL LANGUAGE FOR ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAYS:
Stating Your Position:
457
 I strongly believe that...
 It is evident that...
 There is no doubt that...
 This essay argues that...
 The evidence clearly shows...
Introducing Arguments:
 First/Firstly, To begin with, First of all
 Second/Secondly, Furthermore, Moreover, Additionally
 Third/Thirdly, Finally, Lastly
 Another reason is...
 Most importantly...
Providing Evidence:
 For example, For instance
 According to research/statistics...
 Studies show that...
 Evidence suggests...
 This is illustrated by...
 Consider the case of...
Introducing Counter-Arguments:
 Some people argue/believe that...
 Opponents claim that...
 Critics suggest that...
 It may be argued that...
458
 On the other hand...
 Admittedly...
 While it is true that...
Refuting Counter-Arguments:
 However, this view fails to consider...
 Nevertheless, this argument is flawed because...
 Despite this claim, evidence shows...
 This overlooks the fact that...
 While this may be partially true, ...
 On the contrary...
Concluding:
 In conclusion, To conclude, To sum up
 Therefore, Thus, Hence
 It is clear that...
 Based on the evidence presented...
 For these reasons...
SAMPLE ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY:
ROAD TRANSPORTATION IS SAFER THAN AIR TRANSPORTATION
When planning a journey, the question of safety often arises. While many consider air travel the
safest mode of transportation, a closer examination reveals that road transportation offers
superior safety advantages. Road travel provides better control, accessibility to emergency
services, and survivability in accidents. This essay argues that road transportation is safer than air
transportation based on practical, statistical, and logical considerations.
459
Firstly, road transportation offers greater personal control and immediate response to danger.
When traveling by road, drivers can stop instantly if they notice a problem, change routes to
avoid hazards, or pull over during emergencies. If a vehicle develops mechanical issues, the
driver can safely park and seek help. In contrast, airplane passengers are entirely dependent on
pilots and have no control over the aircraft. If problems occur mid-flight, there is limited room
for maneuvering or emergency stops. For example, if a car's brakes fail, the driver can use the
handbrake and gradually reduce speed. However, if an aircraft experiences engine failure at high
altitude, the consequences are far more severe with limited options for immediate intervention.
Secondly, road accidents are more survivable than air crashes. Statistics show that passengers
involved in road accidents have a much higher survival rate compared to aviation accidents.
Most road accidents occur at relatively low speeds within cities, allowing passengers to survive
with injuries that can be treated. Modern cars are equipped with safety features such as airbags,
seatbelts, and crumple zones designed to protect occupants during collisions. When road
accidents happen, emergency services can quickly reach the scene to provide medical assistance.
Conversely, airplane crashes typically occur from great heights at high speeds, leaving little
chance of survival. Even with safety protocols, the impact of an aircraft hitting the ground from
thousands of feet is usually catastrophic. Furthermore, rescue operations for plane crashes are
complicated by remote locations, fire hazards, and the difficulty of accessing survivors quickly.
Moreover, road transportation provides better access to emergency assistance and medical care.
If someone falls ill while traveling by road, the vehicle can immediately divert to the nearest
hospital. Emergency vehicles can reach road accident victims within minutes in most areas,
significantly improving survival chances. Ambulances, fire services, and police can respond
quickly to road incidents. On the other hand, if a medical emergency occurs during a flight,
passengers must wait until the plane lands, which could take hours depending on the flight
duration and availability of nearby airports. The limited medical equipment on board aircraft
cannot match the resources available at hospitals. In remote areas where planes may crash,
rescue teams may take hours or even days to reach survivors, if any.
Admittedly, some argue that air travel has fewer accidents per mile traveled and that modern
aircraft technology makes flying extremely safe. Statistics show that flying has a lower accident
rate compared to driving. However, this argument fails to consider the severity and survivability
460
of accidents. While airplane accidents may be rarer, they are almost always fatal when they
occur. A person may be involved in several minor road accidents during their lifetime and
survive them all, whereas a single plane crash is likely to be fatal. Furthermore, road safety
continues to improve with better vehicle technology, improved road infrastructure, and stricter
traffic laws. The mere fact that airplane accidents are less frequent does not make them safer
when we consider the devastating outcomes and lack of survival chances.
In conclusion, road transportation is safer than air transportation when considering control,
survivability, and access to emergency services. The ability to stop immediately, higher survival
rates in accidents, and quick access to medical help make road travel the safer option. While air
travel may have statistical advantages in accident frequency, the catastrophic nature of plane
crashes and limited survival possibilities cannot be ignored. For these reasons, road
transportation remains the safer choice for travelers who prioritize survival and immediate access
to assistance in emergencies. When safety is measured not just by accident frequency but by
survivability and control, the evidence clearly favors road transportation.
KEY POINTS FOR THIS TOPIC:
Arguments Supporting Road Transportation:
1. Greater control (can stop, change routes, respond to danger)
2. Higher survival rates in accidents
3. Better access to emergency services
4. Modern safety features (airbags, seatbelts)
5. Lower-speed collisions (more survivable)
6. Immediate medical assistance available
7. Can avoid dangerous situations
8. Ability to escape quickly from vehicles
461
Arguments Supporting Air Transportation (Counter-arguments):
1. Fewer accidents per mile traveled
2. Highly trained pilots
3. Strict safety regulations
4. Advanced technology
5. Regular maintenance checks
6. Statistics show it's statistically safer
How to Refute Air Transportation Arguments:
1. While accidents are rare, they're almost always fatal
2. Technology failures still occur with devastating effects
3. No control for passengers in emergencies
4. Difficult rescue operations
5. High-altitude crashes are non-survivable
6. Statistics don't account for severity
WRITING TIPS:
DO:
 State your position clearly in the introduction
 Use strong, convincing evidence
 Address counter-arguments fairly
 Use formal language
 Provide specific examples
462
 Connect paragraphs logically
 Conclude powerfully
 Proofread carefully
DON'T:
 Use emotional language without evidence
 Ignore opposing viewpoints
 Make unsupported claims
 Use informal language or slang
 Write in first person excessively
 Make personal attacks
 Contradict yourself
 Leave arguments unsupported
Other Possible Topics for Practice:
 School uniforms should be compulsory
 Social media does more harm than good
 Homework should be abolished
 Sports are more important than academics
 Television is harmful to children
 Boarding schools are better than day schools
EVALUATION:
463
1. Define argumentative essay and state five characteristics
2. Explain the structure of an argumentative essay (introduction, body, conclusion) and
what each part should contain
3. What is a counter-argument and why is it important in argumentative writing?
4. List five useful phrases for: (a) stating your position (b) introducing counter-arguments
5. What is the difference between argumentative and narrative essays?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a complete argumentative essay (300-350 words) on the topic: "Road
Transportation is Safer than Air Transportation"
2. Identify and label: (a) thesis statement (b) three main arguments (c) counter-argument (d)
refutation (e) conclusion in your essay
3. Write the introduction paragraph for an argumentative essay on: "Students Should Wear

WEEK 6 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXT
What is a Prose Text? Prose is writing that is structured in sentences and paragraphs, following
natural speech patterns. Unlike poetry (which uses verses) or drama (which uses dialogue and
stage directions), prose tells stories through continuous narrative.
Types of Prose:
1. Fiction (Imaginative/Made-up stories):
 Novels (long stories)
 Novellas (medium-length stories)
 Short stories (brief stories)
2. Non-Fiction (True/Factual):
 Biographies
 Autobiographies
 Essays
 Articles
For JSS1, recommended prose texts are usually:
 Short novels or novellas
 Collection of short stories
 Young adult fiction
 African literature
 Adventure/moral stories
465
COMMON RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXTS FOR JSS1:
1. "The Drummer Boy" by Cyprian Ekwensi
2. "The Passport of Mallam Ilia" by Cyprian Ekwensi
3. "Tales from the Forest" by Eno Okoko
4. "Chike and the River" by Chinua Achebe
5. "The Last Days at Forcados High School" by A.H. Mohammed
6. "Mr. Johnson" by Joyce Cary
7. African Folktales Collection
8. "Beautiful Feathers" by Cyprian Ekwensi
(Note: The specific text varies by school and state. Students should use the text assigned by their
school.)
HOW TO STUDY A PROSE TEXT:
1. UNDERSTAND THE STORY:
Plot:
 What happens in the story?
 Beginning (exposition)
 Middle (rising action and climax)
 End (resolution)
 Main events in chronological order
Setting:
 Where does the story take place? (location)
 When does it happen? (time period)
466
 What is the physical and social environment?
2. KNOW THE CHARACTERS:
Main Characters:
 Who is the protagonist (hero)?
 Who is the antagonist (villain/opponent)?
 What are their personalities?
 What motivates them?
 How do they change?
Character Types:
 Round characters: Complex, realistic, develop
 Flat characters: Simple, one-dimensional, don't change
 Dynamic characters: Change throughout the story
 Static characters: Remain the same
Character Analysis Questions:
 Physical appearance?
 Personality traits?
 Relationships with others?
 Role in the story?
 What they want/desire?
 Strengths and weaknesses?
3. IDENTIFY THEMES:
467
Theme: Central message or lesson
Common Themes in Prose:
 Good vs. evil
 Growing up/coming of age
 Courage and bravery
 Honesty and integrity
 Friendship and loyalty
 Family relationships
 Overcoming challenges
 Justice and injustice
 Love and sacrifice
 Tradition vs. modernity
 Corruption
 Ambition
Questions to Find Theme:
 What lesson does the story teach?
 What is the author trying to say about life?
 What universal truths are explored?
 What message is conveyed?
4. UNDERSTAND THE CONFLICT:
Types of Conflict:
 Man vs. Man: Character against another character
468
 Man vs. Self: Internal struggle within character
 Man vs. Society: Character against social norms/rules
 Man vs. Nature: Character against natural forces
 Man vs. Fate/Supernatural: Character against destiny or supernatural forces
5. ANALYZE STYLE:
Point of View:
 First Person: "I" narrator (character tells story)
 Third Person Limited: Narrator knows one character's thoughts
 Third Person Omniscient: Narrator knows everything
 Second Person: "You" (rare)
Language:
 Simple or complex?
 Descriptive or straightforward?
 Use of dialogue?
 Figurative language?
Tone:
 Serious, humorous, sad, hopeful?
 Author's attitude toward subject?
6. EXAMINE LITERARY DEVICES:
Common devices in prose:
 Foreshadowing: Hints about future events
469
 Flashback: Scene from the past
 Symbolism: Objects representing ideas
 Irony: Opposite of what's expected
 Imagery: Descriptive language appealing to senses
 Metaphor/Simile: Comparisons
HOW TO WRITE ABOUT YOUR PROSE TEXT:
Character Question Example: "Describe the character of [name] in the novel."
Answer Structure:
1. Introduction: Who is the character?
2. Physical description
3. Personality traits with examples
4. Role in the story
5. Relationships with others
6. Character development/change
7. Conclusion: Significance
Theme Question Example: "What is the main theme of the novel?"
Answer Structure:
1. State the theme clearly
2. Explain what it means
3. Give examples from the story
4. Show how different events relate to theme
5. Explain the lesson/message
470
6. Conclusion: Why it's important
Plot Question Example: "Narrate the story/Give the plot summary."
Answer Structure:
1. Introduction: Title, author, brief overview
2. Beginning: Introduce characters, setting, initial situation
3. Middle: Main events, complications, conflicts
4. Climax: Turning point, most exciting moment
5. End: Resolution, how conflicts are resolved
6. Conclusion: Final situation
Relationship Question Example: "Discuss the relationship between [Character A] and
[Character B]."
Answer Structure:
1. Introduction: Who are they?
2. How they meet/know each other
3. Nature of relationship (friends, enemies, family)
4. How they interact
5. Conflicts or harmony between them
6. How relationship develops/changes
7. Conclusion: Significance of relationship
STUDY TIPS:
Reading the Text:
1. Read the entire text at least twice
471
2. Take notes while reading
3. Mark important passages
4. Note unfamiliar words and find meanings
5. Pay attention to chapter titles
6. Identify turning points
Character Study:
1. Create character charts
2. Note physical descriptions
3. Track character actions
4. Quote significant dialogues
5. Trace character development
Plot Study:
1. Create plot timeline
2. Identify cause-and-effect relationships
3. Note chapter summaries
4. Identify key events
5. Understand story structure
Theme Study:
1. Identify recurring ideas
2. Note moral lessons
3. Connect events to themes
4. Understand author's message
5. Relate themes to real life
472
Exam Preparation:
1. Read sample questions
2. Practice essay answers
3. Memorize key quotes
4. Understand major events
5. Know character names and spellings
6. Review chapter by chapter
7. Discuss with classmates
8. Create study guides
COMMON EXAM QUESTIONS ON PROSE:
Plot/Summary Questions:
 Give an account of...
 Narrate the story of...
 What happened when...?
 Describe the events leading to...
Character Questions:
 Describe the character of...
 Compare and contrast [two characters]
 Discuss the role of...
 How does [character] change?
Theme Questions:
 What is the main theme?
473
 Discuss the theme of...
 What lessons are taught?
 What message does the author convey?
Relationship Questions:
 Discuss the relationship between...
 How do [characters] interact?
 Describe the friendship/conflict between...
Literary Device Questions:
 Identify examples of...
 Discuss the author's use of...
 How does the author create suspense?
Personal Response Questions:
 What did you learn from...?
 Which character do you admire? Why?
 What is your favorite part?
 How would you have handled...?
EVALUATION:
