WEEK 8 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL CONTRASTS: /ɒ/ VS /Ɔ:/ (HOT/PORT); /ɑ:/ VS /æ/ (BAG/PART)
CONTRAST 1: /ɒ/ vs /ɔ:/
Short Vowel /ɒ/ (as in "hot"):
 Short, quick sound
 Lips slightly rounded
 Jaw moderately open
 Examples: hot, pot, got, box, top, dog, shop, clock, doctor, coffee
Long Vowel /ɔ:/ (as in "port"):
 Long, prolonged sound
 Lips more rounded and protruded
 Jaw less open
 Examples: port, ought, thought, bought, caught, ball, call, tall, warn, morning
Practice Pairs:
 cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔ:/
 not /ɒ/ - nought /ɔ:/
 spot /ɒ/ - sport /ɔ:/
 pot /ɒ/ - port /ɔ:/
 shot /ɒ/ - short /ɔ:/
Key Difference: /ɒ/ is SHORT with relaxed lips; /ɔ:/ is LONG with rounded, protruded lips.
CONTRAST 2: /ɑ:/ vs /æ/
Long Vowel /ɑ:/ (as in "part"):
476
 Long, open sound
 Mouth wide open
 Tongue low and flat
 Examples: part, car, far, hard, start, father, class, grass, past, heart
Short Vowel /æ/ (as in "bag"):
 Short sound
 Mouth moderately open
 Tongue higher than /ɑ:/
 Examples: bag, cat, hat, man, hand, bad, sad, apple, happy, family
Practice Pairs:
 cap /æ/ - carp /ɑ:/
 hat /æ/ - heart /ɑ:/
 bad /æ/ - bard /ɑ:/
 pat /æ/ - part /ɑ:/
 mad /æ/ - marred /ɑ:/
Key Difference: /ɑ:/ is LONG with wide-open mouth; /æ/ is SHORT with less mouth opening.
Common Mistakes:
 Confusing "hot" /ɒ/ with "heart" /ɑ:/
 Pronouncing "bad" /æ/ like "bard" /ɑ:/
 Using /ɒ/ when /ɔ:/ is needed: "caught" not "cot"
EVALUATION:
1. Explain the difference between /ɒ/ and /ɔ:/ sounds with three examples each
477
2. Pronounce and identify the correct vowel sound: (a) hot (b) port (c) shot (d) caught (e)
spot
3. Explain the difference between /ɑ:/ and /æ/ sounds with three examples each
4. Write five word pairs showing /ɑ:/ vs /æ/ contrast
5. Correct the pronunciation: A student says "I bought a hot dog" but pronounces both
words with /ɒ/. Which word should use /ɔ:/?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List ten words each for: (a) /ɒ/ sound (b) /ɔ:/ sound (c) /ɑ:/ sound (d) /æ/ sound
2. Write five sentences using word pairs that contrast /ɒ/ and /ɔ:/
3. Write five sentences using word pairs that contrast /ɑ:/ and /æ/
4. Practice reading aloud: "The hot pot was caught in the short spot. My father had a bad
heart, so he parked his car far from the grass."
5. Create a chart showing the four vowel sounds with their characteristics (mouth position,
length, lip shape)
478
WEEK 8 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: MAKING SENTENCES WITH SIMPLE PRESENT & SIMPLE PAST TENSES
REVIEW: SIMPLE PRESENT TENSE
Form: Subject + base verb (+ s/es for he/she/it)
Uses:
1. Habits/Routines: I wake up at 6 AM
2. General truths: The sun rises in the east
3. Permanent situations: She lives in Lagos
Time Markers: always, usually, often, sometimes, every day/week
REVIEW: SIMPLE PAST TENSE
Form: Subject + past verb (regular: -ed; irregular: special forms)
Uses:
1. Completed past actions: I visited Abuja yesterday
2. Past habits: He played football every Saturday (when young)
3. Series of past actions: She woke up, ate, and left
Time Markers: yesterday, last week/month/year, ago, in 2020
MAKING CORRECT SENTENCES:
Simple Present Examples:
 My sister teaches Mathematics
 We go to church every Sunday
 The train leaves at 8 PM
479
 He doesn't eat meat
 Do you speak French?
Simple Past Examples:
 My sister taught Mathematics last year
 We went to church last Sunday
 The train left at 8 PM yesterday
 He didn't eat meat
 Did you speak to him?
COMMON ERRORS TO AVOID:
Error 1 - Mixing Tenses:
 ✗ Yesterday, I go to school (mixing time marker with wrong tense)
 ✓ Yesterday, I went to school
Error 2 - Wrong Verb Forms:
 ✗ She don't go (wrong auxiliary with third person)
 ✓ She doesn't go
 ✗ He goed (wrong past form)
 ✓ He went
Error 3 - Forgetting -s/-es in Present:
 ✗ He go to school
 ✓ He goes to school
Error 4 - Using Past Form in Negatives/Questions:
480
 ✗ Did you went? / He didn't came
 ✓ Did you go? / He didn't come
SENTENCE PATTERNS:
Affirmative:
 Present: Subject + verb(s/es)
 Past: Subject + past verb
Negative:
 Present: Subject + don't/doesn't + base verb
 Past: Subject + didn't + base verb
Interrogative:
 Present: Do/Does + subject + base verb?
 Past: Did + subject + base verb?
PRACTICE TRANSFORMATIONS:
Present to Past:
 I eat rice → I ate rice yesterday
 She plays tennis → She played tennis last week
 They are happy → They were happy
Past to Present:
 I went to school → I go to school every day
 She wrote a letter → She writes letters often
481
 They were students → They are students
TIME SIGNAL MATCHING:
Present Signals: now, every day, always, usually, on Mondays Past Signals: yesterday, last
night, ago, in 1999, this morning (if past)
Correct Usage:
 I always wake up early (present - habit)
 I woke up early yesterday (past - completed)
EVALUATION:
1. Write five sentences in Simple Present Tense about your daily routine
2. Convert these to Simple Past Tense: (a) I play football (b) She eats rice (c) They go to
school (d) He is happy (e) We have a car
3. Correct the errors: (a) Yesterday I go home (b) She don't like it (c) Did he went there? (d)
He teach every day (e) They was happy
4. Fill in with correct tense: (a) Every Sunday, we _____ (go) to church. (b) Last Sunday,
we _____ (go) to church. (c) She _____ (not/eat) meat now. (d) She _____ (not/eat) meat
yesterday.
5. Write sentences matching these time signals: (a) always (b) yesterday (c) every morning
(d) last week (e) usually
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write ten sentences in Simple Present Tense using different subjects and verbs
2. Write ten sentences in Simple Past Tense about what you did last weekend
482
3. Transform: Change these present sentences to past (add appropriate time signals): (a) I
study English (b) She cooks dinner (c) They play games (d) We watch TV (e) He goes to
work
4. Create sentences using: (a) always + present (b) yesterday + past (c) every day + present
(d) last year + past (e) usually + present
5. Write a short paragraph (8 sentences) mixing Simple Present (your habits) and Simple
Past (what you did yesterday)
483
WEEK 8 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: READING TO IDENTIFY OVERALL IMPRESSION FROM AUTHOR'S
STYLE
What is Author's Style? Author's style is the unique way a writer uses language, structure, and
literary techniques to express ideas. It creates an overall impression or feeling in the reader.
ELEMENTS OF AUTHOR'S STYLE:
1. WORD CHOICE (Diction):
 Simple/Plain: Common, everyday words - easy to understand
o Example: "The man was sad"
 Complex/Elevated: Difficult, sophisticated words - formal
o Example: "The gentleman was melancholic"
 Informal/Colloquial: Casual, conversational language
o Example: "The guy was really down"
2. SENTENCE STRUCTURE:
 Short sentences: Create tension, urgency, simplicity
o Example: "He ran. The door slammed. Silence."
 Long sentences: Create flow, detail, complexity
o Example: "He ran swiftly through the winding streets, his heart pounding loudly
as the door slammed behind him, leaving only eerie silence."
3. TONE:
 Formal vs. Informal
 Serious vs. Humorous
 Objective vs. Subjective
484
 Optimistic vs. Pessimistic
4. FIGURATIVE LANGUAGE:
 Heavy use: Poetic, imaginative style
 Minimal use: Straightforward, direct style
 Types: Metaphors, similes, personification, imagery
5. DESCRIPTIVE DETAIL:
 Highly descriptive: Rich imagery, sensory details
 Minimally descriptive: Focuses on action, dialogue
6. NARRATIVE TECHNIQUE:
 First person (I): Personal, intimate
 Third person (he/she): Distant, objective
 Present tense: Immediate, urgent
 Past tense: Reflective, storytelling
OVERALL IMPRESSIONS FROM STYLE:
Example 1 - Simple, Direct Style: "The boy walked to school. He was late. His teacher was
angry. He sat down quietly."
Impression: Straightforward, childlike, factual, no-nonsense
Example 2 - Descriptive, Flowery Style: "The young lad strolled leisurely along the winding
path to the magnificent educational institution, his heart heavy with the knowledge of his
tardiness. The stern educator, her countenance displaying unmistakable displeasure, cast a
disapproving glance as he settled into his seat with cautious movements."
Impression: Formal, elaborate, sophisticated, slow-paced
485
Example 3 - Emotional, Dramatic Style: "The boy's heart pounded as he raced toward school.
Late again! What would the teacher say? Her face, twisted with anger, made him freeze. He
crept to his seat, trembling."
Impression: Tense, emotional, suspenseful, engaging
Example 4 - Humorous, Casual Style: "So there I was, sprinting to school like my life
depended on it—spoiler alert: it kinda did. The teacher's face? Picture a volcano about to erupt.
I slithered into my seat, trying to become invisible."
Impression: Funny, relatable, conversational, lighthearted
HOW TO IDENTIFY OVERALL IMPRESSION:
Step 1: Read the entire passage Step 2: Notice word choices (simple/complex?) Step 3: Observe
sentence length (short/long?) Step 4: Identify tone (serious/funny/sad?) Step 5: Check for
figurative language Step 6: Consider the feeling it creates Step 7: Summarize the overall
impression
PRACTICE PASSAGE:
"The forest whispered ancient secrets. Shadows danced between towering trees as moonlight
filtered through emerald leaves. A gentle breeze carried the sweet scent of jasmine, while distant
owls sang their haunting melodies. In this magical realm, time seemed to stand still."
Style Analysis:
 Diction: Poetic, descriptive words (whispered, ancient, towering, haunting)
 Sentences: Flowing, moderate length
 Figurative Language: Personification (forest whispered, shadows danced)
486
 Imagery: Strong sensory details (sight, smell, sound)
 Overall Impression: Magical, peaceful, mysterious, dreamlike, enchanting
DIFFERENT STYLES FOR DIFFERENT PURPOSES:
Scientific/Technical Writing:
 Clear, precise, objective
 Formal vocabulary
 Factual presentation
 Example: "Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius at sea level."
Literary/Creative Writing:
 Imaginative, expressive
 Figurative language
 Emotional appeal
 Example: "The water danced and bubbled joyously over the flames."
Journalistic Writing:
 Informative, concise
 Active voice
 Factual with some opinion
 Example: "The water reached boiling point during the experiment."
EVALUATION:
1. Define author's style and list five elements that contribute to it
487
2. Read this passage and identify the overall impression: "Thunder roared. Lightning struck.
The child screamed. Rain poured. Darkness everywhere."
3. What impression does this style create? "The magnificent sunset painted the sky in
brilliant hues of orange, pink, and purple, while gentle waves whispered softly against
the golden shore."
4. Explain how sentence structure affects the overall impression of a text
5. Compare these two sentences and explain which creates a more formal impression: (a)
"He went home" (b) "The gentleman proceeded to his residence"
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Rewrite this sentence in three different styles (simple, formal, dramatic): "The girl cried
when she lost her bag"
2. Read any short story or article and identify: (a) Type of diction used (b) Sentence
structure pattern (c) Tone (d) Use of figurative language (e) Overall impression created
3. Write two paragraphs (5 sentences each) about a rainy day: First paragraph - simple,
direct style; Second paragraph - descriptive, emotional style
4. Analyze this passage for style: "Bang! The door flew open. A stranger stood there.
Nobody moved. Everyone stared." What impression does it create?
5. Explain how an author's word choice affects the reader's overall impression. Give three
examples.

WEEK 8 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: EXPOSITORY ESSAY – HIV/AIDS
What is an Expository Essay? An expository essay explains, informs, or describes a topic
clearly and factually. It presents information objectively without trying to persuade or tell a story.
Purpose: To educate the reader about a subject
Characteristics:
 Factual and informative
 Clear and logical organization
 Objective tone (no personal opinions)
 Uses facts, statistics, examples
 Third-person point of view
 Formal language
STRUCTURE:
INTRODUCTION:
 Hook (interesting opening)
 Background information
 Thesis statement (main idea)
BODY (3-4 paragraphs):
 Each paragraph covers one main point
 Topic sentence
 Supporting details, facts, examples
 Smooth transitions
489
CONCLUSION:
 Restate main points
 Summary
 Final thought
TOPIC: HIV/AIDS
Key Information to Include:
Definition:
 HIV: Human Immunodeficiency Virus (virus that attacks immune system)
 AIDS: Acquired Immune Deficiency Syndrome (advanced stage of HIV)
How HIV is Transmitted:
 Unprotected sexual contact
 Sharing needles/syringes
 Mother to child (pregnancy, childbirth, breastfeeding)
 Blood transfusion (with infected blood)
 Contact with infected blood/body fluids
How HIV is NOT Transmitted:
 Casual contact (hugging, shaking hands)
 Sharing food, utensils
 Mosquito bites
 Toilet seats
 Swimming pools
 Airborne transmission
490
Symptoms:
 Early: Flu-like illness, fever, fatigue, swollen glands
 Later: Weight loss, persistent cough, night sweats
 AIDS stage: Severe infections, opportunistic diseases
Prevention:
 Abstinence
 Safe sex (condom use)
 Avoid sharing needles
 Screen blood before transfusion
 Antiretroviral drugs for pregnant women
 Avoid contact with infected blood
Treatment:
 Antiretroviral Therapy (ART)
 No cure, but treatment controls virus
 Regular medical monitoring
 Healthy lifestyle
Effects:
 Weakened immune system
 Susceptibility to infections
 Social stigma
 Economic burden
 Psychological impact
Important Facts:
491
 HIV can remain undetected for years
 Anyone can be infected
 People with HIV can live long, healthy lives with treatment
 Stigma and discrimination are wrong
 Testing is important
USEFUL VOCABULARY:
 Immune system, virus, infection, transmission
 Antiretroviral therapy (ART), treatment, prevention
 Symptoms, diagnosis, testing, screening
 Stigma, discrimination, awareness
 Counseling, support, care
TRANSITIONS FOR EXPOSITORY WRITING:
 First, Second, Third, Finally
 Additionally, Furthermore, Moreover
 For example, For instance, Such as
 In addition, Also, As well as
 Therefore, Consequently, As a result
 In conclusion, To summarize, In summary
SAMPLE EXPOSITORY ESSAY:
HIV/AIDS: UNDERSTANDING THE DISEASE
492
HIV/AIDS is one of the most serious health challenges facing the world today. Understanding
this disease is crucial for prevention and reducing stigma. HIV (Human Immunodeficiency Virus)
is a virus that attacks the body's immune system, while AIDS (Acquired Immune Deficiency
Syndrome) is the advanced stage of HIV infection. This essay examines the transmission,
prevention, and treatment of HIV/AIDS.
HIV is transmitted through specific body fluids including blood, semen, vaginal fluids, and
breast milk. The primary modes of transmission are unprotected sexual contact with an infected
person, sharing needles or syringes, mother-to-child transmission during pregnancy or
breastfeeding, and transfusion of infected blood. However, it is important to understand that HIV
cannot be transmitted through casual contact such as hugging, shaking hands, sharing food, or
using the same toilet. Mosquitoes also cannot transmit the virus. This knowledge helps reduce
unnecessary fear and discrimination.
Prevention of HIV/AIDS involves several strategies. The most effective method is abstinence
from sexual activity. For those who are sexually active, consistent and correct use of condoms
significantly reduces transmission risk. Avoiding the sharing of needles and ensuring blood is
screened before transfusion are also critical preventive measures. Pregnant women living with
HIV can take antiretroviral drugs to prevent transmission to their babies. Additionally, regular
testing allows for early detection and treatment, preventing further spread.
Although there is no cure for HIV/AIDS, treatment has advanced significantly. Antiretroviral
Therapy (ART) helps people living with HIV control the virus, maintain their immune system,
and live long, healthy lives. With proper treatment and medical care, HIV-positive individuals
can prevent progression to AIDS. Treatment also reduces the risk of transmitting the virus to
others. Regular medical monitoring, adherence to medication, and a healthy lifestyle are essential
components of HIV management.
In conclusion, HIV/AIDS is a serious but manageable disease. Understanding how it is
transmitted helps in prevention efforts, while knowing about available treatment offers hope to
those affected. Society must combat stigma and discrimination through education and awareness.
With continued research, prevention efforts, and compassionate support, we can work toward
reducing the impact of HIV/AIDS on individuals and communities worldwide.
493
WRITING TIPS:
DO:
 Use factual, accurate information
 Organize ideas logically
 Use formal, objective language
 Include specific details and examples
 Use transitions between paragraphs
 Cite sources if using statistics
 Proofread carefully
DON'T:
 Include personal opinions or arguments
 Use emotional or biased language
 Write in first person (I, we)
 Include unverified information
 Make judgmental statements
 Use informal language
 Deviate from the main topic
EVALUATION:
1. Define expository essay and state four characteristics that distinguish it from other essay
types
2. What is the difference between HIV and AIDS?
494
3. List five ways HIV can be transmitted and five ways it CANNOT be transmitted
4. Explain the structure of an expository essay (introduction, body, conclusion)
5. Why is objective tone important in expository writing?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a complete expository essay (300-350 words) on the topic: "HIV/AIDS:
Understanding the Disease"
2. Create an outline for an expository essay about HIV/AIDS with: (a) Thesis statement (b)
Three main body points (c) Conclusion summary
3. List ten facts about HIV/AIDS that should be included in an expository essay
4. Write the introduction paragraph for an expository essay titled "Preventing HIV/AIDS"
5. Explain three differences between expository essay and argumentative essay
495
WEEK 8 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: MYTHS AND LEGENDS – COMPARING MYTHS AND LEGENDS;
RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT
PART A: MYTHS AND LEGENDS
DEFINITIONS:
Myth: A traditional story explaining natural phenomena, religious beliefs, or the origins of a
people/culture. Features gods, supernatural beings, and creation stories. Often considered sacred.
Legend: A traditional story about real or believed-to-be-real people and historical events, passed
down through generations. May have some basis in fact but includes exaggerated or fictional
elements.
KEY DIFFERENCES:
Aspect Myth Legend
Main Characters Gods, deities, supernatural beings Heroes, historical figures, humans
Setting Distant past, before time began Specific historical period/place
Purpose Explain origins, natural phenomena Celebrate heroes, teach lessons
Truth Status Sacred, symbolic truth May have historical basis
Time Timeless, ancient past Identifiable time period
Focus Creation, cosmic events Heroic deeds, adventures
CHARACTERISTICS OF MYTHS:
1. Features:
 Gods and goddesses as main characters
496
 Explains creation of world/humans
 Explains natural phenomena (thunder, rain, seasons)
 Religious/spiritual significance
 Set in primordial time
 Supernatural powers and magic
2. Common Themes:
 Creation of the universe
 Origin of death
 Why things are the way they are
 Relationship between gods and humans
 Good vs. evil
3. Examples:
 Greek: Zeus, Prometheus, Pandora's Box
 African: How Death Came to the World, Olodumare and creation
 Egyptian: Ra (sun god), Osiris and Isis
 Norse: Odin, Thor, creation of Yggdrasil
4. Purpose:
 Explain mysteries of life
 Teach religious beliefs
 Provide moral guidance
 Establish cultural identity
CHARACTERISTICS OF LEGENDS:
497
1. Features:
 Heroes and warriors as main characters
 Based on historical events (exaggerated)
 Set in identifiable times/places
 Contains some factual elements
 Extraordinary human achievements
 May include some supernatural elements
2. Common Themes:
 Heroic deeds and bravery
 Good triumphing over evil
 Love and sacrifice
 Founding of nations/cities
 Great battles and conquests
3. Examples:
 African: Sundiata (Mali), Queen Amina of Zaria, Shaka Zulu, Moremi of Ile-Ife
 European: King Arthur, Robin Hood, El Cid
 Asian: Mulan, Rama and Sita
 American: Johnny Appleseed, Paul Bunyan
4. Purpose:
 Preserve history (embellished)
 Inspire heroism
 Build national/cultural pride
 Teach values and courage
498
SIMILARITIES BETWEEN MYTHS AND LEGENDS:
1. Both are traditional stories passed orally
2. Both teach moral lessons
3. Both may include supernatural elements
4. Both preserve culture and heritage
5. Both feature extraordinary events
6. Both reflect values of their society
7. Both have been retold over generations
8. Both can be found in all cultures
AFRICAN EXAMPLES:
Myths:
 How the first man and woman were created
 Why the sky is far from earth
 Origin of death (Mawu-Lisa story)
 Why animals don't speak anymore
 The creation story of Olodumare (Yoruba)
Legends:
 Sundiata Keita (founder of Mali Empire)
 Queen Moremi of Ile-Ife (saved her people)
 Queen Amina of Zaria (warrior queen)
 Shaka Zulu (great warrior king)
499
 Bayajida and the founding of Hausa states
SAMPLE COMPARISON:
Myth Example: "The Creation of Humans"
 Gods molded humans from clay
 Breathed life into them
 Gave them intelligence
 Set rules for them to follow
 Analysis: Explains origin of humanity, features gods, no historical basis
Legend Example: "Queen Moremi"
 Real ruler of Ile-Ife
 Sacrificed herself to save her people
 Discovered enemy secrets
 Became a hero
 Analysis: Historical figure, identifiable place/time, heroic deeds

 Main messages

 Social issues addressed

 Time and place
 Social/cultural context

 Dialogue and language
 Stage directions

 Symbols and motifs

 Describe the setting of Act II

WEEK 9 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: VOWEL CONTRASTS: /e/ VS /ɜ:/ (EGG/BURN, PET/SHIRT)
Short Vowel /e/ (as in "egg"):
 Short, quick sound
 Tongue in mid position
 Lips spread slightly
 Jaw moderately open
 Examples: egg, pen, bed, red, pet, set, get, end, men, best, desk, help, tell, fetch
Long Vowel /ɜ:/ (as in "burn"):
 Long, prolonged sound
 Tongue in central position
 Lips slightly spread
 Tense sound
 Examples: burn, turn, bird, word, hurt, first, learn, earth, girl, work, heard, church, serve,
term
KEY DIFFERENCE:
 /e/ is SHORT with mid-tongue position
 /ɜ:/ is LONG with central tongue position, more tense
PRACTICE PAIRS:
 bed /e/ - bird /ɜ:/
 pen /e/ - burn /ɜ:/
 pet /e/ - pert /ɜ:/
504
 get /e/ - girt /ɜ:/
 end /e/ - earned /ɜ:/
 debt /e/ - dirt /ɜ:/
 dead /e/ - dread /e/ - third /ɜ:/
Practice Sentences:
 The pet bird hurt its head
 Get the first pen from the desk
 The girl in the red shirt learned well
 Ten men worked on the earth
 The nurse kept the medicine on the bed
Articulation Tips:
For /e/:
 Keep sound short
 Relax jaw moderately
 Don't prolong the sound
For /ɜ:/:
 Prolong the sound
 Keep tongue in center
 Make it tense and steady
Common Mistakes:
 Pronouncing "bird" as "bed"
 Using /e/ when /ɜ:/ is needed in "work" → sounds like "weck"
 Saying "girl" with /e/ instead of /ɜ:/
505
EVALUATION:
1. Explain the difference between /e/ and /ɜ:/ sounds with their characteristics
2. Identify the vowel sounds: (a) pen (b) burn (c) pet (d) shirt (e) get (f) first
3. Write five word pairs contrasting /e/ and /ɜ:/ sounds
4. Which sound is used in these words: (a) help (b) turn (c) desk (d) church (e) end?
5. Pronounce and differentiate: bed/bird, pen/burn, get/girl, end/earned
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List fifteen words each containing: (a) /e/ sound (b) /ɜ:/ sound
2. Write ten sentences using word pairs that contrast /e/ and /ɜ:/
3. Practice reading aloud: "The nurse in the red dress served ten men. She kept the first pen
on the desk and learned her lesson well"
4. Circle words with /ɜ:/ sound: bed, word, pet, girl, help, turn, desk, heard, fetch, church
5. Explain why correct pronunciation of these contrasts is important in communication
506
WEEK 9 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: FUTURE TENSE – SIMPLE FUTURE (WILL, SHALL)
WHAT IS SIMPLE FUTURE TENSE?
Simple Future Tense expresses actions that will happen in the future.
FORMATION:
Structure: Subject + will/shall + base verb
Using Will and Shall:
Modern Usage:
 Will: Used for all persons (I, you, he, she, it, we, they)
 Shall: Rarely used in modern English, mainly with I/we in formal contexts
Examples:
 I will go / I shall go
 You will come
 He/She/It will arrive
 We will study / We shall study
 They will play
USES OF SIMPLE FUTURE:
1. Predictions about the future:
 It will rain tomorrow
 She will become a doctor
 They will win the match
 Nigeria will develop
507
2. Spontaneous decisions (made at the moment of speaking):
 I'm thirsty. I'll get some water
 The phone is ringing. I'll answer it
 I'll help you with that
3. Promises:
 I will help you tomorrow
 We will come to your party
 He will pay you back
4. Offers:
 I'll carry your bag
 Shall I open the window?
 Will you have some tea?
5. Threats/Warnings:
 You will regret this
 I'll report you to the principal
 They will punish you
6. Facts about the future:
 The sun will rise tomorrow
 She will be sixteen next year
 School will resume in September
NEGATIVE FORM:
Structure: Subject + will not (won't) / shall not (shan't) + base verb
508
Examples:
 I will not (won't) go
 She will not eat meat
 They won't come tomorrow
 We shall not (shan't) forget (formal)
QUESTION FORM:
Structure: Will/Shall + subject + base verb...?
Examples:
 Will you come tomorrow?
 Will she pass the exam?
 Shall we go now? (suggestion)
 Shall I help you? (offer)
 Will they arrive on time?
Wh- Questions:
 What will you do tomorrow?
 Where will she go?
 When will they arrive?
 Who will help us?
 How will you travel?
TIME EXPRESSIONS USED WITH FUTURE:
 Tomorrow, tonight
509
 Next (week/month/year/Monday)
 In the future, someday, soon
 Later, eventually
 In + time period (in two days, in a month)
 This evening/weekend
CONTRACTIONS:
 I will = I'll
 You will = You'll
 He will = He'll
 She will = She'll
 We will = We'll
 They will = They'll
 Will not = Won't
 Shall not = Shan't (rare)
DIFFERENCE: WILL vs SHALL
Traditional Rules (less common now):
 Shall with I/we for simple future: I shall go
 Will with you/he/she/it/they: They will come
 Will with I/we for determination: I will succeed (promise)
 Shall with you/he/she/it/they for commands: You shall obey
Modern Usage:
510
 Will is used for all persons in most contexts
 Shall is mainly used in:
o Formal British English
o Questions for suggestions: Shall we dance?
o Legal/official documents
o Offers: Shall I help you?
COMMON MISTAKES:
Error 1 - Using base verb alone:
 ✗ I go tomorrow
 ✓ I will go tomorrow
Error 2 - Adding -s/-es to verb:
 ✗ She will goes
 ✓ She will go
Error 3 - Using past form:
 ✗ He will went
 ✓ He will go
Error 4 - Double future markers:
 ✗ I will going to come
 ✓ I will come / I'm going to come
Error 5 - Wrong negative form:
 ✗ He will not goes
511
 ✓ He will not go
BE GOING TO vs WILL:
Be going to:
 Planned intentions: I'm going to study medicine
 Evidence-based predictions: Look at those clouds! It's going to rain
Will:
 Spontaneous decisions: I'll have tea (deciding now)
 Uncertain predictions: It will probably rain
 Promises: I'll call you tomorrow
PRACTICE EXAMPLES:
Affirmative:
 I will visit my grandmother next week
 She will write the exam tomorrow
 They will travel to Abuja soon
Negative:
 I won't forget your birthday
 He won't come to the party
 We won't be late
Interrogative:
 Will you help me?
512
 When will they arrive?
 Shall we start the meeting?
EVALUATION:
1. Define Simple Future Tense and explain how it is formed
2. Write five sentences in Simple Future Tense about your plans for next week
3. Change to negative: (a) I will go (b) She will help (c) They will come (d) He will study (e)
We will travel
4. Change to questions: (a) You will attend (b) She will cook (c) They will play (d) It will
rain (e) He will arrive
5. Correct the errors: (a) I will goes (b) She will went (c) Will he comes? (d) They will not
comes (e) I go tomorrow
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write ten sentences using Simple Future Tense with different subjects and verbs
2. Write five predictions about the future using "will"
3. Write five promises or offers using "will" or "shall"
4. Fill in with correct future forms: (a) Tomorrow, I _____ (visit) my friend. (b) She _____
(not/come) to school. (c) _____ you _____ (help) me? (d) What _____ they _____ (do)
next? (e) We _____ (travel) next month.
5. Change these Present Tense sentences to Future Tense: (a) I study English (b) She cooks
dinner (c) They play football (d) He goes to school (e) We watch TV
513
WEEK 9 - READING AND COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: READING TO INTERPRET DIAGRAMS, MAPS, SKETCHES;
VOCABULARY – WORD FORMATION (PREFIXES)
PART A: INTERPRETING DIAGRAMS, MAPS, AND SKETCHES
WHY INTERPRET VISUAL INFORMATION?
 Not all information is in text form
 Visual aids summarize complex data
 Maps show locations and directions
 Diagrams explain processes/relationships
 Sketches illustrate concepts
1. READING DIAGRAMS
What is a Diagram? A visual representation showing how something works, relationships
between parts, or a process.
Types:
 Flow charts (show processes/steps)
 Venn diagrams (show relationships/comparisons)
 Labeled diagrams (show parts of objects)
 Pie charts (show percentages/proportions)
 Bar graphs (compare quantities)
 Tree diagrams (show hierarchies/classifications)
How to Read Diagrams:
1. Read the title/heading
514
2. Identify what is being shown
3. Look at labels and keys
4. Follow arrows or connecting lines
5. Understand relationships between parts
6. Check for legends or explanations
7. Draw conclusions from the information
Example - Simple Flow Chart:
Start → Wake up → Brush teeth → Bath → Breakfast → Go to school → End
Questions from this diagram:
 What is the first activity? (Wake up)
 What comes after brushing teeth? (Bath)
 What is the final activity? (Go to school)
2. READING MAPS
What is a Map? A visual representation of an area showing locations, directions, distances, and
features.
Map Components:
 Title: What area is shown
 Key/Legend: Symbols and their meanings
 Scale: Distance representation
 Compass/Direction: N, S, E, W
 Labels: Names of places
 Symbols: Icons representing features
515
Common Map Symbols:
 Roads (lines)
 Rivers (wavy blue lines)
 Buildings (rectangles/squares)
 Trees/Forests (green areas)
 Mountains (triangles/brown areas)
 Hospitals (H or +)
 Schools (flag/building symbol)
How to Read Maps:
1. Check the title
2. Find the key/legend
3. Identify cardinal directions (N, S, E, W)
4. Locate specific places
5. Determine relationships (distance, direction)
6. Use scale to estimate distances
Sample Questions from Maps:
 What is located north of the school?
 How many roads lead to the market?
 What is the symbol for hospital?
 Which direction is the river from the church?
 What is between the market and school?
3. READING SKETCHES
516
What is a Sketch? A simple, quick drawing showing basic features of an object, place, or
person.
Types:
 Object sketches (show items)
 Scene sketches (show places/events)
 Layout sketches (show arrangements)
 Anatomical sketches (show body parts)
How to Read Sketches:
1. Identify what is drawn
2. Look for labels
3. Note important features
4. Understand proportions/sizes
5. Identify relationships between parts
TIPS FOR INTERPRETING VISUALS:
Before Reading:
 Read the title first
 Check for instructions
 Look at the whole visual
During Reading:
 Pay attention to labels
 Follow arrows and lines
 Note colors and symbols
517
 Read all text provided
After Reading:
 Summarize what you learned
 Answer questions carefully
 Check your understanding
PART B: WORD FORMATION - PREFIXES
What is a Prefix? A group of letters added to the BEGINNING of a word to change its meaning
or create a new word.
Structure: Prefix + Root Word = New Word
COMMON PREFIXES AND MEANINGS:
1. UN- (not, opposite of):
 happy → unhappy (not happy)
 lucky → unlucky (not lucky)
 able → unable (not able)
 fair → unfair (not fair)
 Other examples: untrue, unkind, uncertain, unfold, unlock, unwrap
2. RE- (again, back):
 write → rewrite (write again)
 read → reread (read again)
 build → rebuild (build again)
 turn → return (turn back)
518
 Other examples: replay, retell, review, redo, refill, reconsider
3. DIS- (not, opposite of, reverse):
 agree → disagree (not agree)
 like → dislike (not like)
 appear → disappear (not appear)
 obey → disobey (not obey)
 Other examples: dishonest, discomfort, disconnect, discover, disqualify
4. MIS- (wrongly, badly):
 use → misuse (use wrongly)
 spell → misspell (spell wrongly)
 understand → misunderstand (understand wrongly)
 lead → mislead (lead wrongly)
 Other examples: misplace, misbehave, miscount, misjudge, misread
5. PRE- (before):
 view → preview (view before)
 pay → prepay (pay before)
 school → preschool (before school)
 fix → prefix (fix before)
 Other examples: prehistoric, preview, precaution, predict, prepare
6. POST- (after):
 war → postwar (after war)
 pone → postpone (put after)
 date → postdate (date after)
519
 Other examples: postgraduate, postscript, postpone
7. OVER- (too much, excessive):
 eat → overeat (eat too much)
 work → overwork (work too much)
 sleep → oversleep (sleep too much)
 charge → overcharge (charge too much)
 Other examples: overflow, overload, overcome, overlook, overseas
8. UNDER- (too little, below):
 pay → underpay (pay too little)
 estimate → underestimate (estimate too low)
 stand → understand (stand under → comprehend)
 Other examples: underground, underwater, underline, undergraduate
9. SUB- (under, below):
 marine → submarine (under sea)
 way → subway (underground way)
 title → subtitle (title below)
 Other examples: subway, subheading, subdivide, substandard
10. INTER- (between, among):
 national → international (between nations)
 act → interact (act between)
 change → interchange (change between)
 Other examples: internet, interview, interrupt, intermediate
11. TRANS- (across, beyond):
520
 port → transport (carry across)
 late → translate (carry meaning across languages)
 Other examples: transfer, transform, transmit, transparent
12. BI- (two):
 cycle → bicycle (two wheels)
 lingual → bilingual (two languages)
 weekly → bi-weekly (every two weeks)
 Other examples: bilateral, biannual
13. TRI- (three):
 angle → triangle (three angles)
 cycle → tricycle (three wheels)
 Other examples: tripod, trilogy, triple
14. MULTI- (many):
 colored → multicolored (many colors)
 purpose → multipurpose (many purposes)
 Other examples: multiply, multimedia, multinational
15. ANTI- (against):
 social → antisocial (against social)
 clockwise → anticlockwise (against clockwise)
 Other examples: antibiotic, antiseptic, anti-war
FORMING NEW WORDS:
Example Process:
521
 Base word: happy
 Add prefix un-: unhappy (not happy)
 Can add suffix too: unhappiness (state of not being happy)
Practice:
 appear → disappear → disappearance
 use → misuse → misused
 do → redo → redone
PREFIX RULES:
1. Prefix changes meaning, not word class
o happy (adjective) → unhappy (adjective)
o Some exceptions exist
2. No spelling changes usually
o un + necessary = unnecessary (double n)
o dis + satisfied = dissatisfied (double s)
3. Some prefixes have hyphens
o ex-president, self-made, co-operate (sometimes)
EVALUATION:
1. Explain what diagrams, maps, and sketches are and why we need to interpret them
2. List five components of a map and explain their purposes
3. Define prefix and give five examples with their meanings
522
4. Form new words by adding appropriate prefixes to: (a) happy (b) write (c) agree (d)
understand (e) national
5. Using the prefix "un-", "re-", or "dis-", create three words each and use them in sentences

1. Draw a simple map of your school compound showing at least five locations. Include a
title, key, and direction indicator
2. Look at any diagram in your textbook and write five sentences explaining what it shows
3. Complete this table:
Prefix Meaning Example New Word Meaning

place (d) behave (e) historic

morning (at least 6 steps)
WEEK 9 - COMPOSITION
ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY: ARRANGING IDEAS IN LOGICAL SEQUENCE
WHAT IS AN ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY?
An argumentative essay presents a position on a topic with reasons and evidence to convince
readers.
523
Basic Structure:
 Introduction (thesis statement)
 Body paragraphs (arguments with evidence)
 Counter-argument and refutation
 Conclusion
WHY LOGICAL SEQUENCE MATTERS
 Makes essay easy to follow
 Strengthens arguments
 Convinces readers effectively
 Prevents confusion
Remember: Poor Sequence = Weak Essay | Good Sequence = Strong Essay
METHODS OF ARRANGING IDEAS
1. ORDER OF IMPORTANCE
Climactic (Weakest to Strongest) - Most common
 Build up to strongest argument
 Save best for last
 Creates memorable ending
Example: "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory"
1. Saves time in morning (weakest)
2. Reduces peer pressure (medium)
3. Promotes equality (strongest)
2. CHRONOLOGICAL ORDER
524
Arrange by time sequence for historical topics or processes.
3. PROBLEM-SOLUTION ORDER
 Present problem
 Explain seriousness
 Propose solution
 Explain benefits
4. CAUSE-AND-EFFECT ORDER
Show what leads to what, or start with result and trace back to causes.
5. COMPARE-AND-CONTRAST ORDER
 Block Method: Discuss all of A, then all of B
 Point-by-Point: Compare A and B for each point
BODY PARAGRAPH STRUCTURE
Each paragraph should:
1. Have ONE main idea
2. Start with topic sentence
3. Provide evidence
4. Explain how evidence supports argument
5. Link to next paragraph
IMPORTANT TRANSITION WORDS
Adding: Additionally, Furthermore, Moreover, Also
Order: First, Second, Third, Finally, Next
525
Importance: Most importantly, Significantly, Above all
Contrast: However, Nevertheless, On the other hand, Although
Cause/Effect: Therefore, Thus, Consequently, As a result
Examples: For example, For instance, Such as
Concluding: In conclusion, To summarize, Finally, Overall
STEPS TO ARRANGE IDEAS
1. BRAINSTORM: List all arguments and evidence
2. EVALUATE: Identify strongest arguments, remove weak ones
3. ORDER: Arrange (usually climactic order is best)
4. OUTLINE: Create detailed outline
5. CONNECT: Add transitions for smooth flow
SAMPLE OUTLINE
Topic: "Students Should Wear School Uniforms"
I. Introduction: Thesis statement
II. Argument 1 (Weakest): Save time
III. Argument 2 (Medium): Reduce peer pressure
IV. Argument 3 (Strongest): Promote equality
V. Counter-argument: Address opposing view
VI. Conclusion: Restate thesis and summarize
COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID
526
❌ Jumping between topics randomly
❌ Placing strongest argument first (usually)
❌ No transitions between ideas
❌ Multiple ideas in one paragraph
❌ Misplaced counter-argument
✓ Follow logical progression
✓ Build to strongest point
✓ Use smooth transitions
✓ One paragraph = one point
✓ Counter-argument before conclusion
EVALUATION
1. Why is logical sequence important in argumentative essays?
2. Describe climactic and problem-solution arrangements
3. When do you use climactic vs anticlimactic order?
4. List five transition words and their functions
5. Create an outline for "Mobile Phones Should Be Banned in Schools"
ASSIGNMENT
1. Arrange in climactic order: (a) Improves vocabulary (b) Develops imagination (c)
Increases knowledge (d) Enhances critical thinking. Explain your choice.
527
2. Write an outline for "Sports Are More Important Than Academics" with thesis, three
arguments, counter-argument, and conclusion.
3. Add transitions: "School uniforms are beneficial. They save money. Parents don't buy
expensive clothes. Uniforms promote equality. Rich and poor students look the same."
4. Identify the arrangement method: Problem introduction → effects → solutions →
benefits.
5. Create a 5-point checklist for reviewing logical sequence in your essay.
528
WEEK 9 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
MYTHS AND LEGENDS: FEATURES (MAGIC, SUPERSTITION, ETC.); DRAMA
TEXT
PART A: FEATURES OF MYTHS AND LEGENDS
FEATURES OF MYTHS
1. SUPERNATURAL BEINGS
o Gods and goddesses with special powers
o Immortal characters controlling natural forces
o Examples: Zeus (Greek), Olodumare (Yoruba), Ra (Egyptian)
2. MAGIC AND SUPERNATURAL POWERS
o Transformation/shape-shifting
o Flying, invisibility, creating from nothing
o Controlling weather, healing, immortality
3. CREATION AND ORIGINS
o Explains how world/humans were created
o Origin of natural phenomena (thunder, rain, day/night)
o How death came to the world
4. TIMELESSNESS
o Set in ancient/primordial times
o "In the beginning," "Long before time"
o No specific dates
5. SACRED/RELIGIOUS SIGNIFICANCE
529
o Explains religious beliefs
o Moral and spiritual lessons
o Basis for rituals and ceremonies
6. UNIVERSAL THEMES
o Good vs. evil
o Creation and destruction
o Life, death, and rebirth
o Punishment and reward
7. SYMBOLIC ELEMENTS
o Objects representing ideas (tree of life)
o Animals symbolizing qualities
o Significant numbers and colors
8. EXPLANATORY PURPOSE
o Answers "why" questions
o Explains mysteries of existence
o Gives meaning to natural phenomena
FEATURES OF LEGENDS
1. HEROIC CHARACTERS
o Brave warriors and leaders
o Exceptional human abilities
o Historical or believed-to-be-real people
o Examples: Sundiata, Queen Amina, Shaka Zulu
530
2. HISTORICAL BASIS
o Connected to real events
o Identifiable time periods and places
o Exaggerated but rooted in fact
3. EXTRAORDINARY DEEDS
o Impossible achievements
o Superhuman strength or intelligence
o Founding cities/nations, defeating enemies
4. EXAGGERATION
o Feats enlarged beyond reality
o Numbers inflated (killed 1000 enemies)
o Makes heroes larger than life
5. MORAL LESSONS
o Teaches courage, loyalty, honesty
o Shows consequences of actions
o Warns against vices
6. CULTURAL PRIDE
o Celebrates national heroes
o Preserves historical memory
o Builds identity and unity
7. SOME SUPERNATURAL ELEMENTS
o Magic weapons or objects
o Prophetic dreams
531
o Divine assistance (less than myths)
8. ORAL TRANSMISSION
o Passed through storytelling
o Multiple versions exist
o Performed by griots/storytellers
SHARED FEATURES (Myths & Legends)
 Oral tradition passed down through generations
 Teach moral, social, cultural values
 Cultural preservation
 Archetypal characters (heroes, villains, helpers)
 Symbolic meaning
 Dramatic structure
 Entertainment value
SUPERSTITION IN MYTHS AND LEGENDS
Definition: Beliefs in supernatural causation, magic, and forces beyond scientific explanation.
Common Elements:
1. Omens and Signs: Animal behavior, dreams, natural signs predicting events
2. Taboos: Forbidden actions with supernatural consequences
3. Charms and Amulets: Magical objects for protection
4. Curses and Blessings: Words with magical power
5. Magical Rituals: Ceremonies, sacrifices, incantations
532
6. Supernatural Creatures: Spirits, ghosts, demons, shape-shifters
MAGIC IN MYTHS AND LEGENDS
Types of Magic:
1. Transformation Magic: Changing form, size, nature of objects
2. Elemental Magic: Controlling fire, water, earth, air
3. Healing/Harmful Magic: Curing diseases, causing illness, love potions
4. Divination: Seeing the future, reading omens, prophetic visions
5. Protective Magic: Shields, invisibility, invulnerability, charms
African Examples:
 Myths: Anansi stories, Tortoise tales, Mami Wata
 Legends: Moremi of Ife, Sundiata, Shango
PART B: DRAMA TEXT ANALYSIS
KEY ELEMENTS TO ANALYZE
1. PLOT ANALYSIS
o Exposition, rising action, climax, falling action, resolution
2. CHARACTER ANALYSIS
o Protagonist's goals and obstacles
o Character development and relationships
o Static vs. dynamic characters
3. THEME IDENTIFICATION
o Central message revealed through dialogue, actions, conflicts
533
4. DRAMATIC TECHNIQUES
o Dialogue, stage directions, soliloquies, asides
o Dramatic irony, symbolism
5. SETTING AND ATMOSPHERE
o Time period and location significance
o Mood created
6. CONFLICT TYPES
o Internal (within character)
o External (with others, society, nature)
STUDY QUESTIONS FOR DRAMA
Plot: Summarize acts/scenes, identify main conflict, climax, resolution
Character: Describe main characters, their changes, motivations
Theme: Identify central theme, lessons taught, author's development
Technical: Stage directions, props, dialogue significance, title meaning
EVALUATION
1. List and explain five features of myths
2. List and explain five features of legends
3. What role does magic play in myths and legends? Give three examples
4. Define superstition and give five examples in African myths/legends
5. State your drama text title/author and describe one conflict
534
ASSIGNMENT
1. Create a comparison table: Myths vs. Legends (characters, magic, purpose, time setting,
historical basis)
2. Write a short myth (150 words) explaining a natural phenomenon. Include magical
elements.
3. List ten superstitious beliefs from your/African culture with explanations
4. Read Act II of your drama text and write:
o Summary
o Main conflict

WEEK 10 - SPEECH WORK: INTRODUCTION TO DIPHTHONGS
WHAT IS A DIPHTHONG?
A vowel sound that glides from one vowel to another within the same syllable.
Characteristics:
 Two vowel sounds in one syllable
 Tongue/lips move during pronunciation
 First sound is longer and stronger
 Creates a gliding effect
THE EIGHT ENGLISH DIPHTHONGS
1. /eɪ/ (day): make, rain, they, eight, play
2. /aɪ/ (my): fly, time, like, high, night
3. /ɔɪ/ (boy): toy, oil, coin, noise, choice
4. /aʊ/ (now): how, house, town, cloud, sound
5. /əʊ/ (go): no, boat, show, hope, home
6. /ɪə/ (here): near, ear, fear, clear, year
7. /eə/ (hair): care, chair, fair, bear, wear
8. /ʊə/ (pure): sure, tour, poor, cure (disappearing in modern English)
GROUPING DIPHTHONGS
Closing Diphthongs (glide to /ɪ/ or /ʊ/):
 /eɪ/, /aɪ/, /ɔɪ/ → glide to /ɪ/
 /aʊ/, /əʊ/ → glide to /ʊ/
536
Centering Diphthongs (glide to /ə/):
 /ɪə/, /eə/, /ʊə/ → glide to schwa
DIPHTHONGS VS PURE VOWELS
Pure Vowel: Single sound, no movement (e.g., /iː/ in "see") Diphthong: Two sounds blended,
tongue/lips move (e.g., /eɪ/ in "say")
COMMON SPELLING PATTERNS
 /eɪ/: a_e (make), ai (rain), ay (day), ey (they)
 /aɪ/: i_e (like), igh (high), y (my), ie (tie)
 /ɔɪ/: oy (boy), oi (coin)
 /aʊ/: ou (house), ow (now)
 /əʊ/: o_e (home), oa (boat), ow (show)
ARTICULATION TIPS
✓ Start clearly with first vowel ✓ Glide smoothly to second sound ✓ Don't pause between
sounds ✓ First sound stronger/longer ✓ Keep within one syllable
Common Mistakes:
 Pronouncing as two separate sounds
 Making both sounds equally strong
 Confusing similar diphthongs
EVALUATION
537
1. Define diphthong and explain difference from pure vowel
2. List eight English diphthongs with examples
3. Identify diphthongs in: time, boat, boy, house, hair
4. Write five sentences using at least two different diphthongs
ASSIGNMENT
1. Create table: phonetic symbol, example word, spelling patterns for all eight diphthongs
2. List ten words each for /eɪ/, /aɪ/, /ɔɪ/
3. Identify diphthongs in: "My friend and I like to play near the house."
4. Explain difference between monophthongs and diphthongs with examples
538
WEEK 10 - GRAMMAR: SIMPLE PAST TENSE SENTENCES
AFFIRMATIVE SENTENCES
Structure: Subject + Past Verb + Object
Regular Verbs:
 I walked to school yesterday
 She cooked rice last night
 They played football last Saturday
Irregular Verbs:
 I went to Lagos last month
 She ate breakfast early
 We had a party on Friday
NEGATIVE SENTENCES
Structure: Subject + did not (didn't) + base verb
Examples:
 I didn't go to school yesterday
 She didn't cook dinner
 They didn't play football
Common Errors:
 ✗ I didn't went → ✓ I didn't go
 ✗ She didn't cooked → ✓ She didn't cook
539
INTERROGATIVE SENTENCES
Structure: Did + subject + base verb...?
Examples:
 Did you go to school yesterday?
 Did she cook rice?
 Did they play football?
Short Answers: Yes, I did. / No, I didn't.
WH- QUESTIONS
Structure: Wh-word + did + subject + base verb...?
Examples:
 What did you do yesterday?
 Where did she go last week?
 When did they arrive?
 Why did he leave early?
Special Case: Who came? (NOT: Who did come?)
COMBINING IDEAS
Using Conjunctions:
 I went to school and played football
 She studied hard but failed the exam
 They didn't come because they were sick
Time Clauses:
540
 When I arrived, they had left
 After she ate, she slept
 While I studied, he played
COMMON IRREGULAR VERBS
 go → went, come → came, see → saw
 eat → ate, drink → drank, write → wrote
 take → took, make → made, give → gave
 buy → bought, say → said, think → thought
EVALUATION
1. Write five affirmative sentences about last weekend
2. Change to negative: (a) I went to school (b) She ate breakfast (c) They played well
3. Change to questions: (a) You saw the film (b) She cooked dinner (c) They came early
4. Make Wh-questions: He went to Lagos (Where...?)
5. Correct errors: (a) I didn't went (b) Did she came? (c) He didn't ate
ASSIGNMENT
1. Write paragraph (10 sentences) about your last birthday in past tense
2. Write five compound sentences using: and, but, or, so, because
3. Transform to past, negative, then question: (i) I play football (ii) She reads books
4. Complete with past tense: Yesterday, I ___ (wake) up early. I ___ (brush) my teeth...
5. Write five sentences using: saw, bought, went, ate, wrote
541

WEEK 10 - READING: HOW DIAGRAMS/MAPS CLARIFY TEXT
WHY USE DIAGRAMS AND MAPS?
 Make complex information easier to understand
 Show relationships and connections
 Help locate information quickly
 Support written explanations
 Appeal to visual learners
HOW DIAGRAMS CLARIFY TEXT
1. SHOW PROCESSES
 Arrows connecting steps
 Visual cycle easier to remember
 Example: Water cycle diagram
2. ILLUSTRATE STRUCTURES
 Show location of parts
 Display relative sizes
 Example: Parts of a flower
3. COMPARE AND CONTRAST
 Venn diagrams show similarities/differences
 Clear visual comparison
4. SHOW RELATIONSHIPS
 Organizational charts show hierarchy
 Chain of command visible
543
5. DISPLAY DATA
 Bar graphs/pie charts
 Easy comparison of numbers
HOW MAPS CLARIFY TEXT
1. SHOW LOCATIONS
 Exact positions
 Spatial relationships
 Distances between places
2. ILLUSTRATE ROUTES
 Visual path to follow
 Landmarks along the way
3. SHOW GEOGRAPHICAL FEATURES
 Topographical features
 Size and extent
 Use of symbols and colors
STEPS TO READ TEXT WITH DIAGRAMS/MAPS
1. Preview Both: Read titles, scan content
2. Read Text First: Understand main ideas
3. Study Diagram/Map: Examine all parts, labels, key
4. Cross-Reference: Match text to visual
5. Integrate Understanding: Combine both sources
544
WHAT TO LOOK FOR
In Diagrams:
 Labels and arrows
 Scale and key
 Relationships between parts
 Direction of flow
In Maps:
 Compass/direction
 Scale for distance
 Symbols and legend
 Starting/ending points
EVALUATION
1. Explain three ways diagrams clarify text
2. What five elements to look for in diagrams/maps?
3. Describe steps for reading text with diagrams
4. How does a map clarify written directions?
5. How would a labeled diagram clarify plant parts?
ASSIGNMENT
1. Draw map from house to school. Write directions (5 sentences). Explain how map
clarifies.
545
2. Create flow chart for morning routine. Explain how it clarifies (3 sentences).
3. Find diagram in textbook. Explain what text and diagram show, how they work together.
4. Explain why these need diagrams: (a) solar system (b) food chain (c) math problem
5. Create Venn diagram comparing two animals. Explain what it shows.

WEEK 10 - COMPOSITION: GUIDED COMPOSITION - A FESTIVAL
WHAT IS GUIDED COMPOSITION?
Writing based on given points/guidelines. Organize provided ideas into complete, structured
paragraphs.
STRUCTURE FOR "A FESTIVAL IN MY VILLAGE"
Paragraph 1 - Introduction:
 Name and type of festival
 When celebrated
 Brief background/origin
 Importance
Paragraph 2 - Preparations:
 Cleaning homes
 Cooking special foods
 Buying new clothes
 Organizing committees
Paragraph 3 - Main Events:
 Traditional dances/music
 Processions/parades
 Masquerade displays
 Cultural performances
Paragraph 4 - Food and People:
 Special dishes prepared
547
 Who participates
 Community involvement
Paragraph 5 - Conclusion:
 Significance of festival
 Personal reflection
 Benefits to community
USEFUL VOCABULARY
Festival Words: celebration, ceremony, ritual, procession, masquerade, festivities
Action Verbs: celebrate, honor, perform, display, participate, feast
Descriptive: colorful, vibrant, joyous, traditional, cultural, abundant
TRANSITIONAL PHRASES
Between Paragraphs: On the festival day..., During celebrations..., More importantly...
Within Paragraphs: First, Additionally, Meanwhile, For example, Therefore
WRITING TIPS
DO:
 Follow guided points
 Expand points into full sentences
 Use descriptive language
 Connect with transitions
 Include specific details
548
DON'T:
 Deviate from given points
 Write single-sentence paragraphs
 Use informal language
 Make paragraphs too long/short
EVALUATION
1. What is guided composition?
2. List five types of festivals in Nigeria
3. Outline structure for "A Festival in My Village"
4. List ten festival-related vocabulary words
5. Why are festivals important? (three reasons)
ASSIGNMENT
1. Write complete composition (250-300 words) on "A Festival in My Village"
2. Create outline: Introduction, three body paragraphs, conclusion
3. List ten special foods during festivals
4. Describe three traditional festival activities (5 sentences each)
5. Write introduction paragraph for any festival

WEEK 10 - LITERATURE: DRAMATIZATION & TEXT REVIEW
PART A: DRAMATIZATION
WHAT IS DRAMATIZATION? Acting out scenes from a play through dialogue, movement,
gestures, and emotions.
STEPS TO PREPARE
1. SELECT SCENE
 Manageable length (5-10 minutes)
 Important dialogue
 Contains conflict
 Suitable number of characters
2. ANALYZE SCENE
 What happens?
 Who are the characters?
 What is the conflict?
 What is the mood?
3. ASSIGN ROLES
 Match actors to characters
 Assign director, props coordinator
 Assign prompter
4. MEMORIZE LINES
 Read multiple times
 Understand meaning
550
 Practice with emotion
 Rehearse with partners
5. DEVELOP CHARACTERIZATION
Voice: Tone, volume, pace, pitch
Body Language: Facial expressions, gestures, posture, movement
Emotions: Show feelings believably
6. BLOCKING (Stage Movement)
 Where actors stand/sit
 When/where they move
 Entrances and exits
 Face audience when speaking
7. PROPS AND COSTUMES
Props: Objects actors use (book, letter, crown)
Costumes: Simple but representative of character
8. REHEARSE
1. First reading with scripts
2. Blocking rehearsal (add movements)
3. Line rehearsal (memorization)
4. Character rehearsal (emotions)
5. Technical rehearsal (props/costumes)
6. Dress rehearsal (full performance)
9. PERFORM
During Performance:
551
 Project your voice
 Face audience
 Stay in character
 Listen to other actors
 Show energy and enthusiasm
COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID
 Speaking too softly
 Rushing lines
 Turning back to audience
 Breaking character
 Standing still
 Over-acting
 Not listening to cues
PART B: REVIEW OF TEXTS
PROSE TEXT REVIEW
1. PLOT: Beginning, rising action, climax, resolution
2. CHARACTERS: Protagonist, antagonist, supporting characters; their traits and development
3. SETTING: Time, place, social environment
4. THEMES: Central messages, moral lessons (honesty, courage, family)
5. LITERARY DEVICES: Foreshadowing, flashback, symbolism, irony
552
DRAMA TEXT REVIEW
1. STRUCTURE: Acts and scenes, plot development
2. DIALOGUE: Character revelation through speech
3. STAGE DIRECTIONS: Instructions for actors, setting descriptions
4. DRAMATIC TECHNIQUES: Aside, soliloquy, dramatic irony
5. THEMES: Social issues, moral lessons
MYTHS AND LEGENDS REVIEW
Myths: Gods, supernatural beings, explain creation, timeless
Legends: Heroes, historical basis, exaggerated deeds, cultural pride
Common Features: Magic, moral lessons, oral tradition
STUDY TIPS
1. Create summary notes (plot timeline, character charts)
2. Practice questions
3. Reread key sections
4. Make connections to real life
5. Test yourself without notes
EVALUATION

553
4. Review prose text: summarize plot, describe main character, state theme
5. Three common dramatization mistakes to avoid
ASSIGNMENT

emotions, planning movements, identifying props
