JSS1 ENGLISH STUDIES LESSON NOTES
THIRD TERM
WEEK 1 - SPEECH WORK: DIPHTHONGS
A diphthong is a complex vowel sound that begins with one vowel sound and glides into another
within the same syllable. Unlike pure vowels (monophthongs), diphthongs involve continuous
tongue movement during pronunciation, creating a smooth glide between two vowel qualities.
The eight English diphthongs are:
 /eɪ/ as in place, make, day, eight, gate, remain
 /əʊ/ as in home, go, boat, phone, stone, although
 /aʊ/ as in mouse, house, now, found, cloud, coward
 /ɪə/ as in clear, hear, beer, fear, near, sincere
 /eə/ as in care, hair, bear, wear, chair, staircase
 /ɔɪ/ as in boy, coin, noise, join, voice, exploit
 /aɪ/ as in find, time, like, fight, sky, describe
 /ʊə/ as in tour, poor, sure, pure, mature, allure
Key Points:
 Diphthongs differ from two separate vowels because they form a single syllable
 The first vowel sound is typically longer and receives more stress
 The glide to the second sound is smooth and continuous
 Incorrect pronunciation of diphthongs can lead to communication errors
 Regional accents may influence diphthong pronunciation
555
EVALUATION:
1. Define a diphthong and explain how it differs from a pure vowel sound.
2. Pronounce five words containing the diphthong /eɪ/ and five words containing /aɪ/.
3. Identify the diphthongs in these words: "noise," "tour," "home," "fear," "mouth."
4. Why is the first vowel sound in a diphthong usually stronger than the second?
5. Write two sentences using at least three different diphthongs, then circle the words
containing diphthongs.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Create a list of ten words for each of these diphthongs: /əʊ/, /aʊ/, and /ɔɪ/.
2. Record yourself (or practice with a partner) pronouncing all eight diphthongs with
example words.
3. Find five words in your recommended prose text that contain diphthongs and identify
which diphthong each contains.
4. Write a short paragraph (5-7 sentences) about your school, deliberately using words with
at least five different diphthongs. Underline these words.
5. Explain in your own words why learning correct diphthong pronunciation is important for
effective communication.
556
WEEK 1 - GRAMMAR: INTRODUCTION TO ACTIVE AND PASSIVE VOICE
Voice in grammar refers to the relationship between the subject of a sentence and the action
expressed by the verb. There are two voices in English: active voice and passive voice.
Active Voice: In active voice, the subject performs the action expressed by the verb. The focus is
on who or what is doing the action.
Structure: Subject + Verb + Object
Examples:
 The teacher teaches English grammar. (Subject: teacher performs the action)
 Students write essays every week. (Students perform the action)
 The chef prepared a delicious meal. (Chef performs the action)
 My brother painted the fence yesterday. (Brother performs the action)
 The committee will announce the results tomorrow. (Committee performs the action)
Passive Voice: In passive voice, the subject receives the action expressed by the verb. The focus
shifts from the doer to the receiver of the action.
Structure: Subject + Form of "be" + Past Participle (+ by + Agent)
Examples:
 English grammar is taught by the teacher. (English grammar receives the action)
 Essays are written by students every week. (Essays receive the action)
 A delicious meal was prepared by the chef. (Meal receives the action)
 The fence was painted by my brother yesterday. (Fence receives the action)
 The results will be announced by the committee tomorrow. (Results receive the action)
Formation of Passive Voice:
557
1. Identify the object of the active sentence
2. Make the object the subject of the passive sentence
3. Use the appropriate tense of the verb "to be"
4. Add the past participle of the main verb
5. Optionally, add "by + agent" (the original subject)
When to Use Passive Voice:
 When the doer is unknown: "My bicycle was stolen last night."
 When the action is more important than the doer: "The bridge was completed in 2020."
 When the doer is obvious: "The suspect has been arrested."
 In formal or scientific writing: "The experiment was conducted under controlled
conditions."
 To maintain focus on a particular subject throughout a text
When to Use Active Voice:
 For clear, direct, and concise writing
 When the doer of the action is important
 In most everyday communication
 To make writing more engaging and dynamic
Key Points:
 Active voice is generally preferred for clarity and directness
 Passive voice can make sentences wordier
 The agent (by + doer) can be omitted in passive voice
 Not all verbs can be made passive (intransitive verbs cannot)
558
EVALUATION:
1. Explain the difference between active and passive voice with one example of each.
2. Convert these active sentences to passive: (a) "The dog bit the man." (b) "She will
complete the project tomorrow."
3. Identify whether these sentences are in active or passive voice: (a) "The letter was
4. delivered by the postman." (b) "The children are playing football."
5. Why might a writer choose to use passive voice instead of active voice?
6. Rewrite this passive sentence in active voice: "The school was built by the government in
1995."
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write five sentences in active voice about activities in your school.
2. Convert all five sentences from question 1 to passive voice.
3. Find three examples of passive voice sentences in your recommended prose text and
rewrite them in active voice.
4. Write a short paragraph (6-8 sentences) describing what happens in your school cafeteria
during lunchtime. Use both active and passive voice sentences, then label each sentence
as A (active) or P (passive).
5. Explain in your own words when it is appropriate to use passive voice in writing.
559
WEEK 1 - READING & COMPREHENSION: READING TO MAKE MEANING FROM
SPATIAL DESCRIPTION
Spatial description refers to the method of describing the physical location, arrangement, position,
and relationship of objects, people, or places in space. Understanding spatial descriptions is
crucial for comprehending settings in texts, following directions, and visualizing scenes
accurately.
ELEMENTS OF SPATIAL DESCRIPTION:
1. Position and Location Words:
 Above, below, beneath, under, over, on top of
 Beside, next to, adjacent to, alongside
 Between, among, amid, amidst
 In front of, behind, in back of
 Inside, outside, within, without
 Near, close to, far from, distant from
 Opposite, across from, facing
2. Directional Words:
 Toward, away from, to, from
 Into, out of, onto, off of
 Through, across, along, past
 Up, down, upward, downward
 North, south, east, west (cardinal directions)
 Left, right, straight ahead
 Forward, backward, sideways
560
3. Relative Position: Writers often describe locations relative to known landmarks or reference
points. Example: "The library is located between the science lab and the cafeteria, directly
opposite the main administrative block."
4. Layered Description: Effective spatial descriptions often work from general to specific (or
vice versa), helping readers build a complete mental picture. Example: "The airport is a massive
structure. In the center stands the main terminal building. To its left, three hangars house aircraft
under maintenance. The control tower rises prominently on the right side, overseeing the
runways that stretch out before it."
Vocabulary - Air Transport:
 Aircraft/Airplane: A powered vehicle designed for air travel
 Runway: A long strip of ground where aircraft take off and land
 Terminal: The building where passengers check in, board, and collect luggage
 Hangar: A large building where aircraft are stored and maintained
 Control Tower: The tall building from which air traffic is monitored and directed
 Cockpit: The area where the pilot and co-pilot operate the aircraft
 Fuselage: The main body of an aircraft
 Wings: The flat surfaces that provide lift for the aircraft
 Tarmac/Apron: The paved area where aircraft park, load, and unload
 Boarding Pass: A document that allows a passenger to board an aircraft
 Check-in Counter: Where passengers register and receive boarding passes
 Baggage Claim: The area where passengers collect their luggage after a flight
 Immigration: The process of entering or leaving a country
 Customs: Where officials check goods being brought into a country
 Flight Attendant: Crew members who ensure passenger safety and comfort
561
 Pilot/Captain: The person who flies the aircraft
 Passenger: A person traveling in an aircraft
 Cargo: Goods transported by aircraft
 Altitude: The height of an aircraft above sea level
 Turbulence: Irregular air movements that cause bumpy flights
Reading Strategy: When reading spatial descriptions:
1. Identify the main reference point or central location
2. Note directional and positional words
3. Visualize the scene mentally or sketch it
4. Pay attention to distances and relationships between objects
5. Consider the perspective (bird's eye view, ground level, etc.)
EVALUATION:
1. What is spatial description and why is it important in reading comprehension?
2. List five position words and five directional words used in spatial descriptions.
3. Read this passage and draw a simple diagram of the airport layout: "Murtala Muhammed
International Airport in Lagos has two terminals. The international terminal is on the
western side, while the domestic terminal sits on the eastern side. Between them lies a
large parking area. The control tower stands prominently behind the international
terminal, overlooking the three parallel runways that stretch northward."
4. Define the following air transport terms: runway, hangar, terminal, cockpit.
5. Using spatial description words, describe the location of your classroom within your
school building in four sentences.
ASSIGNMENT:
562
1. Write a detailed paragraph (8-10 sentences) describing the layout of your home using at
least ten different spatial description words. Underline the spatial words.
2. Create a vocabulary list of fifteen additional words related to air transport beyond those
provided in the lesson.
3. Read the first chapter of your prose text and identify at least three examples of spatial
descriptions used by the author. Write them out and underline the spatial words.
4. Draw and label a diagram of an airport, including at least eight different components
(terminal, runway, control tower, etc.).
5. Write five sentences describing the route from your classroom to the school library using
directional and positional words.
563
WEEK 1 - COMPOSITION: INTRODUCTION TO EXPOSITORY ESSAY
An expository essay is a type of writing that explains, informs, describes, or clarifies a topic to
the reader using facts, statistics, examples, and logical explanations. The word "expository"
comes from "expose," meaning to reveal or explain. Unlike narrative essays (which tell stories)
or persuasive essays (which argue a point), expository essays focus on providing clear, objective
information.
Purpose of Expository Essays:
 To explain a process or concept
 To inform readers about a topic
 To describe something in detail
 To analyze a situation or idea
 To present facts without personal opinions
Characteristics of Expository Essays:
1. Objective Tone: Written in third person, avoiding personal opinions
2. Clear Thesis Statement: States the main topic that will be explained
3. Factual Information: Uses facts, examples, statistics, and evidence
4. Logical Organization: Information presented in a clear, systematic manner
5. Formal Language: Uses standard English without slang or colloquialisms
6. Transitions: Uses connecting words to link ideas smoothly
STRUCTURE OF AN EXPOSITORY ESSAY:
1. Introduction (Opening Paragraph):
 Hook: An interesting opening sentence to grab attention (question, fact, or quote)
 Background Information: Brief context about the topic
564
 Thesis Statement: A clear statement explaining what the essay will explain
Example: "Have you ever wondered how airplanes stay in the sky? Air travel has revolutionized
global transportation since the Wright brothers' first flight in 1903. This essay will explain the
basic principles that allow aircraft to fly safely through the atmosphere."
2. Body Paragraphs (Usually 3-5 paragraphs): Each body paragraph should:
 Begin with a topic sentence introducing the main point
 Provide supporting details, facts, examples, or explanations
 Use transition words to connect ideas
 End with a concluding sentence that relates back to the thesis
Example Topic Sentences:
 "The first principle of flight is lift, created by air flowing over the aircraft's wings."
 "In addition to lift, thrust from the engines propels the aircraft forward."
 "Understanding drag and gravity helps explain how pilots control aircraft movement."
3. Conclusion (Closing Paragraph):
 Restate the thesis in different words
 Summarize main points briefly
 Leave the reader with a final thought or implication
 Do NOT introduce new information
Example: "Understanding these four forces—lift, thrust, drag, and gravity—reveals how aircraft
achieve and maintain flight. Modern aviation depends on balancing these forces effectively. As
technology advances, engineers continue to refine these principles to make air travel safer and
more efficient."
TYPES OF EXPOSITORY ESSAYS:
565
1. Process Essay: Explains how to do something or how something works
2. Descriptive Essay: Describes characteristics of a person, place, or thing
3. Compare and Contrast Essay: Shows similarities and differences
4. Cause and Effect Essay: Explains reasons and results
5. Problem and Solution Essay: Identifies a problem and proposes solutions
6. Classification Essay: Divides a topic into categories
Language Features:
 Present tense (mostly)
 Third person (he, she, it, they)
 Technical or specific vocabulary related to the topic
 Transition words: firstly, secondly, furthermore, moreover, however, therefore, in
conclusion
 Definition of key terms
 Examples and illustrations
Writing Tips:
 Research your topic thoroughly before writing
 Organize information logically
 Use specific, concrete examples
 Define technical terms for readers
 Avoid personal pronouns (I, we, you) unless appropriate
 Write in clear, straightforward sentences
566
 Proofread for clarity and accuracy
EVALUATION:
1. Define an expository essay and explain its main purpose.
2. What are three key characteristics that distinguish expository essays from other essay
types?
3. Outline the three main parts of an expository essay and briefly explain what each part
should contain.
4. Why is it important to use facts and examples rather than personal opinions in expository
writing?
5. Write a thesis statement for an expository essay on "How Students Can Improve Their
Reading Skills."
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Choose one of these topics and write a complete introduction paragraph (4-6 sentences)
with a clear thesis statement: (a) How to Prepare for Examinations (b) The Importance of
Clean Water (c) How Mobile Phones Work.
2. Write three topic sentences for body paragraphs that could follow your introduction in
question 1.
3. List ten transition words commonly used in expository writing and use five of them in
complete sentences.
4. Read an article from your textbook or a newspaper and identify whether it is expository
writing. Explain your answer by identifying three features of expository writing in the
article.
5. Write a conclusion paragraph (4-5 sentences) for an expository essay on "The Process of
Photosynthesis in Plants."
567
WEEK 1 - LITERATURE: THEMES AND CHARACTERIZATION IN PROSE
THEMES: A theme is the central idea, message, or underlying meaning that runs throughout a
literary work. It is the author's insight about life, human nature, or society that the reader can
extract from the story. Themes are universal truths or observations that transcend the specific
story being told.
DIFFERENCE BETWEEN TOPIC AND THEME:
 Topic: What the story is about (love, war, friendship)
 Theme: What the story says about that topic (love requires sacrifice; war destroys
innocence; true friendship overcomes obstacles)
Types of Themes:
1. Major/Central Theme: The most important message running throughout the work
2. Minor Theme: Secondary ideas that support or complement the major theme
Common Universal Themes in Literature:
 Good versus evil
 Coming of age/loss of innocence
 The power of love
 Individual versus society
 Courage and heroism
 The corruption of power
 Survival and resilience
 Betrayal and loyalty
 Appearance versus reality
 The search for identity
 Justice and injustice
568
 The effects of war
 Family relationships
 Death and mortality
 Tradition versus change
How to Identify Themes:
1. Consider the title—it often hints at the theme
2. Examine what characters learn or how they change
3. Look for repeated ideas, symbols, or motifs
4. Analyze conflicts and how they are resolved
5. Consider the author's message or purpose
6. Note important statements made by characters
7. Think about the story's outcome and its implications
Expressing Themes: Themes should be stated as complete sentences expressing an idea, not just
as single words.
Weak: "Friendship" (This is just a topic) Strong: "True friendship requires honesty and
sacrifice." (This is a theme)
Weak: "War" Strong: "War destroys the innocence of those who fight in it."
CHARACTERIZATION: Characterization is the process by which an author creates, develops,
and reveals the personalities, traits, and qualities of characters in a story. It helps readers
understand why characters behave the way they do and makes them believable.
Types of Characters:
1. By Role:
 Protagonist: The main character around whom the story revolves
569
 Antagonist: The character or force opposing the protagonist
 Supporting Characters: Minor characters who help develop the plot
 Foil: A character who contrasts with another to highlight particular qualities
2. By Development:
 Round Characters: Complex, multi-dimensional characters with many traits
 Flat Characters: Simple, one-dimensional characters with few traits
 Dynamic Characters: Characters who change significantly throughout the story
 Static Characters: Characters who remain essentially the same throughout
Methods of Characterization:
1. Direct Characterization: The author explicitly tells readers about a character's traits.
Example: "John was a brave and honest young man who never told lies."
2. Indirect Characterization (STEAL Method): The author shows character traits through:
S - Speech: What the character says and how they say it
 Dialogue reveals personality, education, emotions, and attitudes
 Example: A character who speaks rudely shows disrespect
T - Thoughts: What the character thinks, feels, and believes
 Internal monologue reveals motivations and values
 Example: A character constantly worrying about others shows compassion
E - Effect on Others: How other characters react to and speak about the character
 Other characters' opinions provide insight
 Example: If everyone respects a character, they likely possess admirable qualities
A - Actions: What the character does; their behavior and choices
 Actions often speak louder than words
570
 Example: A character who shares food with the poor demonstrates generosity
L - Looks: The character's physical appearance and how they present themselves
 Physical descriptions can reflect personality or social status
 Example: A character always dressed neatly suggests discipline and self-respect
Character Traits: These are the distinctive qualities and characteristics that define a character's
personality:
 Physical traits: tall, short, strong, weak, beautiful, scarred
 Personality traits: brave, cowardly, kind, cruel, honest, deceitful
 Emotional traits: calm, anxious, cheerful, melancholy
 Intellectual traits: intelligent, foolish, wise, naive
 Moral traits: good, evil, virtuous, corrupt
Analyzing Characters: When analyzing characters, consider:
1. Their role in the story (protagonist, antagonist, etc.)
2. Their physical description and significance
3. Their personality traits and how they're revealed
4. Their relationships with other characters
5. Their motivations and goals
6. How they change or develop (or don't)
7. What they represent symbolically
8. Their contribution to the theme
Relationship Between Theme and Characterization: Characters often embody or learn the
themes of a story. Their development and the choices they make frequently illustrate the author's
message. For example, a character who learns to value honesty despite consequences might
illustrate the theme "Integrity is more important than personal gain."
571
EVALUATION:
1. Define theme and explain the difference between a topic and a theme. Give one example.
2. What is characterization? Describe the two main types of characterization with examples.
3. Explain the STEAL method of indirect characterization and give one example for each
letter.
4. Identify one major theme in your recommended prose text and explain how it is
developed through the story.
5. Choose one character from your prose text. Is this character round or flat? Dynamic or
static? Explain your answer with evidence from the text.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Identify three themes in your recommended prose text. Write each theme as a complete
sentence and provide evidence from the story that supports each theme.
2. Select the protagonist from your prose text and write a detailed character analysis (one
paragraph of 8-10 sentences) describing their personality, motivations, and role in the
story.
3. Find three examples of indirect characterization in your prose text. For each example,
identify which STEAL method is being used and explain what it reveals about the
character.
4. Create a character profile for one character in your prose text including: name, physical
description, personality traits, role in the story, relationships with other characters, and
one important action they perform.
5. Write a paragraph (6-8 sentences) explaining how one character in your prose text helps
to develop or illustrate one of the story's major themes.
572
WEEK 2 - SPEECH WORK: IDENTIFICATION OF DIPHTHONGS IN WORDS
This lesson focuses on recognizing and pronouncing diphthongs /eə/ and /ɔɪ/ in words.
Diphthong /eə/ (as in "air"): Glides from /e/ to /ə/. Common spellings: "air," "are," "ear," "ere,"
"eir"
Examples:
 Mary, care, fair, hair, pair, stair, chair, dare, rare, spare
 Bear, pear, wear, swear, tear (rip)
 There, where, their, prayer, layer, mayor
 Square, aware, declare, parent, apparent
Diphthong /ɔɪ/ (as in "boy"): Glides from /ɔ/ to /ɪ/. Common spellings: "oi," "oy"
Examples:
 Boy, toy, joy, employ, destroy, enjoy, annoy
 Boil, coil, foil, oil, soil, spoil, toil
 Coin, join, point, joint, appoint
 Noise, voice, choice, rejoice
 Avoid, loyal, royal, voyage
Identification Strategies:
1. Learn spelling patterns (air, oi, oy)
2. Listen for the gliding quality between two vowel sounds
3. Feel tongue movement during pronunciation
4. Practice minimal pairs: bear/beer, boil/bile, care/car
Common Errors:
 Pronouncing /eə/ as pure /e/
 Confusing /eə/ with /eɪ/ (Mary vs. merry)
573
 Pronouncing /ɔɪ/ as two syllables
 Confusing /ɔɪ/ with /aɪ/ (boil vs. bile)
EVALUATION:
1. Identify the diphthong in these words: chair, loyal, repair, destroy, aware.
2. Provide five words containing /eə/ and five containing /ɔɪ/.
3. Explain the pronunciation difference between: bear/beer, boil/bile.
4. Underline words with diphthongs in this sentence: "The boy's hair was fair, and he
enjoyed playing near the stairs."
5. Why is correct diphthong pronunciation important? Give two examples.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List ten words with /eə/ and ten with /ɔɪ/, organized by spelling patterns.
2. Write five sentences using words with /eə/ or /ɔɪ/. Underline the diphthong words.
3. Find eight words containing /eə/ or /ɔɪ/ in your prose text. Note the page numbers.
4. Practice these minimal pairs: bear/beer, care/car, fair/fear, boil/bile, boy/buy.
5. Write a paragraph (6 sentences) using at least six different words with /eə/ or /ɔɪ/.
574
WEEK 2 - GRAMMAR: HIGHLIGHTING ACTIVE VOICE
Active voice occurs when the subject performs the action. It creates clear, direct, and effective
sentences.
Structure: Subject + Verb + Object
Why Use Active Voice:
1. Clarity: Makes it clear who does what
o Active: "The teacher explained the lesson."
o Passive: "The lesson was explained."
2. Directness: Straightforward and easy to understand
o Active: "The committee made a decision."
o Passive: "A decision was made by the committee."
3. Brevity: Uses fewer words
o Active: "Lightning struck the tree." (4 words)
o Passive: "The tree was struck by lightning." (6 words)
4. Engagement: Creates dynamic writing
o Active: "The detective solved the mystery."
5. Accountability: Shows who is responsible
o Active: "The company increased prices."
Identifying Active Voice:
 Subject comes before verb
 Subject performs the action
 Answers "Who did what?"
 No "be" + past participle
Examples Across Tenses:
Simple Present:
575
 Students write essays.
 The sun rises in the east.
 Dogs bark at strangers.
Simple Past:
 The chef cooked a meal.
 She finished her homework.
 They built a new school.
Present Continuous:
 The children are playing football.
 I am reading a novel.
Present Perfect:
 She has completed the project.
 We have visited the museum.
Future Tense:
 The school will announce results tomorrow.
 I will finish the assignment tonight.
When Active Voice is Most Effective:
 Everyday communication and conversation
 News reports and journalism
 Academic writing (in many cases)
 Business correspondence
 Instructions and directions
 Narrative and creative writing
 When the doer is important
576
Practice Identifying Active Voice: Identify which sentences are active:
1. "The farmer planted corn." (Active)
2. "The cake was baked by Mary." (Passive)
3. "John rides his bicycle daily." (Active)
4. "The letter was delivered yesterday." (Passive)
EVALUATION:
1. Define active voice and explain why it is preferred in most writing.
2. Write five sentences in active voice using these verbs: teach, play, build, write, cook.
3. Identify whether these sentences are active or passive: (a) "The boy kicked the ball." (b)
"The ball was kicked by the boy." (c) "Mother prepared breakfast."
4. Rewrite these passive sentences in active voice: (a) "The book was read by Sarah." (b)
"The house was painted by workers."
5. Explain three benefits of using active voice in writing.

1. Write ten sentences in active voice about daily activities at home and school.
2. Identify five active voice sentences in your prose text. Write them out with page numbers.
3. Create five sentences in each of these tenses using active voice: simple present, simple
past, present continuous.
4. Find three passive voice sentences in a newspaper or textbook and rewrite them in active
voice.

active voice.

WEEK 2 - READING & COMPREHENSION: APPROPRIATE INTRODUCTIONS AND
CONCLUSIONS; FISHING VOCABULARY
A. INTRODUCTIONS
An introduction is the opening paragraph that presents the topic and captures the reader's
attention.
Functions:
 Grab reader's attention
 Introduce the topic
 Provide background information
 Present the thesis/main idea
 Set the tone
Types of Introductions:
1. Question: "Have you ever wondered why fish can breathe underwater?"
2. Fact/Statistic: "Over 70% of the Earth's surface is covered by water, making fishing one
of humanity's oldest professions."
3. Quotation: "Benjamin Franklin once said, 'Give a man a fish and you feed him for a day;
teach a man to fish and you feed him for a lifetime.'"
4. Anecdote: "Last summer, I watched a fisherman patiently cast his net into the river at
dawn."
5. Definition: "Fishing is the activity of catching fish from seas, rivers, or lakes for food or
sport."
6. Bold Statement: "Fishing has sustained human civilization for thousands of years."
Structure: Hook (1 sentence) → Context (2-3 sentences) → Thesis Statement (1 sentence)
Example: "Have you ever wondered how fish end up on your dinner plate? Fishing is one of the
world's oldest industries, providing food and employment for millions. From small river
578
fishermen to large ocean trawlers, fishing methods have evolved over centuries. This essay
explores the various methods of fishing and their importance to human society."
Characteristics of Good Introductions:
 Brief and focused (4-6 sentences)
 Relevant and engaging
 Clear thesis statement
 Appropriate tone
 No unnecessary details
B. CONCLUSIONS
A conclusion is the closing paragraph that summarizes main points and leaves a final impression.
Functions:
 Signal the end
 Restate the main idea
 Summarize key points
 Provide final thoughts
 Leave lasting impression
Types of Conclusions:
1. Summary: "In conclusion, fishing provides food, employment, and economic benefits to
communities worldwide."
2. Call to Action: "We must all work together to prevent overfishing and protect marine
life for future generations."
3. Prediction: "As technology advances, fishing methods will become more efficient and
sustainable."
4. Question: "Will we manage our fish resources wisely, or will future generations face
empty oceans?"
579
5. Recommendation: "Governments should enforce fishing regulations to ensure
sustainable practices."
Structure: Transition word → Thesis restatement → Summary (2-3 sentences) → Final thought
Example: "In conclusion, fishing remains vital to human survival and economic development.
Traditional and modern methods each have advantages, from simple nets to advanced sonar
technology. Sustainable practices ensure fish populations for future generations. By balancing
human needs with environmental protection, fishing can continue to nourish humanity for
centuries to come."
Characteristics of Good Conclusions:
 Brief (4-6 sentences)
 No new information
 Clear ending
 Connects to introduction
 Appropriate tone
Common Mistakes to Avoid:
Introductions: Starting too broadly, lacking clear thesis, using clichés, being too long
Conclusions: Introducing new ideas, repeating introduction word-for-word, apologizing, being
too abrupt
C. VOCABULARY - FISHING WORDS
General Terms:
 Fishing: Catching fish
 Fisherman/Fisher: Person who catches fish
 Angler: Fishes with rod and line
 Catch: Fish caught
 Bait: Food to attract fish
580
 Hook: Sharp curved device for catching fish
 Line: Cord attached to rod
 Rod: Long flexible stick for fishing
 Reel: Device for winding line
 Net: Mesh for catching fish
Types of Fishing:
 Trawling: Dragging large nets through water
 Angling: Fishing with rod, line, and hook
 Spear fishing: Using sharp pointed weapon
 Netting: Using nets
 Ice fishing: Fishing through holes in frozen water
Equipment:
 Trawler: Large fishing boat
 Canoe: Small narrow boat
 Tackle: Fishing equipment
 Lure: Artificial bait
 Sinker: Weight to sink line
 Float/Bobber: Shows when fish bites
 Creel: Basket for caught fish
 Trap: Device for catching fish
Locations:
 River: Flowing watercourse
 Stream: Small river
 Lake: Large still water body
 Pond: Small still water body
 Ocean/Sea: Large salt water body
 Shore: Land along water's edge

 Bank: Sloping land beside river
Fish and Marine Life:
 School: Group of fish swimming together
 Spawn: Lay eggs (reproduction)
 Fry: Young fish
 Scale: Protective plates on fish
 Gill: Breathing organ
 Fin: Extension for swimming
Actions:
 Cast: Throw line into water
 Reel in: Pull line back
 Bite: When fish takes bait
 Land: Bring fish to shore

WEEK 2 - COMPOSITION: ELEMENTS OF WRITING (CONTINUED)
THE FIVE ELEMENTS:
1. PURPOSE: Why you're writing
 To inform (expository)
 To persuade (argumentative)
 To entertain (creative)
 To describe (descriptive)
 To narrate (narrative)
2. AUDIENCE: Who will read it
 Consider: age, knowledge level, interests, culture
 Adjust language and complexity accordingly
3. TONE: Attitude conveyed
 Formal: Professional, serious
 Informal: Casual, friendly
 Others: optimistic, serious, humorous, respectful
4. ORGANIZATION: Logical arrangement
 Chronological: Time sequence
 Spatial: By location
 Order of importance: Most to least
583
 Comparison/contrast: Similarities/differences
 Problem-solution
 Cause-effect
5. CLARITY: Easy to understand
 Use simple language
 Write complete sentences
 Be specific
 Avoid ambiguity
 Use transitions
 Stay focused
 Define technical terms
Additional Elements:
 Unity: All sentences support one main idea
 Coherence: Ideas flow smoothly

WEEK 2 - LITERATURE: SETTING AND PLOT IN PROSE
A. SETTING
Setting refers to the time and place where a story occurs.
Components of Setting:
1. Place/Location:
 Geographical: Country, city, village, building
 Physical Environment: Indoors/outdoors, natural/urban
 Atmosphere: Weather, lighting, sounds, smells
Examples: "A small fishing village on the coast of Lagos" / "The dark, dusty attic filled with old
trunks"
2. Time:
 Historical Period: Past, present, future, specific era
 Season: Summer, rainy season, harmattan
 Time of Day: Morning, noon, night, dawn, dusk
 Duration: One day, several years
Examples: "During the Nigerian Civil War in the late 1960s" / "One hot afternoon in July"
3. Social/Cultural Environment:
 Customs and traditions
 Social class structure
 Political climate
 Religious beliefs
 Economic conditions
586
Examples: "A traditional Igbo community where elders hold authority" / "A wealthy urban
neighborhood in modern Lagos"
Importance of Setting:
1. Creates Mood: Dark night = suspense; sunny day = happiness
2. Influences Characters: Village character may be more traditional than city character
3. Advances Plot: Storm prevents characters from reaching destination
4. Reveals Theme: Decaying mansion symbolizes moral corruption
5. Provides Authenticity: Makes story believable
Types of Setting:
 Integral Setting: Essential; story couldn't happen elsewhere (pearl divers need ocean)
 Backdrop Setting: Less important; could occur anywhere (friendship story in any school)
B. PLOT
Plot is the sequence of events that make up a story.
Five Elements of Plot Structure:
1. EXPOSITION (Introduction):
 Introduces characters
 Establishes setting
 Provides background
 Presents initial situation
Example: "Okonkwo was a wealthy farmer in Umuofia village, known for his strength."
2. RISING ACTION (Complication):
 Conflict develops
587
 Tension builds
 Characters face challenges
 Suspense increases
Example: "When Okonkwo accidentally kills a clansman, he must flee for seven years."
3. CLIMAX (Turning Point):
 Highest point of tension
 Most intense moment
 Main conflict peaks
 Point of no return
Example: "Okonkwo kills the messenger, realizing his people will not fight the colonizers."
4. FALLING ACTION:
 Events after climax
 Tension decreases
 Consequences unfold
 Loose ends tie up
Example: "Okonkwo realizes he stands alone. Colonial officials come looking for him."
5. RESOLUTION (Denouement):
 Conclusion
 Conflicts resolved
 Final outcome revealed
 Story ends
Example: "The District Commissioner finds Okonkwo has taken his own life."
Types of Conflict:
588
 Man vs. Man: Character against another character
 Man vs. Self: Internal struggle
 Man vs. Society: Against social norms/rules
 Man vs. Nature: Against natural forces
 Man vs. Fate/Supernatural: Against destiny or supernatural
Plot Devices:
 Foreshadowing: Hints about future ("Dark clouds suggested trouble ahead")
 Flashback: Return to past events
 Suspense: Creates uncertainty
 Surprise/Twist: Unexpected turn
Characteristics of Good Plot:
 Causality (cause and effect)
 Unity (all events contribute)
 Plausibility (believable)
 Suspense (keeps interest)
 Conflict (provides tension)
Relationship between Setting and Plot:
 Setting creates conflict (harsh environment challenges characters)
 Plot events change setting (character moves)
 Time setting affects possibilities (technology, social norms)
 Place setting limits or enables actions
EVALUATION:
589
1. Define setting and list its three main components.
2. Explain how setting can influence plot with one example.
3. List and briefly describe the five plot elements.
4. Identify conflict type: (a) Character surviving a hurricane (b) Student fighting unfair
school rule.
5. Describe the setting (time and place) of your prose text.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a detailed description of your prose text's setting covering place, time, and
social/cultural environment (8-10 sentences).
2. Identify and describe the five plot elements in your prose text with specific examples.
3. Draw a plot diagram for your prose text, labeling all five elements and key events.
4. Explain how setting influences plot in your prose text (three specific examples).
5. Choose one chapter: identify (a) setting details (b) main conflict (c) how events
contribute to overall plot.

WEEK 3 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO CONSONANT SOUNDS
Meaning of Consonant Sounds:
Consonant sounds are speech sounds produced when the flow of air from the lungs is partly or
completely blocked by the tongue, teeth, or lips. In English, there are 24 consonant sounds
represented by different letters or combinations of letters.
Consonant sounds are categorized according to place of articulation (where the sound is made
in the mouth) and manner of articulation (how the sound is made).
Classification of English Consonant Sounds:
1. Plosives (Stops): Air is completely blocked and suddenly released.
Examples: /p/, /b/, /t/, /d/, /k/, /g/
o Examples in words: pen, ball, table, dog, cat, goat.
2. Fricatives: Air passes through a narrow opening causing friction.
Examples: /f/, /v/, /θ/, /ð/, /s/, /z/, /ʃ/, /ʒ/, /h/
o Examples in words: fish, van, thin, this, sun, zoo, shoe, vision, hat.
3. Affricates: A combination of a plosive and a fricative sound.
Examples: /tʃ/, /dʒ/
o Examples in words: church, judge.
4. Nasals: Air passes through the nose while the mouth is closed.
Examples: /m/, /n/, /ŋ/
o Examples in words: man, nine, song.
5. Lateral: The air passes along the sides of the tongue.
Example: /l/
o Examples in words: love, bell.
591
6. Approximants (Semi-vowels): Produced with little obstruction of air.
Examples: /r/, /w/, /j/
o Examples in words: red, window, yes.
Importance of Consonant Sounds:
 They help in correct pronunciation.
 They differentiate meaning between words (e.g., fan vs van).
 They contribute to rhythm and clarity in speech.
EVALUATION:
1. Define consonant sounds.
2. How many consonant sounds are there in English?
3. Mention three types of consonant sounds and give one example each.
4. Differentiate between /p/ and /b/ sounds with examples.
5. Explain why consonant sounds are important in speech.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. List all 24 English consonant sounds and write two example words for each.
2. Identify the consonant sounds in the following words: goat, fish, man, shoe, judge.
3. Write five pairs of words that differ by one consonant sound (e.g., fan–van, pat–bat).
592
WEEK 3 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: USE OF PASSIVE VOICE WITH EXAMPLE
The passive voice is used when the subject of the sentence receives the action instead of
performing it. The focus is on the action or the object rather than the doer.
Structure:
Object + Verb (be + past participle) + by + Subject
Example:
 Active: The boy kicked the ball.
 Passive: The ball was kicked by the boy.
Formation of Passive Voice:
To form the passive, the verb “to be” is combined with the past participle of the main verb.
 is/are + past participle (present tense)
o Active: They clean the room.
o Passive: The room is cleaned.
 was/were + past participle (past tense)
o Active: They wrote the letter.
o Passive: The letter was written.
Uses of the Passive Voice:
1. When the doer of the action is unknown:
o The window was broken last night.
2. When the action is more important than the doer:
o The new bridge was opened yesterday.
3. In formal or scientific writing:
o The experiment was conducted in a laboratory.
593
More Examples:
 Active: The teacher marked the scripts.
Passive: The scripts were marked by the teacher.
 Active: A mechanic repaired the car.
Passive: The car was repaired by a mechanic.
EVALUATION:
1. What is the passive voice?
2. Write the structure of a sentence in the passive voice.
3. Change these sentences to passive voice:
a. The boy ate the mango.
b. The nurse treated the patient.
4. State two reasons for using passive voice.
5. Convert: They will build a new school. to passive.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write five sentences in the active voice and change each to the passive voice.
2. Identify the tense used in each sentence after conversion.
3. Explain the difference in meaning or focus between the active and passive forms.
594
WEEK 3 – READING & COMPREHENSION
TOPIC: IDENTIFYING KEYWORDS IN PASSAGES THAT SIGNAL DIRECTION;
VOCABULARY – KITCHEN WORDS
Understanding Keywords in Directional Passages:
When reading a passage that gives directions or describes movement, certain keywords help the
reader to follow the movement or arrangement of things. These are known as directional words
or prepositions of movement and place.
Examples of Keywords That Signal Direction:
 Prepositions: in, on, under, between, behind, beside, near, through, across, along,
around, opposite, behind.
 Directional Words: left, right, north, south, forward, backward, inside, outside, top,
bottom.
Example Passage:
From the main gate, walk straight ahead until you reach the roundabout. Turn right and
continue until you see a tall building with a green roof. The kitchen is behind that building, next
to the dining hall.
Explanation:
The words straight ahead, right, behind, next to are keywords that show direction and help the
reader visualize the path described.
VOCABULARY – KITCHEN WORDS:
Kitchen utensils and tools are used in food preparation and cooking. Examples include:
 Spoon, fork, knife, plate, pot, frying pan, gas cooker, stove, cup, bowl, kettle, tray,
blender, chopping board, grater.
EVALUATION:
595

2. Mention five examples of words that signal direction.

4. Explain the importance of identifying directional words in a passage.

WEEK 3 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: EXPOSITORY ESSAY – THE EBOLA VIRUS
Meaning of Expository Essay:
An expository essay explains, informs, or describes a topic in a clear and logical manner. The
writer gives facts, examples, and explanations without expressing personal opinion or argument.
Features of an Expository Essay:
1. Introduction that states the topic clearly.
2. Logical presentation of facts.
3. Use of examples and illustrations.
4. Conclusion that summarizes the main points.
Topic: The Ebola Virus
The Ebola Virus Disease (EVD) is a severe and contagious illness that affects humans and some
animals. It was first discovered in the Democratic Republic of Congo near the Ebola River in
1976.
Causes:
 The disease is caused by the Ebola virus, which is transmitted from infected animals like
fruit bats, monkeys, and chimpanzees to humans.
 It also spreads through direct contact with blood, body fluids, or contaminated materials
of an infected person.
Symptoms:
 Fever, headache, muscle pain, sore throat, vomiting, diarrhea, internal bleeding, and rash.
Prevention:
 Avoid contact with infected people or animals.
 Wash hands regularly with soap and water.
597
 Use gloves and protective clothing when caring for the sick.
 Avoid eating bush meat.
 Report any suspected case to health authorities immediately.
EVALUATION:
1. What is an expository essay?
2. What causes the Ebola Virus Disease?
3. Mention four symptoms of Ebola.
4. List three ways of preventing Ebola.
5. Why is the Ebola Virus dangerous?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write an expository essay of about 200 words on “The Ebola Virus.”
2. Include introduction, body, and conclusion in your essay.
3. Suggest two ways the government can help prevent the spread of Ebola.

WEEK 3 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: POETRY – IDENTIFYING LANGUAGE IN A SELECTED POEM
Meaning of Poetry:
Poetry is a type of literary work written in lines and stanzas to express feelings, ideas, or
experiences through rhythm, imagery, and sound. Poets use special language called poetic
language to make their poems more expressive and beautiful.
Language in Poetry:
Poetic language includes figures of speech, sound devices, and imagery that create musicality
and emotion.
Features of Poetic Language:
1. Figures of Speech:
o Simile: comparison using “like” or “as.”
Example: Her smile is as bright as the morning sun.
o Metaphor: comparison without using “like” or “as.”
Example: Time is a thief.
o Personification: giving human qualities to non-human things.
Example: The wind whispered through the trees.
2. Sound Devices:
o Rhyme: repetition of similar sounds at the end of lines.
Example: The sun is high / up in the sky.
o Alliteration: repetition of initial consonant sounds.
Example: Peter Piper picked a peck of pickled peppers.
3. Imagery:
o Words that appeal to the senses (sight, sound, smell, touch, taste).
Example: The fragrant roses danced under the soft moonlight.
599
Example Poem:
“The Sun”
The golden sun climbs up the hill,
Warming the earth so calm and still.
Birds sing sweet songs to greet the day,
As children laugh and run to play.
Language Analysis:
 Rhyme: hill / still and day / play
 Personification: The sun climbs (as if it were human).
 Imagery: golden sun, warm earth, birds sing — appeals to sight and sound.
EVALUATION:
1. What is poetry?
2. Mention three features of poetic language.
3. Identify two figures of speech used in the poem above.
4. Explain one example of imagery from the poem.
5. Why do poets use poetic language?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Select any short poem and identify three figures of speech used in it.
2. Explain how the poet uses sound devices to create beauty in the poem.
3. Write your own four-line poem using rhyme and imagery.
600
WEEK 4 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: SYLLABLES – WORDS OF TWO, THREE, AND FOUR SYLLABLES
A syllable is a single, uninterrupted sound of a spoken word. It is the smallest unit of
pronunciation containing one vowel sound, with or without surrounding consonants. Every
English word is made up of one or more syllables.
How to Identify Syllables:
1. Clapping or tapping method: Each time you clap when pronouncing a word, you hear
one syllable.
o Example: teacher (tea/cher) – two claps → two syllables.
2. Listening for vowel sounds: Count the number of vowel sounds (not letters) in the word.
Each vowel sound represents a syllable.
o Example: important (im/por/tant) – three vowel sounds → three syllables.
3. Using pronunciation stress patterns: Long words can be divided into syllables based on
where natural pauses or stress occur.
Examples of Words According to Syllable Count:
 Two-syllable words: mother, father, table, garden, pencil.
 Three-syllable words: beautiful, remember, hospital, tomorrow, exciting.
 Four-syllable words: electricity, calculator, opportunity, motivation, celebration.
Importance of Syllable Division:
 It helps in accurate pronunciation and spelling.
 It aids in word stress and rhythm in speech.
 It assists in poetic meter and musical rhythm in language.
601
EVALUATION:
1. Define a syllable.
2. State two methods of identifying syllables in words.
3. Classify these words by the number of syllables: computer, doctor, opportunity, banana,
education.
4. Explain two reasons why syllable identification is important.
5. Write down three words each for two-, three-, and four-syllable categories.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write ten English words and divide them into syllables.
2. Identify the stressed syllable in the following words: teacher, banana, celebration,
tomorrow, electricity.
3. Write five four-syllable words and use each in a sentence to show their meaning.

WEEK 4 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: EXERCISES ON ACTIVE VOICE
The active voice is used when the subject of a sentence performs the action expressed by the
verb. The focus is on the doer of the action rather than the receiver.
Structure:
Subject + Verb + Object
 Example: The boy (subject) kicked (verb) the ball (object).
In contrast, the passive voice focuses on the receiver of the action:
 The ball was kicked by the boy.
Features of the Active Voice:
1. The subject performs the action.
2. The verb form is usually simple and direct.
3. The sentence sounds stronger and clearer.
4. It makes writing more engaging.
Examples:
 Ngozi baked a cake. (Active)
 A cake was baked by Ngozi. (Passive)
 The driver repaired the car. (Active)
 The car was repaired by the driver. (Passive)
Importance of Using Active Voice:
 It creates clearer and more concise sentences.
 It improves communication by showing responsibility for actions.
603
 It enhances reader engagement.
EVALUATION:
1. What is the active voice?
2. Mention two features of the active voice.
3. Change these sentences to active voice:
a. The prize was won by the pupil.
b. The song was sung by the choir.
4. Construct two original sentences in active voice.
5. State one reason why the active voice is preferred in speech and writing.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write five sentences in the active voice.
2. Convert each into the passive voice.
3. Identify the subject, verb, and object in each sentence.

WEEK 4 – READING
TOPIC: MAKING MEANING FROM SPATIAL DESCRIPTION; VOCABULARY –
WORDS FROM THE SICK BAY
Spatial Description means describing where things, people, or objects are located in a particular
space. It helps the reader to visualize or picture a place in their mind.
Key Words Used in Spatial Description:
 Prepositions of place: in, on, under, beside, between, behind, near, above, below,
around.
 Directional words: north, south, east, west, left, right, front, back, opposite.
Example Passage:
When I entered the sick bay, I noticed two white beds placed on the left side of the room.
Between the beds stood a small table with a water jug and a glass on it. On the right side, there
was a large cupboard where medicine bottles were neatly arranged. The nurse sat behind her
desk near the window, and a patient lay quietly on one of the beds.
Making Meaning:
From the passage, we can picture how the room looks based on the arrangement of objects and
their positions. The use of words like on the left, between, behind, near, and on the right helps us
create a mental image.
Vocabulary – Words from the Sick Bay:
Doctor, nurse, thermometer, injection, medicine, patient, stretcher, syringe, plaster, bandage,
prescription, tablet.
EVALUATION:
1. What is spatial description?
2. Mention four prepositions used in describing position.
605
3. From the passage, where was the nurse sitting?
4. Explain how spatial description helps a reader understand a passage.
5. Give the meanings of these words: thermometer, stretcher, plaster.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a short paragraph describing your classroom using spatial description.
2. Underline all the prepositions of place used in your paragraph.
3. List ten vocabulary words related to health or the sick bay.

WEEK 4 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY – TRIBALISM IS A LESSER EVIL THAN
CULTISM
Meaning of Argumentative Essay:
An argumentative essay is a form of writing in which the writer expresses opinions for or against
a given topic. The aim is to convince the reader to agree with your point of view using logical
reasoning and evidence.
Features of an Argumentative Essay:
1. Introduction: States the topic and the side you are supporting.
2. Body Paragraphs: Present your arguments with examples and evidence.
3. Opposing Viewpoint: Acknowledge the opposite side and refute it.
4. Conclusion: Summarize your arguments and restate your stand.
Topic Discussion: Tribalism is a Lesser Evil than Cultism
 For the Motion (Agreeing):
o Tribalism, though wrong, mainly shows preference for one’s tribe; it rarely leads
to crime or violence.
o Cultism involves killings, drug abuse, and destruction in schools and society.
o Tribalism can be corrected through education and unity campaigns.
 Against the Motion (Disagreeing):
o Tribalism destroys national unity and peace.
o It promotes hatred and discrimination in employment and leadership.
o Cultism affects mostly individuals, but tribalism divides a nation.
607
EVALUATION:
1. What is an argumentative essay?
2. List three features of an argumentative essay.
3. Mention two arguments for the motion.
4. Mention two arguments against the motion.
5. What should be included in the conclusion of an argumentative essay?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write an argumentative essay of about 250 words either for or against the motion
“Tribalism is a lesser evil than cultism.”
2. Highlight the topic sentence of each paragraph.
3. Identify and label the introduction and conclusion of your essay.

WEEK 4 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: MYTHS/LEGENDS – IDENTIFYING MORAL LESSONS; RECOMMENDED
DRAMA TEXT
Meaning of Myths:
Myths are ancient traditional stories that explain natural or supernatural events. They often
involve gods, spirits, or ancestors and are used to explain the origin of life, customs, or
phenomena.
Meaning of Legends:
Legends are old stories about real people who performed extraordinary deeds. They are partly
historical and partly imaginative.
FEATURES OF MYTHS AND LEGENDS:
1. They are passed down orally from generation to generation.
2. They involve supernatural beings or heroes with special powers.
3. They explain natural events or historical happenings.
4. They usually teach moral lessons or reflect the values of a community.
Examples:
 Myth: The story of Sango, the Yoruba god of thunder, teaches that pride and anger can
lead to destruction.
 Legend: The story of Queen Amina of Zazzau teaches courage, leadership, and patriotism.
 Myth: The Igbo story of The Tortoise and the Birds teaches against greed and deceit.
IDENTIFYING MORAL LESSONS IN MYTHS AND LEGENDS:
 Good triumphs over evil.
609
 Greed and pride lead to downfall.
 Honesty and hard work bring success.
 Disobedience leads to punishment.
Recommended Drama Text (Example):
In a play such as The Gods Are Not to Blame by Ola Rotimi, moral lessons are conveyed through
the fate of characters who disobey divine instruction, showing that destiny and pride can bring
tragedy.
EVALUATION:
1. Define myth and legend.
2. Mention three features of myths or legends.
3. State three moral lessons common to myths.
4. Mention one myth or legend from your culture and its moral lesson.
5. Explain one moral lesson from your recommended drama text.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a short myth or legend from your culture and state two moral lessons it teaches.
2. Identify any character from your recommended drama text who learned a moral lesson
and explain it.
3. List three ways myths and legends are useful to society today.

WEEK 6 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: SYLLABLES (CONTINUED)
A syllable is the smallest unit of pronunciation in a word. It usually contains a single vowel
sound, with or without surrounding consonants. Every word in English has at least one syllable.
In this lesson, we continue our study of syllables with a focus on word stress and syllable
division in longer words.
1. Word Stress:
In English, not all syllables are pronounced with equal strength. One syllable in a word is said
more strongly or loudly than the others — this is the stressed syllable. The others are
unstressed.
 Examples:
o TAble – the first syllable is stressed.
o beGIN – the second syllable is stressed.
o comPUter – the second syllable is stressed.
o eLECtricity – the third syllable is stressed.
Rules for Stress Placement (General Guide):
1. Two-syllable nouns – stress the first syllable.
o TAble, WINdow, DOCtor
2. Two-syllable verbs – stress the second syllable.
o beGIN, reLAX, aRRIVE
3. Longer words (3+ syllables) – stress often falls on the third syllable from the end.
o eDUcation, opPORtunity, comPUter.
2. Dividing Words into Syllables:
Divide long words based on vowel sounds and pronunciation patterns.
611
 Information → in/for/ma/tion (4 syllables)
 Hospital → hos/pi/tal (3 syllables)
 Electricity → e/lec/tri/ci/ty (5 syllables)
Importance of Syllable Awareness:
 It improves pronunciation and spelling.
 It helps in correct stress placement.
 It aids rhythm and fluency in speech.
EVALUATION:
1. What is a syllable?
2. Define word stress.
3. Identify the stressed syllable in these words: banana, begin, teacher, important,
opportunity.
4. Mention two rules for placing stress in words.
5. Divide these words into syllables: hospital, education, wonderful, electricity, table.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write ten English words of three or more syllables and mark the stressed syllable in each.
2. Explain how stress affects the meaning of words (e.g., record as a noun vs record as a
verb).
3. Write five two-syllable nouns and five two-syllable verbs.
612
WEEK 6 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: EXERCISES ON PASSIVE VOICE
The passive voice is used when the focus is on the receiver of the action, not the doer. It is
formed by combining the verb “to be” with the past participle of the main verb.
Structure:
Object + be (is/are/was/were) + Past Participle + by + Subject (optional)
Example:
 Active: The teacher praised the student.
 Passive: The student was praised (by the teacher).
Step-by-Step Conversion:
1. Identify the subject, verb, and object.
o The boy (subject) kicked (verb) the ball (object).
2. Move the object to the front to make it the new subject.
3. Add the correct form of be (is, are, was, were, etc.).
4. Use the past participle of the main verb.
5. Add the original subject (if needed) after by.
o The ball was kicked by the boy.
Examples in Different Tenses:
 Present tense: They clean the room → The room is cleaned.
 Past tense: They built the house → The house was built.
 Future tense: They will open the shop → The shop will be opened.
 Present perfect: They have written the test → The test has been written.
613
Reasons for Using Passive Voice:
1. When the doer of the action is unknown or unimportant.
o The letter was posted yesterday.
2. To emphasize the action or result rather than the doer.
o The bridge was completed last year.
3. In formal or scientific writing.
o The data were collected and analyzed.
EVALUATION:
1. What is the passive voice?
2. Write the structure of the passive voice.
3. Convert the following to passive voice:
a. The tailor sewed the dress.
b. The students answered the questions.
4. State two uses of the passive voice.
5. Change this sentence to active: The car was repaired by the mechanic.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write five sentences in the active voice and change them to the passive voice.
2. Identify the tense in each passive sentence.
3. Explain one situation where the passive voice is preferred in writing.
614
WEEK 6 - READING
TOPIC: READING COMPREHENSION PRACTICE; VOCABULARY – COOKING
WORDS
Reading Comprehension Practice involves understanding, interpreting, and answering
questions based on a given passage. It requires identifying main ideas, supporting details, cause
and effect, and vocabulary meanings from context.
Example Passage (Excerpt):
Cooking is an important daily activity in every home. It involves preparing food to make it safe
and enjoyable to eat. In the kitchen, different tools such as pots, frying pans, spoons, and knives
are used. Good cooking requires patience, cleanliness, and knowledge of ingredients. A person
who cooks well is respected in the family because food brings people together.
Comprehension Focus:
 Main Idea: The importance and process of cooking in homes.
 Supporting Details: Cooking involves patience, cleanliness, and use of utensils.
 Moral Lesson: Cooking is both a skill and a responsibility.
Vocabulary – Cooking Words:
1. Utensils: tools used in cooking, e.g., spoon, pot, knife.
2. Ingredients: materials used to prepare a meal, e.g., salt, pepper, oil, tomatoes.
3. Boil: to heat a liquid until it bubbles.
4. Fry: to cook in hot oil.
5. Stew: a dish made by cooking meat or vegetables slowly in liquid.
6. Simmer: to cook just below boiling point.
7. Recipe: a set of instructions for preparing a dish.
615
EVALUATION:

WEEK 6 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY – CULTISM ERODES TRADITIONAL VALUES
Meaning of Argumentative Essay:
An argumentative essay presents two opposing opinions about a topic. The writer must choose
one side and support it with reasons, examples, and evidence.
Topic Discussion: Cultism Erodes Traditional Values
FOR THE MOTION (Agreeing):
1. Cultism destroys respect for elders and authority. In many schools, cult members
disobey teachers and parents.
2. It promotes violence and hatred. Traditional values such as peace, unity, and love are
replaced by rivalry and fear.
3. Cultism encourages immorality. Members engage in evil acts like fighting, stealing,
and drug use, contrary to traditional discipline.

divides students and groups.
AGAINST THE MOTION (Disagreeing):
1. Traditional values are already declining due to modernization, not cultism alone.
2. Some people join cults to seek protection or belonging, not to destroy values.
3. The real problem lies in poor moral upbringing and lack of parental guidance.
Features of an Argumentative Essay:
1. Introduction stating your stand.
2. Logical arguments supported by examples.
3. Counter-arguments and rebuttals.
4. Conclusion restating your position.

WEEK 6 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: POETRY – MEANING OF SELECTED POEM; IDENTIFICATION OF
FIGURES OF SPEECH
Meaning of Poetry:
Poetry is a form of literary expression that uses carefully chosen words arranged in lines and
stanzas to convey emotions, ideas, or experiences. Poets often use figures of speech, imagery,
and sound devices to make their poems more expressive and musical.
Meaning of a Poem:
To find the meaning of a poem, readers must understand:
 The subject matter (what the poem is about).
 The theme (the central idea or message).
 The tone (the poet’s feeling or attitude).
Selected Poem (Example):
“Peace”
Peace is like a gentle dove,
Flying softly high above.
It calms the hearts of those who fight,
And fills the world with shining light.
Meaning of the Poem:
The poem describes peace as a gentle and precious quality that brings calmness and unity to the
world. The poet uses the image of a dove, which symbolizes love, purity, and harmony.
Identification of Figures of Speech:
1. Simile: Peace is like a gentle dove – compares peace to a dove using “like.”
2. Personification: It calms the hearts of those who fight – peace is given human qualities.
3. Imagery: shining light – appeals to the sense of sight.
619
4. Rhyme: dove/above and fight/light create musicality.
Themes Found in the Poem:
 The value of peace in society.
 The power of love and unity.
 The hope for a better world.
EVALUATION:
1. What is poetry?
2. State the theme of the poem “Peace.”
3. Identify two figures of speech used in the poem.
4. Explain the imagery used in the poem.
5. Why is rhyme important in poetry?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Select any short poem and write out its central theme.
2. Identify three figures of speech used in your selected poem.
3. Write a short four-line poem about “Peace” or “Unity” using at least one figure of speech.

WEEK 8 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: CONSONANT CLUSTERS (ASKS, SCHOOL, THRIVE, PLEASE, RESPECT)
A consonant cluster is a group of two or more consonant sounds that appear together in a word
without any vowel sound between them. These clusters can occur at the beginning, middle, or
end of words.
1. Definition:
A consonant cluster is a combination of consonant sounds pronounced closely together, such that
each sound is heard distinctly.
2. Types of Consonant Clusters:
 Initial clusters: occur at the beginning of a word.
o Examples: school (sk), thrive (thr), please (pl), respect (sp), blue (bl), drive (dr).
 Medial clusters: occur in the middle of a word.
o Examples: respect (sp), transport (ns), complain (mpl).
 Final clusters: occur at the end of a word.
o Examples: asks (sks), helps (lps), camps (mps), months (nθs).
3. Pronunciation Practice:
Students should pronounce each consonant distinctly but smoothly without inserting extra
vowels.
 Asks → /æsks/ (not /askiz/)
 School → /skuːl/
 Thrive → /θraɪv/
 Please → /pliːz/
 Respect → /rɪˈspekt/
621
4. Common Difficulties:
Learners often insert vowels between consonants, especially in clusters like str-, spl-, thr-, skr-,
etc.
For instance:
 School (not “iskool”)
 Thrive (not “tirive”)
 Respect (not “irespect”).
5. Importance of Learning Clusters:
 It improves pronunciation and fluency.
 It enhances spelling accuracy.
 It helps in correct reading of English words.
EVALUATION:
1. What is a consonant cluster?
2. Identify the cluster in each of these words: school, thrive, respect, blue, asks.
3. Give two examples of initial and final consonant clusters.
4. Explain one pronunciation challenge of clusters.
5. Pronounce these words correctly: please, thrive, scream, camps, months.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write ten English words containing consonant clusters and underline the clusters.
2. Record yourself pronouncing the words school, respect, thrive, helps, and asks for selfassessment.
3. State three reasons why learning consonant clusters is important in speech.
622
623
WEEK 8 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: STATEMENTS AND TAG QUESTIONS
A statement is a sentence that expresses a fact, opinion, or idea. It usually ends with a full stop.
A tag question is a short question added to the end of a statement, often to seek agreement or
confirmation.
1. Examples of Statements:
 She is a teacher.
 They play football every evening.
 The weather is hot today.
2. Structure of Tag Questions:
A tag question consists of an auxiliary verb and a pronoun that refers to the subject of the main
sentence.
Basic Rules:
1. If the statement is positive, the tag is negative.
o She is a teacher, isn’t she?
o They play football, don’t they?
2. If the statement is negative, the tag is positive.
o She isn’t here, is she?
o They don’t like rice, do they?
3. The auxiliary verb in the tag must agree with the tense of the main verb.
o He was at school, wasn’t he?
o They will come, won’t they?
3. Uses of Tag Questions:
624
 To confirm information: You’re coming, aren’t you?
 To show politeness: It’s a lovely day, isn’t it?
 To express doubt or surprise: You didn’t see him, did you?
4. Common Errors:
 Using the wrong auxiliary verb: He can swim, isn’t he? ❌ → He can swim, can’t he? ✅
 Using the wrong pronoun: Mary is kind, isn’t it? ❌ → Mary is kind, isn’t she? ✅
EVALUATION:
1. What is a statement?
2. Define a tag question.
3. Change these into tag questions:
a. She is very tall.
b. They have gone home.
c. He cannot drive.
4. Mention two uses of tag questions.
5. Correct this error: They will come, isn’t it?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write five statements and attach correct tag questions.
2. Explain the rule for forming negative tags.
3. Write two reasons why people use tag questions in speech.
625
WEEK 8 - READING
TOPIC: IDENTIFYING TOPIC SENTENCES; VOCABULARY – TEACHING WORDS
A topic sentence expresses the main idea of a paragraph. It tells what the paragraph is about. It
can appear at the beginning, middle, or end of a paragraph.
1. Characteristics of a Good Topic Sentence:
 It states one main idea clearly.
 It is general enough to allow for supporting details.
 It gives direction to the paragraph.
Example:
 Teaching is one of the most important professions in the world.
Supporting details may then explain why teaching is important.
2. Identifying Topic Sentences:
To identify a topic sentence, look for:
 The sentence that captures the main idea.
 Sentences that other sentences explain or support.
Example Paragraph:
Teachers play an important role in the development of every nation. They prepare students for
future leadership by teaching knowledge and values. Without teachers, society cannot progress.
 Topic Sentence: “Teachers play an important role in the development of every nation.”
3. Vocabulary – Teaching Words:
Word Meaning Example
Teacher A person who educates students The teacher explained the lesson clearly.
Lesson A period of instruction The first lesson is on Mathematics.
Classroom A room where students learn Our classroom is clean and bright.
626
Subject A branch of learning English is my favorite subject.
Homework Work assigned to be done at home We submitted our homework this morning.
Pupil/Student A learner in a school The pupils listened attentively.
EVALUATION:
1. What is a topic sentence?
2. Identify the topic sentence in this paragraph: Education shapes the future of every child.
It teaches discipline and knowledge. It helps individuals become useful citizens.
3. Write three features of a good topic sentence.
4. Mention three teaching-related words and their meanings.
5. Use “teacher” and “lesson” in meaningful sentences.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write a short paragraph on “My Best Teacher” and underline the topic sentence.
2. List five teaching-related words and use each in a sentence.
3. Explain why identifying topic sentences is important in reading comprehension.

WEEK 8 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: DIFFERENCES BETWEEN ARGUMENTATIVE AND EXPOSITORY ESSAYS
Both argumentative and expository essays aim to inform, but they differ in purpose, tone, and
method.
1. Argumentative Essay:
 The writer takes a stand on an issue (for or against).
 It involves persuasion using reasons, facts, and examples.
 The writer presents both sides but supports one.
Example Topic: School uniforms should be abolished.
 For: Uniforms limit freedom.
 Against: Uniforms promote equality.
2. Expository Essay:
 The writer explains or describes something.
 It gives information without taking sides.
 It uses facts, definitions, and examples.
Example Topic: The Importance of Good Eating Habits.
 Explains what good eating habits are and their benefits.
3. Key Differences Between Argumentative and Expository Essays:
Feature Argumentative Essay Expository Essay
Purpose To argue or persuade To explain or describe
Tone Subjective and persuasive Objective and informative
Structure Presents two sides of an issue Explains one topic logically
Language Uses emotional and logical appeal Uses clear, factual language
628
Examples For/Against Cultism, Capital
Punishment
How Rain Is Formed, The Effects of
Pollution
EVALUATION:
1. Define an argumentative essay.
2. Define an expository essay.
3. Mention two differences between both types of essays.
4. Write one topic suitable for each essay type.
5. Why is an argumentative essay persuasive in nature?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write an expository essay on “How to Prevent Malaria.”
2. Write an argumentative essay on “Television does more harm than good.”
3. List four major differences between argumentative and expository essays.

WEEK 8 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT – WRITE A SHORT PLAY OR STORY
Meaning of Drama:
Drama is a story written to be performed by actors on stage. It involves characters, dialogue,
actions, and conflict.
1. Elements of Drama:
 Characters: The people in the play.
 Setting: Where and when the story happens.
 Dialogue: The words spoken by the characters.
 Plot: The sequence of events.
 Theme: The main idea or message of the play.
2. Writing a Short Play:
When writing a short play, include the following:
 Title: e.g., Respect for Elders
 Characters: e.g., Father, Mother, Bayo, Teacher.
 Setting: In a school compound.
 Dialogue Example:
Sample Scene:
Title: Respect for Elders
Characters: Teacher, Bayo, Mother
Teacher: Bayo, why did you walk away when your mother called you?
Bayo: I was in a hurry, sir.
Mother: You must learn to respect elders, my son.
630
Bayo: I’m sorry, mother. I won’t do that again.
Teacher: Good. Respect brings peace to the home and society.
Theme: The importance of respect and good manners.
3. Features of a Good Play:
 Short and clear dialogue.
 Teaches moral lessons.
 Realistic actions and characters.

WEEK 9 - SPEECH WORK
TOPIC: WORD BOUNDARIES – LINKING SOUNDS (MATTER OF FACT, BREAD
AND BUTTER, POOR AND BLIND)
In connected speech, English speakers often link words together to make speech flow smoothly.
This is known as linking sounds or connected speech. The boundaries between words are not
always clearly heard; instead, one word runs into the next.
1. Meaning of Word Boundaries:
Word boundaries are the places where one word ends and another begins in speech. When we
speak naturally, we do not pause between every word; instead, we link the final sound of one
word with the beginning sound of the next.
2. Linking Sounds:
Linking happens when the final consonant of one word is joined with the initial vowel sound of
the next word.
Examples:
 Matter of fact → /ˈmætər əv fækt/ (sounds like matər əv fækt)
 Bread and butter → /bred ən bʌtə/ (sounds like bredan bʌtə)
 Poor and blind → /pɔːr ən blaɪnd/ (sounds like pooran blaind)
3. Types of Linking:
 Consonant to Vowel Linking:
o Look at it → /luk æt ɪt/
o Go on → /gəʊ wɒn/
 Vowel to Vowel Linking (with /j/ or /w/ sound):
o He is → /hiː jɪz/
o Go out → /gəʊ waʊt/
632
4. Why Linking Is Important:
 It makes speech sound natural and fluent.
 It improves listening comprehension.
 It reduces unnatural pauses between words.
EVALUATION:
1. What are word boundaries?
2. Define linking sounds.
3. Identify the linking sounds in: bread and butter, matter of fact, poor and blind.
4. Mention two benefits of linking sounds.
5. Give two examples of consonant-to-vowel linking.
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write five examples of linked expressions in English.
2. Record and listen to yourself pronouncing bread and butter, matter of fact, poor and
blind.
3. Explain why word boundaries are important in fluent speech.
633
WEEK 9 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: DIFFERENTIATING BETWEEN POLAR AND TAG QUESTIONS
1. Meaning of Questions:
Questions are sentences used to ask for information. English questions can be grouped into polar
questions and tag questions.
2. Polar Questions:
These are questions that require only two possible answers — Yes or No.
 Examples:
o Are you coming?
o Did she pass the test?
o Will it rain today?
o Has he eaten?
Features of Polar Questions:
 Begin with auxiliary verbs (is, are, do, did, will, have, etc.).
 Expect short answers like Yes, I am or No, I’m not.
 Used for confirmation, inquiry, or curiosity.
3. Tag Questions:
A tag question is a short question added at the end of a statement to seek agreement or
confirmation.
 She is a nurse, isn’t she?
 They didn’t come, did they?
 You can drive, can’t you?
4. Differences Between Polar and Tag Questions:
634
Feature Polar Question Tag Question
Form Begins a sentence Comes at the end of a statement
Answer Type Yes/No Agreement/Confirmation
Example Is he ready? He is ready, isn’t he?
Use To ask for information To confirm or agree
EVALUATION:
1. Define a polar question.
2. Define a tag question.
3. Give two examples of each.
4. State two differences between polar and tag questions.
5. Complete this: You were there yesterday, ____?
ASSIGNMENT:
1. Write three polar questions and their expected answers.
2. Convert these to tag questions:
a. You are happy.
b. She can sing.
c. They didn’t come.
3. Explain why tag questions are used in conversation.

 Main idea

WEEK 9 - COMPOSITION
TOPIC: EXPOSITORY ESSAY – HOW TO PLANT TREES
Meaning of Expository Essay:
An expository essay explains how something works or how something is done. It gives clear
information without trying to argue.
Example Topic: How to Plant Trees
Outline:
1. Introduction – Importance of tree planting.
2. Steps in planting trees.
3. Benefits of tree planting.
4. Conclusion – Encourage everyone to plant trees.
Sample Essay:
Tree planting helps protect the environment and beautify the surroundings. To plant a tree, first,
choose good soil and dig a hole about one foot deep. Next, place the young plant in the hole and
cover it with soil. Water the plant regularly and protect it from animals. Trees provide shade,
prevent erosion, and produce oxygen. Everyone should plant at least one tree to make the world a
better place.
EVALUATION:
1. Define an expository essay.
2. State three steps involved in planting trees.
3. Mention two benefits of tree planting.
4. What is the purpose of an expository essay?
5. Write one sentence from the essay that shows conclusion.

ASSIGNMENT:

WEEK 9 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH
TOPIC: RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT – USING SIMILES AND METAPHORS IN
SENTENCES
1. Revision of Figures of Speech:
 Simile: A direct comparison using like or as.
o He is as brave as a lion.
 Metaphor: An indirect comparison without like or as.
o He is a lion in battle.
2. Application in Drama:
Writers use similes and metaphors to make dialogue more vivid.
Examples in Dialogue:
 Mother: “My son works like a machine!” (Simile)
 Teacher: “Knowledge is light.” (Metaphor)
3. Exercises:
Convert similes to metaphors and vice versa:
 As white as snow → Snow-white (metaphor).
 He is a rock → He is as strong as a rock.
EVALUATION:
1. Define simile.
2. Define metaphor.
3. Give two examples of each.
640
4. Convert these similes to metaphors:
a. As wise as an owl.
b. As busy as a bee.
5. Why do writers use these figures of speech?
ASSIGNMENT:

2. Create a short dialogue using at least two similes.
